# SIE Practice Question Bank

782 original questions, grouped by chapter. Answers and explanations follow each question. The same questions power the quizzes and timed exams in `app/index.html`; the machine-readable source is `question-bank.json` (fields: `ch` chapter, `sec` section, `fs` FINRA outline section 1–4, `q`, `o` options, `a` answer index, `e` explanation, `k` rule to remember, `w` why each wrong option is wrong, `m` exam tip, `steps` worked arithmetic). This file is generated by `app/build.py`.

---

## Chapter 1: Common Stock

**Q1** (§1.1 · FINRA section 2) — A corporation's charter authorizes 10 million shares. It has sold 6 million shares to the public and later repurchased 1 million of them, which it holds as treasury stock. How many shares are outstanding?

- A. 10 million
- B. 6 million
- C. 5 million
- D. 4 million

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Outstanding = issued − treasury = 6M − 1M = 5 million. Treasury stock carries no vote and receives no dividends.

1. Issued = 6M
2. Treasury = 1M
3. Outstanding = 6M − 1M = 5M

*Remember:* Outstanding shares = issued shares − treasury shares.

</details>

**Q2** (§1.2.2 · FINRA section 2) — An investor owns 200 shares of a company that is electing four directors. Under cumulative voting, the investor may cast:

- A. 200 votes for each of the four seats
- B. 800 votes total, allocated in any way
- C. 50 votes for each seat
- D. 800 votes, but no more than 200 for any one candidate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Cumulative voting gives shares × seats = 800 votes that can be distributed in any way, including all on one candidate. This benefits small shareholders. Statutory voting would be 200 votes per seat.

1. Votes = 200 shares × 4 seats
2. = 800 votes, allocated freely

*Remember:* Cumulative voting: total votes = shares × seats, spread any way, even all on one candidate.

</details>

**Q3** (§1.2.4 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is TRUE of a warrant compared to a stock right?

- A. A warrant is short-term and its exercise price is below market at issuance
- B. A warrant is long-term and its exercise price is above market at issuance
- C. Warrants are issued only to existing shareholders under preemptive rights
- D. Warrants cannot be traded separately from the bond they accompany

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Warrants are long-term (often years), issued with an exercise price above the current market, and typically attached to bond or preferred offerings as sweeteners. Rights are short-term, priced below market, and given to existing shareholders.

*Remember:* Warrants: long-term, exercise price above market. Rights: short-term, price below market, for existing holders.

</details>

**Q4** (§1.3.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer buys 100 shares of a listed stock on Tuesday, June 3. Under regular-way settlement, the trade settles on:

- A. Tuesday, June 3
- B. Wednesday, June 4
- C. Thursday, June 5
- D. Friday, June 6

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Corporate securities settle T+1 — one business day after the trade date. Wednesday, June 4. The Reg T payment deadline would be S+2 (Friday).

*Remember:* Regular-way corporate stock trades settle T+1, one business day after trade date.

</details>

**Q5** (§1.3.4 · FINRA section 2) — A company declares a $0.50 dividend payable to holders of record on Thursday, March 20. To receive the dividend, an investor must purchase the stock no later than:

- A. Tuesday, March 18
- B. Wednesday, March 19
- C. Thursday, March 20
- D. Friday, March 21

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Under T+1 settlement the ex-dividend date is the same day as the record date (March 20). To be a holder of record, the purchase must settle by March 20, so the trade must occur by March 19 — the last day before the ex-date.

*Remember:* Under T+1, ex-date equals record date; buy by the business day before the record date.

</details>

**Q6** (§1.3.5 · FINRA section 2) — The maximum potential loss for an investor who sells a stock short is:

- A. The sale proceeds
- B. The margin deposit
- C. Unlimited
- D. The current market value minus the margin deposit

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A short seller must eventually buy the stock back, and there is no ceiling on how high a stock can rise, so the loss potential is unlimited. Maximum gain is the sale proceeds (stock goes to zero).

*Remember:* Short sellers face unlimited loss; their maximum gain is the sale proceeds.

</details>

**Q7** (§1.3.6 · FINRA section 2) — An investor holding an American Depositary Receipt is exposed to all of the following EXCEPT:

- A. Currency risk
- B. Market risk of the foreign company's stock
- C. Having to receive dividends in a foreign currency
- D. Political risk in the foreign country

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** ADRs are priced in US dollars and pay dividends in US dollars (converted by the depositary bank). The holder still bears currency risk because the conversion rate affects the dollar value.

*Remember:* ADRs trade and pay dividends in US dollars, but holders still bear currency and political risk.

</details>

**Q8** (§1.2.5 · FINRA section 2) — An investor owns 300 shares of XYZ at $90. XYZ announces a 3-for-2 stock split. After the split the investor will own:

- A. 450 shares at $60
- B. 200 shares at $135
- C. 450 shares at $90
- D. 600 shares at $45

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** 3-for-2: shares × 3/2 = 450; price × 2/3 = $60. Total value stays $27,000. Ownership percentage is unchanged.

1. Shares = 300 × 3/2 = 450
2. Price = $90 × 2/3 = $60
3. Check: 450 × $60 = $27,000 = 300 × $90

*Remember:* Stock split: new shares = old × ratio; new price = old ÷ ratio; total value unchanged.

</details>

**Q9** (§1.5 · FINRA section 2) — A company has net income of $8 million, pays $2 million in preferred dividends, and has 3 million common shares outstanding. Its stock trades at $40. The P/E ratio is:

- A. 15
- B. 20
- C. 13.3
- D. 12

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** EPS = ($8M − $2M) ÷ 3M = $2.00. P/E = $40 ÷ $2 = 20.

1. Earnings to common = $8M − $2M = $6M
2. EPS = $6M ÷ 3M = $2.00
3. P/E = $40 ÷ $2 = 20

*Remember:* EPS = (net income − preferred dividends) ÷ common shares; P/E = price ÷ EPS.

</details>

**Q10** (§1.3.7 · FINRA section 3) — A tender offer for a company's shares must remain open for at least:

- A. 10 business days
- B. 20 business days
- C. 30 calendar days
- D. 45 calendar days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** SEC rules require tender offers to stay open at least 20 business days. Investors may only tender shares they actually own (net long).

*Remember:* Tender offers must stay open at least 20 business days.

</details>

**Q1001** (§1.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following statements about treasury stock is TRUE?

- A. It must be permanently retired once repurchased
- B. It is counted in shares outstanding when computing EPS
- C. It receives dividends but cannot be voted
- D. It has no voting rights and receives no dividends

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Treasury stock is stock the issuer has repurchased and holds. It is not outstanding, so it cannot vote and receives no dividends.

*Remember:* Treasury stock: issued but repurchased; no vote, no dividends, not outstanding.

</details>

**Q1002** (§1.1 · FINRA section 2) — A company has 40 million shares outstanding trading at $60 per share. Based on common market-cap ranges, the company is best described as:

- A. Mid-cap
- B. Small-cap
- C. Large-cap
- D. Micro-cap

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Market cap = $60 x 40 million = $2.4 billion, which falls in the roughly $2 billion to $10 billion mid-cap range.

1. $60 x 40,000,000 = $2,400,000,000
2. $2.4B is between $2B and $10B = mid-cap

*Remember:* Market cap = share price x shares outstanding; mid-cap is about $2B-$10B.

</details>

**Q1003** (§1.1 · FINRA section 2) — An investor's entire portfolio is one technology company's stock. She worries that the company's main product will fail. Which risk is she describing, and which can be reduced by diversifying?

- A. Inflation risk
- B. Market (systematic) risk
- C. Legislative risk
- D. Business (non-systematic) risk

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Risk tied to one company's performance is business, or non-systematic, risk. Spreading money across many companies reduces it.

*Remember:* Business (non-systematic) risk is diversifiable; market (systematic) risk is not.

</details>

**Q1004** (§1.1 · FINRA section 2) — Under the SEC's penny stock rules, which of the following would MOST likely be a penny stock?

- A. An NYSE-listed stock trading at $4.50
- B. A Nasdaq-listed stock trading at $3
- C. An unlisted OTC stock trading at $4
- D. An unlisted OTC stock trading at $8

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A penny stock is a stock that is not listed on an exchange and trades under $5 per share. Only the $4 OTC stock meets both tests.

*Remember:* Penny stock: non-exchange-listed and under $5 per share (SEC Rule 15g-9).

</details>

**Q1005** (§1.2.1 · FINRA section 2) — A corporation with 5 million common shares outstanding declares dividends totaling $2 million. A customer who owns 25,000 shares will receive:

- A. $25,000
- B. $0.40
- C. $4,000
- D. $10,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The dividend is $2 million / 5 million = $0.40 per share, paid pro rata. 25,000 shares x $0.40 = $10,000.

1. $2,000,000 / 5,000,000 = $0.40 per share
2. 25,000 x $0.40 = $10,000

*Remember:* Dividend per share = total dividend / shares outstanding; customer gets per-share amount x shares owned.

</details>

**Q1006** (§1.2.2 · FINRA section 2) — An investor owns 300 shares of a company that uses statutory voting. Four director seats are up for election. The investor may cast:

- A. 1,200 votes that may all go to one candidate
- B. Up to 300 votes for each of the four seats
- C. 300 votes in total, divided among the seats as she wishes
- D. 75 votes for each seat

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Statutory voting gives one vote per share for each seat, so she can cast up to 300 votes for each of the four seats. She cannot stack them on one candidate.

*Remember:* Statutory: shares per seat, no stacking. Cumulative: shares x seats, may stack.

</details>

**Q1007** (§1.2.2 · FINRA section 2) — Common stockholders generally vote on all of the following EXCEPT:

- A. Whether to declare a quarterly cash dividend
- B. A proposed merger with another company
- C. A 2-for-1 stock split
- D. Election of the board of directors

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Declaring dividends is the board's decision. Shareholders elect the board and vote on major matters such as mergers, splits and charter changes.

*Remember:* The board declares dividends; shareholders do not vote on them.

</details>

**Q1008** (§1.2.3 · FINRA section 2) — A common stockholder asks to review company records. She is generally NOT entitled to inspect:

- A. The list of shareholders
- B. The annual report
- C. Audited financial statements
- D. Management's day-to-day internal records

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The inspection right covers the annual report, audited financials and the shareholder list. It does not extend to day-to-day internal records.

*Remember:* Inspection right: annual report, audited financials and shareholder list; not daily internal records.

</details>

**Q1009** (§1.2.4 · FINRA section 2) — A company announces a rights offering. A shareholder who receives rights but does NOT want to buy more stock may:

- A. Sell the rights in the secondary market
- B. Return the rights to the company for a cash refund
- C. Convert the rights into long-term warrants
- D. Hold the rights for several years until the stock rises

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Because the subscription price is below market, rights have value and trade in the secondary market. A holder may exercise, sell, or let them expire.

*Remember:* Rights: exercise, sell, or let expire; short-term, priced below market.

</details>

**Q1010** (§1.2.4 · FINRA section 2) — A shareholder owns 2% of a company's common stock. The company plans to issue a large block of new shares. Which right lets her buy enough new shares to keep her 2% stake?

- A. Cumulative voting right
- B. Preemptive right
- C. Inspection right
- D. Residual claim right

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The preemptive right gives existing shareholders the first chance to buy new shares in proportion to their ownership. It is carried out through a rights offering.

*Remember:* Preemptive right = maintain proportionate ownership, delivered through a rights offering.

</details>

**Q1011** (§1.2.4 · FINRA section 2) — A corporation is selling a new bond issue and wants to lower the interest rate it must pay. It would MOST likely attach:

- A. An ADR
- B. Subscription rights
- C. A call feature
- D. Warrants

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Warrants are often attached to bond or preferred offerings as a sweetener. The added value lets the issuer pay a lower rate.

*Remember:* Warrants are long-term sweeteners attached to bonds/preferred to cut the issuer's borrowing cost.

</details>

**Q1012** (§1.2.5 · FINRA section 2) — An investor owns 2,000 shares of a stock trading at $1.20. The company does a 1-for-8 reverse split. After the split, the investor will own:

- A. 250 shares at $1.20
- B. 16,000 shares at $0.15
- C. 250 shares at $9.60
- D. 2,000 shares at $9.60

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A 1-for-8 split leaves 1/8 of the shares at 8 times the price: 250 shares at $9.60. The total value stays $2,400.

1. Shares: 2,000 x 1/8 = 250
2. Price: $1.20 x 8 = $9.60
3. Check: 250 x $9.60 = $2,400 = 2,000 x $1.20

*Remember:* Split A-for-B: new shares = old x A/B; new price = old x B/A; total value unchanged.

</details>

**Q1013** (§1.2.5 · FINRA section 2) — An investor owns 200 shares of a stock trading at $60. The company pays a 20% stock dividend. Assuming no other price change, the investor will then hold:

- A. 240 shares at $50
- B. 240 shares at $60
- C. 200 shares at $50 plus $2,400 in cash
- D. 220 shares at $54.55

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A 20% stock dividend adds 40 shares (240 total). The $12,000 total value is spread over 240 shares, so the price adjusts to $50.

1. New shares = 200 x 1.20 = 240
2. New price = $60 / 1.20 = $50
3. Value stays $12,000

*Remember:* Stock dividend: shares x (1 + %); price = old price / (1 + %); total value unchanged.

</details>

**Q1014** (§1.2.5 · FINRA section 2) — After a 2-for-1 forward stock split, which of the following will change for a shareholder?
I. Number of shares held
II. Price per share
III. Total market value of the holding
IV. Percentage ownership of the company

- A. I and II
- B. I and III
- C. II and IV
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A split changes the share count and the per-share price. Total value and ownership percentage stay the same.

*Remember:* Splits change shares and price only, never total value or ownership %.

</details>

**Q1015** (§1.2.6 · FINRA section 2) — A corporation is liquidated. Among the following, which group is paid FIRST?

- A. General unsecured creditors
- B. Preferred stockholders
- C. Subordinated debenture holders
- D. Common stockholders

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** General unsecured creditors are paid before subordinated debenture holders, then preferred stockholders, then common stockholders.

*Remember:* Liquidation: secured, admin/wages/taxes, unsecured, subordinated, preferred, common last.

</details>

**Q1016** (§1.2.7 · FINRA section 2) — Which party makes sure a corporation never has more shares outstanding than its charter authorizes?

- A. The transfer agent
- B. The registrar
- C. The Depository Trust Company
- D. The managing underwriter

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The registrar audits the transfer agent's work and makes sure shares outstanding never exceed authorized shares.

*Remember:* Transfer agent records ownership and pays dividends; registrar checks outstanding does not exceed authorized.

</details>

**Q1017** (§1.2.7 · FINRA section 3) — A customer's shares are held in street name. This means the shares are:

- A. Held by the transfer agent in the issuer's name
- B. Registered in the customer's name and mailed as certificates
- C. Owned outright by the broker-dealer as firm inventory
- D. Registered in the broker-dealer's name, with the customer as beneficial owner

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** In street name, the broker-dealer is the registered owner for convenience, while the customer is the beneficial owner.

*Remember:* Street name: registered to the broker-dealer; customer is the beneficial owner.

</details>

**Q1018** (§1.3.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is a redeemable security rather than a negotiable one?

- A. Closed-end fund shares
- B. Common stock
- C. Open-end mutual fund shares
- D. Exchange-traded fund shares

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Open-end fund shares are redeemed by the issuing fund. Common stock, closed-end funds and ETFs are sold to other investors in the secondary market.

*Remember:* Redeemable = sold back to the issuer (open-end funds, UITs, variable annuities).

</details>

**Q1019** (§1.3.2 · FINRA section 1) — A customer buys 1,000 shares of XYZ on the NYSE from another investor. The money the customer pays goes to:

- A. XYZ Corporation
- B. The selling investor
- C. The NYSE
- D. XYZ's underwriter

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** An exchange trade is a secondary-market trade between investors, so the proceeds go to the seller. The issuer gets money only in the primary market.

*Remember:* Primary market: proceeds to issuer. Secondary market: proceeds to selling investor.

</details>

**Q1020** (§1.3.2 · FINRA section 1) — A private company sells newly issued shares to the public for the first time. This transaction occurs in the:

- A. Secondary market and is governed mainly by the Securities Act of 1933
- B. Primary market and is governed mainly by the Securities Act of 1933
- C. Primary market and is governed mainly by the Securities Exchange Act of 1934
- D. Third market and is governed mainly by the Securities Exchange Act of 1934

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** An IPO is the issuer selling new shares, which is the primary market. The Securities Act of 1933 governs new issues.

*Remember:* Primary market/new issues = 1933 Act; secondary trading = 1934 Act.

</details>

**Q1021** (§1.3.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer buys stock in a cash account on Monday. There are no holidays that week. Under Regulation T, the customer must pay no later than:

- A. Tuesday
- B. Thursday
- C. Wednesday
- D. The following Monday

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Regular-way settlement is T+1 (Tuesday). Reg T payment is due two business days after settlement, or T+3, which is Thursday.

1. Trade date Monday = T
2. Settlement T+1 = Tuesday
3. Reg T = S+2 = Thursday

*Remember:* Reg T payment deadline = S+2 = T+3 business days.

</details>

**Q1022** (§1.3.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer fails to pay for a stock purchase by the Regulation T deadline, and the firm sells out the position. The customer's account will be:

- A. Frozen for 30 days, with purchases allowed on credit
- B. Closed permanently and reported to FINRA
- C. Frozen for 90 days, requiring cash in the account before any purchase
- D. Restricted to margin purchases only

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** After a Reg T sell-out, the account is frozen for 90 days. During that time the customer must have cash in the account before buying.

*Remember:* Reg T non-payment: sell out, then a 90-day freeze (cash up front).

</details>

**Q1023** (§1.3.4 · FINRA section 3) — A stock closes at $52.40 the day before its ex-dividend date. The company declared a quarterly dividend of $0.60. On the ex-date, the stock's opening price will be adjusted to:

- A. $50.00
- B. $52.40
- C. $53.00
- D. $51.80

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** On the ex-date the opening price is reduced by the dividend amount: $52.40 - $0.60 = $51.80.

1. Prior close = $52.40
2. $52.40 - $0.60 = $51.80

*Remember:* Ex-date opening price = prior close - dividend amount.

</details>

**Q1024** (§1.3.5 · FINRA section 3) — A customer sells short 400 shares of a stock at $35. Her maximum potential gain is:

- A. $14,000
- B. Unlimited
- C. $14,000 plus any dividends paid during the short
- D. $1,400

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The best case is the stock falling to zero, so she keeps the full $35 x 400 = $14,000. Loss, not gain, is unlimited.

1. Best case: stock falls to $0
2. 400 x $35 = $14,000

*Remember:* Short sale: max gain = sale price x shares; max loss = unlimited.

</details>

**Q1025** (§1.3.5 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following is TRUE of a customer's short sale of stock?

- A. The firm must locate borrowable shares before executing the order
- B. It may be done in a cash account if the customer has enough cash
- C. The short seller receives any dividends paid while the position is open
- D. The order may be marked long if the customer owns other stocks

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Regulation SHO requires the firm to locate shares that can be borrowed before a short sale. Short sales must be done in a margin account and marked short.

*Remember:* Reg SHO: locate before shorting; mark the order short; margin account required.

</details>

**Q1026** (§1.3.6 · FINRA section 2) — A US investor owns an ADR of a Japanese company. The share price in yen is unchanged, but the yen weakens against the US dollar. The ADR's value will most likely:

- A. Remain unchanged, because ADRs are priced in dollars
- B. Rise
- C. Fall
- D. Rise, because ADR dividends are paid in dollars

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The underlying shares are now worth fewer dollars, so the ADR's dollar price falls. This is currency risk.

*Remember:* ADRs carry currency risk: a weaker foreign currency lowers the ADR's dollar value.

</details>

**Q1027** (§1.3.6 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is TRUE about sponsored ADRs?

- A. They are issued with the foreign company's cooperation and can list on the NYSE or Nasdaq
- B. They give holders direct voting rights in the foreign company
- C. They trade only over the counter
- D. They eliminate currency risk because the depositary bank hedges it

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Sponsored ADRs are created with the foreign issuer's cooperation and can be listed on US exchanges. Unsponsored ADRs trade OTC.

*Remember:* Sponsored ADRs: issuer cooperates, can list on exchanges; unsponsored trade OTC.

</details>

**Q1028** (§1.3.7 · FINRA section 3) — A customer owns 1,000 shares of ABC and is also short 400 shares of ABC. ABC is the subject of a tender offer. How many shares may the customer tender?

- A. 1,000
- B. 600
- C. 1,400
- D. 400

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Only a net long position may be tendered: 1,000 long - 400 short = 600 shares.

1. Long 1,000 - short 400
2. Net long = 600 shares

*Remember:* Tender offers: tender only your net long position (long minus short).

</details>

**Q1029** (§1.4 · FINRA section 3) — A 68-year-old customer will use money in one year for a home down payment and needs to preserve principal. Which investment is LEAST suitable for this money?

- A. Short-term Treasury bills
- B. A money market fund
- C. Small-cap growth stocks
- D. A 1-year bank CD

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Common stock, especially small-cap growth stock, is volatile and does not suit a short horizon or a need to preserve principal.

*Remember:* Common stock is not suitable for principal preservation or very short time horizons.

</details>

**Q1030** (§1.4 · FINRA section 3) — A customer expects a recession but wants to keep some money in stocks. Which stocks are MOST appropriate?

- A. Steel producers
- B. Cyclical stocks, such as automakers and airlines
- C. Defensive stocks, such as food and pharmaceutical companies
- D. Small-cap speculative stocks

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Defensive stocks have steady earnings regardless of the economy, so they tend to hold up best in a recession.

*Remember:* Expect recession = defensive stocks; expect expansion = cyclical stocks.

</details>

**Q1031** (§1.5 · FINRA section 2) — A company has shareholders' equity of $50 million, including $10 million of preferred stock, and 4 million common shares outstanding. Its book value per share is:

- A. $15.00
- B. $12.50
- C. $10.00
- D. $2.50

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Book value per share = (equity - preferred) / common shares = ($50M - $10M) / 4M = $10.00.

1. $50M - $10M = $40M for common
2. $40M / 4M shares = $10.00

*Remember:* Book value per share = (shareholders' equity - preferred stock) / common shares outstanding.

</details>

**Q1032** (§1.5 · FINRA section 2) — A company has current assets of $900,000, including $300,000 of inventory, and current liabilities of $400,000. Its quick (acid-test) ratio is:

- A. 0.75
- B. 2.25
- C. 1.5
- D. 0.67

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Quick ratio = (current assets - inventory) / current liabilities = $600,000 / $400,000 = 1.5.

1. $900,000 - $300,000 = $600,000
2. $600,000 / $400,000 = 1.5

*Remember:* Quick ratio = (current assets - inventory) / current liabilities.

</details>

**Q1033** (§1.5 · FINRA section 2) — A stock trades at $32. The company has EPS of $4.00 and pays annual dividends of $1.60 per share. Its dividend payout ratio is:

- A. 250%
- B. 5%
- C. 8
- D. 40%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Payout ratio = dividends per share / EPS = $1.60 / $4.00 = 40%.

1. $1.60 / $4.00 = 0.40
2. Payout ratio = 40%

*Remember:* Dividend payout ratio = dividends per share / EPS.

</details>

**Q1034** (§1.5 · FINRA section 2) — A fundamental analyst would consider all of the following EXCEPT:

- A. Support and resistance levels on a price chart
- B. The company's debt-to-equity ratio
- C. The quality of the company's management
- D. Trends in the company's industry

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Support and resistance are chart patterns used in technical analysis. Fundamental analysts study financial statements, management, industry and the economy.

*Remember:* Fundamental = financials, management, industry, economy; technical = price and volume charts.

</details>

**Q1035** (§1.5 · FINRA section 2) — An analyst believes stock prices reflect all public information, so studying financial statements cannot give a lasting edge, but trading on inside information could. This view matches which form of the efficient market hypothesis?

- A. Technical form
- B. Strong form
- C. Weak form
- D. Semi-strong form

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The semi-strong form holds that all public information is already priced in. Only the strong form says inside information is priced in too.

*Remember:* EMH: weak = past prices; semi-strong = all public info; strong = all info, including inside.

</details>

**Q9001** (§1.2.4 · FINRA section 2) — XYZ announces a rights offering in which 5 rights plus $28 buy one new share. A customer owns 2,000 shares and exercises all of her rights. How many new shares does she get, and what does she pay?

- A. 2,000 shares for $56,000
- B. 400 shares for $11,200
- C. 10,000 shares for $280,000
- D. 400 shares for $56,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** She receives one right per share (2,000 rights). At 5 rights per new share she can buy 400 shares, paying the $28 subscription price on each: $11,200.

1. 2,000 shares = 2,000 rights
2. 2,000 ÷ 5 = 400 new shares
3. 400 × $28 = $11,200

*Remember:* Rights = shares owned; new shares = rights ÷ rights needed per share; cost = new shares × subscription price

</details>

**Q9002** (§1.3.4 · FINRA section 3) — A customer sells 300 shares of a stock in a regular-way trade on the stock's ex-dividend date. Who is entitled to the dividend that was previously declared?

- A. The seller, because the buyer purchased on the ex-dividend date
- B. The buyer, because the buyer will own the shares on the payable date
- C. The buyer and seller split it based on days held
- D. Neither; the dividend goes back to the issuer

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** With T+1 settlement the ex-date equals the record date, so a trade on the ex-date settles after the record date. The seller is still the holder of record and keeps the dividend.

*Remember:* Buy before the ex-date to get the dividend; sell on or after the ex-date and you keep it

</details>

**Q9003** (§1.5 · FINRA section 2) — A company's balance sheet shows current assets of $6 million (including $1.5 million of inventory), current liabilities of $2.5 million and long-term debt of $4 million. What are its working capital and current ratio?

- A. $3.5 million; 1.8
- B. $2.0 million; 2.4
- C. $3.5 million; 2.4
- D. $2.0 million; 1.8

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Working capital = current assets − current liabilities = $6M − $2.5M = $3.5M. Current ratio = $6M ÷ $2.5M = 2.4. Inventory is subtracted only for the quick ratio, and long-term debt is not a current liability.

1. Working capital = $6.0M − $2.5M = $3.5M
2. Current ratio = $6.0M ÷ $2.5M = 2.4

*Remember:* Working capital = CA − CL; current ratio = CA ÷ CL

</details>

**Q9004** (§1.3.5 · FINRA section 3) — A customer is short 500 shares of ABC in her margin account when ABC pays a cash dividend of $0.40 per share. Which statement is TRUE?

- A. She receives $200 because she sold the shares
- B. No one is owed the dividend because the shares were borrowed
- C. The broker-dealer pays the $200 to the lender on her behalf at its own cost
- D. She must pay $200 to the lender of the borrowed shares

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A short seller borrowed the shares, so she must pay the lender any dividends declared while the position is open: 500 × $0.40 = $200 is charged to her account.

*Remember:* Short sellers owe dividends to the lender of the borrowed shares

</details>

**Q9005** (§1.3.7 · FINRA section 2) — A corporation uses excess cash to buy back 10% of its outstanding shares in the open market. Assuming net income is unchanged, which result is MOST likely?

- A. Earnings per share falls because the company has less cash
- B. Earnings per share rises because fewer shares are outstanding
- C. The repurchased shares continue to vote and receive dividends
- D. The number of issued shares falls by 10%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Repurchased shares become treasury stock, which reduces shares outstanding. The same net income spread over fewer shares produces higher EPS.

*Remember:* Buyback → treasury stock → fewer outstanding shares → higher EPS

</details>

## Chapter 2: Preferred Stock

**Q11** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — A corporation has $6 cumulative preferred stock outstanding. It paid no preferred dividends in the last two years. Before paying a common dividend this year, it must pay preferred holders:

- A. $6
- B. $12
- C. $18
- D. Nothing — the missed dividends are forfeited

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Cumulative preferred accumulates dividends in arrears: two missed years ($12) plus the current year ($6) = $18 per share must be paid before any common dividend.

1. Arrears = 2 × $6 = $12
2. Current year = $6
3. Total = $12 + $6 = $18 per share

*Remember:* Cumulative preferred: pay all arrears plus current dividend before any common dividend.

</details>

**Q12** (§2.1 · FINRA section 2) — When market interest rates rise, the price of a fixed-rate preferred stock will most likely:

- A. Rise
- B. Fall
- C. Stay the same
- D. Rise only if the issuer is highly rated

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Preferred pays a fixed dividend, so like a bond its price moves inversely to interest rates. Adjustable-rate preferred is the exception because its dividend resets.

*Remember:* Fixed-rate preferred prices move inversely to interest rates, like bonds.

</details>

**Q13** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which type of preferred stock would an issuer most likely redeem when interest rates fall?

- A. Cumulative
- B. Participating
- C. Callable
- D. Convertible

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Issuers call preferred (and bonds) when rates fall so they can reissue at a lower dividend rate. Investors are compensated with a higher stated dividend on callable issues.

*Remember:* Issuers call callable preferred when rates fall to refinance at a lower dividend.

</details>

**Q14** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — A $100 par preferred stock is convertible at $20 per common share. If the preferred is trading at $110, the parity price of the common stock is:

- A. $20
- B. $22
- C. $25
- D. $5.50

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Conversion ratio = $100 ÷ $20 = 5 shares. Parity for the common = $110 ÷ 5 = $22.

1. Conversion ratio = $100 ÷ $20 = 5 shares
2. Common parity = $110 ÷ 5 = $22

*Remember:* Conversion ratio = par ÷ conversion price; common parity = preferred price ÷ conversion ratio.

</details>

**Q15** (§2.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which investor would benefit MOST from the tax treatment of preferred stock dividends?

- A. A corporation investing excess cash
- B. An individual in the lowest tax bracket
- C. A traditional IRA
- D. A municipal pension fund

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Corporations can exclude a large portion of dividends received from other domestic corporations (the dividends-received deduction), making preferred especially attractive to corporate investors.

*Remember:* Corporations get a dividends-received deduction, excluding much of dividends from other domestic corporations.

</details>

**Q16** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which type of preferred stock has the LEAST interest-rate risk?

- A. Straight preferred
- B. Cumulative preferred
- C. Adjustable-rate preferred
- D. Callable preferred

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Because the dividend on adjustable-rate preferred resets with market rates, its price stays close to par and it is far less sensitive to rate changes.

*Remember:* Adjustable-rate preferred resets its dividend with market rates, so its price stays near par.

</details>

**Q17** (§2.1 · FINRA section 2) — Compared with common stockholders, preferred stockholders generally have:

- A. Greater voting rights and greater growth potential
- B. Priority in dividends and liquidation but usually no voting rights
- C. Preemptive rights and a maturity date
- D. A legal right to receive dividends each year

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Preferred is 'preferred' for dividends and in liquidation (after creditors, before common). It generally has no voting rights, no maturity, and dividends are still not a legal obligation.

*Remember:* Preferred has priority over common for dividends and liquidation, but usually no vote.

</details>

**Q18** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which feature allows preferred stockholders to receive more than the stated dividend in a very profitable year?

- A. Cumulative
- B. Participating
- C. Convertible
- D. Callable

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Participating preferred receives its fixed dividend plus a share of additional dividends when the company does well.

*Remember:* Participating preferred can receive extra dividends beyond its stated rate in profitable years.

</details>

**Q19** (§2.1 · FINRA section 2) — A 7% preferred stock with a $100 par value is trading at $87.50. Its current yield is:

- A. 7.0%
- B. 8.0%
- C. 6.1%
- D. 8.75%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Annual dividend = 7% × $100 = $7. Current yield = $7 ÷ $87.50 = 8.0%.

1. Annual dividend = 7% × $100 = $7
2. Current yield = $7 ÷ $87.50 = 8.0%

*Remember:* Current yield = annual dividend ÷ current market price.

</details>

**Q20** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — Convertible preferred stock typically pays a dividend that is _____ than non-convertible preferred from the same issuer because _____.

- A. higher; it carries call risk
- B. lower; the conversion feature benefits the investor
- C. higher; it is subordinate to convertible bonds
- D. lower; it has no liquidation preference

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Investor-friendly features (convertibility, cumulative, participating) let the issuer pay a lower dividend; issuer-friendly features (callability) require a higher dividend.

*Remember:* Investor-friendly features lower the dividend; issuer-friendly features like callability raise it.

</details>

**Q1036** (§2.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer owns an 8% preferred stock with a $50 par value. Each quarterly dividend payment is:

- A. $1.00
- B. $4.00
- C. $2.00
- D. $8.00

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The annual dividend is 8% x $50 = $4.00. Paid quarterly, each payment is $4.00 / 4 = $1.00.

1. 8% x $50 = $4.00 per year
2. $4.00 / 4 = $1.00 per quarter

*Remember:* Preferred dividend = rate x par; quarterly payment = annual / 4.

</details>

**Q1037** (§2.1 · FINRA section 2) — A 5% preferred stock with a $100 par value was issued when comparable yields were 5%. If comparable yields rise to 6.25%, the preferred's price will move toward approximately:

- A. $100
- B. $125
- C. $93.75
- D. $80

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The $5 fixed dividend must yield 6.25% to compete, so price = $5 / 0.0625 = $80. Price moves inversely to rates.

1. Annual dividend = 5% x $100 = $5
2. $5 / 0.0625 = $80

*Remember:* Approximate preferred price = annual dividend / market yield.

</details>

**Q1038** (§2.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following does preferred stock share with common stock but NOT with a typical corporate bond?

- A. A fixed periodic payment
- B. No maturity date
- C. Usually no voting rights
- D. Prices that fall when interest rates rise

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Preferred and common are both equity with no maturity date, while bonds mature. The other traits are shared with bonds rather than with common.

*Remember:* Preferred is perpetual like common; it pays a fixed amount and trades like a bond.

</details>

**Q1039** (§2.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following are characteristics of most preferred stock?
I. A fixed dividend based on par value
II. Voting rights
III. Priority over common stock in liquidation
IV. Preemptive rights

- A. II and IV
- B. I and II
- C. I and III
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Preferred normally has a fixed dividend tied to par and ranks ahead of common in liquidation. It usually lacks voting and preemptive rights.

*Remember:* Preferred: fixed dividend, priority over common; usually no vote, no preemptive rights.

</details>

**Q1040** (§2.1 · FINRA section 2) — A retiree plans to live on the fixed dividend of a straight preferred stock for the next 20 years. As the cost of living rises, which risk will affect her MOST?

- A. Legislative risk
- B. Currency risk
- C. Liquidity risk
- D. Purchasing-power (inflation) risk

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A fixed dividend buys less as prices rise, so the main risk is purchasing-power (inflation) risk.

*Remember:* Fixed payments carry purchasing-power (inflation) risk.

</details>

**Q1041** (§2.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is TRUE about preferred stock dividends?

- A. Missing a payment puts the issuer in default
- B. They must be declared by the board and are not a legal obligation of the issuer
- C. They rise automatically when the company's earnings increase
- D. They are paid only after common stockholders receive their dividends

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Like all dividends, preferred dividends must be declared by the board and are not legally required. Missed preferred dividends block common dividends, so issuers try hard to pay them.

*Remember:* Preferred dividends are prioritized but not guaranteed; bond interest is a legal obligation.

</details>

**Q1042** (§2.1 · FINRA section 2) — An investor buys a 6% preferred stock with a $25 par value at a market price of $20. The current yield is:

- A. 30.0%
- B. 6.0%
- C. 7.5%
- D. 4.8%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Annual dividend = 6% x $25 = $1.50. Current yield = $1.50 / $20 = 7.5%.

1. Dividend = 6% x $25 = $1.50
2. $1.50 / $20 = 7.5%

*Remember:* Current yield = annual dividend / market price; dividend = rate x par.

</details>

**Q1043** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — A company has a $4 straight (non-cumulative) preferred stock. It skipped the preferred dividend for the past two years. To pay a common dividend this year, it must first pay preferred holders, per share:

- A. $12
- B. $4
- C. $8
- D. $0

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Skipped dividends on non-cumulative preferred are lost for good. The company only needs to pay this year's $4 before paying common.

1. Missed dividends on straight preferred are not owed
2. Current year only = $4

*Remember:* Non-cumulative: only the current dividend is owed; cumulative: arrears plus current.

</details>

**Q1044** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — A 7%, $100-par cumulative preferred pays quarterly dividends. The issuer has missed the last three quarterly payments. To pay a common dividend this quarter, it must first pay each preferred share:

- A. $7.00
- B. $5.25
- C. $1.75
- D. $28.00

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Each quarterly payment is $7 / 4 = $1.75. Three missed quarters plus the current one = 4 x $1.75 = $7.00.

1. Quarterly = 7% x $100 / 4 = $1.75
2. 3 missed + 1 current = 4 payments
3. 4 x $1.75 = $7.00

*Remember:* Cumulative: arrears + current dividend before any common dividend.

</details>

**Q1045** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — A $100-par preferred stock is convertible into common at $40 per share. The common trades at $46. What is the value of the common shares received if one preferred share is converted?

- A. $92
- B. $100
- C. $115
- D. $46

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Conversion ratio = $100 / $40 = 2.5 shares. 2.5 x $46 = $115.

1. Ratio = $100 / $40 = 2.5 shares
2. 2.5 x $46 = $115

*Remember:* Conversion ratio = par / conversion price; conversion value = ratio x common price.

</details>

**Q1046** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — Compared with an otherwise identical non-callable preferred, a callable preferred will typically:

- A. Pay a lower dividend, because the call premium compensates investors
- B. Pay a higher dividend, because the call feature benefits the issuer
- C. Pay the same dividend, since it can only be called at par
- D. Rise more in price when interest rates fall

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The call feature benefits the issuer, so investors demand a higher dividend to accept it.

*Remember:* Issuer-friendly features (callable) = higher dividend; investor-friendly = lower dividend.

</details>

**Q1047** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — An investor holds a 7% callable preferred. Interest rates fall to 4%, and the issuer calls the stock at par. The investor now faces mainly:

- A. Currency risk
- B. Rising interest-rate risk
- C. Liquidity risk
- D. Reinvestment risk

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** After the call, the investor must reinvest the money at today's lower rates. That is reinvestment risk, which comes with callable preferred.

*Remember:* Callable preferred is called when rates fall, leaving the holder with reinvestment risk.

</details>

**Q1048** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — A customer wants preferred stock where any dividends skipped during a downturn must be made up before common stockholders get paid. She should buy:

- A. Adjustable-rate preferred
- B. Participating preferred
- C. Cumulative preferred
- D. Callable preferred

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Cumulative preferred builds up missed dividends as arrears, which must be paid in full before any common dividend.

*Remember:* Cumulative = dividends in arrears paid before common.

</details>

**Q1049** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — The dividend on adjustable-rate preferred stock is:

- A. Reset whenever the common dividend changes
- B. Adjusted each year based on the company's earnings
- C. Reset periodically based on a benchmark such as Treasury bill rates
- D. Fixed, while the call price adjusts to market rates

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Adjustable-rate preferred resets its dividend periodically to a benchmark rate. That keeps its price near par.

*Remember:* Adjustable-rate preferred: dividend tied to a benchmark rate, so price stays near par.

</details>

**Q1050** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — A convertible preferred trades at $90 and converts into 4 shares of common. The common trades at $25. Which statement is TRUE?

- A. Converting is profitable, since the common trades above its parity price of $22.50
- B. Converting is unprofitable, since the common's parity price is $25
- C. Converting is unprofitable, since conversion is a taxable event
- D. The common trades below parity, so the preferred is overpriced

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Parity = $90 / 4 = $22.50. The common at $25 is above parity, so 4 shares are worth $100, more than the $90 preferred.

1. Parity = $90 / 4 = $22.50
2. 4 x $25 = $100 > $90

*Remember:* Common parity = preferred price / conversion ratio; convert when common trades above parity.

</details>

**Q1051** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following preferred stock features benefit the investor and therefore generally lower the dividend rate?
I. Cumulative
II. Callable
III. Convertible
IV. Participating

- A. II, III and IV
- B. I and II
- C. II and III
- D. I, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Cumulative, convertible and participating features help investors, so issuers can pay less. Callable helps the issuer, so it requires a higher dividend.

*Remember:* Investor-friendly features lower the dividend; callable (issuer-friendly) raises it.

</details>

**Q1052** (§2.3 · FINRA section 2) — A customer wants steady income and also hopes to benefit if the issuer's common stock rises. Which security BEST fits?

- A. Callable preferred
- B. Convertible preferred
- C. Straight preferred
- D. Adjustable-rate preferred

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Convertible preferred pays a fixed dividend and tracks the common when the common rises, giving income plus growth potential.

*Remember:* Income + growth potential = convertible preferred.

</details>

**Q1053** (§2.3 · FINRA section 2) — Why is straight preferred stock generally unsuitable for an investor whose main objective is long-term capital appreciation?

- A. It ranks behind common stock in liquidation
- B. Its fixed dividend limits price appreciation
- C. Its maturity date limits the holding period
- D. Its price tends to rise when interest rates rise

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Because the dividend is fixed, the price does not rise much when the company does well. Growth investors are better served by common stock.

*Remember:* Preferred offers limited growth potential, except convertible.

</details>

**Q1054** (§2.3 · FINRA section 2) — A customer insists that her income must be a legal obligation of the issuer. Which recommendation BEST fits?

- A. Cumulative preferred, since arrears make the dividend a legal obligation
- B. A corporate bond, since interest is legally required
- C. Adjustable-rate preferred, since its dividend is guaranteed to track rates
- D. Participating preferred, since it guarantees a dividend above the stated rate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Bond interest is a legal obligation, and missing it is a default. Preferred dividends, even cumulative ones, are not legally required.

*Remember:* Bond interest is legally required; preferred dividends are prioritized but not guaranteed.

</details>

**Q1055** (§2.3 · FINRA section 2) — Compared with a corporate bond of the same issuer, preferred stock:
I. Is an equity security
II. Has a stated maturity date
III. Makes payments that are legally required
IV. Ranks behind the bond in liquidation

- A. I and IV
- B. I and II
- C. II and III
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Preferred stock is equity, ranks after the issuer's bonds in liquidation, and has no maturity. Its dividends are not legally required.

*Remember:* Preferred: equity, no maturity, payment not required, junior to bonds.

</details>

**Q9006** (§2.2 · FINRA section 2) — A corporation has several preferred issues outstanding. Which one would MOST likely carry the highest stated dividend rate?

- A. Cumulative convertible preferred
- B. Cumulative participating preferred
- C. Non-cumulative callable preferred
- D. Non-callable cumulative preferred

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Features that favor the investor (cumulative, convertible, participating, non-callable) let the issuer pay less. A non-cumulative, callable issue gives the investor the least, so it must pay the most.

*Remember:* Investor-friendly features lower the dividend; issuer-friendly features (callable, non-cumulative) raise it

</details>

**Q9007** (§2.1 · FINRA section 2) — A $50-par, 6% preferred stock was issued when comparable yields were 6%. If comparable yields fall to 5%, the preferred's market price will move toward approximately:

- A. $50.00
- B. $60.00
- C. $120.00
- D. $41.67

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The annual dividend is 6% × $50 = $3. To yield 5%, the price must rise to $3 ÷ 0.05 = $60. Preferred prices move inversely to interest rates.

1. Annual dividend = 6% × $50 = $3
2. Price ≈ $3 ÷ 0.05 = $60

*Remember:* Approximate price = annual dividend ÷ market yield

</details>

## Chapter 3: Debt Securities (Bond Fundamentals)

**Q21** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — A corporate bond is quoted at 102 3/8. The dollar price is:

- A. $1,020.38
- B. $1,023.75
- C. $1,002.375
- D. $1,023.00

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** 102 3/8 = 102.375% of $1,000 par = $1,023.75. Corporates are quoted in eighths; each point is $10.

1. 102 3/8 = 102.375% of par
2. 102.375% × $1,000 = $1,023.75

*Remember:* Bond quotes are percent of $1,000 par; 1 point = $10, 1/8 point = $1.25.

</details>

**Q22** (§3.5 · FINRA section 2) — A bond with a 5% coupon is trading at $1,100. Which of the following correctly ranks its yields from highest to lowest?

- A. YTC, YTM, CY, nominal
- B. Nominal, CY, YTM, YTC
- C. CY, nominal, YTM, YTC
- D. YTM, YTC, CY, nominal

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** For a premium bond, nominal > current yield > YTM > YTC. The investor paid more than par and loses the premium, so the 'all-in' yields are lower than the coupon.

*Remember:* Premium bond yields, highest to lowest: nominal, current, YTM, YTC.

</details>

**Q23** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which bond would experience the GREATEST price decline if interest rates rise 1%?

- A. A 2-year 6% bond
- B. A 10-year 6% bond
- C. A 30-year 6% bond
- D. A 30-year zero-coupon bond

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Price volatility increases with longer maturity and lower coupon. A long-term zero-coupon bond has the highest duration and is the most sensitive to rate changes.

*Remember:* Longer maturity and lower coupon mean greater price sensitivity; long zeros are most volatile.

</details>

**Q24** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — The lowest rating that is still considered investment grade is:

- A. A
- B. BBB
- C. BB
- D. B

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** BBB (S&P/Fitch) or Baa (Moody's) is the lowest investment-grade tier. Anything below is speculative/high-yield ('junk').

*Remember:* BBB (S&P/Fitch) or Baa (Moody's) is the lowest investment-grade rating.

</details>

**Q25** (§3.4 · FINRA section 2) — An investor wants to accumulate exactly $50,000 for a child's college tuition in 12 years and does not want reinvestment risk. The most appropriate investment is:

- A. A 12-year 5% corporate bond
- B. A 12-year zero-coupon bond
- C. A bond mutual fund
- D. A 12-year callable municipal bond

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Zero-coupon bonds pay no interim interest, so there is nothing to reinvest; the investor locks in a known maturity value. The annual accretion is taxed unless it's a muni or held in a tax-deferred account.

*Remember:* Zero-coupon bonds eliminate reinvestment risk and lock in a known maturity value.

</details>

**Q26** (§3.4 · FINRA section 2) — An investor who owns a puttable bond would most likely exercise the put when:

- A. Interest rates fall
- B. Interest rates rise
- C. The issuer's credit rating is upgraded
- D. The bond trades at a premium

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A put lets the holder sell the bond back at par. When rates rise, bond prices fall, so the holder puts the bond at par and reinvests at the higher rates.

*Remember:* Holders put bonds back at par when rates rise and bond prices fall.

</details>

**Q27** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — A corporate bond pays interest on January 1 and July 1. A trade settles on April 16. How many days of accrued interest does the buyer owe the seller?

- A. 105
- B. 106
- C. 104
- D. 107

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Corporate bonds use 30/360: January (30) + February (30) + March (30) + April 1–15 (15) = 105 days. Accrued interest runs up to but not including settlement.

1. Jan + Feb + Mar = 3 × 30 = 90 days
2. April 1–15 = 15 days (settlement day excluded)
3. Total = 90 + 15 = 105 days

*Remember:* Corporate accrued interest uses 30/360, counting up to but not including settlement date.

</details>

**Q28** (§3.5 · FINRA section 1) — An inverted yield curve exists when:

- A. Long-term rates exceed short-term rates
- B. Short-term rates exceed long-term rates
- C. Corporate yields exceed Treasury yields
- D. All maturities yield approximately the same

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** An inverted (negative) yield curve has short-term yields above long-term yields, usually from Fed tightening, and historically signals a coming recession.

*Remember:* Inverted yield curve: short-term rates exceed long-term rates; often signals recession.

</details>

**Q29** (§3.4 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is TRUE about a callable bond compared to a non-callable bond from the same issuer?

- A. It pays a lower coupon
- B. It is most likely to be called when rates rise
- C. It pays a higher coupon to compensate for call risk
- D. Its yield to call is always higher than its yield to maturity

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Callability benefits the issuer (called when rates fall), so investors demand a higher coupon. For a premium bond YTC is lower than YTM.

*Remember:* Callable bonds pay higher coupons to compensate investors for call risk.

</details>

**Q30** (§3.3 · FINRA section 1) — Corporate bond transactions must be reported to:

- A. EMMA
- B. TRACE
- C. The Consolidated Tape
- D. RTRS

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** FINRA's TRACE (Trade Reporting and Compliance Engine) captures corporate bond trades. Municipal trades go to the MSRB's RTRS, displayed on EMMA.

*Remember:* Corporate bond trades are reported to FINRA's TRACE.

</details>

**Q2001** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — A Treasury bond is quoted at 101.24. What is the dollar price of one $1,000 par bond?

- A. $1,012.40
- B. $1,017.50
- C. $1,030.00
- D. $1,010.24

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Treasury quotes are in 32nds: 101.24 = 101 24/32 = 101.75% of par. 101.75% x $1,000 = $1,017.50.

1. 24/32 = 0.75
2. 101.75% of par
3. 1.0175 x $1,000 = $1,017.50

*Remember:* Treasury notes/bonds quoted in 32nds: price = (points + n/32)% x $1,000.

</details>

**Q2002** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — An 8% corporate bond pays interest March 1 and September 1. A customer buys it in a trade that settles June 10. How much accrued interest does the buyer owe the seller per bond?

- A. $22.00
- B. $22.22
- C. $21.78
- D. $20.00

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Corporate bonds use 30/360 and accrue up to but not including settlement: March 30 + April 30 + May 30 + June 9 = 99 days. $80 x 99/360 = $22.00.

1. Days: 30 + 30 + 30 + 9 = 99
2. Annual interest = $80
3. $80 x 99/360 = $22.00

*Remember:* Accrued interest = annual interest x days/360; count to the day before settlement.

</details>

**Q2003** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following does a corporate bondholder have that a common stockholder of the same company does NOT?

- A. The right to vote for the board of directors
- B. A claim on assets ahead of stockholders in liquidation
- C. Preemptive rights on new share issues
- D. A share of earnings growth through rising dividends

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Bondholders are creditors, not owners. They have no vote or ownership rights but rank ahead of all stockholders if the company is liquidated.

*Remember:* Bondholders = creditors: no vote, fixed interest, priority over stockholders in liquidation.

</details>

**Q2004** (§3.1 · FINRA section 1) — Under the Trust Indenture Act of 1939, which of the following offerings would require a trust indenture?

- A. A $75 million general obligation municipal bond
- B. A $75 million corporate debenture sold to the public
- C. A $75 million Treasury note auction
- D. A $75 million public offering of common stock

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The Trust Indenture Act applies to corporate debt offered to the public (over $50 million per the chapter); it requires an indenture and an independent trustee. Government and municipal debt are exempt.

*Remember:* Trust Indenture Act of 1939: public corporate bond issues need an indenture and trustee.

</details>

**Q2005** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — A retired customer owns several 25-year bonds paying fixed coupons. She worries that rising grocery and medical costs will make her interest checks buy less each year. Which risk is she describing?

- A. Credit (default) risk
- B. Purchasing-power (inflation) risk
- C. Liquidity risk
- D. Call risk

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Fixed payments lose real value as prices rise. Long-term fixed-rate bonds carry the most purchasing-power (inflation) risk.

*Remember:* Inflation risk: fixed payments buy less over time; greatest for long-term fixed-rate bonds.

</details>

**Q2006** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — A bond is rated Ba by Moody's. The equivalent S&P rating and its category are:

- A. BBB, lowest investment grade
- B. A, upper-medium investment grade
- C. D, in default
- D. BB, speculative (high-yield) grade

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Moody's Ba corresponds to S&P/Fitch BB, the highest speculative tier. Investment grade ends at Baa/BBB.

*Remember:* Investment grade: AAA-BBB (Aaa-Baa). Ba/BB and below = junk.

</details>

**Q2007** (§3.6 · FINRA section 2) — A customer in the 35% tax bracket wants fixed income inside her traditional IRA. Which choice is generally MOST appropriate for that account?

- A. General obligation municipal bonds
- B. Investment-grade corporate bonds
- C. A fixed annuity
- D. Municipal revenue bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Inside a tax-deferred account, taxes do not matter until withdrawal, so the investor should take the higher taxable yield of corporates rather than lower-yielding tax-exempt munis.

*Remember:* Tax-deferred accounts: hold higher-yielding taxable bonds; munis do not belong in IRAs.

</details>

**Q2008** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer bought a 5% bond at par. Since then, market interest rates on comparable bonds have risen to 6%. What has most likely happened to the customer's bond?

- A. Its price has fallen below par and its current yield has risen
- B. Its price has risen above par and its current yield has fallen
- C. Its price is unchanged because the coupon is fixed
- D. Its coupon has reset to 6% to match the market

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Price and rates move inversely. With rates at 6%, a 5% coupon is less attractive, so the bond's price drops below par, which raises its current yield toward market levels.

*Remember:* Rates up, prices down, yields up.

</details>

**Q2009** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — All of the following statements about accrued interest are true EXCEPT:

- A. The buyer pays the seller interest earned since the last coupon date
- B. Interest accrues up to, but not including, the settlement date
- C. Corporate bonds compute accrued interest on an actual/365 basis
- D. US Treasury notes and bonds use actual days elapsed

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Corporate and municipal bonds use 30/360. Actual/365 is the US government convention.

*Remember:* Corp/muni accrual: 30/360. Treasuries: actual/365.

</details>

**Q2010** (§3.2 · FINRA section 1) — A county is bringing a new municipal bond issue to market. Instead of a prospectus filed with the SEC, the disclosure document investors receive is the:

- A. Official statement
- B. Statutory prospectus
- C. Preliminary red herring
- D. Trust indenture

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Municipal securities are exempt from SEC registration, so the issuer prepares an official statement as the offering disclosure document.

*Remember:* Munis: exempt from registration; disclosure via the official statement.

</details>

**Q2011** (§3.2 · FINRA section 2) — A customer inherits an old bond certificate with paper coupons attached and no owner's name recorded anywhere. Which statement about this bond is TRUE?

- A. It is a registered bond; interest is mailed to the recorded owner
- B. It is a book-entry bond held electronically with no certificate
- C. It is a bearer bond; interest is collected by clipping and presenting coupons
- D. Bonds in this form are still routinely issued by US corporations

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Bearer bonds have no owner of record; whoever holds the certificate owns it and clips coupons to collect interest. New US bearer issues ended in 1982, though old ones may still exist.

*Remember:* Bearer bond: no ownership record, coupons clipped; no new US issues since 1982.

</details>

**Q2012** (§3.2 · FINRA section 1) — A city invites underwriting syndicates to submit sealed bids for its general obligation bonds and awards the issue to the bid with the lowest interest cost. This method is called:

- A. Negotiated underwriting
- B. A private placement
- C. Best efforts underwriting
- D. Competitive bid underwriting

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** In a competitive sale the issuer solicits bids and awards to the best (lowest-cost) bid. It is common for GO munis; negotiated deals are common for corporates and revenue bonds.

*Remember:* GO munis: usually competitive bid. Corporates and revenue bonds: usually negotiated.

</details>

**Q2013** (§3.3 · FINRA section 2) — The yield on a bond rises from 3.85% to 4.10%. By how many basis points did the yield increase?

- A. 2.5 basis points
- B. 250 basis points
- C. 25 basis points
- D. 0.25 basis points

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** One basis point is 0.01%. The change is 4.10% - 3.85% = 0.25%, which equals 25 basis points.

1. 4.10% - 3.85% = 0.25%
2. 0.25% / 0.01% = 25 bp

*Remember:* 1 basis point = 0.01%; 100 bp = 1%. bp = percentage-point change x 100.

</details>

**Q2014** (§3.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer buys a corporate bond in a regular-way trade on Monday. Assuming no holidays, the trade settles on:

- A. Monday, the trade date
- B. Wednesday
- C. Thursday
- D. Tuesday

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Regular-way settlement for corporate bonds (and munis and Treasuries) is T+1, one business day after the trade date.

*Remember:* Regular-way settlement: T+1 for corporates, munis and Treasuries.

</details>

**Q2015** (§3.3 · FINRA section 3) — Which statement about FINRA's 5% markup policy is TRUE?

- A. It is a guideline; a markup must be fair and reasonable given all the circumstances
- B. Any markup over 5% is automatically a violation
- C. Any markup under 5% is automatically fair
- D. It applies to new issues sold at the public offering price

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The 5% policy is a guideline, not a rule, for secondary-market trades. Fairness depends on factors such as the security, size, price, and the dealer's services.

*Remember:* 5% policy = guideline, not a rule; markups must be fair and reasonable.

</details>

**Q2016** (§3.3 · FINRA section 2) — A customer wants to sell 5 corporate bonds. The dealer's quote is 96 1/4 - 96 7/8. Before any markdown, the customer would receive approximately:

- A. $4,812.50
- B. $4,843.75
- C. $4,831.25
- D. $481.25

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Customers sell at the dealer's bid. 96 1/4 = 96.25% x $1,000 = $962.50 per bond; 5 bonds = $4,812.50.

1. Bid 96 1/4 = $962.50
2. 5 x $962.50 = $4,812.50

*Remember:* Customer sells at the bid, buys at the ask; price = quote% x $1,000.

</details>

**Q2017** (§3.4 · FINRA section 2) — A bond's indenture requires the issuer to deposit money with the trustee each year to retire part of the issue before maturity. This provision:

- A. Is call protection and prevents any early redemption
- B. Is a sinking fund and generally reduces default risk
- C. Is a put feature that lets investors demand repayment
- D. Is an advance refunding using escrowed Treasuries

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A sinking fund sets aside money regularly to retire bonds over time, so less debt is outstanding at maturity and default risk is reduced.

*Remember:* Sinking fund: issuer sets aside money to retire bonds over time; lowers default risk.

</details>

**Q2018** (§3.4 · FINRA section 2) — Why are pre-refunded (advance-refunded) bonds generally considered among the safest bonds available?

- A. Proceeds of a new issue are escrowed in Treasuries to pay them off at the call date
- B. They carry a sinking fund backed by the issuer's future revenues
- C. Investors can put them back to the issuer at par at any time
- D. They are insured by the FDIC up to $250,000 per holder

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** In a pre-refunding, the issuer sells a new bond and places the proceeds in escrow, typically in Treasuries, to retire the old bond on its call date, making the old bond very safe.

*Remember:* Pre-refunded bonds: escrowed Treasuries pay them off at call; extremely low credit risk.

</details>

**Q2019** (§3.4 · FINRA section 2) — An investor buying a new callable bond wants to lock in its coupon for as long as possible. Which feature should the investor look for?

- A. A sinking fund
- B. A long call protection period
- C. A call price set at par
- D. A partial call selected by lot

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Call protection is the period during which the issuer may not call the bond. A longer period guarantees the coupon for more years.

*Remember:* Call protection = years before the issuer may call; longer is better for investors.

</details>

**Q2020** (§3.4 · FINRA section 2) — A bond's coupon resets every six months to a short-term benchmark rate. Compared with a fixed-rate bond of similar maturity, its market price will tend to:

- A. Swing more widely as rates change
- B. Stay close to par because the coupon adjusts with rates
- C. Rise when interest rates rise
- D. Fall steadily toward zero at maturity

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Variable-rate (floating-rate) bonds reset their coupons to market levels, so their prices stay near par and carry minimal interest-rate risk.

*Remember:* Floating-rate bonds: coupon resets, price stays near par, minimal interest-rate risk.

</details>

**Q2021** (§3.4 · FINRA section 2) — A customer holds a corporate zero-coupon bond in a taxable brokerage account. How is the bond taxed while she owns it?

- A. No tax is owed until the bond matures or is sold
- B. The interest is exempt from federal income tax
- C. She owes tax each year on the annual accretion, even though no cash is paid
- D. The full discount is taxed as a capital gain at maturity

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The annual accretion of a corporate zero's discount is taxable interest each year (phantom income), which is why zeros are often placed in tax-deferred accounts.

*Remember:* Corporate zeros: annual accretion taxed yearly as interest (phantom income).

</details>

**Q2022** (§3.4 · FINRA section 2) — A $1,000 bond is convertible at $40. The issuer then does a 2-for-1 stock split. Under a standard anti-dilution provision, the new conversion price and conversion ratio are:

- A. $40 conversion price; 25 shares per bond
- B. $80 conversion price; 12.5 shares per bond
- C. $20 conversion price; 50 shares per bond
- D. $20 conversion price; 25 shares per bond

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Anti-dilution provisions adjust the conversion terms for splits. A 2-for-1 split halves the conversion price to $20, so the ratio becomes $1,000 / $20 = 50 shares.

1. Old ratio = $1,000 / $40 = 25
2. 2-for-1: price $40 / 2 = $20
3. New ratio = $1,000 / $20 = 50

*Remember:* Conversion ratio = par / conversion price; splits adjust both to preserve conversion value.

</details>

**Q2023** (§3.5 · FINRA section 2) — A 7% corporate bond is trading at 87 1/2. What is its current yield?

- A. 8.00%
- B. 7.00%
- C. 6.13%
- D. 8.75%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Current yield = annual interest / market price = $70 / $875 = 8.00%. A discount bond's current yield exceeds its coupon.

1. Price = 87.5% x $1,000 = $875
2. Annual interest = $70
3. $70 / $875 = 8.00%

*Remember:* Current yield = annual interest / current market price.

</details>

**Q2024** (§3.5 · FINRA section 2) — A callable 4% bond is purchased at 92. Which of its yields is the HIGHEST?

- A. Nominal yield
- B. Current yield
- C. Yield to maturity
- D. Yield to call

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** For a discount bond, nominal < CY < YTM < YTC. If called, the investor collects the discount over fewer years, so YTC is highest.

*Remember:* Discount bond: Nominal < CY < YTM < YTC.

</details>

**Q2025** (§3.5 · FINRA section 3) — A customer buys a callable corporate bond at 108. On the trade confirmation, the dealer must show:

- A. Yield to maturity, because it is the higher yield
- B. Current yield only, since the bond is callable
- C. Nominal yield, because the coupon is fixed
- D. Yield to call, because it is the lower yield on this premium bond

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** For callable bonds, the confirmation must show yield to worst. On a premium bond, YTC is lower than YTM because the premium is lost sooner, so YTC is the yield to worst.

*Remember:* Confirmations show yield to worst; for a premium callable bond that is YTC.

</details>

**Q2026** (§3.5 · FINRA section 1) — Economists widely forecast a recession, and investors grow worried about corporate defaults. The yield spread between BBB-rated corporate bonds and Treasuries of the same maturity will most likely:

- A. Narrow
- B. Invert, with Treasuries yielding more
- C. Widen
- D. Stay the same, since both move with the Fed

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The credit spread is compensation for default risk. When investors fear defaults, they demand more yield on corporates relative to Treasuries, so spreads widen.

*Remember:* Credit spreads widen in recessions/default fears and narrow in good times.

</details>

**Q2027** (§3.5 · FINRA section 2) — Market interest rates fall sharply. Which of the following are TRUE for outstanding fixed-rate bonds?
I. Their prices rise
II. Their yields to maturity fall
III. Their coupon rates fall
IV. Their current yields rise

- A. I and IV
- B. I and II
- C. II and III
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** When rates fall, existing bond prices rise, and yields (YTM, current yield) fall with them. The fixed coupon rate never changes.

*Remember:* Price and yield move inversely; yields move with interest rates; coupons are fixed.

</details>

**Q2028** (§3.5 · FINRA section 1) — Short-term and long-term Treasury yields are nearly the same. This yield curve is described as:

- A. Normal, and it reflects a healthy growing economy
- B. Inverted, with long-term yields above short-term
- C. Positive, because investors demand more to lend long
- D. Flat, and it often precedes an economic slowdown

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A flat curve shows similar yields across maturities and often signals a slowdown. Normal slopes upward; inverted slopes downward.

*Remember:* Yield curve: normal = up, flat = level (slowdown), inverted = down (recession signal).

</details>

**Q2029** (§3.6 · FINRA section 2) — A customer expects interest rates to rise over the next two years and wants to limit losses in his bond portfolio. Which of the following would be suitable?
I. Short-term bonds
II. Floating-rate notes
III. Long-term zero-coupon bonds
IV. Puttable bonds

- A. I and II only
- B. II, III and IV
- C. I, III and IV
- D. I, II and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Short maturities, floating-rate coupons, and puts (sell back at par, reinvest higher) all limit damage from rising rates. Long zeros have the highest duration.

*Remember:* Rising rates: go short, floating, or puttable; avoid long and low-coupon bonds.

</details>

**Q2030** (§3.6 · FINRA section 2) — A customer invests equal amounts in bonds maturing in 2, 4, 6, 8 and 10 years, planning to reinvest each as it matures. This strategy is a:

- A. Ladder, which reduces interest-rate and reinvestment risk
- B. Barbell, which concentrates in the shortest and longest maturities
- C. Bullet, which has all bonds maturing around one date
- D. Sinking fund, which retires bonds before maturity

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Staggered maturities form a ladder. Money comes due regularly, so the investor is never forced to reinvest everything at one rate.

*Remember:* Ladder = staggered maturities; reduces interest-rate and reinvestment risk.

</details>

**Q9008** (§3.5 · FINRA section 2) — A 6% corporate bond with 10 years to maturity is trading at 90. Using the approximate yield-to-maturity formula, its YTM is closest to:

- A. 6.00%
- B. 6.67%
- C. 7.37%
- D. 7.78%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Annual interest is $60 and the $100 discount accretes about $10 per year. Divide $70 by the average of par and price ($950): about 7.37%. As a discount bond, YTM exceeds both the coupon and the 6.67% current yield.

1. Annual accretion = ($1,000 − $900) ÷ 10 = $10
2. Average price = ($1,000 + $900) ÷ 2 = $950
3. YTM ≈ ($60 + $10) ÷ $950 ≈ 7.37%

*Remember:* YTM ≈ [interest + (par − price) ÷ years] ÷ [(par + price) ÷ 2]

</details>

**Q9009** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — A school district's $40 million bond issue has roughly equal portions maturing each year for 20 years, and dealers quote each maturity on a yield basis. This issue is a:

- A. Term bond
- B. Serial bond
- C. Balloon issue
- D. Bullet issue

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A serial issue has portions maturing in successive years, which is common for municipals, and serial bonds are quoted on yield (basis).

*Remember:* Serial = maturities each year, quoted on yield; term = one maturity date, quoted on price

</details>

**Q9010** (§3.6 · FINRA section 2) — A customer puts half of her bond money in 1- and 2-year notes and the other half in 25- and 30-year bonds, with nothing in between. This approach is a:

- A. Bullet strategy
- B. Ladder strategy
- C. Sinking fund strategy
- D. Barbell strategy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A barbell concentrates holdings at the short and long ends of the maturity range, with little or nothing in the middle.

*Remember:* Barbell = short + long ends; ladder = staggered maturities; bullet = one maturity date

</details>

**Q9011** (§3.1 · FINRA section 2) — Interest rates have been falling. Which bondholder faces the GREATEST reinvestment risk?

- A. Holder of a high-coupon callable corporate bond trading at a premium
- B. Holder of a 20-year Treasury STRIPS
- C. Holder of a low-coupon non-callable bond trading at a discount
- D. Holder of a 5-year puttable bond with a low coupon

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Large coupons must be reinvested at the new lower rates, and a callable bond is likely to be called when rates fall, returning principal that must also be reinvested at lower rates.

*Remember:* Reinvestment risk is highest for high-coupon callable bonds; zero-coupon bonds have none

</details>

## Chapter 4: Corporate Debt

**Q31** (§4.1 · FINRA section 2) — Commercial paper is issued with a maximum maturity of 270 days primarily because:

- A. Banks will not guarantee longer paper
- B. Debt of 270 days or less is exempt from SEC registration
- C. The Fed limits corporate borrowing beyond 270 days
- D. Money market funds cannot buy longer maturities

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The Securities Act of 1933 exempts short-term debt with a maturity of 270 days or less from registration, so issuers cap commercial paper at 270 days.

*Remember:* Debt maturing in 270 days or less is exempt from SEC registration under the 1933 Act.

</details>

**Q32** (§4.2 · FINRA section 2) — A bond secured by rolling stock such as railcars or aircraft is known as a(n):

- A. Mortgage bond
- B. Collateral trust bond
- C. Equipment trust certificate
- D. Debenture

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Equipment trust certificates are backed by movable equipment held by a trustee; mortgage bonds by real property; collateral trust bonds by securities; debentures are unsecured.

*Remember:* Equipment trust certificates are secured by movable equipment like railcars and aircraft.

</details>

**Q33** (§4.3 · FINRA section 2) — A $1,000 bond is convertible at $40. The common stock is trading at $45. The parity price of the bond is:

- A. $1,000
- B. $1,125
- C. $1,111
- D. $900

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Conversion ratio = 1,000 ÷ 40 = 25 shares. Parity = 25 × $45 = $1,125.

1. Conversion ratio = $1,000 ÷ $40 = 25 shares
2. Parity = 25 × $45 = $1,125

*Remember:* Conversion ratio = par ÷ conversion price; bond parity = ratio × stock price.

</details>

**Q34** (§4.4 · FINRA section 2) — In a corporate liquidation, which claim is paid FIRST?

- A. Subordinated debentures
- B. Preferred stock
- C. Secured bonds
- D. General creditors

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Order: secured creditors → general (unsecured) creditors → subordinated debentures → preferred → common.

*Remember:* Liquidation order: secured creditors, general creditors, subordinated debt, preferred, then common.

</details>

**Q35** (§4.1 · FINRA section 2) — A banker's acceptance is most commonly used to finance:

- A. Corporate payroll
- B. Import and export transactions
- C. Municipal construction projects
- D. Margin lending to broker-dealers

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** BAs are bank-guaranteed time drafts used in international trade; they trade at a discount with maturities up to 270 days.

*Remember:* Banker's acceptances are bank-guaranteed time drafts used to finance import/export trade.

</details>

**Q36** (§4.7 · FINRA section 2) — A US corporation issues dollar-denominated bonds in London that are not registered with the SEC. These are:

- A. Yankee bonds
- B. Eurodollar bonds
- C. Treasury receipts
- D. Brady bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
