**B.** Eurodollar bonds are USD-denominated bonds issued outside the US; they're unregistered and usually pay interest annually. A Yankee bond is a foreign issuer's dollar bond sold and registered in the US.

*Remember:* Eurodollar bonds: USD-denominated, issued outside the US, not SEC-registered.

</details>

**Q37** (§4.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which corporate bond trades 'flat' (without accrued interest)?

- A. A first-mortgage bond
- B. An income (adjustment) bond
- C. A convertible debenture
- D. An equipment trust certificate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Income bonds pay interest only if the company earns enough, so they trade flat. Bonds in default also trade flat.

*Remember:* Income (adjustment) bonds and defaulted bonds trade flat, without accrued interest.

</details>

**Q38** (§4.6 · FINRA section 2) — A negotiable certificate of deposit differs from a regular bank CD in that it:

- A. Is not FDIC insured
- B. Can be sold in the secondary market before maturity
- C. Has a minimum denomination of $1,000
- D. Pays interest monthly

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Negotiable (jumbo) CDs are $100,000+ and can be traded before maturity, so there's no early-withdrawal penalty but the holder faces interest-rate risk. FDIC coverage still applies up to $250,000.

*Remember:* Negotiable CDs ($100,000+) can be traded in the secondary market before maturity.

</details>

**Q39** (§4.3 · FINRA section 2) — A corporation would most likely force conversion of its convertible bonds by:

- A. Lowering the conversion price
- B. Calling the bonds when the stock is trading above parity
- C. Suspending interest payments
- D. Issuing additional common stock

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** If the bond is called at a price below its conversion value, holders will convert rather than accept the call price — eliminating the issuer's debt.

*Remember:* Calling convertibles when conversion value exceeds the call price forces holders to convert.

</details>

**Q40** (§4.2 · FINRA section 2) — Interest paid on corporate bonds is attractive to the issuing corporation compared with dividends on stock because:

- A. Interest is tax-deductible to the corporation; dividends are not
- B. Interest payments are optional
- C. Bondholders have voting rights
- D. Interest is tax-free to the bondholder

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Interest expense reduces taxable income; dividends are paid from after-tax profits. This makes debt a cheaper source of capital, but it adds leverage and a fixed obligation.

*Remember:* Bond interest is tax-deductible to the issuer; dividends come from after-tax income.

</details>

**Q2031** (§4.1 · FINRA section 2) — A government securities dealer sells Treasury bills to an investor and agrees to buy them back the next day at a slightly higher price. From the dealer's side, this transaction is a:

- A. Repurchase agreement
- B. Reverse repurchase agreement
- C. Banker's acceptance
- D. Federal funds loan

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** In a repo the dealer sells securities and agrees to repurchase them at a higher price, effectively borrowing short-term against Treasury collateral.

*Remember:* Repo: sell securities now, repurchase later at a higher price = collateralized short-term loan.

</details>

**Q2032** (§4.1 · FINRA section 1) — Bank A has excess reserves and lends them to Bank B overnight so Bank B can meet its reserve needs. The interest rate on this loan is the:

- A. Discount rate
- B. Federal funds rate
- C. Prime rate
- D. Broker call rate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Overnight bank-to-bank loans of excess reserves are federal funds, and the fed funds rate is the most-watched short-term rate, targeted by the Fed.

*Remember:* Fed funds rate: overnight loans of reserves between banks.

</details>

**Q2033** (§4.1 · FINRA section 2) — Money market instruments generally have all of the following characteristics EXCEPT:

- A. Maturities of one year or less
- B. High liquidity
- C. High returns that compensate for significant credit risk
- D. Issuance at a discount to face value

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Money market instruments are short-term, high-quality, liquid and low-risk, so they offer low returns, not high returns for credit risk.

*Remember:* Money market: <= 1 year, high quality, liquid, low risk, low return, usually discounted.

</details>

**Q2034** (§4.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following statements about commercial paper are TRUE?
I. It is an unsecured promissory note
II. Maturities can be up to 270 days
III. It is typically secured by the issuer's inventory
IV. Buyers are mainly institutions and money market funds

- A. I and III
- B. I, II and IV
- C. II and III
- D. I, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Commercial paper is unsecured short-term corporate debt with maturities up to 270 days. Large denominations (typically $100,000+) mean institutions and money funds are the main buyers.

*Remember:* Commercial paper: unsecured, <= 270 days, discounted, large denominations, institutional buyers.

</details>

**Q2035** (§4.1 · FINRA section 2) — A money market fund manager is limiting purchases to commercial paper in the highest short-term rating tier. Which ratings would qualify?

- A. AAA from S&P or Aaa from Moody's
- B. BBB from S&P or Baa from Moody's
- C. BB from S&P or Ba from Moody's
- D. A-1 from S&P or P-1 from Moody's

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Commercial paper carries short-term ratings; the top tier is A-1 (S&P) and P-1 (Moody's).

*Remember:* Top commercial paper ratings: A-1 (S&P) / P-1 (Moody's).

</details>

**Q2036** (§4.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is NOT a standard Treasury bill maturity?

- A. 4 weeks
- B. 17 weeks
- C. 52 weeks
- D. 39 weeks

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** T-bills are offered in 4, 8, 13, 17, 26 and 52-week maturities. There is no 39-week bill.

*Remember:* T-bill maturities: 4, 8, 13, 17, 26, 52 weeks.

</details>

**Q2037** (§4.2 · FINRA section 2) — A holding company issues bonds secured by shares of its subsidiaries, which are deposited with a trustee. These bonds are:

- A. Mortgage bonds
- B. Equipment trust certificates
- C. Collateral trust bonds
- D. Debentures

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Collateral trust bonds are secured by securities the issuer owns, such as stock of a subsidiary, held by a trustee.

*Remember:* Collateral trust bond = secured by securities held by a trustee.

</details>

**Q2038** (§4.2 · FINRA section 2) — A small subsidiary issues bonds whose payments are promised by its large parent company if the subsidiary cannot pay. The credit quality of these bonds depends mainly on the:

- A. Subsidiary alone, since it is the issuer
- B. Value of the subsidiary's real estate
- C. Parent company, as guarantor of the bonds
- D. Trustee bank that administers the indenture

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Guaranteed bonds are backed by a company other than the issuer, usually a parent. Their credit quality depends on the guarantor.

*Remember:* Guaranteed bond: credit quality rests on the guarantor (usually the parent).

</details>

**Q2039** (§4.2 · FINRA section 2) — A corporation has the following bonds outstanding, all with the same maturity. Which would be expected to offer the HIGHEST yield?

- A. First-mortgage bonds
- B. Equipment trust certificates
- C. Senior debentures
- D. Subordinated debentures

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Subordinated debentures rank below other debentures in a liquidation, so they carry the most risk of the issuer's bonds and pay the highest yield.

*Remember:* Lower claim priority = more risk = higher yield; subordinated debentures rank last among bonds.

</details>

**Q2040** (§4.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following corporate bonds are secured?
I. Mortgage bonds
II. Equipment trust certificates
III. Debentures
IV. Collateral trust bonds

- A. I and II only
- B. I, II and IV
- C. II, III and IV
- D. I, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Mortgage bonds (real estate), ETCs (equipment) and collateral trust bonds (securities) all pledge specific collateral. Debentures rely only on the issuer's credit.

*Remember:* Secured: mortgage, equipment trust, collateral trust. Unsecured: debentures, subordinated, income bonds.

</details>

**Q2041** (§4.2 · FINRA section 2) — From the issuing corporation's point of view, which is a DISADVANTAGE of raising capital by selling bonds instead of common stock?

- A. Interest payments are not tax-deductible
- B. Bondholders gain voting control of the company
- C. Existing shareholders' ownership is diluted
- D. Interest is a fixed legal obligation that increases financial leverage

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Debt is cheaper and non-dilutive, but interest must be paid regardless of earnings, and heavy borrowing adds leverage, raising risk for bondholders and stockholders.

*Remember:* Debt: tax-deductible interest and no dilution, but fixed obligations and added leverage.

</details>

**Q2042** (§4.3 · FINRA section 2) — A $1,000 par convertible bond can be exchanged for 40 shares of the issuer's common stock. What is the conversion price?

- A. $25
- B. $40
- C. $2.50
- D. $250

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Conversion price = par / conversion ratio = $1,000 / 40 = $25 per share.

1. Conversion price = par / ratio
2. $1,000 / 40 = $25

*Remember:* Conversion price = par / conversion ratio (and ratio = par / price).

</details>

**Q2043** (§4.3 · FINRA section 2) — A $1,000 bond is convertible at $50 per share. The bond is trading at 96. What is the parity price of the common stock?

- A. $50
- B. $52
- C. $19.20
- D. $48

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Conversion ratio = $1,000 / $50 = 20. Parity of the stock = bond price / ratio = $960 / 20 = $48.

1. Ratio = $1,000 / $50 = 20
2. Bond price = 96% x $1,000 = $960
3. $960 / 20 = $48

*Remember:* Parity of stock = bond market price / conversion ratio.

</details>

**Q2044** (§4.3 · FINRA section 2) — A $1,000 bond is convertible at $20. The stock trades at $24 and the bond trades at $1,150. Which statement is TRUE?

- A. The bond is below parity, so buying the bond and converting is cheaper than buying the stock
- B. The bond is above parity, so converting would lose money
- C. The bond is exactly at parity, so no advantage exists
- D. The stock is below its parity price, so buy the stock instead

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Ratio = 50 shares. Bond parity = $24 x 50 = $1,200. Buying the bond at $1,150 and converting delivers $1,200 of stock, an arbitrage opportunity.

1. Ratio = $1,000 / $20 = 50
2. Bond parity = $24 x 50 = $1,200
3. $1,150 < $1,200: bond below parity

*Remember:* Parity of bond = stock price x conversion ratio; bond below parity = arbitrage.

</details>

**Q2045** (§4.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer bought a convertible bond at par ($1,000) and converts it into 25 shares when the stock is at $48. Which of the following are TRUE?
I. The conversion is not a taxable event
II. The bond's $1,000 cost basis carries over to the shares
III. The customer must report a $200 gain at conversion
IV. The cost basis per share is $40

- A. I and II only
- B. I, II and IV
- C. II, III and IV
- D. III and IV only

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Converting is not a taxable event. The $1,000 basis carries over, giving $1,000 / 25 = $40 per share; the $200 unrealized gain is taxed only when the shares are sold.

1. Basis carries over: $1,000
2. Per share: $1,000 / 25 = $40
3. Market value $1,200: gain unrealized

*Remember:* Conversion: not taxable; basis carries over (basis per share = bond cost / shares).

</details>

**Q2046** (§4.3 · FINRA section 2) — The common stock of a company falls far below the conversion price of its convertible bonds. The bonds' market price will now be driven mainly by:

- A. Their parity value, which tracks the stock
- B. The conversion price, which floats with the stock
- C. Their call price, which sets a minimum value
- D. Their investment value as straight bonds, based on yield

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** When conversion is far out of reach, a convertible trades like an ordinary bond on its yield. This investment value acts as a bond floor.

*Remember:* Stock far below conversion price: convertible trades on investment value (bond floor).

</details>

**Q2047** (§4.3 · FINRA section 2) — All of the following are advantages to a corporation of issuing convertible bonds EXCEPT:

- A. They carry a lower coupon than straight debt
- B. Conversion eliminates the debt from the balance sheet
- C. Conversion avoids diluting existing shareholders
- D. The issuer may force conversion by calling the bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Conversion creates new shares, which dilutes existing common stockholders. That is a drawback, not an advantage.

*Remember:* Convertibles: lower coupon and debt elimination for issuer; dilution for existing shareholders.

</details>

**Q2048** (§4.4 · FINRA section 2) — In a corporate liquidation, which group is paid immediately BEFORE preferred stockholders?

- A. Senior debenture holders
- B. Common stockholders
- C. Subordinated debenture holders
- D. Secured bondholders

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Order: secured creditors, then general creditors (including debentures), then subordinated debentures, then preferred, then common.

*Remember:* Liquidation: secured > general creditors > subordinated > preferred > common.

</details>

**Q2049** (§4.4 · FINRA section 2) — In a liquidation, first-mortgage bondholders are owed $10 million, but the pledged plant sells for only $7 million. How is the remaining $3 million claim treated?

- A. It becomes an unsecured claim alongside the general creditors
- B. It is forgiven once the collateral is sold
- C. It moves ahead of all other creditors as a priority claim
- D. It is converted into preferred stock of the company

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Secured creditors are paid first up to the value of their collateral. Any shortfall joins the general (unsecured) creditors for the remainder.

*Remember:* Secured priority extends only to the collateral's value; any shortfall becomes a general claim.

</details>

**Q2050** (§4.4 · FINRA section 2) — A struggling corporation continues operating under court supervision while it restructures its debts, and its old bondholders receive new securities in the company. This process is:

- A. A Chapter 7 liquidation
- B. An advance refunding
- C. A Chapter 11 reorganization
- D. A sinking fund redemption

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Chapter 11 lets the company keep operating while debts are reorganized; bondholders often receive new securities, sometimes income bonds or stock.

*Remember:* Chapter 11 = reorganize and keep operating; Chapter 7 = liquidate.

</details>

**Q2051** (§4.5 · FINRA section 2) — A customer owns one bond described as "DEF 6 1/2s of '41." How much interest does she receive with each regular payment?

- A. $65.00
- B. $6.50
- C. $16.25
- D. $32.50

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** "6 1/2s" means a 6.5% coupon: $65 per year on $1,000 par, paid semiannually as $32.50.

1. 6.5% x $1,000 = $65 per year
2. $65 / 2 = $32.50 per payment

*Remember:* Semiannual payment = (coupon % x $1,000) / 2.

</details>

**Q2052** (§4.5 · FINRA section 2) — A 5% corporate bond pays interest F&A 15. A trade settles on October 3. Using 30/360, how much accrued interest does the buyer pay the seller per bond?

- A. $6.81
- B. $6.53
- C. $8.33
- D. $6.67

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** From August 15 up to (not including) October 3: 16 days in August + 30 in September + 2 in October = 48 days. $50 x 48/360 = $6.67.

1. Aug 15-30 = 16, Sep = 30, Oct 1-2 = 2
2. Total = 48 days
3. $50 x 48/360 = $6.67

*Remember:* Accrued interest = annual interest x days/360; count to the day before settlement.

</details>

**Q2053** (§4.5 · FINRA section 2) — A customer buys 10 corporate bonds at 101 5/8. Ignoring commissions and accrued interest, the total cost is:

- A. $10,158.00
- B. $10,016.25
- C. $10,162.50
- D. $10,625.00

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** 101 5/8 = 101.625% of par = $1,016.25 per bond; 10 bonds = $10,162.50.

1. 5/8 = 0.625
2. 101.625% x $1,000 = $1,016.25
3. 10 x $1,016.25 = $10,162.50

*Remember:* Bond price = quote% x $1,000; 1/8 point = $1.25.

</details>

**Q2054** (§4.5 · FINRA section 3) — A broker-dealer sells a corporate bond to a retail customer from its own inventory and buys the bond the same day. What must the customer's confirmation generally disclose?

- A. Nothing about compensation, since it was a principal trade
- B. The firm's total profit on its bond inventory for the year
- C. The dealer's markup on the transaction
- D. The names of the parties who sold the bond to the dealer

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** FINRA rules adopted in 2018 require retail confirmations on most corporate and agency bond trades to disclose the markup or markdown when the dealer buys and sells the bond as principal the same day.

*Remember:* Same-day principal corporate/agency bond trades: retail confirmation must disclose the markup/markdown.

</details>

**Q2055** (§4.6 · FINRA section 2) — A customer has $300,000 in a single-name certificate of deposit at one bank and no other accounts there. How much of the CD is NOT covered by FDIC insurance?

- A. $0
- B. $200,000
- C. $50,000
- D. $300,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** FDIC insurance covers $250,000 per depositor, per bank, per ownership category. $300,000 - $250,000 = $50,000 uninsured.

1. Coverage limit = $250,000
2. $300,000 - $250,000 = $50,000 uninsured

*Remember:* FDIC limit: $250,000 per depositor, per bank, per ownership category.

</details>

**Q2056** (§4.6 · FINRA section 2) — All of the following statements about brokered CDs are true EXCEPT:

- A. They are FDIC-insured, with insurance passing through to the customer
- B. The broker-dealer guarantees full principal if the CD is sold before maturity
- C. They can be sold in a secondary market, possibly below par
- D. Some are callable by the issuing bank

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Brokered CDs are FDIC-insured and may be callable, but if sold before maturity in the secondary market the price may be below par. No one guarantees principal on an early sale.

*Remember:* Brokered CDs: FDIC pass-through, may be callable, secondary-market price can be below par.

</details>

**Q2057** (§4.6 · FINRA section 2) — A customer buys a market-linked (structured) CD whose return is tied to a stock index. Which statement is MOST accurate?

- A. The index return is guaranteed regardless of market performance
- B. Principal protection generally applies only if the CD is held to maturity
- C. It pays a fixed rate like a traditional CD
- D. It can be redeemed at par at any time without penalty

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Structured CDs tie returns to an index; principal is protected only if held to maturity. Their complexity raises suitability concerns.

*Remember:* Structured/market-linked CD: index-based return; principal protected only at maturity.

</details>

**Q2058** (§4.7 · FINRA section 2) — A Japanese corporation issues US-dollar-denominated bonds in the United States and registers them with the SEC. These bonds are:

- A. Yankee bonds
- B. Eurodollar bonds
- C. Eurodollar CDs
- D. Euroyen bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Yankee bonds are dollar-denominated bonds sold in the US by foreign issuers and registered with the SEC; US investors face no currency risk.

*Remember:* Yankee bond: foreign issuer, in the US, in dollars, SEC-registered.

</details>

**Q2059** (§4.7 · FINRA section 2) — Compared with a typical US domestic corporate bond, a Eurodollar bond generally:

- A. Pays interest annually and is not registered with the SEC
- B. Pays interest semiannually and is SEC-registered
- C. Is denominated in euros and carries currency risk
- D. Is issued in the US by a foreign corporation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Eurodollar bonds are dollar-denominated, issued outside the US, not SEC-registered, and usually pay interest annually, often in bearer form.

*Remember:* Eurodollar bond: USD, issued outside the US, unregistered, annual interest.

</details>

**Q2060** (§4.7 · FINRA section 1) — A US company deposits $5 million in US dollars at a bank in Singapore. Which statement about this deposit is TRUE?

- A. It is a domestic deposit insured by the FDIC up to $250,000
- B. It cannot be a Eurodollar deposit because Singapore is not in Europe
- C. It is subject to US bank reserve requirements
- D. It is a Eurodollar deposit and is not FDIC-insured

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Eurodollars are US-dollar deposits in banks outside the United States, anywhere in the world. They are not subject to US reserve requirements or FDIC insurance.

*Remember:* Eurodollars: USD deposits in banks outside the US; no FDIC, no US reserve requirements.

</details>

**Q9012** (§4.3 · FINRA section 2) — A $1,000 par bond is convertible at $40. The common stock trades at $44, and the bond trades at $1,130. Which statement is TRUE?

- A. The bond trades $30 above parity, so converting now is not advantageous
- B. The bond trades below parity, creating an arbitrage opportunity
- C. The bond's parity price is $1,760
- D. The stock's parity price is $45.20, so the stock trades above parity

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The conversion ratio is 25 shares, so bond parity is 25 × $44 = $1,100. The bond's $1,130 price is $30 above parity, so converting would give shares worth less than the bond's market value.

1. Ratio = $1,000 ÷ $40 = 25 shares
2. Bond parity = 25 × $44 = $1,100
3. $1,130 − $1,100 = $30 premium over parity

*Remember:* Ratio = par ÷ conversion price; bond parity = stock price × ratio

</details>

**Q9013** (§4.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which money-market instrument typically pays interest at maturity rather than being sold at a discount from face value?

- A. Commercial paper
- B. Banker's acceptance
- C. Treasury bill
- D. Negotiable (jumbo) CD

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Negotiable CDs are bank time deposits of $100,000 or more that pay stated interest at maturity. Commercial paper, banker's acceptances and T-bills are sold at a discount and mature at face value.

*Remember:* Negotiable CDs pay interest at maturity; CP, BAs and T-bills are discount instruments

</details>

**Q9014** (§4.7 · FINRA section 2) — A US investor who spends and saves only in dollars buys each of the following. Which one exposes her to currency risk?

- A. A Yankee bond issued in the US by a Japanese company
- B. A Eurodollar bond issued in London by a US company
- C. A yen-denominated Eurobond sold in London
- D. A US Treasury note

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A bond denominated in a foreign currency pays interest and principal in that currency, so a dollar-based investor bears exchange-rate risk. Yankee and Eurodollar bonds are both denominated in US dollars.

*Remember:* Currency risk depends on the bond's currency, not where it is issued

</details>

**Q9015** (§4.6 · FINRA section 2) — A customer holds a $200,000 CD in her individual name and a $200,000 CD in her IRA at the same FDIC-insured bank. She has no other deposits there. How much of her money is NOT FDIC-insured?

- A. $150,000
- B. $0
- C. $200,000
- D. $50,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** FDIC coverage is $250,000 per depositor, per bank, per ownership category. Individual accounts and IRAs are separate categories, so each $200,000 CD is fully covered.

*Remember:* FDIC: $250,000 per depositor, per bank, per ownership category

</details>

## Chapter 5: Municipal Debt

**Q41** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — An investor in the 35% federal tax bracket is comparing a municipal bond yielding 4.2% with a corporate bond. The corporate bond must yield at least approximately:

- A. 5.67%
- B. 6.46%
- C. 2.73%
- D. 4.20%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Tax-equivalent yield = 4.2% ÷ (1 − 0.35) = 4.2% ÷ 0.65 = 6.46%.

1. 1 − 0.35 = 0.65
2. 4.2% ÷ 0.65 = 6.46%

*Remember:* Tax-equivalent yield = muni yield ÷ (1 − tax bracket).

</details>

**Q42** (§5.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is TRUE of general obligation bonds but NOT revenue bonds?

- A. They are backed by user fees from a specific project
- B. They are usually subject to voter approval and statutory debt limits
- C. They require a feasibility study
- D. They are exempt from federal taxation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** G.O. bonds pledge the issuer's taxing power, so they typically need voter approval and are subject to debt limits. Revenue bonds rely on a project's revenue and a feasibility study.

*Remember:* G.O. bonds are backed by taxing power, so they need voter approval and face debt limits.

</details>

**Q43** (§5.4 · FINRA section 2) — A municipality issues short-term notes to be repaid from the proceeds of a long-term bond issue it plans to sell next year. These are:

- A. Tax anticipation notes
- B. Revenue anticipation notes
- C. Bond anticipation notes
- D. Grant anticipation notes

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** BANs are interim financing paid off by a future bond sale. TANs come from future taxes; RANs from future non-tax revenue; GANs from expected grants.

*Remember:* BANs are repaid from a future bond sale; TANs, RANs, GANs from taxes, revenues, grants.

</details>

**Q44** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — Interest from which of the following municipal bonds may be subject to the alternative minimum tax?

- A. General obligation bonds
- B. Public housing authority bonds
- C. Private activity (industrial development) bonds
- D. Pre-refunded bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Private activity bonds — those that primarily benefit a private entity, such as industrial development bonds — are AMT preference items.

*Remember:* Private activity bond interest is a preference item that may be subject to the AMT.

</details>

**Q45** (§5.5 · FINRA section 4) — The MSRB's authority is best described as:

- A. Making and enforcing rules for municipal issuers
- B. Making rules for municipal dealers, which are enforced by FINRA, the SEC and bank regulators
- C. Registering municipal securities with the SEC
- D. Insuring municipal bonds against default

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The MSRB writes rules for broker-dealers, bank dealers and municipal advisors but has no enforcement power and no jurisdiction over issuers.

*Remember:* MSRB writes rules for muni dealers and advisors; FINRA, SEC and bank regulators enforce them.

</details>

**Q46** (§5.5 · FINRA section 4) — Under MSRB Rule G-37, a municipal finance professional who contributes $500 to the campaign of a mayoral candidate for whom she is entitled to vote will cause her firm to be:

- A. Fined $500
- B. Banned from negotiated underwriting with that issuer for two years
- C. Required to disclose the contribution on EMMA only
- D. Unaffected, because she can vote for the candidate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The de minimis exception is $250 per election for candidates the MFP can vote for. A $500 contribution exceeds it and triggers the two-year ban on negotiated business with that issuer.

*Remember:* G-37: MFP may give $250 per election to candidates she can vote for; more triggers a two-year ban.

</details>

**Q47** (§5.6 · FINRA section 2) — Which account is LEAST appropriate for municipal bonds?

- A. A taxable brokerage account of a high-income investor
- B. A corporate cash management account
- C. A traditional IRA
- D. A joint account of a retired couple in a high bracket

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Muni interest is already tax-exempt; inside a tax-deferred IRA the benefit is wasted and withdrawals become taxable ordinary income. Munis yield less than taxable bonds, so the investor loses.

*Remember:* Tax-exempt munis don't belong in tax-deferred accounts like IRAs; the tax benefit is wasted.

</details>

**Q48** (§5.3 · FINRA section 2) — A revenue bond's debt service coverage ratio of 1.8 means:

- A. Debt service is 1.8 times net revenue
- B. Net revenue is 1.8 times the annual principal and interest
- C. The bond is rated 1.8 notches above investment grade
- D. The issuer may borrow 1.8 times its current debt

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Coverage ratio = net revenue available ÷ annual debt service. 1.8 means revenue covers debt service 1.8 times — higher is safer.

*Remember:* Debt service coverage = net revenue ÷ annual debt service (principal + interest); higher is safer.

</details>

**Q49** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — A resident of Texas buys a bond issued by the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico. The interest is:

- A. Subject to federal tax but exempt from Texas tax
- B. Exempt from federal tax only
- C. Exempt from federal, state and local taxes
- D. Fully taxable

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Bonds of US territories (Puerto Rico, Guam, USVI) are triple tax-exempt for investors in every state.

*Remember:* US territory bonds (Puerto Rico, Guam, USVI) are triple tax-exempt for residents of any state.

</details>

**Q50** (§5.5 · FINRA section 1) — A municipal G.O. bond issue is most commonly awarded through:

- A. A negotiated underwriting
- B. A competitive bid based on the lowest net interest cost
- C. A best-efforts offering
- D. A private placement under Regulation D

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** G.O. issues typically publish a notice of sale and award the bonds to the syndicate with the lowest NIC/TIC. Revenue bonds are usually negotiated.

*Remember:* G.O. bonds are usually awarded by competitive bid to the syndicate with the lowest interest cost.

</details>

**Q3001** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer in the 24% federal bracket is offered a corporate bond yielding 5.5%. To earn the same after-tax income, a municipal bond would need to yield approximately:

- A. 7.24%
- B. 1.32%
- C. 4.18%
- D. 5.24%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Tax-free equivalent yield = taxable yield × (1 − bracket) = 5.5% × 0.76 = 4.18%. Any muni yielding more than 4.18% beats the corporate after tax for this investor.

1. 1 − 0.24 = 0.76
2. 5.5% × 0.76 = 4.18%

*Remember:* Tax-free equivalent yield = corporate yield × (1 − tax bracket).

</details>

**Q3002** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — A client in the 37% federal bracket is choosing between a 3.15% municipal bond and a 4.80% corporate bond of similar quality and maturity. Which statement is accurate?

- A. The muni's TEY is 4.32%, so the corporate bond pays more after tax
- B. The muni's TEY is 5.00%, so the muni pays more after tax
- C. The corporate's after-tax yield is 3.02%, so the corporate pays more after tax
- D. The muni's TEY is 8.51%, so the muni pays more after tax

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** TEY = 3.15% ÷ (1 − 0.37) = 3.15% ÷ 0.63 = 5.00%, which beats the 4.80% corporate. Equivalently, the corporate nets only 4.80% × 0.63 = 3.02% after tax, below the muni's 3.15%.

1. 1 − 0.37 = 0.63
2. 3.15% ÷ 0.63 = 5.00%
3. 5.00% > 4.80%, so the muni wins

*Remember:* TEY = muni yield ÷ (1 − tax bracket).

</details>

**Q3003** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer bought a newly issued municipal bond at par ($1,000), collected tax-free interest for three years, then sold it for $1,060 after rates fell. How is the transaction taxed?

- A. Neither the interest nor the $60 is taxable because the issuer is a municipality
- B. The interest and the $60 are both taxed as ordinary income
- C. The interest is taxable, but the $60 is tax-exempt
- D. The interest was federally tax-exempt, but the $60 is a taxable capital gain

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Only the interest on a muni is tax-exempt. Profit from selling the bond above its cost basis is a capital gain and is fully taxable.

*Remember:* Muni interest is tax-exempt; muni capital gains are taxable.

</details>

**Q3004** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — Bond counsel delivers an unqualified legal opinion on a new municipal issue. This means counsel:

- A. Has no reservations that the bonds are valid obligations and the interest is tax-exempt
- B. Found the issuer's credit to be investment grade
- C. Verified that the underwriter's pricing is fair and reasonable
- D. Expressed concerns that must be disclosed in the official statement

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The legal opinion addresses legality and tax status. 'Unqualified' means counsel has no reservations; a qualified opinion would raise concerns.

*Remember:* Unqualified legal opinion = no reservations on validity or tax exemption (good).

</details>

**Q3005** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — A resident of Ohio buys a general obligation bond issued by the State of Michigan. The interest is generally:

- A. Exempt from federal income tax but subject to Ohio income tax
- B. Taxable federally but exempt from Ohio income tax
- C. Exempt from federal, Ohio and local income tax
- D. Subject to federal and Ohio income tax

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Muni interest is federally exempt for everyone, but the state exemption usually applies only to residents of the issuing state. An Ohio resident owning a Michigan bond generally pays Ohio tax on the interest.

*Remember:* Federal exemption for all; state exemption usually only for in-state residents.

</details>

**Q3006** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer insists on interest that is exempt from federal income tax. Which of the following would NOT meet that objective?

- A. A Build America Bond issued by a city
- B. A G.O. bond issued by a county
- C. A revenue bond issued by a water authority
- D. A bond issued by Guam

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Build America Bonds are taxable municipal bonds; the issuer receives a federal subsidy instead of the investor getting a tax exemption. The other choices pay federally tax-exempt interest.

*Remember:* Taxable munis (e.g., Build America Bonds) pay federally taxable interest.

</details>

**Q3007** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — An investor wants to read the official statement and recent trade prices for a school district bond she owns. Which free resource provides both?

- A. EDGAR
- B. TRACE
- C. BrokerCheck
- D. EMMA

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The MSRB's Electronic Municipal Market Access (EMMA) site publishes official statements, continuing disclosures and municipal trade data.

*Remember:* EMMA = MSRB site for muni official statements, continuing disclosure and trade data.

</details>

**Q3008** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following are TRUE of municipal securities?
I. They are exempt from registration under the Securities Act of 1933
II. They are exempt from the Trust Indenture Act
III. They are exempt from the anti-fraud provisions of federal securities law
IV. The issuer prepares an official statement instead of a prospectus

- A. I and II only
- B. I, II and IV
- C. II, III and IV
- D. I, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Munis are exempt from 1933 Act registration and the Trust Indenture Act, and use an official statement for disclosure. Anti-fraud rules apply to every security, including munis.

*Remember:* Munis: exempt from registration, never from anti-fraud rules.

</details>

**Q3009** (§5.2 · FINRA section 2) — A homeowner's property is assessed at $250,000, and the town levies 12 mills to service its G.O. debt. What is the annual tax for debt service?

- A. $300
- B. $30,000
- C. $12,000
- D. $3,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** One mill is $1 per $1,000 of assessed value (0.1%). 12 mills × 250 = $3,000.

1. 12 mills = 0.012
2. $250,000 × 0.012 = $3,000

*Remember:* Property tax = assessed value × mills ÷ 1,000.

</details>

**Q3010** (§5.2 · FINRA section 2) — An analyst reviewing a city's G.O. bonds adds in the county and school district debt that is also paid by the city's taxpayers. This added amount is called:

- A. Net direct debt
- B. Debt service coverage
- C. Overlapping debt
- D. The statutory debt limit

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Overlapping debt is debt of other jurisdictions that share the same tax base. It matters because the same taxpayers must support all of it.

*Remember:* Overlapping debt = debt of other jurisdictions levied on the same taxpayers.

</details>

**Q3011** (§5.2 · FINRA section 2) — All of the following are useful in evaluating a general obligation bond EXCEPT:

- A. Trends in property values and population
- B. Debt per capita
- C. The size of unfunded pension liabilities
- D. An engineering feasibility study for the financed project

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** G.O. bonds are repaid from taxes, so analysis focuses on the tax base, debt burden and budget health. A feasibility study projects a single project's revenue — a revenue bond tool.

*Remember:* Feasibility study = revenue bond analysis; tax base and debt load = G.O. analysis.

</details>

**Q3012** (§5.2 · FINRA section 2) — A city issues bonds for its water system that are paid from water fees, with the city's taxing power pledged if fees fall short. These bonds are:

- A. Moral obligation bonds, backed by a non-binding legislative pledge
- B. Double-barreled bonds, generally rated like G.O. bonds
- C. Special assessment bonds, backed by levies on benefited property
- D. Industrial development bonds, backed by a corporate lease

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Double-barreled bonds have two sources of repayment: project revenue and the issuer's full faith and credit. Because taxing power stands behind them, they are rated as G.O.s.

*Remember:* Double-barreled = revenue plus taxing power backup; rated as G.O.

</details>

**Q3013** (§5.3 · FINRA section 2) — A bridge authority has gross revenues of $15 million and operating and maintenance expenses of $6 million. Annual debt service is $4 million ($1.5 million principal and $2.5 million interest). What is the debt service coverage ratio?

- A. 3.75
- B. 3.60
- C. 2.25
- D. 1.50

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Coverage = net revenue ÷ annual debt service. Net revenue is $15M − $6M = $9M, and $9M ÷ $4M = 2.25 times.

1. Net revenue = $15M − $6M = $9M
2. Debt service = $1.5M + $2.5M = $4M
3. $9M ÷ $4M = 2.25

*Remember:* Debt service coverage = (gross revenue − O&M) ÷ (principal + interest).

</details>

**Q3014** (§5.3 · FINRA section 2) — Under a typical net revenue pledge, the first use of a revenue bond project's gross revenues is to pay:

- A. Debt service on the bonds
- B. Operating and maintenance expenses
- C. The debt service reserve fund
- D. Surplus and capital improvement funds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** In the flow of funds, operations and maintenance are paid first, then debt service, then reserves, then surplus. A project that isn't kept running can't generate revenue for bondholders.

*Remember:* Flow of funds: O&M → debt service → reserves → surplus.

</details>

**Q3015** (§5.3 · FINRA section 2) — A toll road authority promises bondholders that it will set tolls high enough to cover operating costs and debt service. This promise is a:

- A. Rate covenant
- B. Additional-bonds test
- C. Maintenance covenant
- D. Insurance covenant

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A rate covenant obligates the issuer to charge user fees sufficient to cover expenses and debt service.

*Remember:* Rate covenant = issuer will charge fees high enough to cover costs and debt service.

</details>

**Q3016** (§5.3 · FINRA section 2) — A revenue bond indenture states that the issuer may sell new bonds with an equal claim on the project's revenues only if earnings meet a specified coverage level. This protects existing bondholders and is known as:

- A. A non-discrimination covenant
- B. A rate covenant
- C. A flow of funds provision
- D. An additional-bonds test

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The additional-bonds test keeps the issuer from diluting existing bondholders' claim on revenue unless coverage is adequate.

*Remember:* Additional-bonds test limits new parity debt to protect existing holders.

</details>

**Q3017** (§5.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which statement about a moral obligation bond is accurate?

- A. If project revenues fall short, the state legislature may, but is not legally required to, appropriate funds
- B. The state's full faith and credit is legally pledged as backup to project revenues
- C. It is repaid solely from a dedicated tax such as a gasoline or hotel tax
- D. It is backed by the US government and considered the safest municipal bond

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A moral obligation bond is a revenue bond with a non-binding promise that the legislature may step in. The backup is moral, not legal.

*Remember:* Moral obligation = non-binding legislative backup; requires a vote to appropriate.

</details>

**Q3018** (§5.3 · FINRA section 2) — A county issues industrial development revenue bonds to build a plant leased to a manufacturer. The bonds' credit quality depends primarily on the:

- A. County's property tax base
- B. State legislature's willingness to appropriate funds
- C. Manufacturer's ability to make its lease payments
- D. Federal guarantee on the plant's mortgage

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** IDRs are repaid from the private corporation's lease payments, so their credit is essentially the corporation's credit. They are private activity bonds, often subject to AMT.

*Remember:* IDR credit = the private corporate lessee's credit; usually AMT bonds.

</details>

**Q3019** (§5.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which type of municipal bond is considered the safest because it is ultimately backed by the US government?

- A. Public housing authority (PHA) bonds
- B. Moral obligation bonds
- C. Special tax bonds
- D. Certificates of participation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** PHA/NHA bonds are backed by the US government, making them the safest municipal bonds.

*Remember:* PHA/NHA bonds: US government backed, the safest munis.

</details>

**Q3020** (§5.4 · FINRA section 2) — A town must meet payroll this month but won't collect its semiannual property taxes for four more months. To bridge the gap, it would most likely issue:

- A. Bond anticipation notes
- B. Tax anticipation notes
- C. Grant anticipation notes
- D. Construction loan notes

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** TANs are short-term notes repaid from upcoming tax collections, bridging the gap between spending and tax receipts.

*Remember:* TAN = repaid from future tax collections.

</details>

**Q3021** (§5.4 · FINRA section 2) — A state transportation department will receive federal highway funds next year and borrows short-term now against that money. The notes are:

- A. Tax anticipation notes
- B. Bond anticipation notes
- C. Construction loan notes
- D. Grant anticipation notes

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** GANs are repaid from expected federal or state grant money.

*Remember:* GAN = repaid from expected federal/state grants.

</details>

**Q3022** (§5.4 · FINRA section 2) — Which feature of a variable-rate demand obligation (VRDO) allows it to be treated as a money-market instrument even though its stated maturity is long-term?

- A. A put allowing holders to tender at par on reset dates
- B. A call allowing the issuer to redeem after 10 years
- C. A sinking fund that retires bonds annually
- D. A fixed coupon guaranteed by bond insurance

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** VRDO rates reset daily or weekly, and the demand (put) feature lets investors sell back at par on reset dates, giving short-term liquidity.

*Remember:* VRDO = periodic rate reset plus put at par on reset dates.

</details>

**Q3023** (§5.4 · FINRA section 2) — Moody's uses which rating scale specifically for short-term municipal notes?

- A. Aaa through Baa
- B. P-1 through P-3
- C. MIG 1 through MIG 3
- D. SP-1 through SP-3

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Moody's rates municipal notes MIG 1–3 (VMIG for variable-rate demand notes). S&P's municipal note scale is SP-1 to SP-3.

*Remember:* Moody's muni notes: MIG 1–3 (VMIG for VRDOs); S&P: SP-1 to SP-3.

</details>

**Q3024** (§5.5 · FINRA section 1) — A municipal syndicate has more orders than bonds. Under the usual priority, which type of order is filled LAST?

- A. Pre-sale order
- B. Group net order
- C. Designated order
- D. Member order

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Standard priority is pre-sale, then group net, then designated, then member orders. Member orders benefit only the placing member, so they come last.

*Remember:* Muni order priority: pre-sale → group net → designated → member.

</details>

**Q3025** (§5.5 · FINRA section 1) — A hospital revenue authority picks one underwriting firm and works out the bond's interest rates and price directly with it. This is a:

- A. Negotiated underwriting, typical for revenue bonds
- B. Competitive sale awarded on lowest net interest cost
- C. Competitive sale announced through a notice of sale
- D. Private placement exempt from MSRB rules

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** In a negotiated sale the issuer selects an underwriter and negotiates terms, the usual method for revenue bonds. Competitive sales use a notice of sale and award to the best bid.

*Remember:* Negotiated sale = issuer picks underwriter; typical for revenue bonds.

</details>

**Q3026** (§5.5 · FINRA section 3) — A dealer sells a municipal bond to a customer. Within what time frame must the trade generally be reported to the MSRB's RTRS?

- A. 10 seconds
- B. 15 minutes
- C. End of the trading day
- D. By settlement on T+1

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Municipal trades are reported to the MSRB's Real-Time Transaction Reporting System within 15 minutes and then displayed on EMMA.

*Remember:* Muni trades: report to RTRS within 15 minutes.

</details>

**Q3027** (§5.5 · FINRA section 3) — A municipal bond with a 6% coupon pays interest January 1 and July 1. A customer buys $1,000 par in a regular-way trade on Tuesday, March 9. How much accrued interest does the buyer pay the seller?

- A. $11.18
- B. $11.33
- C. $11.50
- D. $11.67

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Regular-way settlement is T+1, Wednesday, March 10. Using 30/360, interest accrues from January 1 up to but not including March 10: 30 + 30 + 9 = 69 days, so $60 × 69/360 = $11.50.

1. Settlement = T+1 = March 10
2. Days (30/360) = 30 + 30 + 9 = 69
3. $60 × 69/360 = $11.50

*Remember:* Muni accrued interest = annual interest × 30/360 days to (not including) settlement.

</details>

**Q3028** (§5.5 · FINRA section 4) — Under MSRB Rule G-20, the most a municipal securities representative may give in gifts to a person, in connection with that person's employer's business, per year is:

- A. $100
- B. $250
- C. $300
- D. $500

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** MSRB G-20 now caps gifts at $300 per person per year, matching FINRA Rule 3220. Ordinary business entertainment is excluded from the limit.

*Remember:* MSRB G-20 and FINRA 3220 gift limit: $300 per person per year.

</details>

**Q3029** (§5.5 · FINRA section 4) — A municipal finance professional who lives in Connecticut contributes $150 to the campaign of a New York City mayoral candidate. If the candidate wins, under MSRB Rule G-37 the MFP's firm:

- A. Is barred from negotiated underwriting with the city for two years
- B. Must report the gift but may continue all business with the city
- C. Is unaffected because the contribution is under $250
- D. Is permanently barred from all municipal business with the city

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The $250 de minimis applies only to candidates the MFP is entitled to vote for. A Connecticut resident can't vote in a New York City election, so any contribution triggers the two-year ban on negotiated business.

*Remember:* G-37: $250 per election only if MFP can vote for candidate; else two-year ban.

</details>

**Q3030** (§5.5 · FINRA section 3) — A customer buys a municipal bond in the secondary market at 92. It was originally issued at par, and she holds it to maturity. How is the 8-point discount taxed?

- A. As ordinary income when realized
- B. As tax-exempt interest, like original issue discount
- C. As a long-term capital gain
- D. It is not taxed because the issuer is a municipality

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A market discount on a muni bought in the secondary market is taxed as ordinary income when realized. Only original issue discount accretes tax-free.

*Remember:* Muni market discount = ordinary income; muni OID = tax-exempt accretion.

</details>

**Q3031** (§5.5 · FINRA section 3) — A customer buys a municipal bond at 108 and holds it until it matures at par. What is the tax result?

- A. An 8-point capital loss deductible at maturity
- B. An 8-point ordinary loss spread over the bond's life
- C. No gain or loss, because the amortized premium lowered her cost basis to par
- D. A capital gain because the bond paid tax-exempt interest

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Muni premiums must be amortized, reducing cost basis to par by maturity. Because the interest is tax-free, the amortization isn't deductible, so there's no loss at maturity.

*Remember:* Muni premium: must amortize; basis falls to par; no deductible loss.

</details>

**Q3032** (§5.6 · FINRA section 3) — For which customer would individual municipal bonds be MOST suitable?

- A. A 30-year-old seeking long-term growth in a Roth IRA
- B. A retiree in the 12% bracket who wants the highest after-tax income
- C. A couple in the 35% bracket seeking income in a taxable brokerage account
- D. A 401(k) participant choosing a conservative fund option

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Munis fit high-bracket investors seeking tax-free income in taxable accounts, where the exemption has real value.

*Remember:* Best muni buyer: high tax bracket, wants income, taxable account.

</details>

**Q3033** (§5.6 · FINRA section 2) — Congress debates a proposal to cap the federal tax exemption on municipal bond interest, and muni prices fall. This illustrates:

- A. Legislative risk
- B. Credit risk
- C. Call risk
- D. Reinvestment risk

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Legislative risk is the danger that a change in law, such as the tax code, reduces a security's value. Munis are vulnerable because their appeal rests on the exemption.

*Remember:* Legislative risk: a law change (e.g., tax code) reduces a security's value.

</details>

**Q3034** (§5.6 · FINRA section 2) — A customer owns $15,000 of a small, rarely traded water district bond and may need to sell on short notice. Which risk is her greatest concern?

- A. Purchasing power risk
- B. Legislative risk
- C. Prepayment risk
- D. Liquidity risk

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The muni secondary market is thin, and many small issues rarely trade. Selling a small block quickly may require a price concession.

*Remember:* Munis carry liquidity risk because of a thin secondary market.

</details>

**Q3035** (§5.6 · FINRA section 3) — A customer in a high federal bracket has $20,000 to invest and wants tax-free income with diversification. The most suitable recommendation is:

- A. A single long-term municipal revenue bond
- B. A municipal bond fund
- C. A Treasury STRIPS position
- D. A taxable corporate bond fund

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A muni bond fund or ETF provides tax-free income and diversification to an investor who can't buy enough individual bonds to diversify.

*Remember:* Muni funds/ETFs: tax-free income plus diversification for smaller investors.

</details>

**Q9016** (§5.1 · FINRA section 2) — A municipal bond yields 3.5% and a corporate bond of similar quality and maturity yields 5.0%. For which investor would the municipal bond provide the higher after-tax yield?

- A. An investor in the 12% bracket
- B. An investor in the 22% bracket
- C. An investor in the 24% bracket
- D. An investor in the 32% bracket

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The muni beats the corporate only when its tax-equivalent yield exceeds 5.0%. At 32%, 3.5% ÷ 0.68 ≈ 5.15%. At 24%, 3.5% ÷ 0.76 ≈ 4.61%, which is too low.

1. 32%: 3.5% ÷ 0.68 ≈ 5.15% (> 5.0%)
2. 24%: 3.5% ÷ 0.76 ≈ 4.61% (< 5.0%)

*Remember:* TEY = muni yield ÷ (1 − tax bracket)

</details>

**Q9017** (§5.3 · FINRA section 2) — A town installs new sidewalks and sewer lines in one neighborhood and issues bonds repaid only by charges levied on the properties that benefit from the improvements. These are:

- A. Special assessment bonds
- B. Special tax bonds
- C. General obligation bonds
- D. Lease-rental bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Special assessment bonds are repaid from assessments on the specific properties that benefit from the improvement, not from general taxes.

*Remember:* Special assessment bonds = paid by assessments on benefiting properties

</details>

**Q9018** (§5.4 · FINRA section 2) — A state housing agency borrows short-term to fund construction of an apartment project. The notes will be repaid from permanent mortgage financing once the project is completed. These notes are:

- A. Bond anticipation notes
- B. Revenue anticipation notes
- C. Grant anticipation notes
- D. Construction loan notes

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Construction loan notes provide interim financing for housing projects and are repaid when permanent financing is put in place after construction.

*Remember:* CLNs = interim housing construction financing repaid from permanent financing

</details>

**Q9019** (§5.5 · FINRA section 2) — A small city with an A rating buys bond insurance on its new revenue issue from an insurer rated AA. The insured bonds will generally carry:

- A. The city's own A rating, since insurance does not affect ratings
- B. The insurer's AA rating
- C. An automatic AAA rating
- D. No rating, since insured munis need not be rated

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Municipal bond insurance guarantees principal and interest, so insured bonds carry the insurer's credit rating.

*Remember:* Insured munis carry the insurer's rating

</details>

## Chapter 6: US Government Debt (and the Economy)

**Q51** (§6.1 · FINRA section 2) — Interest earned on US Treasury notes is:

- A. Exempt from federal tax, taxable at the state level
- B. Taxable at the federal level, exempt from state and local tax
- C. Exempt at all levels
- D. Taxable at all levels

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Treasury interest is subject to federal income tax but exempt from state and local tax — the opposite of municipal bonds.

*Remember:* Treasury interest is federally taxable but exempt from state and local tax.

</details>

**Q52** (§6.2 · FINRA section 2) — A Treasury note is quoted at 98.24. Its dollar price is:

- A. $982.40
- B. $987.50
- C. $982.75
- D. $998.24

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Treasury notes and bonds are quoted in 32nds: 98.24 = 98 24/32 = 98.75% of $1,000 = $987.50.

1. 24/32 = 0.75, so price = 98.75% of par
2. 98.75% × $1,000 = $987.50

*Remember:* Treasury notes and bonds are quoted in 32nds: the number after the point = n/32.

</details>

**Q53** (§6.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which agency's mortgage-backed securities are backed by the full faith and credit of the US government?

- A. Fannie Mae
- B. Freddie Mac
- C. Ginnie Mae
- D. Federal Home Loan Banks

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Only GNMA pass-throughs carry the direct full-faith-and-credit backing of the US government. Fannie and Freddie are GSEs with implied support.

*Remember:* Only Ginnie Mae (GNMA) carries the full faith and credit of the US government.

</details>

**Q54** (§6.3 · FINRA section 2) — The holder of a mortgage-backed pass-through certificate is most exposed to prepayment risk when:

- A. Interest rates rise
- B. Interest rates fall
- C. Housing prices fall
- D. The Fed sells Treasuries

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** When rates fall, homeowners refinance, returning principal early that must be reinvested at lower rates. Rising rates cause extension risk instead.

*Remember:* Falling rates cause prepayment risk in MBS; rising rates cause extension risk.

</details>

**Q55** (§6.5.3 · FINRA section 1) — To stimulate a slowing economy, the Federal Open Market Committee would most likely:

- A. Sell Treasury securities
- B. Raise the discount rate
- C. Buy Treasury securities
- D. Raise reserve requirements

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Buying securities injects money into the banking system, increasing reserves and pushing interest rates down (easy money). Selling, raising rates or raising reserve requirements tighten.

*Remember:* The FOMC loosens by buying Treasuries in the open market, adding money to banks.

</details>

**Q56** (§6.5.2 · FINRA section 1) — The rate that banks charge each other for overnight loans of excess reserves is the:

- A. Discount rate
- B. Prime rate
- C. Federal funds rate
- D. Broker call rate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The fed funds rate is bank-to-bank overnight. The discount rate is what the Fed charges banks; prime is what banks charge top customers; broker call is what banks charge broker-dealers for margin loans.

*Remember:* Fed funds rate is what banks charge each other overnight on excess reserves.

</details>

**Q57** (§6.5.4 · FINRA section 1) — Which of the following is considered a LEADING economic indicator?

- A. The unemployment rate
- B. The Consumer Price Index
- C. Building permits
- D. Corporate profits

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Building permits/housing starts, the stock market, M2, new orders and initial jobless claims lead the economy. Unemployment rate, CPI and corporate profits lag.

*Remember:* Building permits, stock prices, M2, new orders and jobless claims lead; unemployment, CPI, profits lag.

</details>

**Q58** (§6.5.5 · FINRA section 1) — Fiscal policy is set by:

- A. The Federal Reserve Board
- B. The Federal Open Market Committee
- C. Congress and the President
- D. The Treasury Department acting alone

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Fiscal policy — taxation and government spending — comes from Congress and the President. The Fed controls monetary policy.

*Remember:* Fiscal policy (taxes and spending) is set by Congress and the President.

</details>

**Q59** (§6.2 · FINRA section 2) — An investor concerned about inflation who wants a Treasury security whose principal adjusts with the CPI should buy:

- A. Treasury STRIPS
- B. Treasury bills
- C. TIPS
- D. Series EE bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Treasury Inflation-Protected Securities adjust principal with CPI; the fixed coupon rate is applied to the adjusted principal.

*Remember:* TIPS principal adjusts with CPI; the fixed coupon rate applies to the adjusted principal.

</details>

**Q60** (§6.5.4 · FINRA section 1) — A weakening US dollar would most directly benefit:

- A. US importers
- B. US consumers buying foreign goods
- C. US exporters
- D. Foreign investors holding US bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A weak dollar makes US goods cheaper abroad, helping exporters; it hurts importers and consumers of foreign goods, and reduces the home-currency value of US assets held by foreigners.

*Remember:* A weak dollar helps US exporters and hurts importers and US buyers of foreign goods.

</details>

**Q3036** (§6.1 · FINRA section 2) — An individual submits a non-competitive bid at a 26-week T-bill auction. Which statement is TRUE?

- A. She names the lowest yield she will accept, and the bid may go unfilled
- B. Her bid is filled only after all competitive bids are satisfied
- C. She accepts the yield set at the auction, and her bid is filled first
- D. She must submit the bid through a primary dealer

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Non-competitive bidders agree to accept the auction's yield and are always filled before competitive bidders.

*Remember:* Non-competitive bid: accepts auction yield; filled first.

</details>

**Q3037** (§6.1 · FINRA section 2) — A Treasury bill is quoted 4.10 bid, 4.05 ask. Which statement is correct?

- A. The ask price is lower than the bid price because 4.05 is less than 4.10
- B. The quote is in 32nds, so the ask price is $1,000.50
- C. The bill pays a coupon of 4.05% at maturity
- D. The bid yield is higher, so the bid price is lower than the ask price

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** T-bills are quoted on a discount yield basis. A higher discount means a lower price, so the 4.10 bid corresponds to a lower dollar price than the 4.05 ask, as dealers expect.

*Remember:* T-bills are quoted by discount yield: higher number = lower price.

</details>

**Q3038** (§6.1 · FINRA section 2) — A Treasury note is quoted at 101-20. What is the dollar price per $1,000 par?

- A. $1,012.00
- B. $1,016.25
- C. $1,020.00
- D. $1,006.25

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Treasury notes are quoted in 32nds. 101-20 = 101 20/32 = 101.625% of par = $1,016.25.

1. 20/32 = 0.625
2. 101.625% × $1,000 = $1,016.25

*Remember:* Treasury price = (points + 32nds ÷ 32)% × $1,000.

</details>

**Q3039** (§6.1 · FINRA section 2) — An investor holding a 30-year Treasury bond faces all of the following risks EXCEPT:

- A. Interest-rate risk
- B. Inflation risk
- C. Reinvestment risk
- D. Default risk

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Treasuries are backed by the full faith and credit of the US and are considered free of default risk. They still carry interest-rate, inflation and reinvestment risk.

*Remember:* Treasuries: no credit risk, but interest-rate, inflation and reinvestment risk.

</details>

**Q3040** (§6.1 · FINRA section 2) — New US Treasury securities are issued:

- A. In book-entry form only, in denominations as small as $100
- B. As registered certificates in minimum denominations of $1,000
- C. In bearer form, in minimum denominations of $5,000
- D. In book-entry form, in minimum denominations of $10,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Treasuries are issued only in book-entry form with a $100 minimum denomination.

*Remember:* Treasuries: book-entry only, $100 minimum.

</details>

**Q3041** (§6.2 · FINRA section 2) — A customer buys $1,000 of TIPS with a 1.5% coupon. Over the next year the CPI rises 4%. What is the annual interest based on the adjusted principal?

- A. $15.00
- B. $15.60
- C. $40.00
- D. $55.00

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** TIPS principal adjusts with CPI: $1,000 × 1.04 = $1,040. The fixed 1.5% coupon rate applies to the adjusted principal: $1,040 × 1.5% = $15.60.

1. Adjusted principal = $1,000 × 1.04 = $1,040
2. Interest = $1,040 × 1.5% = $15.60

*Remember:* TIPS interest = fixed coupon rate × CPI-adjusted principal.

</details>

**Q3042** (§6.2 · FINRA section 2) — Because of deflation over its life, a $1,000 TIPS has an inflation-adjusted principal of $980 at maturity. The investor receives:

- A. $980
- B. $1,000
- C. $1,020
- D. $980 plus a deflation credit set by the Treasury

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** At maturity a TIPS holder receives the greater of the adjusted principal or the original principal, so deflation can't push the redemption below $1,000.

*Remember:* TIPS at maturity: greater of adjusted or original principal.

</details>

**Q3043** (§6.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which tax consequence is a disadvantage of holding TIPS in a taxable account?

- A. The annual inflation increase to principal is taxed in the year it occurs, though not received until maturity
- B. Interest is taxed at the state level but exempt federally
- C. The entire return is taxed as a capital gain at maturity
- D. Interest is subject to the alternative minimum tax

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The yearly inflation adjustment is taxable as income even though the investor doesn't receive it until maturity, creating 'phantom income.' That's why TIPS are often held in tax-deferred accounts.

*Remember:* TIPS principal increases are taxed annually as phantom income.

</details>

**Q3044** (§6.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which zero-coupon security is a direct obligation of the US Treasury?

- A. TIGRs
- B. CATS
- C. STRIPS
- D. A broker-dealer Treasury receipt

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** STRIPS are backed directly by the Treasury. Treasury receipts such as CATS and TIGRs are created by broker-dealers and collateralized by Treasuries held in trust, but aren't direct Treasury obligations.

*Remember:* STRIPS = direct Treasury zeros; Treasury receipts = dealer-created, not direct obligations.

</details>

**Q3045** (§6.2 · FINRA section 2) — If interest rates rise sharply, which Treasury security will likely fall the most in price?

- A. A 2-year floating-rate note
- B. A 30-year Treasury STRIPS
- C. A 10-year Treasury note
- D. A 30-year 4.5% Treasury bond

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Price sensitivity is greatest with long maturity and low (zero) coupon. A 30-year STRIPS has both, so it moves the most.

*Remember:* Most rate-sensitive Treasury: long-term STRIPS (long maturity, zero coupon).

</details>

**Q3046** (§6.2 · FINRA section 2) — A client wants to sell her Series EE savings bonds through her brokerage account. The rep should explain that:

- A. They can be sold in the OTC market at prevailing prices
- B. They must be sold through a primary dealer
- C. They can be sold only after 30 years
- D. They are non-negotiable and can only be redeemed with the Treasury

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Savings bonds are non-negotiable: they cannot be sold or transferred in the market, only redeemed through the Treasury.

*Remember:* Savings bonds (EE, I) are non-negotiable; redeem, don't sell.

</details>

**Q3047** (§6.2 · FINRA section 2) — A Treasury security with a 2-year maturity whose interest rate resets weekly based on the 13-week T-bill auction rate is a:

- A. Treasury floating-rate note
- B. Treasury Inflation-Protected Security
- C. Series I savings bond
- D. Treasury STRIPS

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Treasury FRNs have 2-year maturities and rates that reset weekly to the 13-week bill rate, giving them minimal interest-rate risk.

*Remember:* Treasury FRN: 2-year, resets weekly to 13-week T-bill rate.

</details>

**Q3048** (§6.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which statement about mortgage-backed securities issued by Fannie Mae is accurate?

- A. They carry the explicit full faith and credit of the US government
- B. Their interest is exempt from state and local income tax
- C. They are issued by an agency within HUD
- D. Fannie Mae is a GSE, and its MBS have only implied government backing

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Fannie Mae is a publicly traded GSE (in conservatorship since 2008). Its MBS are not legally backed by the government, and their interest is taxable at all levels.

*Remember:* Fannie and Freddie MBS: implied backing only; taxable at all levels.

</details>

**Q3049** (§6.3 · FINRA section 2) — A customer in a high-tax state wants agency debt whose interest is exempt from state income tax. Which security fits?

- A. Ginnie Mae pass-through certificates
- B. Fannie Mae mortgage-backed securities
- C. Federal Home Loan Bank bonds
- D. A collateralized mortgage obligation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Federal Home Loan Bank and Farm Credit debt pays interest that's federally taxable but state-exempt. Mortgage-backed agency securities are taxable at every level.

*Remember:* FHLB and Farm Credit interest: state-exempt; agency MBS: taxable at all levels.

</details>

**Q3050** (§6.3 · FINRA section 2) — A customer bought a mortgage pass-through expecting an 8-year average life. Interest rates then rise sharply. What is the most likely result?

- A. Prepayments slow and the average life lengthens
- B. Prepayments accelerate and the average life shortens
- C. The issuer must call the certificates at par
- D. Monthly payments switch from principal-and-interest to interest only

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** When rates rise, homeowners don't refinance, so prepayments slow and the security stays outstanding longer — extension risk.

*Remember:* Rising rates → slower prepayments → extension risk.

</details>

**Q3051** (§6.3 · FINRA section 2) — In a CMO, which tranche absorbs most of the prepayment variability so that the planned amortization class (PAC) tranche can have a predictable cash flow?

- A. The Z-tranche
- B. The interest-only strip
- C. The first sequential-pay tranche
- D. The companion (support) tranche

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** PAC tranches get the most predictable cash flows because companion (support) tranches absorb excess or deficient prepayments.

*Remember:* PAC = most predictable; companion/support tranche absorbs prepayment risk.

</details>

**Q3052** (§6.3 · FINRA section 2) — A Ginnie Mae holder receives a monthly payment of $640, of which $500 is interest and $140 is return of principal. How much is taxable, and at what levels?

- A. $640, at the federal level only
- B. $500, at the federal level only
- C. $140, at the federal, state and local levels
- D. $500, at the federal, state and local levels

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Only the interest portion of a pass-through payment is income; return of principal isn't taxed. GNMA interest is taxable at federal, state and local levels.

*Remember:* GNMA: interest taxable at all levels; principal portion is a nontaxable return of capital.

</details>

**Q3053** (§6.4 · FINRA section 1) — Large banks and broker-dealers approved to bid at every Treasury auction and to trade directly with the Fed in open-market operations are called:

- A. Market makers
- B. Primary dealers
- C. Broker's brokers
- D. Designated market makers

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Primary dealers are approved by the Fed to bid in every Treasury auction and to serve as counterparties in open-market operations.

*Remember:* Primary dealers: bid at every Treasury auction; Fed's open-market counterparties.

</details>

**Q3054** (§6.4 · FINRA section 1) — A 10-year corporate bond yields 6.10% while the 10-year Treasury yields 4.30%. The 180-basis-point difference mainly compensates investors for:

- A. Reinvestment risk
- B. Inflation risk
- C. Interest-rate risk
- D. Credit risk

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** With the same maturity, the yield spread over Treasuries reflects the corporate's credit (default) risk, since Treasuries have none.

*Remember:* Spread over same-maturity Treasury = compensation for credit risk.

</details>

**Q3055** (§6.4 · FINRA section 1) — Which yield is the primary benchmark for 30-year fixed mortgage rates?

- A. The 3-month T-bill yield
- B. The federal funds rate
- C. The 10-year Treasury yield
- D. The prime rate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The 10-year Treasury yield is the benchmark for mortgage rates and corporate bonds. The 3-month bill serves as the 'risk-free rate' in financial models.

*Remember:* 10-year Treasury = benchmark for mortgage and corporate rates.

</details>

**Q3056** (§6.5.1 · FINRA section 1) — Besides the seven Fed governors, which official holds a permanent voting seat on the FOMC?

- A. The Secretary of the Treasury
- B. The president of the Federal Reserve Bank of New York
- C. The Comptroller of the Currency
- D. The chair of the House Financial Services Committee

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The FOMC's 12 voting members are the 7 governors, the NY Fed president (always) and 4 regional Fed presidents on a rotating basis.

*Remember:* FOMC = 7 governors + NY Fed president + 4 rotating regional presidents.

</details>

**Q3057** (§6.5.1 · FINRA section 1) — The Federal Reserve's dual mandate is to pursue:

- A. Maximum employment and stable prices
- B. Balanced budgets and low taxes
- C. A strong dollar and a trade surplus
- D. Low interest rates and rising stock prices

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Congress gave the Fed a dual mandate: maximum employment and stable prices (controlling inflation).

*Remember:* Fed dual mandate: maximum employment + stable prices.

</details>

**Q3058** (§6.5.1 · FINRA section 1) — A customer's $50,000 balance in a retail money market fund is counted in which money supply measure?

- A. M1 only
- B. M1 and M2
- C. M2 but not M1
- D. Neither M1 nor M2

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** M1 is currency plus checking (and, since 2020, savings) deposits. M2 adds small time deposits and retail money market funds, so a retail MMF counts in M2 but not M1.

*Remember:* M2 = M1 + small time deposits + retail money market funds.

</details>

**Q3059** (§6.5.1 · FINRA section 1) — Which of the following are likely effects of an easy (expansionary) monetary policy?
I. Lower short-term interest rates
II. A stronger US dollar
III. Increased borrowing and spending
IV. Rising bond prices

- A. I and III only
- B. I, III and IV
- C. II and IV only
- D. I, II, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Easy money lowers rates, which raises bond prices and encourages borrowing. Lower rates tend to weaken the dollar, not strengthen it.

*Remember:* Easy money: rates down, bond prices up, borrowing up, dollar tends to weaken.

</details>

**Q3060** (§6.5.2 · FINRA section 1) — Which of the following rates is set directly by the Federal Reserve?

- A. The federal funds rate
- B. The prime rate
- C. The broker call rate
- D. The discount rate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The Fed directly sets the discount rate it charges member banks at the discount window. The fed funds rate is market-determined within the FOMC's target range.

*Remember:* Discount rate = set directly by the Fed; fed funds = market rate the FOMC targets.

</details>

**Q3061** (§6.5.2 · FINRA section 1) — The interest a broker-dealer charges customers on margin debit balances is typically based on the:

- A. Discount rate
- B. Prime rate
- C. Broker call rate
- D. Federal funds rate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The broker call (call money) rate is what banks charge broker-dealers for loans financing customer margin accounts; firms add a markup when charging customers.

*Remember:* Broker call rate = bank loans to BDs financing margin accounts.

</details>

**Q3062** (§6.5.2 · FINRA section 1) — Which benchmark, published by the New York Fed from repo transactions, replaced LIBOR for most floating-rate products?

- A. SOFR
- B. The prime rate
- C. The federal funds target
- D. The 13-week T-bill rate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The Secured Overnight Financing Rate (SOFR), based on Treasury repo transactions, replaced LIBOR as the main floating-rate benchmark.

*Remember:* SOFR (NY Fed, repo-based) replaced LIBOR.

</details>

**Q3063** (§6.5.2 · FINRA section 1) — If US interest rates rise significantly, which outcome is most likely?

- A. The US dollar weakens as foreign capital leaves
- B. Utility stocks outperform the broad market
- C. The US dollar strengthens as foreign capital flows in
- D. Outstanding bond prices rise

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Higher US rates attract foreign investment, increasing demand for dollars. Rising rates also push bond prices down and hurt rate-sensitive sectors like utilities.

*Remember:* Higher US rates → foreign capital inflow → stronger dollar.

</details>

**Q3064** (§6.5.3 · FINRA section 1) — Which Federal Reserve tool is considered the most powerful but is the least frequently used?

- A. Open-market operations
- B. Changing reserve requirements
- C. Changing the discount rate
- D. Changing interest on reserve balances

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Changing reserve requirements has a sweeping effect on bank lending, so it's rarely used; the ratio has been 0% since 2020.

*Remember:* Reserve requirements: most powerful, least used (0% since 2020).

</details>

**Q3065** (§6.5.3 · FINRA section 1) — To fight rising inflation, the Federal Reserve could take all of the following actions EXCEPT:

- A. Selling Treasuries in the open market
- B. Raising the discount rate
- C. Raising interest on reserve balances
- D. Lowering reserve requirements

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Fighting inflation calls for tightening. Lowering reserve requirements frees banks to lend more, which is an easing move.

*Remember:* Tighten: sell securities, raise discount rate, raise reserves and interest on reserves.

</details>

**Q3066** (§6.5.3 · FINRA section 1) — With short-term rates already near zero, the Fed buys large amounts of long-term Treasuries and MBS to push long-term rates lower. This is called:

- A. Quantitative tightening
- B. A reverse repo
- C. Quantitative easing
- D. Raising the reserve ratio

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Quantitative easing is large-scale buying of long-term securities to lower long rates when short rates can't go much lower.

*Remember:* QE = large-scale long-term bond purchases; QT = letting holdings run off.

</details>

**Q3067** (§6.5.3 · FINRA section 1) — To drain reserves from the banking system temporarily, the Fed's open-market desk would use:

- A. Repurchase agreements
- B. Reverse repurchase agreements
- C. Outright purchases of Treasury bills
- D. Discount window loans

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** In a reverse repo the Fed sells securities with an agreement to buy them back, temporarily taking cash out of the system. A repo is a temporary purchase that adds reserves.

*Remember:* Fed repo = temporary buy (adds reserves); reverse repo = temporary sale (drains).

</details>

**Q3068** (§6.5.4 · FINRA section 1) — Cars built at a Japanese-owned assembly plant in Kentucky are counted in:

- A. US GDP
- B. US GNP only
- C. Japan's GDP
- D. Neither US GDP nor US GNP

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** GDP counts all output produced within US borders regardless of ownership. GNP counts output of US-owned entities anywhere.

*Remember:* GDP = output within the US; GNP = output of US-owned entities anywhere.

</details>

**Q3069** (§6.5.4 · FINRA section 1) — A recession is commonly defined as:

- A. Two consecutive months of rising unemployment
- B. Two consecutive quarters of declining real GDP
- C. Six consecutive quarters of declining GDP
- D. A 20% decline in the S&P 500

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The common definition of a recession is two consecutive quarters of declining GDP; the NBER officially declares recessions.

*Remember:* Recession = 2 straight quarters of declining GDP (NBER declares).

</details>

**Q3070** (§6.5.4 · FINRA section 1) — All of the following are lagging economic indicators EXCEPT:

- A. The average duration of unemployment
- B. The Consumer Price Index
- C. The prime rate
- D. Average weekly hours worked in manufacturing

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Average weekly manufacturing hours is a leading indicator; employers adjust hours before hiring or firing. The others change after the economy turns.

*Remember:* Lagging: unemployment duration, CPI, prime rate, corporate profits, consumer debt.

</details>

**Q3071** (§6.5.4 · FINRA section 1) — Which economic indicator generally moves at the same time as the overall economy?
