- A. Initial unemployment claims
- B. Corporate profits
- C. Industrial production
- D. The S&P 500 index

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Coincident indicators — GDP, industrial production, personal income, nonfarm payrolls — move with the economy.

*Remember:* Coincident: GDP, industrial production, personal income, nonfarm payrolls.

</details>

**Q3072** (§6.5.4 · FINRA section 1) — An economy with little or no growth, high unemployment and rising prices is experiencing:

- A. Deflation
- B. Stagflation
- C. An expansion
- D. A trough

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Stagflation combines stagnant growth with high inflation.

*Remember:* Stagflation = stagnant growth + high inflation.

</details>

**Q3073** (§6.5.5 · FINRA section 1) — During a recession, Congress passes and the President signs a broad tax cut along with new infrastructure spending. This is an example of:

- A. Contractionary fiscal policy
- B. Expansionary monetary policy
- C. Quantitative easing
- D. Expansionary fiscal policy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Fiscal policy is taxation and spending set by Congress and the President. Cutting taxes and raising spending is expansionary and typically widens the deficit.

*Remember:* Expansionary fiscal = cut taxes, raise spending (Congress and President).

</details>

**Q3074** (§6.5.5 · FINRA section 1) — Persistent federal budget deficits require heavy Treasury borrowing, which can push interest rates up and reduce private borrowing. This effect is called:

- A. Crowding out
- B. Quantitative tightening
- C. Disintermediation
- D. Stagflation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Crowding out occurs when government borrowing competes for funds, raising rates and squeezing out private borrowers.

*Remember:* Crowding out: heavy government borrowing raises rates, displacing private borrowing.

</details>

**Q3075** (§6.5.5 · FINRA section 1) — Compared with monetary policy, fiscal policy is generally:

- A. Faster to implement and set by the FOMC
- B. Slower to implement but able to target specific groups
- C. Faster to implement because it needs no legislation
- D. Based on monetarist economic theory

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Fiscal policy requires legislation, so it's slower than Fed action, but taxes and spending can be aimed at particular sectors or groups. It's rooted in Keynesian theory.

*Remember:* Fiscal: Congress/President, slow, targetable, Keynesian. Monetary: Fed, fast, monetarist.

</details>

**Q9020** (§6.2 · FINRA section 2) — A customer wants a direct obligation of the US Treasury that pays semiannual interest and matures in about 7 years. She should buy a:

- A. Treasury bill
- B. Treasury bond
- C. Treasury note
- D. Treasury STRIPS

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Treasury notes mature in 2 to 10 years (including a 7-year note) and pay interest semiannually.

*Remember:* T-bills ≤ 1 yr (discount); T-notes 2–10 yrs; T-bonds 20–30 yrs

</details>

**Q9021** (§6.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which CMO tranche receives no current interest or principal while earlier tranches are being paid, with its interest instead added to its balance like a zero-coupon bond?

- A. Planned amortization class (PAC) tranche
- B. Companion (support) tranche
- C. First sequential-pay tranche
- D. Z-tranche

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A Z-tranche (accrual tranche) accrues interest instead of paying it until earlier tranches are retired, so it behaves like a zero-coupon bond.

*Remember:* Z-tranche accrues like a zero; PAC has the most predictable cash flow

</details>

**Q9022** (§6.5.3 · FINRA section 1) — The Federal Reserve raises the rate it pays banks on reserve balances held at the Fed. This action is most likely intended to:

- A. Push the federal funds rate higher as part of a tighter policy
- B. Encourage banks to lend reserves to each other at lower rates
- C. Add reserves to the banking system to stimulate lending
- D. Lower the discount rate charged at the discount window

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Banks won't lend reserves overnight for less than the Fed pays them to hold reserves. Raising interest on reserve balances lifts the fed funds rate, which tightens policy.

*Remember:* Raise interest on reserves = tighten (fed funds up); lower it = ease

</details>

**Q9023** (§6.5.4 · FINRA section 1) — Which index measures prices at the wholesale level and is often watched as an early signal of consumer inflation?

- A. Consumer Price Index (CPI)
- B. Producer Price Index (PPI)
- C. Real gross domestic product
- D. The M2 money supply

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The PPI measures wholesale (producer) prices. Increases in producer costs tend to pass through to consumers, so the PPI tends to lead the CPI.

*Remember:* PPI = wholesale prices, tends to lead CPI; CPI = consumer prices, main inflation measure

</details>

**Q9024** (§6.5.2 · FINRA section 1) — Which of the following lists these interest rates in their typical order from LOWEST to HIGHEST?

- A. Federal funds rate, broker call rate, prime rate
- B. Prime rate, broker call rate, federal funds rate
- C. Broker call rate, federal funds rate, prime rate
- D. Federal funds rate, prime rate, broker call rate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Fed funds is the overnight bank-to-bank rate. Broker call is usually slightly above fed funds, and prime (about fed funds + 3%) is the highest of the three.

*Remember:* Lowest to highest: fed funds ≈ discount rate < broker call < prime

</details>

**Q9025** (§6.5.4 · FINRA section 1) — A presidential candidate argues that cutting tax rates and easing business regulation will spur production and investment, leading to stronger economic growth. This view reflects which economic theory?

- A. Keynesian
- B. Monetarist
- C. Supply-side
- D. Contractionary fiscal policy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Supply-side economics holds that lower taxes and lighter regulation encourage production, and growth follows.

*Remember:* Supply-side = cut taxes and regulation; Keynesian = government spending; Monetarist = money supply

</details>

## Chapter 7: Investment Companies

**Q61** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — A mutual fund has a NAV of $18.60 and a sales charge of 7%. Its public offering price is:

- A. $19.90
- B. $20.00
- C. $19.30
- D. $17.30

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** POP = NAV ÷ (1 − 0.07) = 18.60 ÷ 0.93 = $20.00. The sales charge is a percentage of the POP.

1. 1 − 0.07 = 0.93
2. $18.60 ÷ 0.93 = $20.00

*Remember:* POP = NAV ÷ (1 − sales charge %); the load is a percent of POP.

</details>

**Q62** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer signs a letter of intent to invest $50,000 in a fund family over the next 13 months. Which statement is TRUE?

- A. The letter may be backdated up to 90 days to include a prior purchase
- B. Appreciation in the account counts toward the $50,000
- C. The reduced sales charge applies only after $50,000 has been invested
- D. The customer is legally obligated to complete the purchases

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** An LOI can be backdated 90 days, gives the reduced charge immediately, counts only new money (not appreciation), and is not binding — unmet LOIs trigger a retroactive charge on escrowed shares.

*Remember:* LOI: 13 months, backdate up to 90 days, non-binding, new money only, reduced charge immediately.

</details>

**Q63** (§7.3.5 · FINRA section 3) — A customer wants to invest $750,000 in a mutual fund and hold it for 15 years. The MOST suitable share class is:

- A. Class A
- B. Class B
- C. Class C
- D. Class B, converting to A later

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Large amounts qualify for breakpoints (possibly a 0% load at $1 million) and long holding periods favor the low 12b-1 fee of Class A. Class B has no breakpoints; Class C's level load never converts.

*Remember:* Large purchases and long holding periods favor Class A shares (breakpoints, low 12b-1).

</details>

**Q64** (§7.3.6 · FINRA section 2) — Under Subchapter M, a regulated investment company avoids tax on income distributed to shareholders if it distributes at least:

- A. 50% of net investment income
- B. 75% of net investment income
- C. 90% of net investment income
- D. 100% of net investment income

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The 90% conduit rule: distribute at least 90% of net investment income and the fund is taxed only on what it retains.

*Remember:* Subchapter M: a fund distributing at least 90% of net investment income is taxed only on retained income.

</details>

**Q65** (§7.4 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is TRUE of a closed-end fund but NOT an open-end fund?

- A. It is priced at NAV plus a sales charge
- B. It may trade at a discount to NAV
- C. It continuously issues new shares
- D. It must redeem shares within seven days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Closed-end funds trade in the secondary market at prices set by supply and demand, so they can trade above or below NAV. They don't issue new shares continuously or redeem shares.

*Remember:* Closed-end funds trade on exchanges at market prices, so they can sell above or below NAV.

</details>

**Q66** (§7.5.2 · FINRA section 2) — A leveraged ETF that seeks 3× the daily return of an index is LEAST suitable for:

- A. A day trader
- B. A buy-and-hold retirement investor
- C. A hedge fund
- D. A sophisticated speculator with a one-day outlook

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Leveraged and inverse ETFs reset daily; over longer periods compounding causes returns to diverge from 3× the index, especially in volatile markets. They're designed for short-term trading.

*Remember:* Leveraged and inverse ETFs reset daily and suit short-term trading, not buy-and-hold.

</details>

**Q67** (§7.5.3 · FINRA section 2) — An exchange-traded note differs from an ETF in that the ETN holder:

- A. Receives dividends from the underlying stocks
- B. Bears the credit risk of the issuing bank
- C. Can only redeem through the fund
- D. Pays a front-end sales charge

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** An ETN is an unsecured debt obligation of a bank; there is no portfolio. If the bank fails, the ETN can become worthless regardless of the index.

*Remember:* An ETN is unsecured bank debt; holders bear the issuer's credit risk.

</details>

**Q68** (§7.6 · FINRA section 2) — A unit investment trust differs from a mutual fund in that a UIT:

- A. Has a board of directors
- B. Has a fixed, unmanaged portfolio and no investment adviser
- C. Issues shares that trade on an exchange
- D. Cannot hold bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** UITs hold a fixed portfolio with no active management or board; units are redeemable, and the trust terminates on a set date.

*Remember:* UITs hold a fixed, unmanaged portfolio with no board or adviser and terminate on a set date.

</details>

**Q69** (§7.7 · FINRA section 2) — A fund has a beta of 1.3. If the market rises 10%, the fund would be expected to:

- A. Rise about 13%
- B. Rise about 7.7%
- C. Rise about 10%
- D. Fall about 3%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Beta measures movement relative to the market (beta 1.0). A beta of 1.3 implies about 1.3 × 10% = 13%.

1. Expected return = 1.3 × 10%
2. = 13% rise

*Remember:* Expected move = beta × market move; beta above 1 means more volatile than the market.

</details>

**Q70** (§7.3.4 · FINRA section 3) — A mutual fund shareholder elects to reinvest all dividends and capital gains distributions. For tax purposes, the distributions are:

- A. Not taxable until shares are sold
- B. Taxable in the year received and added to cost basis
- C. Taxable only if taken in cash
- D. Taxed as long-term gains regardless of type

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Distributions are taxable whether reinvested or not; reinvested amounts increase the investor's cost basis to avoid double taxation on sale.

*Remember:* Fund distributions are taxable when paid, even if reinvested; reinvested amounts raise cost basis.

</details>

**Q71** (§7.1 · FINRA section 2) — A diversified management company must have at least 75% of its assets invested such that no more than ___ is in any one issuer and it owns no more than ___ of any issuer's voting securities.

- A. 10%; 5%
- B. 5%; 10%
- C. 25%; 10%
- D. 5%; 25%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The 75-5-10 test: 75% of assets diversified, no more than 5% in one issuer, no more than 10% of an issuer's voting shares.

*Remember:* Diversified fund test (75-5-10): 75% of assets, max 5% in one issuer, max 10% of its voting stock.

</details>

**Q72** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 3) — A registered representative recommends that a customer invest $24,000 in Fund X and $24,000 in Fund Y, from two different fund families, when each family offers a breakpoint at $25,000. This is:

- A. Appropriate diversification
- B. A prohibited breakpoint sale
- C. Acceptable if the customer signs a waiver
- D. Acceptable because each purchase is below the breakpoint

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Splitting purchases to keep each below a breakpoint (or selling just under one) to earn higher commissions is a prohibited breakpoint sale.

*Remember:* Recommending purchases just under a breakpoint, or splitting them across families, is a prohibited breakpoint sale.

</details>

**Q4001** (§7.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is NOT one of the three classifications of investment companies defined by the Investment Company Act of 1940?

- A. Real estate investment trust
- B. Face-amount certificate company
- C. Unit investment trust
- D. Management company

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The 1940 Act recognizes face-amount certificate companies, UITs and management companies. REITs are real estate companies registered under the 1933 Act, not 1940 Act investment companies.

*Remember:* 1940 Act classes: face-amount certificate companies, UITs, management companies (open-end and closed-end).

</details>

**Q4002** (§7.1 · FINRA section 2) — Before an investment company may make a public offering of its shares, it must have a minimum net worth of:

- A. $50,000
- B. $250,000
- C. $1,000,000
- D. $100,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The 1940 Act requires an investment company to have at least $100,000 of net worth before it offers shares to the public.

*Remember:* Investment company needs $100,000 net worth (seed capital) before a public offering.

</details>

**Q4003** (§7.1 · FINRA section 2) — At least what percentage of a mutual fund's board of directors must be independent (non-interested) directors?

- A. 40%
- B. 25%
- C. 50%
- D. 75%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Under the 1940 Act, at least 40% of a fund's directors must be independent of the adviser and underwriter.

*Remember:* Fund board: at least 40% independent (non-interested) directors.

</details>

**Q4004** (§7.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following actions by a mutual fund requires approval by a majority vote of the fund's shareholders?

- A. Replacing the custodian bank
- B. Declaring an annual capital gains distribution
- C. Hiring a new transfer agent
- D. Changing the investment objective from growth to income

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A fund cannot change its investment objective without a majority shareholder vote. Hiring service providers and declaring distributions are board decisions.

*Remember:* Changing a fund's investment objective requires a majority shareholder vote.

</details>

**Q4005** (§7.1 · FINRA section 2) — A mutual fund shareholder never received her quarterly dividend check and her account statement shows the wrong address. Which party is responsible for these records and payments?

- A. Transfer agent
- B. Custodian
- C. Investment adviser
- D. Underwriter

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The transfer agent issues and redeems shares, maintains shareholder records, and sends statements and distributions.

*Remember:* Transfer agent: shareholder records, issues/redeems shares, sends distributions and statements.

</details>

**Q4006** (§7.1 · FINRA section 2) — For most mutual funds, the largest single annual operating expense is the:

- A. 12b-1 distribution fee
- B. Custodian's safekeeping fee
- C. Transfer agent's record-keeping fee
- D. Management fee paid to the investment adviser

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The investment adviser's management fee is typically the fund's largest expense; its contract is approved by the board and shareholders.

*Remember:* Management (advisory) fee is usually a fund's largest expense.

</details>

**Q4007** (§7.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which statement about money market mutual funds is TRUE?

- A. They seek a stable $1.00 NAV, but it is not guaranteed
- B. They are FDIC-insured up to $250,000 per depositor
- C. They typically carry a front-end sales charge of up to 5.75%
- D. They invest mainly in long-term corporate bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Money market funds hold short-term debt and aim for a stable $1.00 NAV, but the price is not guaranteed or insured.

*Remember:* Money market fund: short-term debt, stable $1.00 NAV goal, not guaranteed, no sales charge.

</details>

**Q4008** (§7.2 · FINRA section 2) — A 30-year-old customer wants a single fund for her 401(k) that will automatically shift toward bonds and cash as she nears her planned retirement in 2061. Which fund BEST fits?

- A. Balanced fund
- B. Asset allocation fund
- C. Growth and income fund
- D. Target-date fund

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A target-date (lifecycle) fund follows a glide path, becoming more conservative as the target date approaches.

*Remember:* Target-date fund: allocation grows more conservative as the target year approaches (glide path).

</details>

**Q4009** (§7.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which type of fund is generally LEAST appropriate for a customer's traditional IRA?

- A. S&P 500 index fund
- B. Growth fund
- C. Corporate bond fund
- D. Municipal bond fund

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Muni fund income is already federally tax-free; inside a tax-deferred IRA that benefit is wasted and withdrawals become taxable.

*Remember:* Never put tax-exempt municipal investments in an IRA; the tax advantage is wasted.

</details>

**Q4010** (§7.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which list ranks fund types from LOWEST to HIGHEST risk?

- A. Government bond, money market, balanced, aggressive growth
- B. Money market, government bond, balanced, aggressive growth
- C. Money market, balanced, government bond, aggressive growth
- D. Balanced, money market, government bond, aggressive growth

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** From least to most risky: money market, government bond, investment-grade bond, balanced, growth and income, growth, aggressive growth.

*Remember:* Risk ladder: money market < government bond < balanced < growth < aggressive growth/sector.

</details>

**Q4011** (§7.3.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer redeems mutual fund shares on Monday. The fund must send the redemption proceeds within:

- A. 1 business day
- B. 2 business days
- C. 7 calendar days
- D. 30 calendar days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The Investment Company Act requires a fund to pay redemption proceeds within 7 calendar days of receiving a proper request.

*Remember:* Mutual fund redemption proceeds must be paid within 7 calendar days.

</details>

**Q4012** (§7.3.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer's order to buy a mutual fund arrives at 2:30 p.m. ET. Yesterday's closing NAV was $15.20. The order will be filled at:

- A. The NAV computed at today's 4:00 p.m. ET close, plus any sales charge
- B. Yesterday's NAV of $15.20, plus any sales charge
- C. The NAV in effect at 2:30 p.m. when the order arrived
- D. Tomorrow's opening market price

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Forward pricing fills orders at the next NAV calculated after the order is received, normally at the 4:00 p.m. ET close.

*Remember:* Forward pricing: orders fill at the next NAV computed after receipt (4:00 p.m. ET).

</details>

**Q4013** (§7.3.1 · FINRA section 3) — A broker-dealer has a selling agreement with a mutual fund's underwriter. The broker-dealer is prohibited from:

- A. Forwarding customer purchase orders to the fund
- B. Delivering the prospectus to customers
- C. Receiving part of the sales charge as a dealer concession
- D. Keeping an inventory of fund shares to resell to customers

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A broker-dealer may not maintain an inventory of fund shares or hold itself out as buying shares at a discount; it places orders with the fund at the next price.

*Remember:* Broker-dealers may not inventory mutual fund shares; orders go to the fund at the next price.

</details>

**Q4014** (§7.3.2 · FINRA section 2) — Mutual fund shareholders must receive financial reports at least:

- A. Semiannually, with the annual report audited
- B. Quarterly, with each report audited
- C. Annually, with no audit required
- D. Monthly, along with account statements

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Funds send shareholders semiannual reports with financial statements and holdings; the annual report must be audited.

*Remember:* Shareholder reports: semiannual; the annual report is audited.

</details>

**Q4015** (§7.3.2 · FINRA section 2) — A customer wants more detailed information than the prospectus provides about a fund's operations and the backgrounds of its directors. The rep should tell the customer to request the:

- A. Summary prospectus
- B. Offering memorandum
- C. Statement of Additional Information (SAI)
- D. Adviser's Form U4

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The SAI supplements the prospectus with more detail on fund operations, policies and management, and is available on request.

*Remember:* The SAI expands on the prospectus and is provided free on request.

</details>

**Q4016** (§7.3.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is NOT required to appear in a mutual fund's prospectus?

- A. The fund's investment objective
- B. A fee table showing sales charges and annual expenses
- C. A projection of next year's expected return
- D. 1-, 5- and 10-year performance figures

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A prospectus shows the objective, risks, fee table and historical 1-, 5- and 10-year performance. Projecting future returns is not allowed.

*Remember:* Prospectus: objective, risks, fee table, 1/5/10-year past performance; never projected returns.

</details>

**Q4017** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — A mutual fund quotes an NAV of $23.00 and a public offering price of $25.00. What is the sales charge percentage?

- A. 7.5%
- B. 8.0%
- C. 8.7%
- D. 9.2%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The sales charge is always stated as a percentage of the POP: ($25 - $23) / $25 = 8.0%.

1. Load = $25.00 - $23.00 = $2.00
2. $2.00 / $25.00 = 0.08
3. Sales charge = 8.0%

*Remember:* Sales charge % = (POP - NAV) / POP.

</details>

**Q4018** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — A mutual fund has total assets of $520 million, total liabilities of $20 million, and 25 million shares outstanding. What is the NAV per share?

- A. $21.60
- B. $20.80
- C. $20.00
- D. $19.20

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** NAV per share = (assets - liabilities) / shares = ($520M - $20M) / 25M = $20.00.

1. Net assets = $520M - $20M = $500M
2. NAV = $500M / 25M shares = $20.00

*Remember:* NAV per share = (Total assets - Total liabilities) / Shares outstanding.

</details>

**Q4019** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which event would NOT change a mutual fund's NAV per share?

- A. An investor buys 100,000 new shares of the fund
- B. The fund's portfolio securities fall in value
- C. The fund pays a capital gains distribution
- D. The fund receives interest on its bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** When investors buy or redeem, cash and shares change proportionally, so NAV per share is unchanged.

*Remember:* Purchases and redemptions do not change NAV per share; portfolio values and distributions do.

</details>

**Q4020** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — A customer signed a letter of intent for $50,000 in a fund family. Over 13 months she invested $42,000 of new money, which has grown to $51,000. What happens?

- A. She has met the LOI because her account value exceeds $50,000
- B. She has not met the LOI, so the higher sales charge is collected from escrowed shares
- C. The LOI is automatically extended another 13 months
- D. She keeps the reduced charge because the LOI is non-binding

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Only new deposits count toward an LOI. Falling short means the fund retroactively collects the higher charge, using shares held in escrow.

*Remember:* LOI: 13 months, new money only; shortfall means higher charge taken from escrowed shares.

</details>

**Q4021** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — A customer originally invested $38,000 in a fund family; the holdings are now worth $47,000. The family's next breakpoint is $50,000. If she invests another $5,000, which is TRUE?

- A. No reduced charge applies because her cost basis is under $50,000
- B. She receives a refund of excess sales charges on her earlier purchases
- C. She must sign a letter of intent to receive any reduction
- D. The $5,000 purchase gets the $50,000 breakpoint rate under rights of accumulation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Rights of accumulation use the current value of existing holdings plus the new purchase: $47,000 + $5,000 = $52,000 reaches the breakpoint.

*Remember:* ROA: current value + new purchase crosses breakpoint; reduced charge on new purchase, not retroactive.

</details>

**Q4022** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following purchasers is NOT eligible to receive breakpoint discounts on a combined purchase?

- A. A husband and wife buying in a joint account
- B. A parent buying for a minor child's custodial account
- C. An investment club pooling its members' money
- D. A trust buying for its beneficiaries

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Breakpoints are available to individuals, spouses, parents with minor children, and most trusts and corporations, but not groups formed to pool purchases.

*Remember:* No breakpoints for investment clubs or groups formed to pool purchases.

</details>

**Q4023** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — Under FINRA rules, a mutual fund may charge the maximum 8.5% sales charge only if it offers which of the following?
I. Breakpoints
II. Rights of accumulation
III. Reinvestment of dividends at NAV
IV. An exchange privilege

- A. I and II only
- B. I, II and III only
- C. II and III only
- D. I, II, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The 8.5% maximum is allowed only when the fund offers breakpoints, rights of accumulation, and automatic reinvestment of dividends at NAV.

*Remember:* 8.5% max load requires breakpoints, ROA and dividend reinvestment at NAV.

</details>

**Q4024** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — A fund sells its shares at NAV with no front-end or back-end sales charge, but charges an annual 12b-1 fee of 0.35%. May it describe itself as a no-load fund?

- A. Yes, because it charges no front-end sales charge
- B. No, because its 12b-1 fee exceeds 0.25%
- C. Yes, because its 12b-1 fee is below the 1.00% maximum
- D. No, because no-load funds may not charge any 12b-1 fee

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A fund may call itself no-load only if it has no sales charges and a 12b-1 fee of no more than 0.25%.

*Remember:* No-load: no sales charge and 12b-1 fee of 0.25% or less.

</details>

**Q4025** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — A customer invests $600 a month in a mutual fund for three months, buying at $20, $30 and $15 per share. What is the customer's average cost per share?

- A. $18.00
- B. $20.00
- C. $21.00
- D. $21.67

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The customer bought 30 + 20 + 40 = 90 shares for $1,800, so the average cost is $20.00, below the $21.67 average price, as dollar cost averaging produces.

1. Shares: 600/20 = 30; 600/30 = 20; 600/15 = 40
2. Total: $1,800 for 90 shares
3. Average cost = $1,800 / 90 = $20.00

*Remember:* Average cost = total invested / total shares bought; DCA cost is below average price.

</details>

**Q4026** (§7.3.4 · FINRA section 2) — A fund's NAV rose from $40.00 to $42.00 over the year, and it paid $1.20 per share in distributions. What was the total return?

- A. 3.0%
- B. 5.0%
- C. 8.0%
- D. 10.5%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Total return = (ending NAV - beginning NAV + distributions) / beginning NAV = ($2.00 + $1.20) / $40 = 8.0%.

1. NAV change = $42.00 - $40.00 = $2.00
2. Add distributions: $2.00 + $1.20 = $3.20
3. $3.20 / $40.00 = 8.0%

*Remember:* Total return = (Ending NAV - Beginning NAV + Distributions) / Beginning NAV.

</details>

**Q4027** (§7.3.4 · FINRA section 2) — A customer bought a mutual fund three months ago. The fund now pays a distribution of long-term capital gains it realized on portfolio sales. How is it taxed to the customer?

- A. As a long-term capital gain
- B. As a short-term capital gain because she held the shares under a year
- C. As ordinary income because it is paid by a fund
- D. It is tax-free if reinvested

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Long-term capital gains distributions are taxed as long-term gains regardless of how long the shareholder has owned the fund.

*Remember:* LT capital gains distributions are LT to the shareholder regardless of holding period.

</details>

**Q4028** (§7.3.4 · FINRA section 3) — In early December, a rep urges a customer to buy a fund right away, before it pays a large capital gains distribution, saying the customer will "get the payout." This is improper because:

- A. Mutual funds may not pay distributions in December
- B. The customer will have to pay a contingent deferred sales charge
- C. Distributions are paid only to shareholders of record for over one year
- D. NAV drops by the distribution, so the customer just gets a tax bill with no economic gain

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Buying right before a distribution ("buying the dividend") gives no gain since NAV falls by the payout, but creates an immediate tax liability.

*Remember:* Buying the dividend: NAV drops by the payout; investor gets only a tax bill.

</details>

**Q4029** (§7.3.4 · FINRA section 2) — Which statement about 12b-1 fees is TRUE?

- A. They are a one-time charge deducted from each purchase
- B. They are paid by the shareholder only when shares are redeemed
- C. They are deducted annually from fund assets to pay for distribution and marketing
- D. They may be adopted by the investment adviser without board approval

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** 12b-1 fees are annual charges against fund assets for distribution and service, capped at 1.00% in total and reviewed annually by the board.

*Remember:* 12b-1: annual fee from fund assets for distribution; max 0.75% + 0.25% service = 1.00%.

</details>

**Q4030** (§7.3.4 · FINRA section 2) — A fund has average net assets of $800 million and total annual operating expenses of $6 million. What is its expense ratio?

- A. 0.075%
- B. 0.60%
- C. 0.75%
- D. 1.33%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Expense ratio = annual operating expenses / average net assets = $6M / $800M = 0.75%.

1. $6,000,000 / $800,000,000 = 0.0075
2. Expense ratio = 0.75%

*Remember:* Expense ratio = Annual operating expenses / Average net assets.

</details>

**Q4031** (§7.3.5 · FINRA section 2) — A customer is investing $20,000 that she expects to withdraw for a home down payment in about two years. Which mutual fund share class is generally MOST appropriate?

- A. Class A
- B. Class B
- C. Class A with a letter of intent
- D. Class C

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Class C shares have little or no front load and a CDSC only in the first year, making them generally best for short holding periods.

*Remember:* Class C: level load, 1% CDSC first year, best for short-term (1-3 years).

</details>

**Q4032** (§7.3.5 · FINRA section 2) — What typically happens to Class B shares once the contingent deferred sales charge schedule runs out?

- A. They convert to Class A shares with a lower 12b-1 fee
- B. They convert to Class C shares with a level load
- C. They must be redeemed at NAV
- D. Their 12b-1 fee rises to the 1.00% maximum

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** After the CDSC declines to zero, usually in 6 to 8 years, Class B shares convert to Class A, which carries a lower 12b-1 fee.

*Remember:* Class B: declining CDSC, then converts to Class A (lower 12b-1).

</details>

**Q4033** (§7.3.5 · FINRA section 2) — A customer bought Class B shares for $10,000. Two years later she redeems them when they are worth $8,000. The CDSC for that year is 4%. What is the CDSC?

- A. $0
- B. $320
- C. $360
- D. $400

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The CDSC is assessed on the lesser of the original purchase price or the current value: 4% x $8,000 = $320.

1. Lesser of $10,000 and $8,000 = $8,000
2. $8,000 x 4% = $320

*Remember:* CDSC = rate x lesser of original cost or current value.

</details>

**Q4034** (§7.3.5 · FINRA section 2) — A rep recommends Class C shares to a customer who plans to hold the fund for 20 years. The main concern with this recommendation is that Class C shares:

- A. Cannot be held for more than 10 years
- B. Carry a higher 12b-1 fee that never ends, making them costly long-term
- C. Charge a front-end load of 8.5%
- D. Lose their dividend reinvestment privilege after 5 years

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Class C shares carry an ongoing 1.00% 12b-1 fee and never convert to A, so over long periods they are usually the most expensive class.

*Remember:* Class C never converts; ongoing 1% 12b-1 makes it costly for long holding periods.

</details>

**Q4035** (§7.3.6 · FINRA section 2) — A fund distributes only 85% of its net investment income to shareholders this year. Under Subchapter M, the fund will:

- A. Be taxed only on the 15% it kept
- B. Pay no tax, because it still distributed most of its income
- C. Be taxed on all of its income like a regular corporation
- D. Be taxed on the 85% it distributed

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A fund must distribute at least 90% of net investment income to be a regulated investment company. Failing the test makes all its income taxable to the fund.

*Remember:* Subchapter M: distribute at least 90% of NII or the fund is taxed on all income.

</details>

**Q4036** (§7.3.6 · FINRA section 2) — A regulated investment company distributes 95% of its net investment income. On what portion does the fund itself pay income tax?

- A. The 5% it retained
- B. None of its income
- C. The full 100%
- D. The 95% it distributed

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A RIC acts as a conduit: it is untaxed on distributed income and pays tax only on what it retains.

*Remember:* A qualifying RIC is taxed only on undistributed income; shareholders pay on distributions.

</details>

**Q4037** (§7.4 · FINRA section 2) — A closed-end fund has an NAV of $15.00 per share, and its shares trade on the NYSE at $13.80. The fund is trading at:

- A. An 8.0% discount to NAV
- B. An 8.0% premium to NAV
- C. An 8.7% discount to NAV
- D. A 1.2% discount to NAV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Market price is below NAV, so it trades at a discount: ($15.00 - $13.80) / $15.00 = 8.0%.

1. $15.00 - $13.80 = $1.20
2. $1.20 / $15.00 = 8.0% discount

*Remember:* Premium/discount = (Market price - NAV) / NAV; price below NAV is a discount.

</details>

**Q4038** (§7.4 · FINRA section 2) — A customer buys shares of a closed-end fund on the NYSE a year after the fund's IPO. Which is TRUE?

- A. She pays the next computed NAV plus a sales charge
- B. She pays a brokerage commission and buys from another investor
- C. She may buy fractional shares
- D. She may later redeem the shares with the fund at NAV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** After the IPO, closed-end shares trade in the secondary market between investors at supply-and-demand prices, with commissions instead of sales charges.

*Remember:* Closed-end fund: one IPO, then exchange trading with commissions; not redeemable.

</details>

**Q4039** (§7.5.1 · FINRA section 2) — What mechanism keeps the market price of an ETF close to its NAV?

- A. The fund redeeming any investor's shares at NAV each day
- B. The exchange halting trading whenever price strays from NAV
- C. Forward pricing of all orders at the 4:00 p.m. close
- D. Authorized participants creating and redeeming large blocks of shares in kind

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Authorized participants exchange baskets of underlying securities for creation units, and vice versa, arbitraging away premiums and discounts.

*Remember:* ETF creation/redemption by authorized participants keeps price near NAV.

</details>

**Q4040** (§7.5.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following can an investor do with an ETF but NOT with a traditional mutual fund?

- A. Reinvest distributions
- B. Own a diversified portfolio
- C. Place a stop order during the trading day
- D. Receive dividend income

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** ETFs trade intraday on exchanges, so investors can use limit and stop orders, margin and short sales; mutual funds trade only with the fund at NAV.

*Remember:* ETFs: intraday trading, limit/stop orders, margin and short sales; mutual funds: none of these.

</details>

**Q4041** (§7.5.2 · FINRA section 2) — A customer expects the S&P 500 to fall over the next few days and wants to profit without opening a margin account or selling short. Which product fits this goal?

- A. A 2x leveraged S&P 500 ETF
- B. An inverse S&P 500 ETF
- C. An S&P 500 index mutual fund
- D. A low-volatility factor ETF

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Inverse ETFs seek the opposite of an index's daily return, letting investors bet against the market without shorting. They reset daily, so they suit short holding periods.

*Remember:* Inverse ETF: opposite of index's daily return; daily reset, short-term tool.

</details>

**Q4042** (§7.5.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which statement about exchange-traded notes (ETNs) is TRUE?

- A. They generally pay no periodic interest; return comes at sale or maturity
- B. They hold the index's securities in a trust for investors
- C. They are registered investment companies under the 1940 Act
- D. They are FDIC-insured up to $250,000 because a bank issues them

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** An ETN is unsecured bank debt that pays an index's return, minus fees, at maturity; it makes no interest payments, and gains are usually capital gains.

*Remember:* ETN: unsecured bank note, no portfolio, no periodic interest; return at sale or maturity.

</details>

**Q4043** (§7.6 · FINRA section 2) — A unit investment trust holds a fixed portfolio of municipal bonds. When the last of the bonds matures, the UIT will:

- A. Have its adviser reinvest the proceeds in new bonds
- B. Hold a board vote on whether to extend the trust
- C. Convert into an open-end mutual fund
- D. Terminate and distribute the proceeds to unitholders

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A UIT is a fixed, unmanaged portfolio created under a trust indenture; it terminates on a set date, such as when its bonds mature, and pays out.

*Remember:* UIT: fixed, unmanaged portfolio; no board or adviser; terminates and distributes proceeds.

</details>

**Q4044** (§7.7 · FINRA section 2) — The risk-free rate is 2%, the market return is 10%, and a fund with a beta of 0.8 returned 9.5%. Using CAPM, what is the fund's alpha?

- A. -0.5%
- B. +1.1%
- C. +1.5%
- D. +7.5%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** CAPM expected return = 2% + 0.8 x (10% - 2%) = 8.4%. Alpha = 9.5% - 8.4% = +1.1%.

1. Market premium = 10% - 2% = 8%
2. Expected = 2% + 0.8 x 8% = 8.4%
3. Alpha = 9.5% - 8.4% = +1.1%

*Remember:* CAPM: Expected = Rf + Beta x (Rm - Rf); Alpha = Actual - Expected.

</details>

**Q4045** (§7.7 · FINRA section 2) — The risk-free rate is 2%. Fund A returned 10% with a standard deviation of 8%; Fund B returned 12% with a standard deviation of 15%. Which statement is TRUE?

- A. Fund B has the higher Sharpe ratio because its return was higher
- B. Both funds have the same Sharpe ratio
- C. Fund A has the higher Sharpe ratio, so it delivered more return per unit of risk
- D. Sharpe ratios can't be compared without each fund's beta

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Sharpe = (return - risk-free) / standard deviation. Fund A: 8/8 = 1.0; Fund B: 10/15 = 0.67. Fund A delivered better risk-adjusted return.

1. A: (10% - 2%) / 8% = 1.0
2. B: (12% - 2%) / 15% = 0.67

*Remember:* Sharpe ratio = (Return - Risk-free rate) / Standard deviation; higher is better.

</details>

**Q9026** (§7.1 · FINRA section 2) — A management company has 20% of its assets in one issuer's stock, a position equal to 4% of that issuer's voting shares. Every other holding is under 5% of the fund's assets and under 10% of each issuer's voting stock. Which statement is TRUE?

- A. It must be classified as non-diversified because one position exceeds 5% of assets
- B. It can be classified as diversified because the 20% position fits within the unrestricted 25%
- C. It must be classified as non-diversified because any position over 10% of assets is prohibited
- D. It can be classified as diversified only if shareholders vote to approve the 20% position

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The 75-5-10 test applies only to 75% of assets; the remaining 25% is unrestricted. Since the other 80% meets the 5% and 10% limits, the fund still qualifies as diversified.

*Remember:* Diversified (75-5-10): on 75% of assets, ≤5% per issuer and ≤10% of any issuer's voting stock.

</details>

**Q9027** (§7.2 · FINRA section 2) — A customer wants a single fund that invests in both US and foreign companies, leaving the country mix to the portfolio manager. Which type of fund BEST fits?

- A. International fund
- B. Sector fund
- C. Emerging markets fund
- D. Global fund

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A global fund holds both US and non-US companies. An international fund invests only outside the US.

*Remember:* Global = US + foreign; international = non-US only.

</details>

**Q9028** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — A customer invests $10,000 in a mutual fund with an NAV of $14.10 and a 6% front-end sales charge. How many shares does she receive?

- A. 626.667 shares
- B. 666 shares
- C. 666.667 shares
- D. 709.220 shares

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** POP = $14.10 ÷ 0.94 = $15.00, and $10,000 ÷ $15.00 = 666.667 shares. Mutual funds issue fractional shares.

1. POP = 14.10 ÷ (1 − 0.06) = $15.00
2. Shares = $10,000 ÷ $15.00 = 666.667

*Remember:* POP = NAV ÷ (1 − sales charge %); shares = dollars invested ÷ POP.

</details>

**Q9029** (§7.3.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer's account in a fund family is worth $32,000, and her husband's separate account in the same family is worth $14,000. She plans to invest another $6,000. The family's next breakpoint is $50,000. Which is TRUE?

- A. Her $6,000 purchase qualifies for the $50,000 breakpoint by combining both spouses' holdings
- B. She does not qualify, because only holdings in her own name count toward breakpoints
- C. She qualifies only if she signed a letter of intent before her earlier purchases
- D. She qualifies, and the lower charge is refunded on all of her earlier purchases

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Married couples may combine holdings, and rights of accumulation use the current value of family holdings plus the new purchase: $32,000 + $14,000 + $6,000 = $52,000. The reduced charge applies to the new purchase only.

*Remember:* ROA: current value of family holdings (spouse included) + new purchase crosses breakpoint → lower charge on new purchase.

</details>

**Q9030** (§7.4 · FINRA section 1) — Which statement BEST describes how a closed-end fund raises its capital?

- A. It continuously issues new shares at NAV plus any sales charge
- B. It sells shares privately to accredited investors under Regulation D
- C. It issues units under a trust indenture and redeems them at NAV
- D. It sells a fixed number of shares once in an IPO, after which they trade between investors

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A closed-end fund raises money once through a prospectus-based IPO. After that, shares trade on exchanges at market prices, and the fund does not redeem them.

*Remember:* Closed-end fund: one-time IPO, fixed shares, then secondary-market trading at a premium or discount.

</details>

**Q9031** (§7.5.2 · FINRA section 2) — An index rises 10% on Monday and falls 10% on Tuesday. Ignoring fees, what is the approximate two-day return of an ETF that seeks 2× the index's daily return?

- A. −2%
- B. −4%
- C. 0%
- D. −1%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The ETF gains 20% Monday and loses 20% Tuesday: 1.20 × 0.80 = 0.96, a 4% loss. Daily leverage resets make multi-day results diverge from 2× the index's −1% return.

1. Monday: +20% → 1.20
2. Tuesday: −20% → 1.20 × 0.80 = 0.96
3. Two-day return = −4% (index: 1.10 × 0.90 = −1%)

*Remember:* Leveraged ETFs reset daily; compound each day's leveraged return: (1 + 2r₁)(1 + 2r₂) − 1.

</details>

**Q9032** (§7.7 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following statements about portfolio risk measures is NOT correct?

- A. Standard deviation measures a fund's total volatility
- B. An R-squared of 100 means the benchmark explains all of a fund's movements
- C. Beta measures the risk that can be eliminated through diversification
- D. A higher Sharpe ratio indicates more return per unit of risk

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Beta measures systematic (market) risk, which diversification cannot eliminate. The other three statements are accurate.

*Remember:* Beta = systematic, non-diversifiable risk; standard deviation = total risk.

</details>

## Chapter 8: Alternative Pooled Investments

**Q73** (§8.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is TRUE of a real estate investment trust?

- A. It passes through both income and losses to shareholders
- B. It must distribute at least 90% of its taxable income to avoid corporate tax
- C. It is regulated under the Investment Company Act of 1940
- D. Its dividends are always taxed as qualified dividends

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** REITs distribute 90%+ of income and hold 75%+ in real estate to avoid corporate tax. They pass through income but NOT losses, are not 1940 Act companies, and dividends are generally ordinary income.

*Remember:* REITs avoid corporate tax by distributing at least 90% of taxable income; losses don't pass through.

</details>

**Q74** (§8.2 · FINRA section 2) — Hedge funds are typically offered as:

- A. Registered public offerings with a prospectus
- B. Private placements under Regulation D to accredited investors
- C. Exchange-listed closed-end funds
- D. Unit investment trusts

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Hedge funds are privately offered limited partnerships sold under Reg D to accredited investors, exempt from the 1940 Act, with lock-ups and limited disclosure.

*Remember:* Hedge funds are private placements under Reg D sold to accredited investors.

</details>

**Q75** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — In a limited partnership, which party has unlimited personal liability for the partnership's debts?

- A. The limited partners
- B. The general partner
- C. The subscription agent
- D. All partners equally

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The general partner manages the business and has unlimited liability; limited partners are liable only for their investment as long as they stay passive.

*Remember:* The general partner has unlimited liability; limited partners risk only their investment.

</details>

**Q76** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — The most significant risk of investing in a direct participation program is generally:

- A. Interest-rate risk
- B. Lack of liquidity
- C. Currency risk
- D. Reinvestment risk

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** DPP interests have no real secondary market; transfers usually need GP approval and holding periods are long. Illiquidity is the primary risk.

*Remember:* Illiquidity is the main risk of DPPs; no real secondary market and GP approval for transfers.

</details>

**Q77** (§8.3 · FINRA section 3) — Losses from a limited partnership may be used by an investor to offset:

- A. Wages and salary
- B. Interest and dividends
- C. Income from other passive activities
- D. Capital gains on stock

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** DPP losses are passive losses, which may offset only passive income (other DPPs, rental activities), never earned or portfolio income.

*Remember:* Passive losses from DPPs can offset only passive income.

</details>

**Q78** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which oil and gas program carries the HIGHEST risk?

- A. Income program
- B. Developmental program
- C. Exploratory (wildcat) program
- D. Combination program

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Exploratory programs drill in unproven areas — the highest chance of dry holes but the largest potential reward. Income programs buy producing wells (lowest risk).

*Remember:* Exploratory (wildcat) oil and gas programs carry the highest risk; income programs the lowest.

</details>

**Q79** (§8.2 · FINRA section 2) — The 'high-water mark' in a hedge fund refers to:

- A. The maximum leverage the fund may use
- B. The level above which performance fees are charged only on new gains
- C. The minimum investment required
- D. The lock-up period

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** With a high-water mark, the manager earns the incentive fee only when the fund's value exceeds its previous peak, preventing fees on recovered losses.

*Remember:* High-water mark: performance fees are charged only on gains above the fund's previous peak.

</details>

**Q80** (§8.1 · FINRA section 2) — A non-traded REIT compared to an exchange-listed REIT generally has:

- A. Greater liquidity and lower fees
- B. Less liquidity and higher upfront fees
- C. The same liquidity but better tax treatment
- D. No SEC registration

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Non-traded REITs are SEC-registered but don't trade on exchanges; redemption programs are limited, and upfront commissions and fees are high.

*Remember:* Non-traded REITs are SEC-registered but illiquid, with high upfront fees.

</details>

**Q4046** (§8.1 · FINRA section 2) — A REIT whose income comes mainly from interest on the real estate loans it makes and the mortgage securities it buys is a(n):

- A. Equity REIT
- B. Hybrid REIT
- C. Private REIT
- D. Mortgage REIT

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Mortgage REITs lend to property owners or buy mortgages and MBS, earning interest; they are especially sensitive to interest rates.

*Remember:* Equity REIT = rents; mortgage REIT = interest; hybrid = both.

</details>

**Q4047** (§8.1 · FINRA section 2) — To avoid corporate income tax, a REIT must distribute at least 90% of its taxable income and hold at least what percentage of its assets in real estate?

- A. 75%
- B. 50%
- C. 90%
- D. 25%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A REIT must have at least 75% of its assets in real estate and earn at least 75% of gross income from real estate, plus distribute 90% of taxable income.

*Remember:* REIT: distribute 90% of taxable income; 75% of assets and income from real estate.

</details>

**Q4048** (§8.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer in a high tax bracket expects her REIT dividends to be taxed at the 15% qualified dividend rate. The rep should explain that REIT dividends are generally taxed as:

- A. Qualified dividends
- B. Ordinary income
- C. Long-term capital gains
- D. Tax-exempt income

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Because the REIT itself pays no corporate tax, its dividends are generally taxed as ordinary income, though investors may get the 20% QBI deduction.

*Remember:* REIT dividends are generally taxed as ordinary income, not qualified dividends.

</details>

**Q4049** (§8.1 · FINRA section 2) — A REIT reports a large net loss after writing down several office properties. How does this affect its shareholders' tax returns?

- A. They deduct their share as a passive loss
- B. They deduct their share as an ordinary loss
- C. They cannot deduct the loss; REITs don't pass losses through
- D. They deduct their share as a capital loss

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** REITs pass through income but not losses. This is a key difference from DPPs and limited partnerships.

*Remember:* REITs pass through income, never losses; DPPs pass through both.

</details>

**Q4050** (§8.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which statement about how REITs are regulated is TRUE?

- A. They are not investment companies under the 1940 Act
- B. They are registered as closed-end funds under the 1940 Act
- C. They are exempt from registration under the Securities Act of 1933
- D. They may be sold only to accredited investors

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** REITs are not 1940 Act investment companies; public REITs register under the 1933 Act and their shares trade under the 1934 Act.

*Remember:* REITs: registered under '33 Act, trade under '34 Act; not 1940 Act investment companies.

</details>

**Q4051** (§8.1 · FINRA section 2) — A REIT that is not registered with the SEC and is sold only to accredited investors is a:

- A. Non-traded public REIT
- B. Private REIT
- C. Exchange-listed REIT
- D. Hybrid REIT

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Private REITs are unregistered and limited to accredited investors; non-traded REITs are registered but don't trade; listed REITs trade on exchanges.

*Remember:* REIT forms: listed (registered, trades), non-traded (registered, illiquid), private (unregistered, accredited only).

</details>

**Q4052** (§8.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer wants real estate exposure, regular income, and the ability to sell on any trading day. Which investment is MOST suitable?

- A. A non-traded REIT
- B. A real estate limited partnership
- C. A raw land DPP
- D. An exchange-listed equity REIT

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Listed REITs trade on exchanges like stocks, offering liquidity along with rental income from the properties.

*Remember:* Listed REITs offer real estate income plus daily liquidity; non-traded REITs and DPPs are illiquid.

</details>

**Q4053** (§8.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which development would MOST likely cause REIT share prices, especially mortgage REITs, to fall?

- A. Rising rents
- B. Falling interest rates
- C. Rising interest rates
- D. Rising property values

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** REITs carry interest-rate risk: rising rates make their dividends less attractive and raise borrowing costs; mortgage REITs are hit hardest.

*Remember:* Rising interest rates hurt REIT prices, mortgage REITs most.

</details>

**Q4054** (§8.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following are TRUE of an equity REIT?
I. It owns and operates income-producing properties
II. Its income comes mainly from rents and property appreciation
III. It passes operating losses through to shareholders
IV. It must distribute at least 90% of taxable income to avoid corporate tax

- A. I and II only
- B. I, II and IV only
- C. II, III and IV only
- D. I, III and IV only

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Equity REITs own properties and earn rents and appreciation, and must distribute 90% of taxable income. No REIT passes losses through.

*Remember:* Equity REIT: owns property, earns rents, 90% distribution, no loss pass-through.

</details>

**Q4055** (§8.2 · FINRA section 2) — A hedge fund charges "2 and 20." An investor puts in $1,000,000 and the fund gains 15% for the year. If the 2% fee is based on the $1,000,000 starting value, what are the investor's total fees?

- A. $20,000
- B. $30,000
- C. $50,000
- D. $53,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Management fee = 2% x $1,000,000 = $20,000; performance fee = 20% x $150,000 profit = $30,000; total $50,000.

1. Management fee: 2% x $1,000,000 = $20,000
2. Profit: 15% x $1,000,000 = $150,000; 20% = $30,000
3. Total = $20,000 + $30,000 = $50,000

*Remember:* 2 and 20: 2% of assets plus 20% of profits.

</details>

**Q4056** (§8.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which customer qualifies as an accredited investor eligible to buy a hedge fund?

- A. A married couple with joint income of $260,000 in each of the last two years
- B. A single person who earned $230,000 in each of the last two years and expects the same this year
- C. A single person with $900,000 net worth excluding her home
- D. A person with $1.4 million net worth, $600,000 of it in her primary residence

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Accredited investors have net worth over $1 million excluding the primary residence, or income over $200,000 individually ($300,000 jointly) in each of the last two years.

*Remember:* Accredited: $1M net worth (ex-home) or $200k individual / $300k joint income for two years.

</details>

**Q4057** (§8.2 · FINRA section 2) — Six months after investing in a hedge fund with a one-year lock-up, a customer needs her money. Generally, she:

- A. Cannot redeem until the lock-up ends, then only at set windows with notice
- B. Can sell her interest on an exchange at market price
- C. Can redeem at NAV on any business day, paying a 2% fee
- D. Can redeem at NAV within 7 days, like a mutual fund

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Hedge funds are illiquid: lock-up periods are common, and redemptions after that are allowed only at quarterly or annual windows with advance notice.

*Remember:* Hedge funds: lock-ups (often 1 year+), then periodic redemption windows with notice.

</details>

**Q4058** (§8.2 · FINRA section 2) — Compared with investing directly in a hedge fund, a key drawback of a registered fund of hedge funds is:

- A. It is available only to qualified purchasers
- B. It cannot hold more than one hedge fund
- C. It removes all exposure to leverage
- D. A second layer of fees on top of the underlying funds' fees

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Funds of hedge funds can be registered and sold to retail investors with lower minimums, but they add a second layer of fees and keep the underlying illiquidity.

*Remember:* Fund of hedge funds: retail access, lower minimums, but double fees.

</details>

**Q4059** (§8.2 · FINRA section 2) — Instead of a prospectus, investors in a privately offered hedge fund typically receive a(n):

- A. Statement of Additional Information
- B. Official statement
- C. Private placement (offering) memorandum
- D. Summary prospectus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Hedge funds are Reg D private placements, not SEC-registered, so they provide an offering memorandum rather than a prospectus.

*Remember:* Private placements like hedge funds use an offering memorandum, not a prospectus.

</details>

**Q4060** (§8.2 · FINRA section 2) — A hedge fund relying on Section 3(c)(1) to avoid registering under the Investment Company Act of 1940 is limited to:

- A. No more than 35 non-accredited investors
- B. Only qualified purchasers
- C. No more than 500 investors
- D. No more than 100 investors

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Section 3(c)(1) funds may have no more than 100 investors; 3(c)(7) funds may have unlimited investors, but all must be qualified purchasers.

*Remember:* 3(c)(1): 100 investors or fewer; 3(c)(7): qualified purchasers only.

</details>

**Q4061** (§8.2 · FINRA section 2) — A customer invests $500,000 as a limited partner in a heavily leveraged hedge fund that collapses and owes its lenders far more than its assets. The customer's maximum loss is:

- A. $500,000
- B. Unlimited, because the fund used leverage
- C. $500,000 plus her pro rata share of the fund's debts
- D. $1,000,000, twice her investment

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A hedge fund itself can be wiped out by leverage, but as a limited partner the investor cannot lose more than she invested.

*Remember:* Hedge fund limited partners can lose their whole investment, but no more.

</details>

**Q4062** (§8.2 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is NOT a typical characteristic of hedge funds?

- A. Use of leverage and short selling
- B. Daily redemption at NAV
- C. Performance-based incentive fees
- D. Returns often uncorrelated with the broad market

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Hedge funds are illiquid, with lock-ups and periodic redemption windows; daily redemption at NAV is a mutual fund feature.

*Remember:* Hedge funds: leverage, shorting, 2-and-20 fees, low liquidity, little transparency.

</details>

**Q4063** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — A limited partner in a real estate program begins negotiating leases and making daily decisions about tenants. The main risk to the limited partner is:

- A. Forfeiting her share of depreciation deductions
- B. Automatic dissolution of the partnership
- C. Losing limited liability and being treated like a general partner
- D. Having her interest converted to a REIT share

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Limited partners must stay passive; participating in management risks losing limited status and facing unlimited liability.

*Remember:* LPs who participate in management risk losing limited liability.

</details>

**Q4064** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — An investor officially becomes a limited partner in a DPP when:

- A. The general partner accepts the investor's subscription agreement
- B. The investor's check is deposited
- C. The certificate of limited partnership is filed with the SEC
- D. The investor's broker-dealer approves the purchase

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The investor signs a subscription agreement, but admission occurs only when the general partner accepts it.

*Remember:* Subscription agreement: investor signs, GP accepts to admit the limited partner.

</details>

**Q4065** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is a restriction on the general partner of a limited partnership?

- A. It must own at least 10% of the partnership
- B. It may not compete with the partnership
- C. It may not receive any compensation
- D. It may not make decisions without an LP vote

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The GP manages the business, owes a fiduciary duty, must keep at least a 1% interest, and cannot compete with the partnership.

*Remember:* GP: manages, unlimited liability, fiduciary duty, at least 1% interest, cannot compete.

</details>

**Q4066** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which real estate DPP provides NO depreciation deductions?

- A. Existing properties
- B. New construction
- C. Government-assisted housing
- D. Raw land

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Land is not depreciable, so raw land programs offer only potential appreciation; they are the most speculative real estate DPPs.

*Remember:* Raw land: no depreciation, no income, appreciation only (most speculative).

</details>

**Q4067** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — An investor wants oil and gas exposure with immediate cash flow and the lowest risk. Which program is MOST suitable?

- A. A developmental program
- B. An exploratory program
- C. An income program
- D. A combination program

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Income programs buy already-producing wells, providing immediate income with the lowest risk among oil and gas programs.

*Remember:* Oil & gas risk: exploratory (highest) > developmental > income (lowest, immediate cash flow).

</details>

**Q4068** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which tax benefit is specific to oil and gas drilling programs?

- A. Depreciation of equipment
- B. Immediate deduction of intangible drilling costs
- C. Low-income housing tax credits
- D. Pass-through of operating losses

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Intangible drilling costs (labor, fuel, site prep) can be deducted in the year incurred; oil and gas programs also get depletion.

*Remember:* Oil & gas: IDCs deducted immediately plus depletion.

</details>

**Q4069** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — A program buys railcars and leases them to railroads. Besides lease income, its main tax benefit for investors is:

- A. Depreciation
- B. Depletion
- C. Intangible drilling cost deductions
- D. Low-income housing tax credits

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Equipment leasing programs provide lease income plus depreciation deductions on the equipment.

*Remember:* Equipment leasing DPP: lease income plus depreciation.

</details>

**Q4070** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — An investor puts $50,000 cash into an equipment leasing limited partnership and is responsible for $30,000 of the partnership's recourse debt. What is the investor's cost basis?

- A. $20,000
- B. $30,000
- C. $50,000
- D. $80,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** DPP basis = cash invested + share of recourse debt = $50,000 + $30,000 = $80,000. Deductible losses can't exceed basis.

1. Cash invested = $50,000
2. Add recourse debt share: $30,000
3. Basis = $80,000

*Remember:* DPP cost basis = cash invested + share of recourse debt (plus nonrecourse for real estate).

</details>

**Q4071** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — A real estate limited partnership sells a building on which partners have taken years of depreciation deductions. Some of the gain may be:

- A. Tax-free because the property was held long-term
- B. Taxed as a qualified dividend
- C. Deferred until the partnership dissolves
- D. Recaptured and taxed as ordinary income

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Depreciation previously deducted can be recaptured on sale and taxed as ordinary income.

*Remember:* Depreciation recapture: prior depreciation taxed as ordinary income when the asset is sold.

</details>

**Q4072** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — When a limited partnership dissolves, limited partners are paid:

- A. Before secured creditors
- B. After the general partner
- C. After general creditors but before the general partner
- D. At the same time as general creditors

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Dissolution order: secured creditors, general creditors, limited partners, then the general partner.

*Remember:* LP dissolution order: secured creditors, general creditors, limited partners, general partner.

</details>

**Q4073** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — When evaluating a direct participation program, the MOST important factor is:

- A. The size of the projected tax deductions
- B. The number of limited partners
- C. Whether the units are listed on an exchange
- D. The economic viability of the business

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A DPP should make sense as a business apart from tax benefits; economic viability comes first, followed by the GP's track record and costs.

*Remember:* Evaluate a DPP on economic viability first, tax benefits second.

</details>

**Q4074** (§8.3 · FINRA section 3) — A rep recommends a real estate limited partnership for a customer's IRA. The main problem with this recommendation is that:

- A. The program's pass-through tax benefits are wasted in a tax-deferred account
- B. IRAs may not hold any securities other than mutual funds
- C. Limited partnerships cannot produce income
- D. The customer would have unlimited liability

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** DPPs are bought largely for their pass-through losses and deductions, which provide no benefit inside an IRA; DPPs are also illiquid.

*Remember:* DPP tax benefits are wasted in retirement accounts.

</details>

**Q4075** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following pass operating losses through to their investors?
I. A REIT
II. A real estate limited partnership
III. A hedge fund organized as a limited partnership

- A. I and II only
- B. I and III only
- C. II and III only
- D. I, II and III

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Partnerships, including DPPs and hedge funds organized as LPs, pass through income and losses. REITs pass through only income.

*Remember:* Losses flow through partnerships (DPPs, hedge fund LPs), never REITs.

</details>

**Q9033** (§8.2 · FINRA section 1) — A hedge fund wants to accept more than 100 investors without registering as an investment company under the Investment Company Act of 1940. Relying on Section 3(c)(7), it must limit its investors to:

- A. Qualified purchasers
- B. Accredited investors
- C. Qualified institutional buyers
- D. Up to 35 non-accredited investors

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Section 3(c)(7) exempts funds whose investors are all qualified purchasers (generally $5 million+ in investments). The 3(c)(1) exemption instead caps a fund at 100 investors.

*Remember:* 3(c)(1): ≤ 100 investors; 3(c)(7): qualified purchasers only ($5M+ in investments).

</details>

**Q9034** (§8.3 · FINRA section 2) — A limited partner in a real estate program may do which of the following without jeopardizing limited liability?
I. Vote on a proposed sale of the partnership's properties
II. Inspect the partnership's books and records
III. Sign leases with new tenants on the partnership's behalf
IV. Sue the general partner for breach of fiduciary duty

- A. I and II only
- B. I, II and IV
- C. II, III and IV
- D. I, II, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Limited partners may vote on major matters, inspect the books, and sue the GP. Signing leases is management activity reserved to the general partner and can cost an LP limited liability.

*Remember:* LPs: vote on major matters, inspect books, sue GP; managing the business risks limited liability.

</details>

## Chapter 9: Options

**Q81** (§9.3.1 · FINRA section 2) — An investor buys 1 ABC Jun 50 call at 4. The breakeven point and maximum loss are:

- A. $46; $400
- B. $54; $400
- C. $54; unlimited
- D. $50; $4

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Call breakeven = strike + premium = 54. Maximum loss for any option buyer = premium = 4 × 100 = $400. Maximum gain is unlimited.

1. Breakeven = 50 + 4 = 54
2. Max loss = premium = 4 × 100 = $400

*Remember:* Long call: breakeven = strike + premium; max loss = premium paid × 100.

</details>

**Q82** (§9.3.2 · FINRA section 2) — The maximum potential loss for the writer of an uncovered (naked) call is:

- A. The premium received
- B. The strike price minus the premium
- C. The strike price plus the premium
- D. Unlimited

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A naked call writer must deliver stock they don't own; since a stock can rise without limit, the loss is unlimited. This is the riskiest basic option position.

*Remember:* Naked call writers face unlimited loss because the stock price has no ceiling.

</details>

**Q83** (§9.2.2 · FINRA section 2) — XYZ stock is trading at $62. An XYZ 55 put is trading at 1. Which statement is TRUE?

- A. The put is in the money by 7 points
- B. The put has 1 point of intrinsic value
- C. The put has no intrinsic value and 1 point of time value
- D. The put is at the money

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A put is in the money only when the stock is below the strike. At $62 vs. a 55 strike, the put is out of the money; the entire premium is time value.

1. Put intrinsic = 55 − 62 → negative, so 0
2. Time value = premium 1 − intrinsic 0 = 1

*Remember:* Puts are in the money only when market price is below strike; premium = intrinsic + time value.

</details>

**Q84** (§9.3.5 · FINRA section 2) — An investor who is long 100 shares of stock wants to protect against a decline while keeping unlimited upside. The BEST strategy is to:

- A. Write a call
- B. Buy a call
- C. Buy a put
- D. Write a put

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A protective put locks in a minimum sale price (the strike) at the cost of the premium while leaving the upside intact. Writing a call provides only limited downside protection and caps the upside.

*Remember:* Long stock is hedged by buying a put: floor at the strike, upside kept, cost is the premium.

</details>

**Q85** (§9.3.6 · FINRA section 2) — An investor owns 100 shares of ABC purchased at $42 and writes 1 ABC 45 call at 2. Maximum gain is:

- A. $200
- B. $300
- C. $500
- D. Unlimited

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Covered call max gain = (strike − cost) + premium = (45 − 42) + 2 = 5 points = $500. The stock is called away at 45.

1. Stock gain to strike: 45 − 42 = 3
2. Add premium: 3 + 2 = 5 points
3. 5 × 100 = $500

*Remember:* Covered call max gain = (strike − stock cost) + premium, × 100.

</details>

**Q86** (§9.2.4 · FINRA section 2) — An investor holds 1 XYZ 80 call. XYZ declares a 2-for-1 stock split. After adjustment the investor holds:

- A. 1 XYZ 40 call for 200 shares
- B. 2 XYZ 40 calls for 100 shares each
- C. 2 XYZ 80 calls for 100 shares each
- D. 1 XYZ 80 call for 200 shares

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Even splits multiply the number of contracts and divide the strike; each contract still covers 100 shares. Uneven splits instead change the shares per contract.

1. Contracts: 1 × 2 = 2
2. Strike: 80 ÷ 2 = 40
3. Check: 2 × 100 × 40 = 1 × 100 × 80 = $8,000

*Remember:* Even splits: multiply contracts and divide strike by the ratio; each contract stays 100 shares.

</details>

**Q87** (§9.3.7 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following is TRUE of broad-based index options?

- A. Exercise results in delivery of the underlying stocks
- B. They are settled in cash
- C. They are always American style
- D. They settle T+2

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Index options settle in cash (the in-the-money amount × $100) the next business day; most are European style.

*Remember:* Index options are cash settled (ITM amount × $100), next business day; most are European style.

</details>

**Q88** (§9.2.1 · FINRA section 1) — Listed options are issued and guaranteed by:

- A. The Cboe
- B. FINRA
- C. The Options Clearing Corporation
- D. The underlying corporation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The OCC is the issuer and guarantor of all listed options, standing between every buyer and seller.

*Remember:* The Options Clearing Corporation (OCC) issues and guarantees all listed options.

</details>

**Q89** (§9.3.4 · FINRA section 2) — An investor writes 1 DEF 30 put at 3. The maximum loss is:

- A. $300
- B. $2,700
- C. $3,000
- D. Unlimited

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Put writer max loss = (strike − premium) × 100 = (30 − 3) × 100 = $2,700; the stock can only fall to zero.

1. Strike − premium = 30 − 3 = 27
2. 27 × 100 = $2,700

*Remember:* Short put max loss = (strike − premium) × 100; stock can only fall to zero.

</details>

**Q90** (§9.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — An investor who is bearish on a stock but wants limited risk should:

- A. Sell the stock short
- B. Buy a put
- C. Write a call
- D. Write a put

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A long put profits from a decline with loss limited to the premium. Short sales and naked calls have unlimited risk; writing a put is bullish.

*Remember:* Buying a put is bearish with risk limited to the premium paid.

</details>

**Q5001** (§9.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which party to a listed option contract is obligated to perform if the option is exercised?

- A. The holder of the call
- B. The holder of the put
- C. The writer of the option
- D. The exchange where the option trades

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The writer (seller) receives the premium and in exchange takes on the obligation to buy or sell the stock if assigned. Buyers of calls and puts have rights, not obligations.

*Remember:* Buyer (long) has the right; writer (short) has the obligation.

</details>

**Q5002** (§9.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer enters an order to "Sell 2 MNO Oct 35 puts @ 1.50." If the order is filled, the customer:

- A. Pays $300 and has the right to sell 200 shares at $35
- B. Receives $300 and may be obligated to buy 200 shares at $35
- C. Receives $150 and may be obligated to buy 100 shares at $35
- D. Receives $300 and may be obligated to sell 200 shares at $35

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Selling (writing) puts brings in premium: 1.50 x 100 x 2 contracts = $300. A put writer is obligated to buy the stock at the strike if assigned, here 200 shares at $35.

1. 1.50 x 100 = $150 per contract
2. $150 x 2 contracts = $300 received

*Remember:* Put writer receives premium; obligated to BUY at strike. Premium x 100 x contracts.

</details>

**Q5003** (§9.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following option positions are bullish?
I. Long call
II. Short call
III. Long put
IV. Short put

- A. I and II
- B. I and IV
- C. II and III
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A call buyer profits when the stock rises, and a put writer profits when the stock stays at or above the strike. Short calls and long puts are bearish.

*Remember:* Bullish: long call, short put. Bearish: short call, long put.

</details>

**Q5004** (§9.1 · FINRA section 2) — Which statement about standard listed equity options is TRUE?

- A. They may be exercised at any time up to expiration
- B. Each contract normally covers 1,000 shares
- C. They may be exercised only on the expiration date
- D. Standard monthly contracts expire on the third Monday

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** All listed equity options are American style, so the holder can exercise at any time before expiration. Standard contracts cover 100 shares and monthly options expire on the third Friday.

*Remember:* Equity options: American style, 100 shares, expire third Friday of the month.

</details>

**Q5005** (§9.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — A customer is opening an account to trade listed options. At or before account approval, the firm must deliver:

- A. A prospectus registered under the Securities Act of 1933
- B. The OCC's audited annual financial statements
- C. The Options Disclosure Document
- D. A copy of the Cboe's exchange rulebook

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Options are not sold with a traditional prospectus. Customers must receive the ODD, Characteristics and Risks of Standardized Options, at or before account approval.

*Remember:* Deliver the ODD (Characteristics and Risks of Standardized Options) at or before options approval.

</details>

**Q5006** (§9.2.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer buys 5 XYZ calls in a regular-way trade on Monday. Absent a holiday, the trade settles on:

- A. Monday
- B. Tuesday
- C. Wednesday
- D. Thursday

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Option trades settle regular way T+1, one business day after the trade date, so a Monday trade settles Tuesday.

*Remember:* Option trades settle T+1; stock delivered on exercise also settles T+1.

</details>

**Q5007** (§9.2.1 · FINRA section 1) — All of the following statements about listed options are true EXCEPT:

- A. The OCC guarantees performance on every listed contract
- B. The OCC standardizes strike prices and expiration dates
- C. The Cboe is the largest options exchange
- D. Each option series is offered with its own registered prospectus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Listed options do not have an issuer in the corporate sense and are not sold with a separate prospectus; customers instead receive the ODD. The OCC issues, guarantees and standardizes contracts.

*Remember:* OCC issues and guarantees listed options; customers get the ODD, not a prospectus.

</details>
