**Q5008** (§9.2.2 · FINRA section 2) — ABC stock is trading at $47. Which of the following options is in the money?

- A. ABC 45 put
- B. ABC 50 call
- C. ABC 50 put
- D. ABC 47 call

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A put is in the money when the stock is below the strike. With ABC at 47, the 50 put has 3 points of intrinsic value.

*Remember:* Call Up, Put Down: call ITM if stock > strike; put ITM if stock < strike.

</details>

**Q5009** (§9.2.2 · FINRA section 2) — XYZ is trading at $38. An XYZ 35 call is quoted at 4.50. The time value of the call is:

- A. 0
- B. 1.50
- C. 3.00
- D. 4.50

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Intrinsic value is 38 - 35 = 3. Time value is the premium minus intrinsic value: 4.50 - 3 = 1.50.

1. Intrinsic = 38 - 35 = 3
2. Time value = 4.50 - 3 = 1.50

*Remember:* Time value = premium - intrinsic value. Call intrinsic = stock - strike.

</details>

**Q5010** (§9.2.2 · FINRA section 2) — All of the following would generally increase an option's premium EXCEPT:

- A. Higher volatility in the underlying stock
- B. The option moving deeper into the money
- C. A longer time remaining until expiration
- D. Expiration drawing closer with no change in the stock price

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** As expiration approaches, time value decays, so the premium falls if nothing else changes. Volatility, intrinsic value and more time all add to the premium.

*Remember:* Premium rises with time remaining, volatility and depth in the money.

</details>

**Q5011** (§9.2.2 · FINRA section 2) — On expiration day, GHI is trading at $55. Ignoring transaction costs, a GHI 60 put should be worth approximately:

- A. $0, because time value is gone
- B. $500 per contract
- C. $5,500 per contract
- D. $6,000 per contract

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** At expiration an option is worth only its intrinsic value. The put is 60 - 55 = 5 points in the money, or $500 per contract.

1. Intrinsic = 60 - 55 = 5
2. 5 x 100 = $500

*Remember:* At expiration, premium = intrinsic value only. Put intrinsic = strike - stock.

</details>

**Q5012** (§9.2.3 · FINRA section 2) — A customer who wrote an XYZ call two months ago wants to eliminate her obligation before expiration. She should enter:

- A. An opening sale
- B. A closing sale
- C. A closing purchase
- D. An opening purchase

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A writer opened the position with a sale, so she closes it by buying the same option back, a closing purchase.

*Remember:* Writer exits with a closing purchase; buyer exits with a closing sale.

</details>

**Q5013** (§9.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — When a broker-dealer receives an exercise notice from the OCC, it may allocate the assignment to customers by any of the following methods EXCEPT:

- A. Random selection
- B. First-in, first-out
- C. Another fair and reasonable method
- D. To the customer holding the largest short position

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Firms must allocate assignments fairly: randomly, first-in-first-out, or another fair method. Assigning by largest position is not permitted.

*Remember:* Assignment: random, FIFO or other fair method; never largest position first.

</details>

**Q5014** (§9.2.3 · FINRA section 2) — A customer holds 1 JKL 25 call. On expiration day JKL closes at $25.03, and the customer gives no instructions. The call will most likely:

- A. Be automatically exercised by the OCC
- B. Expire worthless because it is less than $0.25 in the money
- C. Remain open for three business days for the customer to decide
- D. Be settled in cash for $3

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The OCC automatically exercises equity options that are in the money by $0.01 or more at expiration unless the holder instructs otherwise. This call is $0.03 in the money.

*Remember:* OCC auto-exercises equity options $0.01 or more in the money at expiration.

</details>

**Q5015** (§9.2.3 · FINRA section 2) — An investor bought 1 RST 50 put at 2. She later exercises it, delivering 100 shares she already owned. For tax purposes, her sale proceeds are:

- A. $4,800
- B. $5,000
- C. $5,200
- D. $200

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** When a put is exercised, the buyer's sale proceeds equal the strike minus the premium paid: (50 - 2) x 100 = $4,800.

1. Strike - premium = 50 - 2 = 48
2. 48 x 100 = $4,800

*Remember:* Put exercised: buyer's sale proceeds = strike - premium.

</details>

**Q5016** (§9.2.4 · FINRA section 2) — An investor holds 1 XYZ 90 call. XYZ effects a 3-for-2 stock split. After the OCC adjustment, the investor holds:

- A. 1 XYZ 60 call covering 150 shares
- B. 1.5 XYZ 60 calls covering 100 shares each
- C. 2 XYZ 45 calls covering 100 shares each
- D. 1 XYZ 90 call covering 150 shares

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** For uneven splits, the number of contracts stays the same, shares per contract rise to 150 and the strike falls to 90 x 2/3 = 60. Aggregate value stays $9,000.

1. Shares: 100 x 3/2 = 150
2. Strike: 90 x 2/3 = 60
3. Check: 150 x 60 = 9,000 = 100 x 90

*Remember:* Uneven split: same contracts, more shares per contract, proportionally lower strike.

</details>

**Q5017** (§9.2.4 · FINRA section 2) — ABC declares its regular quarterly cash dividend of $0.40 per share. Holders of ABC listed options will see:

- A. No adjustment to their contracts
- B. Strike prices reduced by $0.40
- C. More shares per contract
- D. Additional contracts at a lower strike

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The OCC does not adjust contracts for ordinary cash dividends; only unusually large or special dividends trigger an adjustment.

*Remember:* No OCC adjustment for ordinary cash dividends.

</details>

**Q5018** (§9.2.4 · FINRA section 2) — An investor owns 1 DEF 66 call. DEF pays a 10% stock dividend. After the adjustment, the investor holds:

- A. 1 DEF 66 call covering 110 shares
- B. 1.1 DEF 60 calls covering 100 shares each
- C. 1 DEF 60 call covering 110 shares
- D. 1 DEF 60 call covering 100 shares

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Stock dividends are treated like uneven splits: one contract, now for 110 shares, with the strike reduced to 6,600 / 110 = 60.

1. Shares: 100 x 1.10 = 110
2. Aggregate: 66 x 100 = 6,600
3. New strike: 6,600 / 110 = 60

*Remember:* Stock dividend: shares per contract increase, strike reduced; total value unchanged.

</details>

**Q5019** (§9.3.1 · FINRA section 2) — An investor buys 1 DEF Sep 70 call at 3.50. At expiration DEF is $72 and the investor closes the position at intrinsic value. The result is:

- A. A $200 profit
- B. A $150 loss
- C. A $350 loss
- D. A $50 profit

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The call is worth its intrinsic value of 72 - 70 = 2 at expiration. The investor paid 3.50, so the result is a 1.50 loss, or $150.

1. Intrinsic at expiration = 72 - 70 = 2
2. 2 - 3.50 = -1.50
3. -1.50 x 100 = $150 loss

*Remember:* Long call: profit only above breakeven = strike + premium.

</details>

**Q5020** (§9.3.1 · FINRA section 2) — A customer expects to receive a large bonus in three months and wants to lock in today's purchase price on a stock she believes will rise. The MOST suitable option strategy is to:

- A. Write a call on the stock
- B. Buy a put on the stock
- C. Write a covered call on the stock
- D. Buy a call on the stock

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A long call gives the right to buy at the strike, locking in a purchase price for a limited cost (the premium) while she waits for the cash.

*Remember:* Long call = bullish; locks in a purchase price; max loss = premium.

</details>

**Q5021** (§9.3.2 · FINRA section 2) — An investor writes 1 uncovered GHI 80 call at 5. At expiration GHI is $92 and the call is assigned. The investor's result is:

- A. A $700 loss
- B. A $1,200 loss
- C. A $500 gain
- D. A $1,700 loss

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The writer must deliver stock worth 92 for 80, a 12-point loss, offset by the 5-point premium received, for a net 7-point loss, or $700.

1. Assignment loss: 92 - 80 = 12
2. Less premium: 12 - 5 = 7
3. 7 x 100 = $700 loss

*Remember:* Short call loss = (stock price - strike) - premium, x 100.

</details>

**Q5022** (§9.3.2 · FINRA section 2) — A customer writes 1 uncovered KLM 45 call at 2.25. At what stock price at expiration does the customer break even?

- A. $42.75
- B. $45.00
- C. $47.25
- D. $49.50

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The breakeven for a short call is the same as for a long call: strike + premium = 45 + 2.25 = 47.25. The writer profits below that price.

1. Call breakeven = strike + premium
2. 45 + 2.25 = 47.25

*Remember:* Call breakeven (long or short) = strike + premium.

</details>

**Q5023** (§9.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — An investor buys 1 TUV 60 put at 4. TUV falls to $51 at expiration and the investor closes the put at intrinsic value. The profit is:

- A. $900
- B. $400
- C. $500
- D. $5,600

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The put is worth 60 - 51 = 9 at expiration. Subtracting the 4 premium paid leaves a 5-point profit, or $500.

1. Intrinsic = 60 - 51 = 9
2. Profit = 9 - 4 = 5
3. 5 x 100 = $500

*Remember:* Long put profit = (strike - stock price) - premium, x 100.

</details>

**Q5024** (§9.3.3 · FINRA section 2) — An investor buys 1 WXY 35 put at 1.25. The maximum potential gain is:

- A. $3,375
- B. $3,500
- C. $3,625
- D. Unlimited

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A long put's best case is the stock falling to zero: the investor sells at 35 a stock worth nothing, minus the 1.25 premium, or 33.75 x 100 = $3,375.

1. Strike - premium = 35 - 1.25 = 33.75
2. 33.75 x 100 = $3,375

*Remember:* Long put max gain = (strike - premium) x 100.

</details>

**Q5025** (§9.3.4 · FINRA section 2) — LMN trades at $52. A customer would gladly own it at $50 and writes 1 LMN 50 put at 2. If the put is assigned, her effective cost per share is:

- A. $52
- B. $50
- C. $54
- D. $48

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** If assigned, she buys at the $50 strike but keeps the $2 premium, so her net cost is 50 - 2 = $48 per share, which is also her breakeven.

1. Pay strike: $50
2. Less premium kept: 50 - 2 = $48

*Remember:* Short put breakeven / effective cost = strike - premium.

</details>

**Q5026** (§9.3.4 · FINRA section 2) — A short put is considered covered (cash-secured) when the writer:

- A. Owns 100 shares of the underlying stock
- B. Has also written a call on the same stock
- C. Owns a call on the same stock and strike
- D. Has cash on deposit equal to the strike price x 100

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The put writer's obligation is to buy the stock, so it is covered by cash equal to the aggregate strike price (or by a short position in the stock).

*Remember:* Short put is covered by cash equal to the strike x 100, or by short stock.

</details>

**Q5027** (§9.3.5 · FINRA section 2) — An investor sells short 100 shares of XYZ at $40 and buys 1 XYZ 45 call at 2. The investor's maximum potential loss is:

- A. $200
- B. $500
- C. $700
- D. Unlimited

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The call caps the repurchase price at 45, a 5-point loss on the short sale, plus the 2-point premium, for a maximum loss of 7 points, or $700.

1. Loss on stock: 45 - 40 = 5
2. Add premium: 5 + 2 = 7
3. 7 x 100 = $700

*Remember:* Protective call max loss = (strike - short sale price) + premium.

</details>

**Q5028** (§9.3.5 · FINRA section 2) — An investor buys 100 shares of RST at $60 and at the same time buys 1 RST 55 put at 3. The breakeven point is:

- A. $52
- B. $57
- C. $58
- D. $63

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** With a protective put, the stock must rise enough to recover the premium paid: 60 + 3 = $63.

1. Protective put breakeven = stock cost + premium
2. 60 + 3 = 63

*Remember:* Protective put breakeven = stock cost + premium.

</details>

**Q5029** (§9.3.5 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following positions provide protection (a hedge) against an adverse move in a stock position?
I. Long stock and long put
II. Long stock and long call
III. Short stock and long call
IV. Short stock and long put

- A. I and II
- B. I and III
- C. II and IV
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A long stock position loses when the price falls, so it is hedged with a put. A short stock position loses when the price rises, so it is hedged with a call.

*Remember:* Hedge long stock with a long put; hedge short stock with a long call.

</details>

**Q5030** (§9.3.6 · FINRA section 2) — An investor owns 100 shares of NOP bought at $30 and writes 1 NOP 35 call at 1.50. The breakeven point is:

- A. $28.50
- B. $31.50
- C. $33.50
- D. $36.50

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** In a covered call, the premium received lowers the effective cost of the stock: 30 - 1.50 = $28.50.

1. Covered call breakeven = stock cost - premium
2. 30 - 1.50 = 28.50

*Remember:* Covered call breakeven = stock cost - premium.

</details>

**Q5031** (§9.3.6 · FINRA section 3) — A retiree has held DEF stock in her IRA for years. She expects the price to stay flat for several months and would like to generate extra income from the shares. The MOST suitable strategy is to:

- A. Write covered calls on DEF
- B. Buy puts on DEF
- C. Buy additional calls on DEF
- D. Buy broad-based index puts

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Writing covered calls on stock already owned produces premium income and suits a neutral to mildly bullish outlook. It is the most conservative option strategy and is commonly permitted in retirement accounts.

*Remember:* Covered call = income on owned stock; neutral to mildly bullish; most conservative.

</details>

**Q5032** (§9.3.6 · FINRA section 2) — The main drawback of writing covered calls on stock already owned is that the investor:

- A. Faces unlimited loss if the stock rises
- B. Must buy more shares if the call is assigned
- C. Gives up any appreciation above the strike price
- D. Can no longer receive dividends on the stock

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** If the stock rises above the strike, the shares are called away at the strike, so gains above that level are given up in exchange for the premium.

*Remember:* Covered call caps upside at the strike; max gain = (strike - cost) + premium.

</details>

**Q5033** (§9.3.7 · FINRA section 2) — An investor exercises 1 SPX 5,800 put when the index settles at 5,740. The investor will receive:

- A. $6,000 in cash
- B. $600 in cash
- C. 100 shares of each stock in the index
- D. $574,000 in cash

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Index options are cash settled. The put is 5,800 - 5,740 = 60 points in the money, and 60 x the $100 multiplier = $6,000, paid the next business day.

1. ITM amount = 5,800 - 5,740 = 60
2. 60 x $100 = $6,000 cash

*Remember:* Index option exercise: ITM points x $100 multiplier, paid in cash T+1.

</details>

**Q5034** (§9.3.7 · FINRA section 2) — A client with a large, diversified stock portfolio is worried about a broad market decline over the next few months but does not want to sell her holdings. Which strategy BEST addresses her concern?

- A. Buy broad-based index calls
- B. Buy puts on her single largest holding
- C. Write broad-based index puts
- D. Buy broad-based index puts

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Long index puts gain value as the overall market falls, offsetting losses in a diversified portfolio without selling the stocks.

*Remember:* Hedge a diversified portfolio by buying broad-based index puts.

</details>

**Q5035** (§9.3.7 · FINRA section 2) — Long-term equity anticipation securities (LEAPS) may have expirations of up to approximately:

- A. 9 months
- B. 12 months
- C. 39 months
- D. 60 months

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** LEAPS are long-dated options with expirations up to 39 months, far longer than standard monthly options.

*Remember:* LEAPS = long-term options, up to 39 months.

</details>

**Q9035** (§9.2.2 · FINRA section 2) — XYZ is trading at $63. Which of the following options has the GREATEST intrinsic value?

- A. XYZ 60 call @ 5
- B. XYZ 70 put @ 9
- C. XYZ 65 put @ 4
- D. XYZ 55 call @ 8.25

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The 55 call is 8 points in the money (63 − 55). The 70 put is only 7 in the money; its higher premium includes 2 points of time value.

*Remember:* Call intrinsic = stock − strike; put intrinsic = strike − stock; premium = intrinsic + time value.

</details>

**Q9036** (§9.2.4 · FINRA section 2) — An investor holds 1 XYZ 15 call. XYZ completes a 1-for-4 reverse stock split. After the OCC adjustment, the investor most likely holds:

- A. 1 XYZ 15 call covering 25 shares
- B. 1 XYZ 60 call covering 100 shares
- C. 1 XYZ 60 call covering 25 shares
- D. 1 XYZ 3.75 call covering 400 shares

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** In a reverse split, shares per contract decrease and the strike increases proportionally. 100 ÷ 4 = 25 shares and $15 × 4 = $60, keeping the aggregate exercise value at $1,500.

*Remember:* Reverse split: fewer shares per contract, higher strike; total value (shares × strike) unchanged.

</details>

**Q9037** (§9.3.6 · FINRA section 2) — An investor owns 100 shares of LMN bought at $55 and writes 1 LMN 60 call at 2.50. What is the investor's maximum potential loss?

- A. $750
- B. $5,250
- C. $5,500
- D. Unlimited

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** If LMN goes to zero, the investor loses the $55 cost but keeps the $2.50 premium: ($55 − $2.50) × 100 = $5,250.

1. Stock falls to $0: loss on stock = $55
2. Premium kept = $2.50
3. Max loss = (55 − 2.50) × 100 = $5,250

*Remember:* Covered call max loss = (stock cost − premium) × 100.

</details>

**Q9038** (§9.3.7 · FINRA section 2) — Two weeks before expiration, a customer holds a European-style S&P 500 index call that is well in the money. She wants to capture its value now. She can:

- A. Sell the call in a closing sale, since she cannot exercise before expiration
- B. Exercise the call today and receive the cash settlement the next business day
- C. Exercise the call today and receive the stocks in the index
- D. Ask the OCC to settle the contract early at its intrinsic value

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** European-style options can be exercised only at expiration, but they can be sold in the market at any time. A closing sale captures both intrinsic and remaining time value.

*Remember:* European style = exercise only at expiration; holder can still close the position anytime by selling.

</details>

## Chapter 10: Taxes

**Q91** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor has $12,000 in long-term capital gains and $20,000 in short-term capital losses this year. The investor may:

- A. Deduct $8,000 against ordinary income this year
- B. Deduct $3,000 against ordinary income and carry forward $5,000
- C. Deduct $20,000 against ordinary income
- D. Carry forward the entire $8,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Net loss = $8,000. Up to $3,000 of net capital loss is deductible against ordinary income each year; the remaining $5,000 carries forward.

1. Net: $12,000 gain − $20,000 loss = −$8,000
2. Deduct $3,000 against ordinary income
3. Carry forward $8,000 − $3,000 = $5,000

*Remember:* Net capital losses offset ordinary income up to $3,000 per year; excess carries forward indefinitely.

</details>

**Q92** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor sells XYZ at a loss on March 10 and buys XYZ call options on March 25. For tax purposes:

- A. The loss is fully deductible
- B. The loss is disallowed under the wash sale rule
- C. The loss is deductible only against gains in XYZ
- D. The loss is converted to a long-term loss

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Buying the same or a substantially identical security — including options or convertibles on it — within 30 days before or after a loss sale is a wash sale; the loss is added to the new position's basis.

*Remember:* Buying the same or substantially identical security (including calls) within 30 days of a loss sale disallows the loss.

</details>

**Q93** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — A father gives his daughter 100 shares of stock he bought at $20 when it is worth $50. She sells it a month later for $55. Her taxable gain is:

- A. $500 short-term
- B. $3,500 short-term
- C. $3,500 long-term (if the father held it more than a year)
- D. $0 — gifts are not taxable to the recipient

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Gifted securities carry over the donor's basis ($20) and holding period. Gain = $55 − $20 = $35 × 100 = $3,500, long-term if the combined holding period exceeds a year.

1. Basis = donor's $20
2. Gain per share = 55 − 20 = 35
3. 35 × 100 = $3,500, long-term via tacked holding period

*Remember:* Gifted stock (at a gain) carries over the donor's cost basis and holding period.

</details>

**Q94** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor inherits stock that the deceased bought for $10,000 and that was worth $40,000 on the date of death. The investor's cost basis is:

- A. $10,000
- B. $40,000
- C. $25,000
- D. $0

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Inherited securities receive a stepped-up basis to the fair market value at death, and the holding period is automatically long-term.

*Remember:* Inherited securities get a stepped-up basis to fair market value at date of death; always long-term.

</details>

**Q95** (§10.2 · FINRA section 3) — The annual accretion on a corporate zero-coupon bond held in a taxable account is:

- A. Not taxable until maturity
- B. Taxed each year as interest income even though no cash is received
- C. Taxed as a capital gain at maturity
- D. Exempt from federal tax

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Original issue discount accretes annually and is taxed as 'phantom' interest income. Municipal zeros are the exception (tax-exempt). Hold corporate zeros in tax-deferred accounts.

*Remember:* Corporate zero-coupon accretion is taxed annually as interest income, even with no cash received.

</details>

**Q96** (§10.1 · FINRA section 3) — Qualified dividends are taxed at:

- A. Ordinary income rates
- B. The long-term capital gains rates
- C. A flat 28%
- D. The investor's state rate only

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Qualified dividends (from US corporations, with the holding period met) are taxed at the 0/15/20% long-term capital gains rates. REIT and money-market dividends are ordinary.

*Remember:* Qualified dividends are taxed at long-term capital gains rates (0/15/20%).

</details>

**Q97** (§10.4 · FINRA section 3) — Which type of income is required for an individual to make an IRA contribution?

- A. Portfolio income
- B. Passive income
- C. Earned income
- D. Any taxable income

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** IRA contributions require earned income (wages, salary, self-employment). Interest, dividends, capital gains, pensions and rental income don't qualify.

*Remember:* IRA contributions require earned income: wages, salary, bonuses, self-employment income.

</details>

**Q98** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — A long-term capital gain requires a holding period of:

- A. At least 6 months
- B. At least one year
- C. More than one year
- D. More than 18 months

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** More than one year (one year and a day). One year or less is short-term, taxed as ordinary income.

*Remember:* Long-term capital gain requires holding MORE than one year; one year or less is short-term.

</details>

**Q99** (§10.4 · FINRA section 3) — A customer refuses to provide a Social Security number when opening an account. The firm must:

- A. Refuse all trading
- B. Apply backup withholding of 24% on dividends, interest and sale proceeds
- C. Report the customer to the SEC
- D. Close the account within 30 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Without a taxpayer ID (Form W-9), the IRS requires backup withholding at 24%.

*Remember:* No taxpayer ID (W-9) means the firm must apply 24% backup withholding.

</details>

**Q100** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — If an investor who bought shares at several different prices does not specify which shares to sell, the IRS assumes:

- A. Average cost
- B. Highest cost first
- C. First-in, first-out
- D. Last-in, first-out

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** FIFO is the default; specific identification lets the investor choose lots; average cost is allowed for mutual funds.

*Remember:* If no lots are identified, the IRS default is first-in, first-out (FIFO).

</details>

**Q5036** (§10.1 · FINRA section 3) — A customer elects to reinvest all dividends from her stock mutual fund into additional fund shares. The reinvested dividends are:

- A. Taxable in the year they are paid
- B. Not taxed until the fund shares are sold
- C. Tax-deferred until she begins withdrawals
- D. Taxable only if they exceed the annual exclusion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Dividends are taxable in the year paid whether taken in cash or reinvested. The reinvested amounts are added to her cost basis.

*Remember:* Dividends are taxable when paid, cash or reinvested; reinvestments add to basis.

</details>

**Q5037** (§10.1 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following distributions would MOST likely be taxed at ordinary income rates rather than qualified dividend rates?

- A. A dividend from a US manufacturer held for two years
- B. A dividend from a real estate investment trust
- C. A long-term capital gains distribution from a mutual fund
- D. A dividend from a US bank stock held for five years

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** REIT dividends generally are non-qualified and taxed as ordinary income. Dividends from US corporations held long enough qualify for long-term capital gains rates.

*Remember:* REIT, money market and short-term gain distributions = ordinary income.

</details>

**Q5038** (§10.1 · FINRA section 3) — An investor owns 200 shares of XYZ with a total cost basis of $6,000. XYZ pays a 25% stock dividend. Which statement is TRUE?

- A. She now owns 250 shares with a $24 basis per share and owes no tax now
- B. She owes tax on the value of the 50 new shares this year
- C. Her basis remains $30 per share on all 250 shares
- D. Her total cost basis increases to $7,500

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Stock dividends are not taxable when received. The total $6,000 basis is spread over 250 shares, lowering the per-share basis to $24.

1. New shares: 200 x 1.25 = 250
2. Basis per share: $6,000 / 250 = $24

*Remember:* Stock dividends/splits: not taxable; same total basis spread over more shares.

</details>

**Q5039** (§10.1 · FINRA section 3) — ABC Corp. owns 5% of the common stock of an unaffiliated US corporation and receives $100,000 of dividends from it. Under the dividends-received deduction, ABC can generally exclude from taxable income:

- A. $0
- B. $65,000
- C. $100,000
- D. $50,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A corporation owning less than 20% of a domestic payer can generally exclude 50% of dividends received, or $50,000. The 65% exclusion requires 20% or more ownership.

1. Ownership under 20% = 50% DRD
2. $100,000 x 50% = $50,000 excluded

*Remember:* DRD: corporations exclude 50% of domestic dividends (65% if they own 20%+).

</details>

**Q5040** (§10.1 · FINRA section 3) — An investor bought shares of a mutual fund four months ago. The fund now pays a long-term capital gains distribution. For the investor, the distribution is taxed as:

- A. Short-term, because she owned the fund less than a year
- B. Tax-free until the fund shares are sold
- C. Ordinary income, like a money market dividend
- D. Long-term, regardless of how long she owned the fund

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Long-term capital gains distributions are long-term to the shareholder no matter how long the fund shares have been held. The fund's holding period, not the investor's, determines the character.

*Remember:* Fund LT capital gains distributions are long-term regardless of the shareholder's holding period.

</details>

**Q5041** (§10.1 · FINRA section 3) — A US investor receives dividends from a foreign company, and taxes are withheld by the company's home country. The investor can usually:

- A. Claim a foreign tax credit on her US return
- B. Recover the withheld tax by filing Form W-8BEN
- C. Deduct the withheld tax as a capital loss
- D. Exclude the foreign dividends from US income

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Foreign taxes withheld on dividends can usually be claimed as a foreign tax credit, reducing US tax owed on the same income.

*Remember:* Foreign tax withheld on dividends: usually claimed as a US foreign tax credit.

</details>

**Q5042** (§10.2 · FINRA section 3) — A resident of a high-tax state wants interest income that is exempt from state income tax but is willing to pay federal tax. Which of the following fits?

- A. Interest on Ginnie Mae pass-through certificates
- B. Interest on Treasury notes
- C. Interest on investment-grade corporate bonds
- D. Interest on bank certificates of deposit

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Interest on US Treasury securities is subject to federal income tax but exempt from state and local income tax.

*Remember:* Treasuries: federally taxable, state and local tax exempt.

</details>

**Q5043** (§10.2 · FINRA section 3) — Interest from which of the following is exempt from state income tax?
I. Treasury bills
II. Ginnie Mae certificates
III. Federal Home Loan Bank bonds
IV. Corporate commercial paper

- A. I and II
- B. II and IV
- C. I and III
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Treasuries and most federal agency debt, such as FHLB bonds, are state tax exempt. Mortgage-backed agency securities like Ginnie Mae, and corporate debt, are taxed at all levels.

*Remember:* Treasuries and most agencies (FHLB, Farm Credit) are state-exempt; GNMA/FNMA/FHLMC are not.

</details>

**Q5044** (§10.2 · FINRA section 3) — An investor buys a municipal bond in the secondary market at 92 and holds it to maturity at 100. The 8-point market discount is generally:

- A. Tax-exempt interest, like the coupon
- B. A long-term capital gain
- C. Taxed as ordinary interest income
- D. Not taxable because the bond is a municipal security

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Market discount on a bond bought below par in the secondary market is taxed as ordinary interest income when the bond is sold or matures. This applies even to munis.

*Remember:* Market discount (secondary purchase below par) = ordinary income at sale/maturity, even on munis.

</details>

**Q5045** (§10.2 · FINRA section 3) — An investor buys a municipal bond at 108 and holds it until it matures at par. For tax purposes:

- A. She must amortize the premium and has no deductible loss
- B. She reports an $80 capital loss per bond at maturity
- C. She deducts the $80 premium against ordinary income
- D. She may choose whether to amortize the premium

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Premium on tax-exempt munis must be amortized, reducing basis to par by maturity. The amortized premium is not deductible, so there is no loss.

*Remember:* Muni premium: amortization required, not deductible; no loss at maturity.

</details>

**Q5046** (§10.2 · FINRA section 3) — An investor holds a municipal zero-coupon bond originally issued at a deep discount. The annual accretion is:

- A. Taxable each year as phantom income
- B. A capital gain taxed only at maturity
- C. Tax-exempt interest that increases cost basis
- D. Taxable as ordinary income only at maturity

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** For municipal OID, the accretion is treated as tax-exempt interest. The investor's basis still rises each year, so there's no gain if held to maturity.

*Remember:* Muni OID accretion = tax-exempt interest; basis still accretes.

</details>

**Q5047** (§10.2 · FINRA section 3) — A buyer of a corporate bond pays the seller $40 of accrued interest at settlement. A few weeks later the buyer receives a $250 semiannual coupon. For tax purposes, the buyer reports interest income of:

- A. $40
- B. $210
- C. $250
- D. $290

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Accrued interest paid to the seller is deducted from the buyer's first interest payment. The seller reports the $40 as interest income, and the buyer reports $250 - $40 = $210.

1. Coupon received: $250
2. Less accrued interest paid: 250 - 40 = $210

*Remember:* Accrued interest paid by the buyer reduces the buyer's first interest payment for tax.

</details>

**Q5048** (§10.2 · FINRA section 3) — Which statement about interest on Series EE savings bonds is TRUE?

- A. It is taxed annually at the federal level as it accrues
- B. It is subject to state and local income tax
- C. It is exempt from federal tax in all cases
- D. It is tax-deferred until redemption and may be federally tax-free if used for qualified education expenses

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Savings bond interest is federally taxable but deferred until redemption, and it can be federally tax-free when used for qualified higher-education expenses, subject to income limits. Like other Treasuries, it is state tax exempt.

*Remember:* EE/I bonds: tax-deferred until redemption; possibly tax-free for education; state exempt.

</details>

**Q5049** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor buys stock with a trade date of March 3, 2025. What is the earliest trade date on which she can sell and receive long-term capital gain treatment?

- A. March 2, 2026
- B. March 3, 2026
- C. March 4, 2026
- D. September 4, 2025

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Long-term requires holding more than one year. The holding period begins the day after the purchase trade date, so a sale on March 3, 2026 is exactly one year (short-term); March 4, 2026 qualifies.

*Remember:* Long-term = held more than one year; holding period starts the day after trade date.

</details>

**Q5050** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor buys 100 shares at $40 and pays a $25 commission. She later sells them at $52 and pays a $30 commission. Her capital gain is:

- A. $1,200
- B. $1,175
- C. $1,255
- D. $1,145

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Cost basis is $4,000 + $25 = $4,025. Net sale proceeds are $5,200 - $30 = $5,170. Gain = $5,170 - $4,025 = $1,145.

1. Basis = 4,000 + 25 = 4,025
2. Net proceeds = 5,200 - 30 = 5,170
3. Gain = 5,170 - 4,025 = $1,145

*Remember:* Gain = net proceeds (less commission) - cost basis (plus commission).

</details>

**Q5051** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — A married investor filing separately has a net capital loss of $5,000 for the year and no other capital gains. She may:

- A. Deduct $3,000 against ordinary income and carry forward $2,000
- B. Deduct $1,500 against ordinary income and carry forward $3,500
- C. Deduct the full $5,000 against ordinary income
- D. Carry forward the loss for up to five years only

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The annual limit on net capital losses deducted against ordinary income is $3,000, or $1,500 for married filing separately. The remaining $3,500 carries forward indefinitely.

1. MFS limit = $1,500
2. Carryforward = 5,000 - 1,500 = $3,500

*Remember:* Net capital loss deduction: $3,000/yr ($1,500 married filing separately); rest carries forward indefinitely.

</details>

**Q5052** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor bought 100 shares of ABC at $50. On November 1 she sells them at $42 for an $800 loss, and on November 20 she buys 100 ABC at $44. Her cost basis in the new shares is:

- A. $4,400
- B. $4,200
- C. $5,000
- D. $5,200

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The repurchase within 30 days triggers the wash sale rule, disallowing the $800 loss. The disallowed loss is added to the new shares' basis: $4,400 + $800 = $5,200.

1. Disallowed loss = (50 - 42) x 100 = $800
2. New cost = 44 x 100 = $4,400
3. Basis = 4,400 + 800 = $5,200

*Remember:* Wash sale: disallowed loss is added to the cost basis of replacement shares.

</details>

**Q5053** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor sells shares of Alpha Motors at a loss. Which of the following purchases within 30 days would NOT cause the loss to be disallowed under the wash sale rule?

- A. Call options on Alpha Motors
- B. Bonds convertible into Alpha Motors common stock
- C. Shares of Beta Motors, a competitor in the same industry
- D. Alpha Motors common stock bought 25 days before the sale

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Buying a different company, even in the same industry, is not substantially identical and does not trigger the wash sale rule.

*Remember:* Wash sale covers the same stock, its options/warrants and convertibles, 30 days before or after.

</details>

**Q5054** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor bought three lots of 100 XYZ shares at $20, $35 and $50. XYZ is now $45 and he wants to sell 100 shares while minimizing his tax bill. He should:

- A. Let the IRS default apply, selling the $20 lot
- B. Use specific identification and sell the $50 lot
- C. Use specific identification and sell the $20 lot
- D. Use average cost, giving a $35 basis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Specific identification lets the investor choose the highest-cost lot. Selling the $50 lot at $45 produces a loss instead of a gain.

*Remember:* Specific identification is most flexible; sell the highest-cost lot to minimize gain.

</details>

**Q5055** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — A donor gives 100 shares she bought at $40 to her nephew when the stock is worth $25. The nephew later sells at $20. His deductible loss is:

- A. $2,000
- B. $500
- C. $1,500
- D. $0

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** When the value at the gift date is below the donor's basis, the recipient's basis for a loss is the fair market value at the gift. Loss = ($25 - $20) x 100 = $500.

1. Loss basis = FMV at gift = $25
2. Loss = (25 - 20) x 100 = $500

*Remember:* Gift at a loss: recipient's loss basis = FMV at gift date.

</details>

**Q5056** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — In 2026, a married couple wants to give cash to their son without filing a gift tax return. The most they can give him together is:

- A. $15,000
- B. $19,000
- C. $30,000
- D. $38,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The 2026 annual gift tax exclusion is $19,000 per donor per recipient, so a married couple can give $38,000 to one person without filing a gift tax return.

*Remember:* 2026 annual gift exclusion: $19,000 per recipient; $38,000 for a married couple.

</details>

**Q5057** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor inherits stock and sells it two months after the owner's death. The gain or loss is treated as:

- A. Short-term, because the heir held it less than a year
- B. Long-term, regardless of the holding period
- C. Long-term only if the deceased held it more than a year
- D. Not taxable, because inherited property is exempt

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Inherited securities automatically receive long-term treatment, with basis stepped up (or down) to the fair market value on the date of death.

*Remember:* Inherited securities: stepped-up basis to date-of-death value; automatically long-term.

</details>

**Q5058** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following are NOT taxable events for the investor when they occur?
I. Exchanging shares of one fund for another in the same fund family
II. Converting a convertible bond into common stock
III. Receiving a return-of-capital distribution
IV. Selling stock at a gain

- A. I and II
- B. II and III
- C. I and IV
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Converting a bond into the issuer's stock is not taxable, and return of capital is not taxed when received but reduces cost basis. Fund exchanges and sales at a gain are taxable.

*Remember:* Not taxable now: bond conversion, return of capital (reduces basis). Fund exchanges are taxable sales.

</details>

**Q5059** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor writes 1 XYZ call at 3, and the option expires unexercised seven months later. The tax result is:

- A. A $300 short-term capital gain
- B. A $300 long-term capital gain
- C. $300 of ordinary interest income
- D. No tax, because the option was never exercised

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A writer whose option expires has a capital gain equal to the premium, and it is short-term.

*Remember:* Expired written option = short-term capital gain equal to the premium.

</details>

**Q5060** (§10.4 · FINRA section 3) — This year an investor has a $50,000 salary, $3,000 of dividends, $5,000 of income from a limited partnership and an $8,000 loss from a different limited partnership. How much of the loss can she use this year?

- A. $8,000, against her salary
- B. $3,000, against her dividends
- C. $8,000, against her dividends and salary
- D. $5,000, against her passive income, carrying forward $3,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Passive losses can offset only passive income. The $8,000 LP loss offsets the $5,000 of LP income, and the remaining $3,000 carries forward.

1. Passive loss used = passive income = $5,000
2. Carryforward = 8,000 - 5,000 = $3,000

*Remember:* Passive losses offset only passive income; unused passive losses carry forward.

</details>

**Q5061** (§10.4 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following is classified as portfolio income?
I. Dividends
II. Rental income from a limited partnership
III. Capital gains
IV. Wages

- A. I and III
- B. I and II
- C. II and IV
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Portfolio income includes interest, dividends, capital gains and royalties. LP rental income is passive, and wages are earned income.

*Remember:* Portfolio = interest, dividends, capital gains, royalties. Passive = LPs. Earned = wages.

</details>

**Q5062** (§10.4 · FINRA section 3) — A high-income client who is subject to the alternative minimum tax asks about municipal bonds. The rep should point out that interest on which bonds may be a tax preference item?

- A. General obligation bonds
- B. Revenue bonds for a public water system
- C. Bonds issued by US territories
- D. Private activity bonds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Interest on private activity municipal bonds is an AMT preference item, so investors subject to AMT should be cautious about them.

*Remember:* Private activity muni interest is an AMT preference item.

</details>

**Q5063** (§10.4 · FINRA section 3) — A taxpayer owes $15,000 of federal income tax on $100,000 of taxable income, and his top bracket is 22%. His effective tax rate is:

- A. 7%
- B. 15%
- C. 22%
- D. 37%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The effective rate is total tax divided by total income: $15,000 / $100,000 = 15%. The 22% is his marginal rate on the next dollar.

1. 15,000 / 100,000 = 0.15
2. Effective rate = 15%

*Remember:* Effective rate = total tax / total income; marginal rate = rate on next dollar.

</details>

**Q5064** (§10.4 · FINRA section 3) — A customer sells stock at a gain with a trade date of December 31, 2026; the trade settles in January 2027. The gain is reported for tax year:

- A. 2027, because settlement occurs in 2027
- B. Either year, at the investor's election
- C. 2026, because the trade date governs
- D. 2027, but only if proceeds are withdrawn in January

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** For securities sales, the trade date determines the tax year. A December 31 trade counts for that year even though it settles in January.

*Remember:* Trade date, not settlement date, determines the tax year of a sale.

</details>

**Q5065** (§10.4 · FINRA section 3) — Under an asset-location strategy, which holding would be LEAST appropriate to place in a traditional IRA?

- A. Real estate investment trust shares
- B. A high-yield corporate bond fund
- C. A municipal bond fund
- D. Treasury STRIPS

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Muni interest is already tax-exempt, so holding it in a tax-deferred account wastes the benefit and turns tax-free income into taxable withdrawals. Munis belong in taxable accounts.

*Remember:* Asset location: tax-inefficient assets in tax-deferred accounts; munis in taxable accounts.

</details>

**Q9039** (§10.1 · FINRA section 3) — An investor buys shares of a US corporation 20 days before the ex-dividend date, collects the quarterly dividend, and sells the shares 15 days after the ex-date. How is the dividend taxed?

- A. At long-term capital gains rates, because it was paid by a US corporation
- B. As a short-term capital gain, because the stock was held for one year or less
- C. As ordinary income, because the holding period requirement was not met
- D. It is not taxed, because the stock was sold before the payable date

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Qualified dividend treatment requires holding the stock more than 60 days around the ex-dividend date. A 35-day holding period fails, so the dividend is non-qualified and taxed as ordinary income.

*Remember:* Qualified dividends: US corporation + held more than 60 days around the ex-date → 0/15/20% rates.

</details>

**Q9040** (§10.2 · FINRA section 3) — A New York resident wants interest that is exempt from both federal and New York State income tax. Which of the following bonds would provide it?
I. A New York City general obligation bond
II. A Puerto Rico general obligation bond
III. A US Treasury note
IV. An Ohio general obligation bond

- A. I and II
- B. I and III
- C. II and III
- D. I, II and III

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** In-state munis and US territory bonds are exempt from both federal and state tax. Treasury interest is federally taxable, and Ohio bond interest is taxable by New York.

*Remember:* Double tax-free: in-state munis and territory bonds (Puerto Rico, Guam, USVI) for any state's residents.

</details>

**Q9041** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor bought 100 shares of a REIT at $25. Over several years she received $3 per share of return-of-capital distributions. She now sells the shares at $30. What is her capital gain?

- A. $200
- B. $300
- C. $500
- D. $800

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Return of capital isn't taxed when received; it reduces cost basis to $22. Selling at $30 produces an $8 gain per share, or $800.

1. Adjusted basis = $25 − $3 = $22
2. Gain per share = $30 − $22 = $8
3. Total = $8 × 100 = $800

*Remember:* Return of capital reduces cost basis; gain = sale price − (cost − ROC).

</details>

**Q9042** (§10.3 · FINRA section 3) — On November 20, an investor buys 100 shares of DEF at $30, adding to 100 shares she bought years ago at $45. On December 10, she sells the original 100 shares at $30 to harvest a loss. What is the tax result?

- A. The $1,500 loss is allowed because the purchase came before the sale
- B. The $1,500 loss is disallowed and added to the cost basis of the November shares
- C. The $1,500 loss is allowed but must be carried forward to next year
- D. The $1,500 loss is disallowed permanently and can never be used

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The wash sale window covers 30 days before and after the sale. Buying DEF 20 days before the loss sale disallows the loss, which is added to the new shares' basis ($30 + $15 = $45 per share).

*Remember:* Wash sale: buying substantially identical securities 30 days before or after a loss sale disallows the loss.

</details>

## Chapter 11: The Primary Market

**Q101** (§11.2 · FINRA section 1) — In a firm commitment underwriting, the underwriters act as:

- A. Agents, earning a commission with no risk
- B. Principals, buying the issue from the issuer and bearing the risk of unsold shares
- C. Brokers for the issuer under an escrow arrangement
- D. Trustees under the Trust Indenture Act

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Firm commitment = underwriters buy the entire issue and resell it; unsold shares are their problem. Best efforts = agents; issuer bears the risk.

*Remember:* Firm commitment: underwriters act as principals, buy the whole issue, and bear the risk of unsold shares.

</details>

**Q102** (§11.4.1 · FINRA section 1) — During the cooling-off period, an underwriter may:

- A. Accept orders and payment from customers
- B. Distribute a preliminary prospectus and take indications of interest
- C. Send sales literature highlighting the offering
- D. Guarantee allocations to favored customers

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** During the cooling-off period (at least 20 days) the red herring may be distributed, tombstone ads run, and non-binding indications of interest collected. No sales, orders, money or sales literature.

*Remember:* Cooling-off period: red herring and indications of interest only; no sales, orders, or money.

</details>

**Q103** (§11.4.3 · FINRA section 1) — When the SEC declares a registration statement effective, it means the SEC:

- A. Has approved the security as a sound investment
- B. Has verified the accuracy of all statements in the prospectus
- C. Has found the registration statement complete
- D. Guarantees the offering price

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The SEC reviews for completeness only; it never approves or passes on the merits or accuracy. Saying the SEC 'approved' an issue is a violation.

*Remember:* SEC effectiveness means the filing is complete; the SEC never approves merits or verifies accuracy.

</details>

**Q104** (§11.4.4 · FINRA section 1) — Under Rule 506(b) of Regulation D, an issuer may sell to:

- A. No more than 35 accredited investors
- B. An unlimited number of accredited investors and up to 35 non-accredited investors
- C. Only qualified institutional buyers
- D. An unlimited number of any investors if the offering is under $5 million

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** 506(b): unlimited accredited investors plus up to 35 sophisticated non-accredited investors, unlimited dollar amount, no general solicitation.

*Remember:* Rule 506(b): unlimited accredited investors plus up to 35 non-accredited; no general solicitation.

</details>

**Q105** (§11.4.4 · FINRA section 1) — An individual qualifies as an accredited investor with a net worth of at least _____ (excluding primary residence) or income of at least _____ in each of the last two years.

- A. $500,000; $100,000
- B. $1 million; $200,000 ($300,000 joint)
- C. $5 million; $250,000
- D. $1 million including primary residence; $150,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Net worth over $1 million excluding the primary residence, or income over $200,000 individually / $300,000 with a spouse.

*Remember:* Accredited individual: net worth over $1M excluding home, or income over $200K ($300K joint) for two years.

</details>

**Q106** (§11.5 · FINRA section 1) — A corporate officer wishes to sell shares under Rule 144. The company has 50 million shares outstanding; average weekly volume over the last four weeks was 600,000 shares. The maximum she may sell in a 90-day period is:

- A. 500,000 shares
- B. 600,000 shares
- C. 50,000 shares
- D. There is no limit for officers

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The limit is the greater of 1% of outstanding (500,000) or the four-week average weekly volume (600,000) — so 600,000 shares.

1. 1% of 50,000,000 = 500,000
2. 4-week average weekly volume = 600,000
3. Greater of the two = 600,000 shares

*Remember:* Rule 144 volume limit per 90 days: greater of 1% of outstanding shares or 4-week average weekly volume.

</details>

**Q107** (§11.4.3 · FINRA section 4) — Under FINRA Rule 5130, which of the following may purchase shares of a new issue of common stock (IPO)?

- A. A registered representative at another broker-dealer
- B. The spouse of a FINRA member firm employee
- C. A retired schoolteacher with no industry connections
- D. A finder in the offering

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Restricted persons include FINRA members, their employees, immediate family members, finders and certain fiduciaries. The schoolteacher is not restricted.

*Remember:* Rule 5130 bars restricted persons (BD employees, their immediate family, finders) from buying IPOs.

</details>

**Q108** (§11.4.4 · FINRA section 1) — Which of the following securities is EXEMPT from registration under the Securities Act of 1933?

- A. A variable annuity
- B. A corporate debenture
- C. A municipal general obligation bond
- D. A closed-end fund share

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Exempt securities include US government and municipal securities, bank securities, commercial paper ≤ 270 days, non-profit issues, and insurance/fixed annuities. Variable annuities are securities that must be registered.

*Remember:* Exempt from '33 Act registration: US government, municipal, bank securities, commercial paper ≤270 days, nonprofits.

</details>

**Q109** (§11.3 · FINRA section 1) — In a secondary offering, the proceeds of the sale go to:

- A. The issuing corporation
- B. The selling shareholders
- C. The underwriting syndicate
- D. The exchange

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A secondary offering is a registered sale of existing shares by current holders (insiders, VCs). The issuer receives nothing. In a primary offering the issuer receives the proceeds.

*Remember:* Secondary offering proceeds go to the selling shareholders; primary offering proceeds go to the issuer.

</details>

**Q110** (§11.4.4 · FINRA section 1) — Rule 144A permits the resale of unregistered securities to:

- A. Accredited individual investors
- B. Qualified institutional buyers with at least $100 million in securities
- C. Residents of the issuer's state
- D. Any investor after a six-month holding period

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Rule 144A creates a liquid institutional market: restricted securities can be freely resold to QIBs (institutions managing $100 million+ in securities).

*Remember:* Rule 144A allows restricted securities to be resold to QIBs owning/managing $100 million+ in securities.

</details>

**Q6001** (§11.1 · FINRA section 1) — An issuer sells 5 million shares at a public offering price of $20. The issuer receives $18.60 per share. The manager's fee is $0.20 and the underwriting fee is $0.40. What is the selling concession per share?

- A. $0.60
- B. $0.80
- C. $1.20
- D. $1.40

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The total spread is $20.00 - $18.60 = $1.40. Subtracting the manager's fee ($0.20) and the underwriting fee ($0.40) leaves an $0.80 selling concession.

1. Spread = $20.00 - $18.60 = $1.40
2. Fees = $0.20 + $0.40 = $0.60
3. Concession = $1.40 - $0.60 = $0.80

*Remember:* Selling concession = total spread - manager's fee - underwriting fee.

</details>

**Q6002** (§11.1 · FINRA section 1) — A broker-dealer joins an offering as a selling group member rather than as a syndicate member. Which statement about the firm is accurate?

- A. It shares liability for any unsold shares in the offering
- B. It earns the underwriting fee on every share in the deal
- C. It runs the books and manages the stabilizing bid
- D. It acts as agent and earns only the selling concession

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Selling group members help distribute the issue but take no underwriting risk. They act as agents and are paid only the selling concession on the shares they sell.

*Remember:* Selling group: no underwriting risk, earns only the selling concession.

</details>

**Q6003** (§11.1 · FINRA section 1) — Which document binds the members of an underwriting syndicate to one another and sets out each member's share of the risk?

- A. The agreement among underwriters
- B. The underwriting agreement with the issuer
- C. The registration statement on Form S-1
- D. The letter of intent filed with FINRA

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The agreement among underwriters (the syndicate agreement) governs the relationship among the syndicate members. The underwriting agreement is the contract between the issuer and the syndicate.

*Remember:* Syndicate agreement = among underwriters; underwriting agreement = issuer and underwriters.

</details>

**Q6004** (§11.1 · FINRA section 1) — In reviewing a registration statement for a new stock issue, the SEC's role is to:

- A. Judge whether the stock is a suitable investment for the public
- B. Guarantee the accuracy of the issuer's financial statements
- C. Set the public offering price with the managing underwriter
- D. Check that the required disclosures are complete

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The SEC reviews registration statements for completeness of disclosure. It never rules on the merits of a security, guarantees accuracy, or sets prices.

*Remember:* SEC reviews for completeness only; never approves merits or accuracy.

</details>

**Q6005** (§11.2 · FINRA section 1) — A small company hires an underwriter on an all-or-none basis to raise $8 million. By the end of the offering period only $6.5 million has been sold. What happens?

- A. The underwriter buys the remaining $1.5 million for its own account
- B. The offering is cancelled and investors' escrowed funds are returned
- C. The issuer keeps the $6.5 million and the deal closes early
- D. The underwriter extends the offering by 90 days automatically

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** In an all-or-none best efforts deal, the entire amount must be sold or the offering is cancelled. Investor funds held in escrow are returned.

*Remember:* All-or-none: whole issue sells or deal is cancelled; funds held in escrow.

</details>

**Q6006** (§11.2 · FINRA section 1) — A company offers new shares to its existing shareholders through a rights offering and arranges for an investment bank to buy any shares the shareholders do not subscribe for. This arrangement is called a:

- A. Mini-max underwriting
- B. Best efforts underwriting
- C. Shelf takedown
- D. Standby underwriting

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A standby underwriting is a form of firm commitment used with rights offerings: the underwriter stands by to purchase any shares left unsubscribed.

*Remember:* Standby underwriting = firm commitment to buy unsubscribed shares in a rights offering.

</details>

**Q6007** (§11.2 · FINRA section 1) — During the distribution of a new issue priced at $25, the managing underwriter may place a stabilizing bid at:

- A. $25.25, to signal strong demand
- B. $25.50, as long as it is disclosed
- C. Any price set by the syndicate
- D. $24.90, below the offering price

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A stabilizing bid may be entered at or below the public offering price, never above it. It must be disclosed in the prospectus.

*Remember:* Stabilizing bid: at or below the POP, never above; disclosed in prospectus.

</details>

**Q6008** (§11.2 · FINRA section 1) — Which of the following statements about a firm commitment underwriting are TRUE?
I. The underwriter acts as principal
II. The issuer bears the risk of unsold shares
III. The underwriter is compensated through the spread
IV. It is commonly used for DPP offerings by small issuers

- A. I and III
- B. I and IV
- C. II and III
- D. II and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** In a firm commitment, the underwriter buys the whole issue as principal and earns the spread; the underwriter (not the issuer) bears the unsold-share risk. Small, risky issuers and DPPs typically use best efforts.

*Remember:* Firm commitment: underwriter is principal, bears risk, earns the spread.

</details>

**Q6009** (§11.2 · FINRA section 1) — A city plans to issue general obligation bonds and invites underwriting syndicates to submit sealed bids, awarding the deal to the lowest interest cost. This is an example of a:

- A. Negotiated underwriting
- B. Competitive bid underwriting
- C. Best efforts mini-max offering
- D. Private placement under Reg D

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** In a competitive bid, syndicates submit sealed bids and the issuer chooses the one with the lowest cost. This method is typical for municipal G.O. bonds.

*Remember:* Competitive bid = sealed bids, lowest cost wins; common for muni G.O. bonds.

</details>

**Q6010** (§11.3 · FINRA section 1) — A public company sells 3 million newly issued shares while two of its founders sell 1 million of their existing shares in the same registered offering. This is a:

- A. Split (combined) offering
- B. Secondary market transaction
- C. Direct listing
- D. Shelf registration

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** An offering that is partly primary (new shares from the issuer) and partly secondary (existing holders selling) is a split, or combined, offering.

*Remember:* Split offering = part primary (issuer's new shares) + part secondary (existing holders).

</details>

**Q6011** (§11.3 · FINRA section 1) — A seasoned issuer files a shelf registration under Rule 415. For how long may it sell securities off the shelf under that registration?

- A. Up to 12 months
- B. Up to 2 years
- C. Up to 3 years
- D. Up to 5 years

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Rule 415 shelf registration lets a seasoned issuer register once and sell over up to 3 years, filing a prospectus supplement with each takedown.

*Remember:* Shelf registration (Rule 415): sell off the shelf for up to 3 years.

</details>

**Q6012** (§11.3 · FINRA section 1) — A private technology company lists its existing shares on a stock exchange without hiring an underwriter or issuing new shares. Which statement is correct?

- A. It is a direct listing and raises no new capital
- B. The company raises new capital at the opening trade
- C. It is a SPAC merger requiring a blank check vehicle
- D. It is a follow-on offering by a seasoned issuer

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** In a direct listing, existing shares begin trading on an exchange without an underwritten offering. No new shares are sold, so the company raises no new capital.

*Remember:* Direct listing: existing shares list on exchange; no underwriter, no new capital.

</details>

**Q6013** (§11.3 · FINRA section 1) — All of the following are primary offerings EXCEPT:

- A. A company's initial public offering of new shares
- B. A follow-on offering of newly created shares by a public company
- C. A rights offering of new shares to existing shareholders
- D. A registered sale of shares owned by a venture capital fund

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** In a primary offering the issuer sells new shares and receives the proceeds. When an existing holder such as a VC fund sells its own shares, it is a secondary offering and the holder gets the money.

*Remember:* Primary = issuer sells new shares, gets proceeds; secondary = existing holders sell.

</details>

**Q6014** (§11.4.1 · FINRA section 1) — Before a company files its registration statement, a registered rep calls clients to ask whether they would like to reserve shares in the upcoming IPO. This is:

- A. Permitted if the rep uses a tombstone script
- B. Permitted because no money changes hands
- C. Prohibited gun-jumping in the pre-filing period
- D. Permitted for accredited investors only

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** No offers or solicitations of interest are allowed before the registration statement is filed. Soliciting reservations in the pre-filing period is gun-jumping.

*Remember:* Pre-filing period: no offers, no sales, no solicitation of interest (gun-jumping).

</details>

**Q6015** (§11.4.1 · FINRA section 1) — During the cooling-off period, a customer asks his rep to put him down for 500 shares of the upcoming IPO and offers to send a check now. The rep should:

- A. Record a non-binding indication of interest and accept no money
- B. Accept the check and hold it until the effective date
- C. Enter a binding order at the expected price range
- D. Refuse to discuss the offering until it becomes effective

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** During cooling-off, a rep may take non-binding indications of interest but may not accept orders or money. Sales can occur only once the registration is effective.

*Remember:* Cooling-off: indications of interest allowed; no orders, no sales, no money.

</details>

**Q6016** (§11.4.1 · FINRA section 1) — The SEC sends an issuer a deficiency letter on day 12 of the cooling-off period. Which statement is correct?

- A. The 20-day cooling-off clock is paused until the issues are fixed
- B. The registration becomes effective automatically on day 20
- C. Sales may begin while the issuer answers the letter
- D. The issuer must withdraw and refile under Regulation A

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A deficiency letter stops the 20-day cooling-off clock until the issuer corrects the problems. The registration does not become effective in the meantime.

*Remember:* Cooling-off lasts at least 20 days; a deficiency letter pauses the clock.

</details>

**Q6017** (§11.4.2 · FINRA section 1) — Which item will NOT appear in a preliminary prospectus (red herring)?

- A. Risk factors
- B. Audited financial statements
- C. The intended use of proceeds
- D. The final public offering price

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The red herring contains everything in the final prospectus except the final offering price and the effective date. It may show an estimated price range.

*Remember:* Red herring lacks the final offering price and effective date.

</details>

**Q6018** (§11.4.2 · FINRA section 1) — An issuer is completing an IPO that is registered with the SEC and will be sold in 12 states. The issuer files with the states at the same time it files with the SEC. This state registration method is:

- A. Qualification
- B. Coordination
- C. Notice filing
- D. Exemption by rule

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Registration by coordination means filing with the state at the same time as with the SEC. It is the typical blue-sky method for IPOs.

*Remember:* Blue-sky: coordination (with SEC filing), qualification (no SEC filing), notice filing (covered securities).

</details>

**Q6019** (§11.4.2 · FINRA section 1) — During the cooling-off period, a rep sends a client the red herring with key passages highlighted and a one-page sales flyer attached. This is:

- A. Acceptable if a principal approves the flyer
- B. Acceptable as long as no order is taken
- C. Prohibited only if the flyer mentions a price
- D. Prohibited; the red herring may not be marked up or sent with sales literature

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The preliminary prospectus may not be marked up, highlighted, or accompanied by sales literature during the cooling-off period.

*Remember:* Red herring: no highlighting, no markings, no accompanying sales literature.

</details>

**Q6020** (§11.4.2 · FINRA section 1) — When is the final public offering price of an IPO typically set?

- A. When the registration statement is first filed
- B. At the start of the cooling-off period
- C. On the evening before the effective date
- D. Thirty days after trading begins

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The underwriter builds the book from indications of interest and sets the final price on the evening before the registration becomes effective.

*Remember:* IPO price is set the evening before the effective date, based on the book.

</details>

**Q6021** (§11.4.3 · FINRA section 1) — An IPO of a company whose shares will be listed on the Nasdaq becomes effective. For how many days after the effective date must dealers deliver a prospectus on aftermarket trades?

- A. 25 days
- B. 40 days
- C. 90 days
- D. 0 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** For an IPO of a security that will be exchange-listed (including Nasdaq), dealers must deliver a prospectus in the aftermarket for 25 days.

*Remember:* Aftermarket prospectus: 90 days non-listed IPO, 25 days listed IPO, 40 days non-listed follow-on.

</details>

**Q6022** (§11.4.3 · FINRA section 1) — A rep tells a customer, "The SEC has reviewed and approved this offering, so you know it's a sound investment." The rep's statement is:

- A. Acceptable once the registration is effective
- B. Acceptable if the issuer is a seasoned company
- C. A violation only if the stock later declines
- D. A violation, because the SEC does not approve securities

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The SEC does not approve, endorse, or pass on the merits of any security. Telling a customer an issue is SEC-approved is a violation regardless of how the stock performs.

*Remember:* SEC never approves or endorses a security; saying so is a violation.

</details>

**Q6023** (§11.4.3 · FINRA section 3) — Under FINRA Rule 5130, which of the following persons may buy shares of a common stock IPO for his own account?

- A. The sister of a registered rep at a FINRA member firm
- B. A portfolio manager at an insurance company
- C. The owner of a broker-dealer
- D. The adult son of a bank teller who works at a bank with no broker-dealer

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Rule 5130 restricts FINRA member employees and their immediate family, portfolio managers buying for themselves, and broker-dealer owners. A bank teller's son has no restricted-person connection.

*Remember:* Rule 5130: no IPO common stock to restricted persons (industry insiders and immediate family).

</details>

**Q6024** (§11.4.3 · FINRA section 3) — Before selling a new issue of common stock to an account, a member firm must obtain a representation that the account is eligible. That representation must have been obtained within the prior:

- A. 30 days
- B. 90 days
- C. 12 months
- D. 36 months

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Under Rule 5130, the firm must obtain a written or electronic eligibility representation from the beneficial owner within the prior 12 months before selling a new issue to the account.

*Remember:* Rule 5130 eligibility representation: obtained within the prior 12 months.

</details>

**Q6025** (§11.4.3 · FINRA section 1) — An underwriter gives allocations of a hot IPO to the CFO of a company in exchange for the expectation that the CFO's company will hire the underwriter for its next bond deal. This practice is called:

- A. Spinning, prohibited by FINRA Rule 5131
- B. Free-riding, permitted with disclosure
- C. Stabilization, allowed under Regulation M
- D. Interpositioning, prohibited by Rule 5310

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Allocating hot IPO shares to executives to win investment banking business is spinning, which FINRA Rule 5131 prohibits.

*Remember:* Spinning (Rule 5131): IPO shares to executives to win banking business is prohibited.

</details>

**Q6026** (§11.4.4 · FINRA section 1) — Which of the following securities must be registered under the Securities Act of 1933?

- A. Commercial paper maturing in 180 days
- B. A variable annuity sold by an insurance company
- C. Stock issued by a national bank
- D. Bonds issued by a nonprofit university

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Variable annuities are securities and must be registered. Short-term commercial paper (270 days or less), bank stock, and nonprofit securities are exempt.

*Remember:* Fixed annuities exempt; variable annuities are securities and must register.

</details>

**Q6027** (§11.4.4 · FINRA section 1) — A married couple has a joint income of $260,000 in each of the last two years and a net worth of $1.4 million, of which $600,000 is equity in their home. Are they accredited investors?

- A. Yes, because their income exceeds $200,000
- B. Yes, because their net worth exceeds $1 million
- C. No, because they fail both the income and net worth tests
- D. No, because joint investors cannot be accredited

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The joint income test is over $300,000, which they miss. Net worth excluding the primary residence is $800,000, which is under $1 million. They fail both tests.

1. Joint income $260,000 < $300,000 needed
2. Net worth $1,400,000 - $600,000 home = $800,000
3. $800,000 < $1,000,000, so neither test is met

*Remember:* Accredited: net worth over $1M ex-home, or income over $200K ($300K joint).

</details>

**Q6028** (§11.4.4 · FINRA section 1) — An issuer sells securities under Regulation D. By when must it file Form D with the SEC?

- A. Before any offers are made
- B. Within 15 days after the first sale
- C. Within 90 days after the last sale
- D. Only if it sells to non-accredited investors

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A Regulation D issuer files Form D with the SEC within 15 days of the first sale of securities in the offering.

*Remember:* Reg D: Form D filed within 15 days after the first sale.

</details>

**Q6029** (§11.4.4 · FINRA section 1) — A company incorporated and doing business in Ohio sells stock only to Ohio residents under Rule 147. A buyer later moves to Indiana. How long after the sale must pass before he may resell to a non-Ohio resident?

- A. 30 days
- B. 90 days
- C. 6 months
- D. 1 year

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Under Rule 147 (intrastate offerings), purchasers may not resell to non-residents of the state for 6 months.

*Remember:* Rule 147 intrastate: sell only to in-state residents; no resale out of state for 6 months.

</details>

**Q6030** (§11.4.4 · FINRA section 1) — A growing company wants to raise $50 million from the public in the next 12 months using an abbreviated SEC filing and an offering circular instead of a full registration. Which exemption fits?

- A. Regulation A, Tier 2
- B. Regulation A, Tier 1
- C. Regulation Crowdfunding
- D. Rule 504 of Regulation D

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Regulation A Tier 2 permits offerings of up to $75 million in 12 months using an offering circular. Tier 1 is capped at $20 million.

*Remember:* Reg A: Tier 1 up to $20M, Tier 2 up to $75M per 12 months; uses an offering circular.

</details>

**Q6031** (§11.5 · FINRA section 1) — A director of a company with 30 million shares outstanding wants to sell stock under Rule 144. Weekly trading volume over the last four weeks was 220,000, 260,000, 280,000, and 240,000 shares. What is the most she may sell in the next 90 days?

- A. 250,000 shares
- B. 280,000 shares
- C. 300,000 shares
- D. 1,000,000 shares

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** 1% of 30 million is 300,000. The four-week average weekly volume is 250,000. She may sell the greater of the two: 300,000 shares.

1. 1% x 30,000,000 = 300,000
2. Average = (220k + 260k + 280k + 240k) / 4 = 250,000
3. Greater of 300,000 and 250,000 = 300,000

*Remember:* Rule 144 volume = greater of 1% of outstanding or 4-week average weekly volume.

</details>

**Q6032** (§11.5 · FINRA section 1) — An affiliate plans to sell 4,000 shares of her company's stock at $15 per share under Rule 144. Must she file Form 144?

- A. No, because she is selling fewer than 5,000 shares
- B. Yes, because the sale exceeds $50,000
- C. No, because affiliates never file Form 144
- D. Yes, but only after the sale settles

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Form 144 is not needed only if the sale is 5,000 shares or fewer AND $50,000 or less. 4,000 x $15 = $60,000, which exceeds $50,000, so she must file at or before the sale.

1. 4,000 shares x $15 = $60,000
2. $60,000 > $50,000 limit
3. Exemption fails, so Form 144 is required

*Remember:* Form 144 exemption requires both 5,000 shares or fewer and $50,000 or less.

</details>

**Q6033** (§11.5 · FINRA section 1) — An investor bought restricted stock in a private placement from an SEC-reporting company and fully paid for it on March 1. She is not an affiliate. When is the earliest she may sell under Rule 144?

- A. September 1 of the same year
- B. June 1 of the same year
- C. March 1 of the following year
- D. Immediately, because she is not an affiliate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Restricted securities of an SEC-reporting issuer must be held 6 months from the date they were fully paid for. March 1 plus 6 months is September 1.

*Remember:* Restricted stock holding period: 6 months (reporting issuer) from full payment; 1 year otherwise.

</details>

**Q6034** (§11.6 · FINRA section 1) — A broker-dealer will underwrite a stock offering and plans to use more than 5% of the proceeds to repay a loan the issuer owes the broker-dealer. Under FINRA Rule 5121, the firm must:

- A. Withdraw from the offering entirely
- B. Have a qualified independent underwriter participate and disclose the conflict
- C. Limit its compensation to the selling concession
- D. Sell the offering only to accredited investors

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Rule 5121 requires a qualified independent underwriter to participate and the conflict to be prominently disclosed when a member has a conflict such as receiving over 5% of proceeds.

*Remember:* Rule 5121 conflicts: qualified independent underwriter plus prominent disclosure.

</details>

**Q6035** (§11.6 · FINRA section 3) — A broker-dealer wants to sell an unlisted $2 stock to a customer. Which customer is considered established, so the firm does NOT need a written suitability statement for this penny stock purchase?

- A. A customer whose account was opened 6 months ago
- B. A new customer with a large account balance
- C. A customer who has made 3 prior penny stock purchases
- D. A customer who signed the risk disclosure document

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Established customers, those whose accounts have been open more than 1 year or who have made 3 or more prior penny stock purchases, are exempt from the suitability-statement requirement.

*Remember:* Established penny stock customer: account open over 1 year or 3+ prior penny stock purchases.

</details>

**Q9051** (§11.4.3 · FINRA section 1) — A company whose common stock already trades over the counter (not listed on any exchange) completes a registered follow-on offering. For how many days after the effective date must dealers deliver a prospectus on aftermarket trades?

- A. 0 days
- B. 25 days
- C. 40 days
- D. 90 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Aftermarket prospectus delivery for a follow-on (non-IPO) offering of a non-listed security lasts 40 days. Listed follow-ons have no aftermarket requirement.

*Remember:* Aftermarket delivery: 90 days non-listed IPO, 25 listed IPO, 40 non-listed follow-on, 0 listed follow-on.

</details>

**Q9052** (§11.2 · FINRA section 1) — An underwriting syndicate plans to sell 8 million shares in an IPO. Demand is strong and the underwriting agreement includes a standard over-allotment (green shoe) option. What is the maximum total number of shares the syndicate may sell?

- A. 8,800,000
- B. 9,200,000
- C. 9,600,000
- D. 12,000,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The green shoe lets underwriters sell up to 15% more shares than planned: 8,000,000 x 15% = 1,200,000 extra, for 9,200,000 total.

1. Over-allotment = 8,000,000 x 15% = 1,200,000
2. Maximum = 8,000,000 + 1,200,000 = 9,200,000

*Remember:* Green shoe = up to 15% over-allotment; maximum = planned shares x 1.15.

</details>

**Q9053** (§11.1 · FINRA section 1) — An IPO is priced at $24.00 with a $1.50 spread: manager's fee $0.25, underwriting fee $0.40 and selling concession $0.85. A syndicate member that is not the manager sells 20,000 shares to its own customers. How much does it earn on those shares?

- A. $8,000
- B. $17,000
- C. $30,000
- D. $25,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A syndicate member that sells shares earns the underwriting fee plus the selling concession ($0.40 + $0.85 = $1.25 per share). $1.25 x 20,000 = $25,000; only the manager also earns the manager's fee.

1. Per share = $0.40 underwriting fee + $0.85 concession = $1.25
2. $1.25 x 20,000 shares = $25,000
