*Remember:* Syndicate member earnings per share = underwriting fee + selling concession (manager's fee goes only to the manager).

</details>

**Q9054** (§11.4.4 · FINRA section 1) — All of the following are exempt from registration under the Securities Act of 1933 EXCEPT:

- A. Common stock issued by a bank holding company
- B. Commercial paper maturing in 180 days
- C. Bonds issued by a charitable nonprofit organization
- D. A certificate of deposit issued by a national bank

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Securities issued by banks are exempt, but bank holding companies are ordinary corporations whose stock must be registered. Short-term paper of 270 days or less, nonprofit securities and bank-issued securities are exempt.

*Remember:* Bank securities are exempt; bank holding company securities are not.

</details>

## Chapter 12: The Secondary Market

**Q111** (§12.1 · FINRA section 3) — A broker-dealer sells a bond to a customer from its own inventory. The firm must disclose on the confirmation that it acted as:

- A. An agent and show the commission
- B. A principal
- C. A market maker for the issuer
- D. A fiduciary

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Selling from inventory is a principal (dealer) transaction compensated by a markup. The capacity must be disclosed; a firm can't charge both a markup and a commission on one trade.

*Remember:* Selling from inventory is a principal trade with a markup; capacity must be disclosed on confirmation.

</details>

**Q112** (§12.3 · FINRA section 1) — A market maker quotes XYZ at 25.10 – 25.20. A customer entering a market order to buy will pay:

- A. $25.10
- B. $25.20
- C. $25.15
- D. The last sale price

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Customers buy at the ask (offer) and sell at the bid. The spread is the market maker's profit.

*Remember:* Customers buy at the ask (offer) and sell at the bid.

</details>

**Q113** (§12.4 · FINRA section 1) — Exchange-listed securities traded over-the-counter by non-member broker-dealers make up the:

- A. First market
- B. Second market
- C. Third market
- D. Fourth market

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** First = listed on exchange; second = unlisted OTC; third = listed securities traded OTC; fourth = institution-to-institution via ECNs.

*Remember:* Third market: exchange-listed securities traded over-the-counter.

</details>

**Q114** (§12.6.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor who is long 500 shares of ABC at $60 wants to limit losses if the stock falls. The appropriate order is a:

- A. Buy stop at 65
- B. Sell limit at 55
- C. Sell stop at 55
- D. Buy limit at 55

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A sell stop is placed below the market; if ABC trades at or below 55 it becomes a market order to sell. Sell limits go above the market.

*Remember:* Sell stops are placed below market to limit losses or protect gains on long positions.

</details>

**Q115** (§12.6.4 · FINRA section 3) — The primary disadvantage of a sell stop-limit order compared with a sell stop order is that:

- A. It may be executed at a much lower price than the stop
- B. It may never be executed if the stock gaps below the limit
- C. It requires a margin account
- D. It cannot be entered as a GTC order

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Once triggered, a stop-limit becomes a limit order; if the price falls through the limit the order sits unfilled, leaving the investor unprotected.

*Remember:* Stop-limit orders become limit orders once triggered; a price gap can leave them unfilled.

</details>

**Q116** (§12.6.5 · FINRA section 3) — Which orders are placed BELOW the current market price?

- A. Buy limit and sell stop
- B. Buy stop and sell limit
- C. Buy limit and buy stop
- D. Sell stop and sell limit

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** BLiSS: Buy Limits and Sell Stops go below the market. SLoBS: Sell Limits and Buy Stops go above.

*Remember:* Buy limits and sell stops go below market; sell limits and buy stops go above.

</details>

**Q117** (§12.6.6 · FINRA section 3) — A customer tells a rep to 'buy 1,000 shares of XYZ today at the best price you can get.' This order is:

- A. Discretionary and requires written authorization
- B. A not-held order that does not require written authorization
- C. Prohibited without principal approval
- D. A market-on-close order

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The customer specified security, action and amount; leaving only time and price to the rep is not discretion. It's a not-held order, good for the day.

*Remember:* Choosing only time and price is not discretion; it's a not-held order, valid only that day.

</details>

**Q118** (§12.5 · FINRA section 4) — The Securities Exchange Act of 1934 did all of the following EXCEPT:

- A. Create the SEC
- B. Require registration of broker-dealers
- C. Require registration of new securities issues with a prospectus
- D. Give the Federal Reserve authority over margin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Registration of new issues and the prospectus requirement come from the Securities Act of 1933. The 1934 Act regulates the secondary market, created the SEC, and covers BDs, exchanges, margin and anti-fraud.

*Remember:* The 1933 Act governs new-issue registration and prospectuses; the 1934 Act regulates secondary trading.

</details>

**Q119** (§12.4.1 · FINRA section 1) — A market-wide circuit breaker halts trading for the remainder of the day when the S&P 500 falls:

- A. 7%
- B. 10%
- C. 13%
- D. 20%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Level 1 (7%) and Level 2 (13%) trigger 15-minute halts (before 3:25 pm); Level 3 (20%) closes the market for the day.

*Remember:* Market-wide circuit breakers: 7% and 13% S&P drops halt 15 minutes; 20% closes trading for the day.

</details>

**Q120** (§12.6.7 · FINRA section 3) — A broker-dealer receives payment for order flow from a market maker. The firm:

- A. May route orders to that market maker regardless of price
- B. Must still obtain best execution for its customers and disclose the arrangement
- C. Must refuse the payment
- D. May accept the payment only for institutional orders

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Payment for order flow is legal but must be disclosed, and it never overrides the duty of best execution.

*Remember:* Payment for order flow is legal if disclosed; it never overrides the duty of best execution.

</details>

**Q6036** (§12.1 · FINRA section 3) — A broker-dealer executes a customer's stock purchase by buying from another dealer and charges the customer a $40 commission plus a $0.25 per share markup. This is:

- A. Permitted if both amounts are disclosed on the confirmation
- B. Permitted for riskless principal trades only
- C. Prohibited only if the total exceeds 5%
- D. Prohibited, because a firm cannot act as broker and dealer on the same trade

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A firm acts as either agent (commission) or principal (markup) on a single trade, never both. Charging both is prohibited regardless of disclosure.

*Remember:* Never charge a commission and a markup on the same trade.

</details>

**Q6037** (§12.1 · FINRA section 3) — FINRA's 5% markup policy applies to which of the following transactions?

- A. A customer's purchase of mutual fund shares
- B. A customer's purchase in an IPO at the offering price
- C. A dealer's sale of a listed stock from inventory to a customer
- D. A customer's purchase of a new bond issue sold by prospectus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The 5% policy is a guideline for secondary market trades such as a dealer's principal sale from inventory. It does not apply to mutual funds or new issues sold at a prospectus price.

*Remember:* 5% policy: secondary market trades only; not new issues or mutual funds.

</details>

**Q6038** (§12.1 · FINRA section 3) — A customer sells $10,000 of stock and immediately uses the proceeds to buy a different stock through the same firm, which acts as principal on both sides. For markup purposes, the firm must:

- A. Treat the two trades as one transaction
- B. Charge up to 5% on each side separately
- C. Charge no markup on the purchase
- D. Act as agent on the purchase side

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** In a proceeds transaction, the sale and the purchase are treated as a single transaction, so the combined markdown and markup must be fair as if it were one trade.

*Remember:* Proceeds transaction: sale and purchase are treated as one trade for markup purposes.

</details>

**Q6039** (§12.1 · FINRA section 3) — A broker-dealer receives a customer's order to buy 500 shares, then buys the shares for its own account and immediately resells them to the customer at a markup. This is a:

- A. Riskless principal transaction
- B. Agency cross
- C. Proceeds transaction
- D. Market-on-close order

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** When a dealer buys a security to fill a customer order it already holds and resells at a markup, it is a riskless principal trade.

*Remember:* Riskless principal: dealer buys to fill an order in hand, resells at a markup.

</details>

**Q6040** (§12.1 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following must appear on the confirmation of a trade in which a firm acted as agent for the customer?

- A. The firm's inventory cost of the security
- B. The amount of the commission charged
- C. The markup over the prevailing market price
- D. The name of the market maker's trader

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** In an agency trade the firm discloses that it acted as agent and the amount of the commission. The other party's name is available on request.

*Remember:* Agency confirm: capacity and commission amount; contra party available on request.

</details>

**Q6041** (§12.2 · FINRA section 1) — An introducing broker differs from a clearing firm in that the introducing broker:

- A. Holds customer funds and securities
- B. Extends margin credit to customers
- C. Sends confirmations and settles every trade
- D. Handles the customer relationship but does not hold customer assets

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** An introducing broker takes orders and handles the relationship but does not hold customer assets. The clearing (carrying) firm holds assets, settles trades, sends confirms and extends margin.

*Remember:* Introducing broker: customer relationship only; clearing firm holds assets and settles.

</details>

**Q6042** (§12.2 · FINRA section 1) — Which entity holds securities in book-entry form for the securities industry?

- A. National Securities Clearing Corporation
- B. Depository Trust Company
- C. Options Clearing Corporation
- D. Securities Investor Protection Corporation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The Depository Trust Company (DTC) holds securities in book-entry form. NSCC nets and guarantees trades; together they form DTCC.

*Remember:* DTC holds securities in book-entry form; NSCC nets and guarantees trades.

</details>

**Q6043** (§12.2 · FINRA section 4) — Which of the following is a self-regulatory organization that writes rules but does NOT enforce them?

- A. FINRA
- B. The New York Stock Exchange
- C. Cboe
- D. The MSRB

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The MSRB makes municipal securities rules but has no enforcement power; FINRA and bank regulators enforce them. FINRA and the exchanges both make and enforce rules for their members.

*Remember:* MSRB writes muni rules but does not enforce them.

</details>

**Q6044** (§12.3 · FINRA section 3) — A stock is quoted 32.40 - 32.48, 12 x 7. Which statement is correct?

- A. A customer could buy up to 1,200 shares at $32.40
- B. A customer could sell up to 700 shares at $32.48
- C. A customer could buy up to 700 shares at $32.48
- D. The spread is $0.80

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The ask is $32.48 for 7 round lots (700 shares), so a customer can buy 700 shares at $32.48. The bid is $32.40 for 1,200 shares; the spread is $0.08.

*Remember:* Quote size is in round lots: first number = bid size, second = ask size.

</details>

**Q6045** (§12.3 · FINRA section 3) — Three market makers quote a stock: A at 18.10 - 18.18, B at 18.12 - 18.20, and C at 18.08 - 18.16. What is the inside market (NBBO)?

- A. 18.12 - 18.16
- B. 18.08 - 18.20
- C. 18.10 - 18.18
- D. 18.12 - 18.20

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The inside market is the highest bid (18.12 from B) and the lowest ask (18.16 from C).

*Remember:* NBBO = highest bid and lowest ask across all market makers.

</details>

**Q6046** (§12.3 · FINRA section 3) — A market maker displays a firm quote for 300 shares, then refuses to buy 300 shares from another dealer at that bid. This is called:

- A. Front-running
- B. A workout quote
- C. A subject quote
- D. Backing away

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Refusing to honor a firm quote for the displayed size is backing away, which is a violation.

*Remember:* Backing away: refusing to honor a firm quote; a violation.

</details>

**Q6047** (§12.3 · FINRA section 3) — A dealer is asked for a price on a large block of a thinly traded stock and says, "I can probably do it around 12, but I'll need a few hours." This is a:

- A. Firm quote
- B. Nominal quote
- C. Inside quote
- D. Workout quote

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A workout quote is an approximate price given for a large or illiquid order that the dealer needs time to work out.

*Remember:* Workout quote: approximate price for a large or illiquid order needing time.

</details>

**Q6048** (§12.4 · FINRA section 1) — A pension fund sells a 400,000-share block of a listed stock directly to a mutual fund through an electronic network, without using a broker-dealer. This trade occurs in the:

- A. First market
- B. Second market
- C. Third market
- D. Fourth market

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Institution-to-institution trading without a broker-dealer, typically through ECNs or ATSs, is the fourth market.

*Remember:* Fourth market = institutions trading directly with each other via ECNs/ATSs.

</details>

**Q6049** (§12.4 · FINRA section 1) — Shares of a small company that is not listed on any exchange trade through dealers on OTC Markets. This trading is part of the:

- A. First market
- B. Second market
- C. Third market
- D. Fourth market

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Unlisted securities traded over the counter make up the second market, a negotiated dealer market.

*Remember:* Second market = unlisted securities traded OTC.

</details>

**Q6050** (§12.4.1 · FINRA section 1) — At 2:00 pm ET, the S&P 500 is down 7.2% from the prior close. What happens?

- A. Trading halts for 15 minutes
- B. Trading halts for the rest of the day
- C. Trading continues with no halt
- D. Only short selling is halted

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A 7% decline triggers a Level 1 market-wide circuit breaker and a 15-minute halt, as long as it occurs before 3:25 pm.

*Remember:* Circuit breakers: 7% and 13% = 15-minute halt (before 3:25 pm); 20% = closed for the day.

</details>

**Q6051** (§12.4.1 · FINRA section 1) — Which of the following is NOT a function of an NYSE designated market maker (DMM)?

- A. Maintaining a fair and orderly market in assigned stocks
- B. Setting the opening and closing prices
- C. Acting as agent for orders left on its book
- D. Guaranteeing that the stock price will not decline

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** DMMs maintain a fair and orderly market, run the opening and closing auctions, and handle orders on their book. They supply liquidity in imbalances but do not guarantee prices.

*Remember:* DMM: fair and orderly market, opening/closing auctions, dealer and agent roles.

</details>

**Q6052** (§12.4.1 · FINRA section 1) — A stock trading on the NYSE moves sharply outside its price band, and trading in that single stock pauses for 5 minutes. Which mechanism caused the pause?

- A. Limit Up-Limit Down (LULD)
- B. A Level 1 market-wide circuit breaker
- C. The Rule 201 short sale circuit breaker
- D. A regulatory news halt

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The Limit Up-Limit Down mechanism pauses trading in an individual stock for 5 minutes when its price moves outside a set band.

*Remember:* LULD: single-stock 5-minute pause when price moves outside its band.

</details>

**Q6053** (§12.4.2 · FINRA section 1) — A retail investor's trading app shows only the best bid and best offer for a Nasdaq stock. A professional trader's screen shows every market maker's quote and size. These are, respectively:

- A. Level 1 and Level 2
- B. Level 2 and Level 3
- C. Level 1 and Level 3
- D. Level 3 and Level 2

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Level 1 shows the inside bid and ask; Level 2 shows all market makers' quotes and sizes. Level 3 lets market makers enter and update quotes.

*Remember:* Nasdaq Level 1 = inside quote; Level 2 = all MM quotes; Level 3 = MMs enter quotes.

</details>

**Q6054** (§12.4.3 · FINRA section 1) — Which tier of OTC Markets Group is home to companies that meet financial standards and is considered the highest tier?

- A. Pink Open Market
- B. OTCQB
- C. OTCQX
- D. Grey Market

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** OTCQX is the top tier, for companies meeting financial standards. OTCQB is the venture market, Pink accepts almost anyone, and the Grey Market has no quotes.

*Remember:* OTC tiers: OTCQX (best), OTCQB (venture, current reporting), Pink (anything), Grey (no quotes).

</details>

**Q6055** (§12.4.3 · FINRA section 1) — A dealer sells a customer a corporate bond over the counter. To which system is the trade reported?

- A. RTRS
- B. TRACE
- C. The ORF
- D. EDGAR

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Corporate bond trades are reported to FINRA's TRACE. Municipal trades go to the MSRB's RTRS, and OTC equity trades to the ORF.

*Remember:* Corporate bonds report to TRACE; munis to RTRS/EMMA; OTC equities to the ORF.

</details>

**Q6056** (§12.4.4 · FINRA section 1) — An investor buys a call option on the Cboe and later closes the position by selling an identical option on NYSE Arca. This is possible because:

- A. The Cboe and NYSE Arca share a single order book
- B. Listed options are fungible since the OCC issues and guarantees them
- C. Options can be traded only on the exchange where they were opened
- D. The DMM transfers the position between exchanges

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The Options Clearing Corporation issues and guarantees every listed option, so identical contracts are fungible across exchanges.

*Remember:* OCC issues and guarantees all listed options, making them fungible across exchanges.

</details>

**Q6057** (§12.5 · FINRA section 4) — Under the Securities Exchange Act of 1934, how is the SEC structured?

- A. Seven commissioners elected by Congress for 7-year terms
- B. Five commissioners appointed by the President for staggered 5-year terms
- C. Five commissioners chosen by FINRA's board for 3-year terms
- D. Three commissioners appointed by the Federal Reserve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The SEC has five commissioners appointed by the President for staggered 5-year terms, with no more than three from one political party.

*Remember:* SEC: 5 presidential appointees, staggered 5-year terms, max 3 from one party.

</details>

**Q6058** (§12.5 · FINRA section 1) — An investor acquires 6% of a public company's stock and plans to push for changes on the board. What must the investor file, and when?

- A. Form 4 within 2 business days
- B. Schedule 13G within 5 business days
- C. Schedule 13D within 5 business days
- D. Form 144 at the time of purchase

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Anyone acquiring more than 5% of a company's equity with intent to influence control must file Schedule 13D within 5 business days. Passive holders file 13G.

*Remember:* Schedule 13D: over 5% with intent to influence, filed within 5 business days.

</details>

**Q6059** (§12.5 · FINRA section 1) — A public company's CEO resigns unexpectedly. The company must report this material event to the SEC on Form 8-K within:

- A. 2 business days
- B. 4 business days
- C. 10 business days
- D. 30 calendar days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Form 8-K reports material events and must generally be filed within 4 business days of the event.

*Remember:* 8-K: material events, within 4 business days. 10-K annual, 10-Q quarterly.

</details>

**Q6060** (§12.5 · FINRA section 3) — A stock closed yesterday at $50 and drops to $44.50 this morning. Under Regulation SHO Rule 201, what restriction applies?

- A. Short sales are banned entirely for 5 trading days
- B. Short sales may not be executed at or below the national best bid for the rest of today and tomorrow
- C. All trading in the stock is halted for 15 minutes
- D. No restriction applies until the stock falls 20%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A drop of 10% or more from the prior close ($45 or lower here) triggers Rule 201: short sales may not be executed at or below the best bid for the rest of the day and the next day.

1. Decline = $50.00 - $44.50 = $5.50
2. $5.50 / $50.00 = 11%
3. 11% >= 10%, so Rule 201 is triggered

*Remember:* Rule 201: 10% drop from prior close restricts shorts at or below best bid, today and tomorrow.

</details>

**Q6061** (§12.6.1 · FINRA section 3) — A customer wants to sell 200 shares of a thinly traded stock immediately and enters a market order. Which risk does the customer face?

- A. The order may execute at a price well below the last quote
- B. The order may not execute at all
- C. The order will be cancelled at the end of the day
- D. The order will be reduced on the ex-dividend date

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A market order guarantees execution but not price. In an illiquid or fast market, the fill can be far from the last quote (slippage).

*Remember:* Market order: guaranteed execution, not price.

</details>

**Q6062** (§12.6.2 · FINRA section 3) — A customer enters an order to buy 300 shares of ABC at 42 limit when ABC is trading at 43.50. Which statement is true?

- A. The order will fill immediately at 43.50
- B. The order may fill only at $42 or lower
- C. The order becomes a market order once ABC trades at 42
- D. The order is placed above the market

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A buy limit sets the maximum price: it executes only at $42 or better (lower). It is placed below the current market and may never fill.

*Remember:* Buy limit: placed below market; fills at the limit or lower.

</details>

**Q6063** (§12.6.2 · FINRA section 3) — A customer has a sell limit order for 100 shares at 60 on the book. Several trades print at exactly $60 but the customer's order is not filled. The most likely reason is:

- A. Sell limit orders execute only below the limit price
- B. Other orders at $60 had time priority ahead of the customer's
- C. The order was converted to a stop order
- D. Limit orders are filled only at the close

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Limit orders are filled by price, then time priority. Orders entered earlier at the same price fill first, so the customer's order may be stuck behind them.

*Remember:* Limit order priority: price first, then time.

</details>

**Q6064** (§12.6.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer enters a sell stop order at 30. The subsequent trades are 30.40, 30.15, 29.90, 29.70, 30.05. At what price is the order executed?

- A. 30.15
- B. 29.90
- C. 29.70
- D. 30.05

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The stop is triggered by the trade at 29.90 (at or below 30). It becomes a market order and fills at the next trade, 29.70.

*Remember:* Stop order: triggered at or through the stop price, then fills at the next trade.

</details>

**Q6065** (§12.6.3 · FINRA section 3) — An investor sold short 500 shares of XYZ at $40. XYZ now trades at $38. To limit losses if the stock rises, she should enter a:

- A. Sell stop at $42
- B. Buy limit at $36
- C. Sell limit at $42
- D. Buy stop at $42

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A short seller loses money if the stock rises. A buy stop placed above the market buys the shares back once XYZ trades at $42, capping the loss.

*Remember:* Buy stop above the market protects a short position.

</details>

**Q6066** (§12.6.3 · FINRA section 3) — A trader believes XYZ, now at $50, will rally sharply if it breaks through resistance at $53. She wants to buy only if it breaks out. Which order should she use?

- A. Buy stop at 53
- B. Buy limit at 53
- C. Sell stop at 53
- D. Market order to buy now

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A buy stop above the market is used to enter a position on a breakout: it becomes a market order once XYZ trades at or above $53.

*Remember:* Buy stop above market: protect a short or buy a breakout.

</details>

**Q6067** (§12.6.4 · FINRA section 3) — A customer long 200 shares of DEF at $60 enters a sell 55 stop, 54 limit order. Bad news hits and DEF opens at $51, then trades between $50 and $52 all day. What happens to the order?

- A. It executes at $51 as a market order
- B. It executes at $55
- C. It is never triggered because the stock gapped past the stop
- D. It is triggered but does not execute because the stock never trades at $54 or higher

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The opening trade at $51 is through the $55 stop, so the order triggers and becomes a sell limit at $54. Since DEF never trades at $54 or higher, it does not execute.

*Remember:* Stop-limit becomes a limit order when triggered; it may not fill if the price gaps.

</details>

**Q6068** (§12.6.5 · FINRA section 3) — Which order type guarantees neither the execution price nor that the order will be executed at a price near the trigger price?

- A. Market order
- B. Buy limit order
- C. Sell stop order
- D. Sell stop-limit order

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A stop order becomes a market order once triggered, so neither the fill price nor a fill near the stop is assured. Market orders guarantee execution; limits and stop-limits guarantee price if filled.

*Remember:* Stop orders guarantee neither price nor a fill near the stop.

</details>

**Q6069** (§12.6.6 · FINRA section 3) — A customer has an open sell stop order at 45 on a stock that goes ex-dividend for a $0.60 dividend. Unless the order is marked DNR, the stop price on the ex-date becomes:

- A. $45.60
- B. $45.00
- C. $44.40
- D. $44.60

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Open orders below the market (buy limits and sell stops) are reduced by the dividend on the ex-date: $45.00 - $0.60 = $44.40.

1. Sell stop is below the market, so it is reduced
2. $45.00 - $0.60 = $44.40

*Remember:* Ex-date: orders below the market reduced by the dividend unless marked DNR.

</details>

**Q6070** (§12.6.6 · FINRA section 3) — A customer enters an order to buy 5,000 shares that must be filled in its entirety immediately or cancelled. This order is:

- A. Immediate-or-cancel
- B. All-or-none
- C. Fill-or-kill
- D. Good-till-cancelled

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A fill-or-kill order must be executed in full immediately or cancelled entirely.

*Remember:* FOK = all, immediately, or cancel. IOC = partial ok. AON = all, no time pressure.

</details>

**Q6071** (§12.6.6 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following orders would require prior written discretionary authorization from the customer?
I. "Buy some shares of a good tech stock for me."
II. "Buy 300 shares of ABC; use your judgment on timing today."
III. "Sell however many shares of XYZ you think best."
IV. "Sell 200 shares of XYZ at the best price you can get."

- A. I and II
- B. II and IV
- C. I and III
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Discretion over the security (I) or the amount (III) requires written authorization. Leaving only time or price to the rep (II, IV) is a not-held order.

*Remember:* Discretion = rep picks security, action, or amount; time/price only is not discretion.

</details>

**Q6072** (§12.6.7 · FINRA section 3) — A firm holds a customer's limit order to buy 1,000 shares at $25.00. The firm then buys 500 shares for its own account at $24.98 without filling the customer's order. This violates:

- A. The Manning rule (FINRA Rule 5320)
- B. Regulation SHO
- C. The 5% markup policy
- D. The Rule 606 disclosure requirement

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A firm holding a customer limit order may not trade for its own account at that price or better without first filling the customer. Buying at $24.98 is better than $25.00.

*Remember:* Manning rule: no trading ahead of customer limit orders at that price or better.

</details>

**Q6073** (§12.6.7 · FINRA section 3) — A rep learns her firm is about to execute a large customer buy order in DEF and buys DEF for her own account first. This is:

- A. Interpositioning
- B. Front-running
- C. Backing away
- D. A wash trade

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Trading ahead of a customer's pending order to profit from its expected price impact is front-running, prohibited by FINRA Rule 5270.

*Remember:* Front-running: trading ahead of a pending customer order or research report (Rule 5270).

</details>

**Q6074** (§12.6.7 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following must be recorded on an order ticket?
I. Whether the order is solicited or unsolicited
II. The customer's annual income
III. The time the order was received
IV. The customer's investment objectives

- A. I and II
- B. I and III
- C. II and IV
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Order tickets record the account, security, side, size, order type, solicited/unsolicited status, discretion, time received and executed, and rep ID. Income and objectives belong on the new account form.

*Remember:* Order ticket: solicited/unsolicited, discretion, times, terms, rep ID; kept 3 years.

</details>

**Q6075** (§12.6.7 · FINRA section 3) — A firm routes a customer's order through a second broker-dealer before reaching the market maker with the best price, so the customer gets a worse net price. This practice is called:

- A. Payment for order flow
- B. Riskless principal trading
- C. Trade-through protection
- D. Interpositioning

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Interpositioning means inserting an unnecessary third party between the firm and the best market, worsening the customer's price. It is prohibited.

*Remember:* Interpositioning: unnecessary middleman between firm and best market; prohibited.

</details>

**Q9055** (§12.4.1 · FINRA section 1) — At 3:40 pm ET, the S&P 500 is down 13.5% from the prior day's close; no market-wide halt has occurred yet today. What happens?

- A. Trading halts for 15 minutes
- B. Trading halts for the rest of the day
- C. Trading continues, because Level 1 and Level 2 halts do not apply after 3:25 pm
- D. Each stock pauses for 5 minutes under Limit Up-Limit Down

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Level 1 (7%) and Level 2 (13%) circuit breakers trigger 15-minute halts only before 3:25 pm. After 3:25 pm only a Level 3 (20%) decline halts trading, and that halt lasts for the rest of the day.

*Remember:* Market-wide breakers: 7% and 13% = 15-minute halt (not after 3:25 pm); 20% = closed for the day.

</details>

**Q9057** (§12.6.2 · FINRA section 3) — A customer sold short 300 shares of XYZ at $45. XYZ now trades at $41. She wants to close the position automatically and lock in her profit if XYZ falls to $36. Which order should she enter?

- A. Buy limit at 36
- B. Buy stop at 36
- C. Sell stop at 36
- D. Sell limit at 36

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Closing a short requires a buy. To buy at a price below the current market ($36 or better), she uses a buy limit order, which is placed below the market.

*Remember:* Buy limits go below the market; a short seller takes profit with a buy limit.

</details>

**Q9058** (§12.6.6 · FINRA section 3) — An institutional customer wants to buy 10,000 shares of a thinly traded stock at 18 limit. She does not want a partial fill, but she is willing to wait during the day until the whole quantity is available. Which order qualifier fits her instructions?

- A. Fill-or-kill
- B. Immediate-or-cancel
- C. Not held
- D. All-or-none

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** An all-or-none order must be filled in its entirety, but unlike fill-or-kill it does not need to execute immediately.

*Remember:* AON = entire order, may wait; FOK = entire order immediately or cancel.

</details>

**Q9059** (§12.6.4 · FINRA section 3) — A customer enters 'Buy 200 ABC at 55 stop, 55.50 limit' when ABC is trading at $53. The next trades are 54.80, 55.10, 55.75, 55.40 and 55.90. At what price is the order executed?

- A. $55.00
- B. $55.10
- C. $55.40
- D. $55.75

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The 55.10 trade (at or above 55) triggers the stop and creates a buy limit at 55.50. The next trade, 55.75, is above the limit, so the first possible fill is at 55.40.

1. 55.10 >= 55 stop: order triggered, becomes buy limit at 55.50
2. 55.75 > 55.50: no fill
3. 55.40 <= 55.50: executed at $55.40

*Remember:* Stop-limit: trigger at or through the stop, then execute only at the limit or better.

</details>

## Chapter 13: Brokerage Accounts

**Q121** (§13.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following is NOT required to open a cash account?

- A. The customer's date of birth
- B. The customer's Social Security number
- C. The customer's signature
- D. A principal's approval

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A customer signature isn't required for a cash account (it is for margin and options accounts). Name, address, DOB, TIN, rep name and principal approval are required.

*Remember:* Cash accounts need no customer signature; margin and options accounts require signed agreements.

</details>

**Q122** (§13.3.2 · FINRA section 3) — In a joint account registered as tenants in common, when one owner dies, that owner's share:

- A. Passes automatically to the surviving owner
- B. Passes to the deceased's estate
- C. Is divided equally among all surviving owners
- D. Is frozen for one year

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** TIC: the deceased's (possibly unequal) share goes to the estate. JTWROS: it passes to the survivor(s).

*Remember:* Tenants in common: a deceased owner's share goes to their estate, not the surviving owners.

</details>

**Q123** (§13.3.5 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following is TRUE of an UTMA account?

- A. There may be multiple custodians for one minor
- B. Gifts to the account are revocable until the minor reaches majority
- C. The account uses the minor's Social Security number and the minor is the beneficial owner
- D. The custodian may trade on margin to maximize growth

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** One custodian, one minor; gifts are irrevocable; the minor is the beneficial owner and taxpayer; no margin; the custodian is a fiduciary.

*Remember:* UTMA/UGMA: one custodian, one minor, minor's SSN, irrevocable gifts, no margin.

</details>

**Q124** (§13.3.4 · FINRA section 3) — Before a registered representative may exercise discretion over which securities to buy in a customer's account, the firm must have:

- A. An oral authorization from the customer
- B. Written authorization from the customer and principal approval of the account
- C. A trusted contact person on file
- D. A signed options agreement

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Discretion over security, action or amount requires written trading authorization and principal approval, plus frequent principal review of the account.

*Remember:* Discretion over asset, action or amount requires written customer authorization plus principal approval.

</details>

**Q125** (§13.4.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer buys $3,000 of stock in a new margin account. The required deposit is:

- A. $1,500
- B. $2,000
- C. $3,000
- D. $750

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Reg T is 50% ($1,500), but FINRA's $2,000 minimum initial equity applies when the purchase exceeds $2,000. If the purchase were under $2,000, the customer would pay in full.

1. Reg T: 50% x $3,000 = $1,500
2. FINRA minimum equity = $2,000
3. Required deposit = greater of the two = $2,000

*Remember:* Initial margin deposit = greater of 50% Reg T or $2,000, but never more than the purchase price.

</details>

**Q126** (§13.4.5 · FINRA section 3) — A long margin account has a market value of $16,000 and a debit balance of $13,000. At the FINRA minimum maintenance requirement, the maintenance call is:

- A. $0
- B. $1,000
- C. $3,000
- D. $4,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Equity = $3,000. Required = 25% × $16,000 = $4,000. Call = $1,000.

1. Equity = $16,000 - $13,000 = $3,000
2. Required = 25% x $16,000 = $4,000
3. Call = $4,000 - $3,000 = $1,000

*Remember:* Long maintenance call = (25% x market value) - (market value - debit balance).

</details>

**Q127** (§13.4.2 · FINRA section 3) — The part of the margin agreement that allows the broker-dealer to pledge the customer's securities to a bank as collateral for the loan is the:

- A. Credit agreement
- B. Hypothecation agreement
- C. Loan consent form
- D. Risk disclosure statement

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The hypothecation agreement lets the firm pledge (rehypothecate) up to 140% of the debit balance. The optional loan consent allows lending the shares to other customers for short sales.

*Remember:* Hypothecation agreement lets the firm pledge customer margin securities as loan collateral.

</details>

**Q128** (§13.5 · FINRA section 3) — A customer's options account was approved on June 1 but the signed options agreement has not been returned by June 20. The customer may:

- A. Continue trading normally
- B. Open new positions only with principal approval
- C. Only close existing positions
- D. Trade only covered calls

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The options agreement must be returned within 15 days of approval; otherwise the account is limited to closing transactions.

*Remember:* Signed options agreement due within 15 days of approval or the account is closing-only.

</details>

**Q129** (§13.6.1 · FINRA section 3) — SIPC protects customers against:

- A. Market losses on securities
- B. Losses from unsuitable recommendations
- C. Loss of cash and securities if the broker-dealer fails
- D. Losses in commodity futures accounts

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** SIPC covers missing customer assets when a member firm becomes insolvent — up to $500,000 per separate customer, including $250,000 cash. It never covers market losses.

*Remember:* SIPC replaces missing customer cash and securities when a broker-dealer fails; never market losses.

</details>

**Q130** (§13.6.1 · FINRA section 3) — A customer has an individual account with $400,000 in securities and $300,000 in cash at a failed broker-dealer. SIPC coverage is:

- A. $500,000
- B. $650,000
- C. $700,000
- D. $250,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** SIPC covers up to $500,000 per separate customer in total, of which no more than $250,000 may be cash. The $400,000 in securities is fully covered and the first $100,000 of cash fills the remaining room, for a total of $500,000. The other $200,000 of cash becomes a general creditor claim against the firm.

1. Securities: $400,000 covered
2. Remaining room: $500,000 - $400,000 = $100,000 of cash
3. Total = $500,000; leftover $200,000 cash is a general creditor claim

*Remember:* SIPC covers up to $500,000 per separate customer, with cash capped at $250,000.

</details>

**Q131** (§13.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — A broker-dealer must file a Suspicious Activity Report with FinCEN for suspicious transactions involving at least:

- A. $3,000
- B. $5,000
- C. $10,000
- D. $25,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** SARs are filed within 30 days for suspicious activity of $5,000 or more; the customer must not be told. CTRs are filed for cash transactions over $10,000.

*Remember:* Broker-dealers file a SAR for suspicious activity of $5,000 or more, within 30 days.

</details>

**Q132** (§13.6.2 · FINRA section 4) — FINRA's business continuity plan rule requires each member firm to:

- A. Keep its BCP confidential from customers
- B. Disclose a summary of the plan to customers at account opening and on its website
- C. Update the plan every five years
- D. Designate a single emergency contact at FINRA

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Firms must review the BCP annually, have senior management approve it, give customers a written summary at account opening (and on request/website), and designate two emergency contacts.

*Remember:* BCP: annual review, senior management approval, customer summary at opening and online, two emergency contacts.

</details>

**Q7001** (§13.1.1 · FINRA section 3) — A customer holds a few mutual fund positions but made no trades, deposits or withdrawals for several months. At a minimum, how often must the firm send her an account statement?

- A. Monthly
- B. Quarterly
- C. Semiannually
- D. Annually

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Statements are required at least once each calendar quarter for any account with a balance or activity. Monthly statements are typical only when there is activity.

*Remember:* Account statements: at least quarterly; monthly is customary when the account has activity.

</details>

**Q7002** (§13.1.1 · FINRA section 3) — A customer leaving for a two-month trip overseas asks that her account mail not be sent while she is away. The firm may:

- A. Hold the mail for that limited period based on her written request
- B. Forward her statements to the rep's home address until she returns
- C. Stop generating statements for the account until she returns
- D. Hold the mail only if she grants the rep a full power of attorney

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A firm may hold a customer's mail for a limited time on the customer's written instruction. Statements still must be produced, and they may never be redirected to the rep.

*Remember:* Mail may be held briefly on the customer's written request; never send customer mail to the rep.

</details>

**Q7003** (§13.1.2 · FINRA section 3) — Under SEC Rule 10b-10, a customer's trade confirmation must be sent:

- A. Within 5 business days after settlement
- B. With the next monthly account statement
- C. Within 30 days of the trade date
- D. At or before completion of the transaction

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Rule 10b-10 requires the confirmation at or before completion of the transaction, which is the settlement date.

*Remember:* Confirmations go out at or before completion of the transaction (settlement).

</details>

**Q7004** (§13.1.2 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following is NOT a required item on a customer trade confirmation?

- A. Whether the firm acted as agent or principal
- B. Whether the firm receives payment for order flow
- C. The trade date and the security's price
- D. The customer's stated investment objective

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Confirmations disclose trade details such as date, price, quantity, capacity and payment for order flow. The customer's investment objective belongs on the new account form, not the confirm.

*Remember:* Confirms show trade date, security, quantity, price, capacity, compensation and payment for order flow.

</details>

**Q7005** (§13.1.3 · FINRA section 4) — Under FINRA rules, which of the following would be treated as a customer complaint?

- A. A customer's angry phone call to the branch about a commission
- B. A customer's email to her rep alleging an unauthorized trade
- C. A customer's spoken remark at a seminar about high fund fees
- D. A rep's internal memo noting that a client seemed unhappy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A complaint is a written statement, including electronic messages, by or for a customer alleging a grievance. An email alleging an unauthorized trade qualifies.

*Remember:* Complaint = written (including electronic) grievance from or for a customer.

</details>

**Q7006** (§13.1.3 · FINRA section 3) — Which statement about FINRA arbitration of a customer's dispute with a broker-dealer is TRUE?

- A. The award is final and binding, with very limited grounds for appeal
- B. Either party may appeal the panel's award to the SEC for a new hearing
- C. A claim may be filed at any time regardless of when the event occurred
- D. Class action claims must be resolved through FINRA arbitration

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Arbitration awards are final and binding with very narrow appeal rights. Claims must be filed within 6 years, and class actions are excluded.

*Remember:* Arbitration: binding, very limited appeal, 6-year eligibility window, class actions excluded.

</details>

**Q7007** (§13.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — A new customer lists only a post office box as her address on the new account form. The firm should:

- A. Obtain a residential or business street address
- B. Use the rep's branch office address instead
- C. Open the account using the P.O. box as listed
- D. Open the account as a numbered account

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Customer identification rules require a residential or business street address; a P.O. box alone is not acceptable.

*Remember:* CIP requires a street address; a P.O. box alone is not acceptable.

</details>

**Q7008** (§13.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — An employee of another FINRA member firm wants to open a brokerage account at your firm. Before opening it, your firm must:

- A. Obtain approval from FINRA's Department of Enforcement
- B. Notify the employing member firm in writing
- C. Accept the account only on a discretionary basis
- D. Refuse the account, since such accounts are prohibited

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Accounts for associated persons of other members require prior written notice to the employing firm, which may request duplicate confirmations and statements.

*Remember:* Employee of another member: notify the employer in writing first; employer may request duplicates.

</details>

**Q7009** (§13.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — After the account is opened, a broker-dealer must furnish each customer with a copy of the account record for review at least every:

- A. 12 months
- B. 24 months
- C. 36 months
- D. 60 months

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Firms send the account record at opening and at least every 36 months thereafter so the customer can correct information.

*Remember:* Account record copy: at opening and at least every 36 months.

</details>

**Q7010** (§13.2.2 · FINRA section 3) — Which statement about a customer's trusted contact person is TRUE?

- A. The customer must name one before the account can be opened
- B. The trusted contact may place trades in the customer's account
- C. The trusted contact must be an immediate family member
- D. The firm must try to obtain one, but the customer may decline

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** FINRA Rule 4512 requires a reasonable attempt to obtain a trusted contact (age 18+) for possible exploitation or diminished-capacity concerns; the customer may refuse.

*Remember:* Trusted contact: firm must ask; customer may decline; contact has no trading authority.

</details>

**Q7011** (§13.2.2 · FINRA section 3) — A new customer refuses to disclose her annual income or net worth. Which action is appropriate?

- A. Decline to open the account until she provides it
- B. Open the account and accept her unsolicited orders
- C. Estimate her income and make recommendations
- D. Open it only as a cash account with a co-signer

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Financial profile information is sought but optional. Without it the account may still be opened, but the rep lacks a basis for recommendations, so unsolicited trading is appropriate.

*Remember:* Customer may refuse financial info; open the account, but recommendations lack a reasonable basis.

</details>

**Q7012** (§13.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — A wealthy customer wants her account designated only by a number to keep her identity private from branch staff. This is permitted if:

- A. The firm keeps a signed statement attesting to her ownership
- B. A principal approves each trade in the account
- C. She waives SIPC protection on the account
- D. The account is opened as a discretionary account

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A numbered account is allowed as long as the firm holds a written, signed statement from the customer attesting to ownership.

*Remember:* Numbered accounts are allowed with a signed statement of ownership on file.

</details>

**Q7013** (§13.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer wires funds from a newly opened account through several offshore accounts in different names to obscure where the money came from. Which money-laundering stage does this illustrate?

- A. Placement
- B. Structuring
- C. Integration
- D. Layering

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Layering uses a series of complex transfers to hide the trail of illicit funds after they enter the financial system.

*Remember:* Money laundering stages: placement, layering, integration.

</details>

**Q7014** (§13.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following are required elements of a broker-dealer's anti-money laundering program?
I. A designated AML compliance officer
II. Independent testing of the program
III. Notifying a customer when a SAR is filed about him
IV. Ongoing employee training

- A. I and III only
- B. II and IV only
- C. I, II and IV
- D. I, II, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** An AML program needs written policies, a designated AML officer, independent testing and ongoing training. Customers must never be told a SAR has been filed.

*Remember:* AML program: policies, AML officer, independent testing, training; never tip off a SAR subject.

</details>

**Q7015** (§13.3.1 · FINRA section 3) — An individual account owner who had filed a transfer-on-death (TOD) designation naming his daughter dies. The account assets:

- A. Pass directly to his daughter without going through probate
- B. Go to his estate and are distributed under his will
- C. Are frozen until his executor names a new beneficiary
- D. Are split equally among all of his legal heirs

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A TOD designation passes the account directly to the named beneficiary at death, bypassing probate.

*Remember:* Individual account with TOD passes directly to beneficiary; without TOD it goes to the estate.

</details>

**Q7016** (§13.3.2 · FINRA section 3) — In a JTWROS account owned by a married couple, the wife asks for a $5,000 withdrawal check made payable to her alone. The firm should:

- A. Make the check payable to both owners or the account title
- B. Issue the check to her, since either tenant may withdraw
- C. Require a full power of attorney from her husband first
- D. Refuse the withdrawal until the account is re-registered

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Either joint owner may trade, but checks and distributions must be payable to all owners (or the account title), not one owner alone.

*Remember:* Joint accounts: any owner can trade; checks must be payable to all owners.

</details>

**Q7017** (§13.3.2 · FINRA section 3) — Two business partners want to own a brokerage account 60% and 40% respectively. Which registration permits this?

- A. Joint tenants with right of survivorship
- B. Tenants in common
- C. Transfer on death individual account
- D. Uniform Transfers to Minors Act

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Tenants in common allows unequal ownership interests; JTWROS ownership is equal and undivided.

*Remember:* TIC allows unequal shares; JTWROS is equal and undivided.

</details>

**Q7018** (§13.3.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer gives her son limited trading authorization over her account. The son may:

- A. Enter buy and sell orders but not withdraw assets
- B. Withdraw cash but not place trades
- C. Trade and withdraw assets to his own account
- D. Only view statements and confirmations

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Limited (trading) authorization allows the third party to trade only. Withdrawals require full power of attorney.

*Remember:* Limited POA = trade only; full POA = trade plus withdraw.

</details>

**Q7019** (§13.3.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer granted his daughter a durable power of attorney over his brokerage account. The POA terminates when the customer:

- A. Becomes mentally incapacitated
- B. Turns age 75
- C. Dies
- D. Moves to another state

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A durable POA survives the principal's incapacity, but every POA ends at death; the executor then controls the estate's assets.

*Remember:* Durable POA survives incapacity; all POAs end at death.

</details>

**Q7020** (§13.3.4 · FINRA section 3) — A customer calls her rep and says, "Buy 500 shares of XYZ today, but use your judgment on the timing and price." Which is TRUE?

- A. Written discretionary authority is required before entry
- B. A principal must approve a power of attorney first
- C. The order must be held until the customer names a price
- D. No written discretionary authority is needed for this order

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Choosing only time or price, when the customer has set the security, action and quantity, is not discretion. Such a not-held order is good for that day.

*Remember:* Discretion = choosing asset, action or amount; time/price alone needs no written authority.

</details>

**Q7021** (§13.3.5 · FINRA section 3) — A grandmother deposits $5,000 into an UTMA account for her grandson. Two years later she needs the money and asks for it back. Which is TRUE?

- A. She may withdraw it as the original donor
- B. The custodian may return it with principal approval
- C. She may withdraw it only if the minor consents
- D. The gift is irrevocable and belongs to the minor

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Gifts to an UGMA/UTMA account are irrevocable; the assets belong to the minor and can't be returned to the donor.

*Remember:* UGMA/UTMA gifts are irrevocable; the minor is the beneficial owner.

</details>

**Q7022** (§13.3.5 · FINRA section 3) — Which action is permitted for the custodian of an UGMA account?

- A. Purchasing blue-chip stock on margin
- B. Being reimbursed for reasonable account expenses
- C. Writing uncovered calls to generate income
- D. Using the funds for the minor's basic food and housing

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A custodian may be reimbursed for reasonable expenses but may not profit, use margin, write uncovered options, or use the assets for parental support obligations.

*Remember:* Custodian: fiduciary; no margin or uncovered writing; may be reimbursed but not profit.

</details>

**Q7023** (§13.3.6 · FINRA section 3) — A court has declared an elderly man unable to manage his own affairs and appointed his niece to manage his assets. To open an account on his behalf, the firm needs:

- A. A durable power of attorney signed by the man
- B. A trust certification naming the niece as trustee
- C. A notarized letter from the man's physician
- D. The court documents appointing the niece as guardian

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A guardian or conservator account requires the court appointment documents, which establish the guardian's authority and usually must be renewed.

*Remember:* Guardianship accounts require court appointment papers; the guardian is a fiduciary.

</details>

**Q7024** (§13.3.7 · FINRA section 3) — A grantor wants to remove assets from her taxable estate by placing them in a trust. Which type of trust accomplishes this?

- A. Irrevocable trust
- B. Revocable living trust
- C. Joint revocable trust
- D. Grantor-controlled custodial trust

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Assets placed in an irrevocable trust leave the grantor's estate, and the trust becomes a separate taxpayer. Revocable trust assets stay in the grantor's estate.

*Remember:* Irrevocable trust removes assets from the estate; revocable trust only avoids probate.

</details>

**Q7025** (§13.3.7 · FINRA section 3) — The trustee of a family trust wants to buy stocks on margin in the trust account. This is permitted only if:

- A. The beneficiaries each consent orally
- B. The trust document specifically permits margin
- C. The trustee is also a registered principal
- D. The trust is revocable rather than irrevocable

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Trustees are fiduciaries under the prudent investor rule; margin and options are generally prohibited unless the trust document specifically authorizes them.

*Remember:* Trust accounts: margin or options only if the trust document permits.

</details>

**Q7026** (§13.3.8 · FINRA section 3) — A corporation wants to open a margin account. In addition to a corporate resolution naming the officers authorized to trade, the firm needs:

- A. The corporation's charter or bylaws showing it may use margin
- B. A personal guarantee from each authorized officer
- C. The corporation's most recent audited financial statements
- D. A copy of the corporation's partnership agreement

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A corporate account needs a resolution; margin or options trading also requires the charter or bylaws showing the corporation is permitted to do so.

*Remember:* Corporate account: resolution; add charter/bylaws for margin or options.

</details>

**Q7027** (§13.3.8 · FINRA section 3) — For FINRA suitability purposes, an entity other than a bank or insurer generally qualifies as an institutional account if it has total assets of at least:

- A. $5 million
- B. $10 million
- C. $25 million
- D. $50 million

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Institutional accounts include banks, insurers, investment companies, RIAs and any entity with at least $50 million in total assets.

*Remember:* Institutional account: bank, insurer, RIA, investment company, or entity with $50 million+ assets.

</details>

**Q7028** (§13.3.9 · FINRA section 3) — A hedge fund executes trades through several broker-dealers but has all trades settled and its assets custodied at one firm, which also lends it securities for short sales. That firm is acting as the fund's:

- A. Executing broker
- B. Clearing agency
- C. Prime broker
- D. Transfer agent

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** In prime brokerage, the client uses multiple executing brokers but settles and custodies everything with one prime broker, which also provides financing and securities lending.

*Remember:* Prime broker: one custody/settlement firm for trades executed through many brokers.

</details>

**Q7029** (§13.3.9 · FINRA section 3) — Under ACATS, after a receiving firm submits a customer's transfer instruction, the delivering firm must validate it (or take exception) and then complete the transfer within:

- A. 1 business day to validate; 3 business days to complete
- B. 3 business days to validate; 5 business days to complete
- C. 1 business day to validate; 5 business days to complete
- D. 2 business days to validate; 3 business days to complete

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The delivering firm has 1 business day to validate or take exception, and must complete the transfer within 3 business days after validation.

*Remember:* ACATS: validate within 1 business day; complete within 3 business days after.

</details>

**Q7030** (§13.4.1 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following may NOT be purchased on margin?

- A. Common stock listed on the NYSE
- B. Shares of a new IPO bought in the offering
- C. Nasdaq-listed common stock
- D. Mutual fund shares owned for more than 30 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** New issues, including IPO shares, can't be bought on margin until 30 days after the offering. Listed stocks and seasoned fund shares are marginable.

*Remember:* New issues and new mutual fund purchases: not marginable for 30 days.

</details>

**Q7031** (§13.4.2 · FINRA section 3) — A customer's margin account has a debit balance of $20,000. Under the hypothecation agreement, the firm may rehypothecate customer securities worth up to:

- A. $10,000
- B. $20,000
- C. $28,000
- D. $40,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Firms may re-pledge customer margin securities to a bank up to 140% of the debit balance: $20,000 x 1.40 = $28,000.

1. Limit = debit balance x 140%
2. $20,000 x 1.40 = $28,000

*Remember:* Rehypothecation limit = 140% x debit balance.

</details>

**Q7032** (§13.4.2 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following accounts may NOT be opened as margin accounts?
I. An UTMA custodial account
II. A traditional IRA
III. A corporate account whose charter permits margin
IV. A JTWROS account of two adults

- A. I and II
- B. I and III
- C. II and IV
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Custodial accounts and retirement accounts such as IRAs can't use margin. A corporation whose charter permits margin, and adult joint owners, may open margin accounts.

*Remember:* No margin in custodial (UGMA/UTMA) or retirement (IRA) accounts.

</details>

**Q7033** (§13.4.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer opens a new margin account and sells short $8,000 of stock, depositing the Reg T requirement. What is the credit balance in the account?

- A. $4,000
- B. $8,000
- C. $12,000
- D. $16,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The short sale proceeds of $8,000 stay in the account, plus the 50% Reg T deposit of $4,000 (above the $2,000 minimum), for a $12,000 credit balance.

1. Reg T deposit = 50% x $8,000 = $4,000 (above $2,000 min)
2. CR = $8,000 proceeds + $4,000 = $12,000

*Remember:* Short credit balance = short sale proceeds + Reg T deposit (50%, minimum $2,000).

</details>

**Q7034** (§13.4.3 · FINRA section 3) — A customer receives a $3,000 Reg T call and wants to meet it by depositing fully paid marginable securities instead of cash. What value of securities must be deposited?

- A. $1,500
- B. $3,000
- C. $4,500
- D. $6,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Deposited securities only provide 50% loan value under Reg T, so securities worth twice the call are needed: $3,000 x 2 = $6,000.

1. Securities count at 50% loan value
2. $3,000 / 0.50 = $6,000

*Remember:* Meet a Reg T call with securities worth 2x the call (cash = 1x).

</details>

**Q7035** (§13.4.4 · FINRA section 3) — A customer buys $40,000 of stock in a margin account and deposits the Reg T requirement. The stock later rises to $50,000. What is the customer's equity?

- A. $20,000
- B. $25,000
- C. $30,000
- D. $50,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The debit balance is fixed at $20,000 (the 50% loan). Equity = LMV - DR = $50,000 - $20,000 = $30,000.

1. DR = 50% x $40,000 = $20,000
2. EQ = $50,000 - $20,000 = $30,000

*Remember:* Long equity = LMV - debit balance; the debit stays fixed as prices move.

</details>

**Q7036** (§13.4.4 · FINRA section 3) — A margin account has a long market value of $36,000 and a debit balance of $15,000. How much buying power has been created?

- A. $3,000
- B. $6,000
- C. $18,000
- D. $21,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Reg T requirement = 50% x $36,000 = $18,000. Equity is $21,000, so excess equity (SMA) is $3,000. Buying power = 2 x SMA = $6,000.

1. EQ = $36,000 - $15,000 = $21,000; Reg T req = $18,000
2. SMA = $21,000 - $18,000 = $3,000
3. Buying power = 2 x $3,000 = $6,000

*Remember:* Excess equity (SMA) = equity - 50% of LMV; buying power = 2 x SMA.

</details>

**Q7037** (§13.4.4 · FINRA section 3) — A customer sells short $20,000 of stock and deposits $10,000. The stock then falls to $14,000. What is the equity in the account?

- A. $4,000
- B. $10,000
- C. $14,000
- D. $16,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Credit balance = $20,000 proceeds + $10,000 deposit = $30,000 and stays fixed. Equity = CR - SMV = $30,000 - $14,000 = $16,000.

1. CR = $20,000 + $10,000 = $30,000
2. EQ = $30,000 - $14,000 = $16,000

*Remember:* Short equity = credit balance - short market value; equity rises as the stock falls.

</details>

**Q7038** (§13.4.5 · FINRA section 3) — A long margin account has a debit balance of $18,000. Assuming the 25% FINRA minimum, below what market value will the account receive a maintenance call?

- A. $22,500
- B. $24,000
- C. $27,000
- D. $36,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A maintenance call occurs when LMV falls below DR / 0.75 = $18,000 / 0.75 = $24,000. At $24,000, equity of $6,000 is exactly 25%.

1. Call point = DR / (1 - 0.25)
2. $18,000 / 0.75 = $24,000

*Remember:* Long maintenance call point = debit balance / 0.75.

</details>

**Q7039** (§13.4.5 · FINRA section 3) — A short margin account has a credit balance of $26,000, and the short market value has risen to $21,000. Using the 30% FINRA minimum, what is the maintenance call?

- A. No call; equity exceeds the minimum
- B. $1,300
- C. $5,000
- D. $6,300

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Equity = $26,000 - $21,000 = $5,000. Required = 30% x $21,000 = $6,300. Call = $6,300 - $5,000 = $1,300.

1. EQ = $26,000 - $21,000 = $5,000
2. Required = 30% x $21,000 = $6,300
3. Call = $6,300 - $5,000 = $1,300

*Remember:* Short maintenance = 30% of SMV; call = required equity - actual equity.

</details>

**Q7040** (§13.4.5 · FINRA section 3) — A customer makes five day trades in his margin account over four business days and is designated a pattern day trader. He must maintain minimum equity of:

- A. $2,000
- B. $5,000
- C. $25,000
- D. $50,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A pattern day trader (4+ day trades in 5 business days) must keep at least $25,000 of equity in the margin account.

*Remember:* Pattern day trader: 4+ day trades in 5 business days; $25,000 minimum equity.

</details>

**Q7041** (§13.5 · FINRA section 3) — Which sequence correctly describes opening an options account?

- A. Customer signs the options agreement, ROP approves, ODD delivered within 15 days
- B. ROP approves, customer trades, ODD delivered with the first confirmation
- C. ODD delivered, rep approves the account, ROP reviews it within 15 days
- D. ODD delivered, ROP approves, signed options agreement returned within 15 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The ODD must be delivered at or before approval, a Registered Options Principal approves the account, and the customer returns the signed options agreement within 15 days of approval.

*Remember:* Options account: ODD at/before approval, ROP approves, agreement signed within 15 days.

</details>

**Q7042** (§13.5 · FINRA section 3) — A customer approved only for covered call writing wants to begin writing uncovered calls. Which is required?

- A. Approval at the firm's highest options level and a margin account
- B. Only a new copy of the ODD delivered by mail
- C. A cash account with a principal's verbal approval
- D. Converting the account to a custodial registration

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Uncovered writing carries the greatest risk, so it requires a margin account, the highest approval level, and a special written statement of uncovered option risks.

*Remember:* Uncovered option writing: margin account + highest approval level + special risk statement.

</details>

**Q7043** (§13.6.1 · FINRA section 3) — A customer of a failed broker-dealer has an individual account with $300,000 in securities, a joint account with his wife holding $400,000 in securities, and an IRA holding $200,000 in securities. What is the total SIPC coverage?

- A. $500,000
- B. $700,000
- C. $900,000
- D. $1,500,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Each account is held in a separate capacity, so each is a separate customer with its own $500,000 limit. All three are fully covered: $300,000 + $400,000 + $200,000.

1. Individual $300,000, joint $400,000, IRA $200,000: each under $500,000
2. Total = $900,000

*Remember:* SIPC: $500,000 per separate customer (capacity); individual, joint and IRA are separate.

</details>

**Q7044** (§13.6.1 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following held at a failed broker-dealer would be eligible for SIPC protection?
I. Common stock
II. Commodity futures contracts
III. A fixed annuity contract
IV. Treasury notes

- A. I and II
- B. I and IV
- C. II and III
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** SIPC protects securities and cash. Commodity futures and fixed annuities are not securities and are not covered.

*Remember:* SIPC excludes commodity futures, fixed annuities, currency and market losses.

</details>

**Q7045** (§13.6.2 · FINRA section 4) — Which of the following are TRUE regarding FINRA's business continuity plan rule?
I. The plan must be reviewed annually
II. A summary must be disclosed to customers at account opening
III. FINRA must approve the plan before it takes effect
IV. The firm designates two emergency contact persons

- A. I and III only
- B. II and IV only
- C. I, II and IV
- D. I, II, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Rule 4370 requires annual review, written disclosure of a plan summary at account opening, and two emergency contacts from senior management. FINRA doesn't pre-approve the plan; senior management does.

*Remember:* BCP: annual review, senior-management approval, customer summary at opening, two emergency contacts.

</details>

**Q9060** (§13.4.4 · FINRA section 3) — A margin account has a long market value of $40,000 and a debit balance of $24,000, so it is restricted. The customer sells $10,000 of stock from the account. Under Regulation T, how much of the sale proceeds may the customer withdraw?

- A. $0
- B. $5,000
- C. $7,500
- D. $10,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Equity is $16,000 (40%), below the 50% Reg T level but above maintenance, so the account is restricted. The retention requirement keeps 50% of sale proceeds to reduce the debit; the other 50% ($5,000) may be withdrawn.

1. Equity = $40,000 - $24,000 = $16,000 (40%), so the account is restricted
2. Retention = 50% x $10,000 = $5,000 applied to the debit
3. Withdrawable = $10,000 - $5,000 = $5,000

*Remember:* Restricted account: retain 50% of sale proceeds to reduce the debit; the rest may be withdrawn.

</details>

**Q9061** (§13.4.5 · FINRA section 3) — A long margin account has a market value of $20,000 and a debit balance of $16,000. The customer wants to meet the FINRA minimum maintenance call by depositing fully paid marginable securities. Approximately what value of securities must she deposit?

- A. $1,000
- B. $1,333
- C. $2,000
- D. $4,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Equity is $4,000 versus a $5,000 requirement (25% of $20,000), a $1,000 call. Deposited securities count only 75% toward equity, so she needs $1,000 / 0.75 = about $1,333.

1. Equity = $20,000 - $16,000 = $4,000; required = 25% x $20,000 = $5,000
2. Call = $5,000 - $4,000 = $1,000
3. Securities needed = $1,000 / 0.75 = $1,333

*Remember:* Maintenance call met with securities: deposit = call / 0.75.

</details>

**Q9062** (§13.3.5 · FINRA section 3) — The custodian of an UTMA account will be overseas for six months and wants to give her brother written authority to enter trades in the account while she is away. The firm should:

- A. Decline, because third-party trading authorization is not allowed in a custodial account
- B. Accept a limited trading authorization so the brother can trade but not withdraw
- C. Accept the authorization if the minor signs a consent form
- D. Accept the authorization if the brother is also a registered representative

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Only the custodian may give instructions in an UGMA/UTMA account; a third-party trading authorization is not permitted.

*Remember:* UGMA/UTMA: one custodian, one minor; only the custodian may enter orders; no third-party authority.

</details>

**Q9063** (§13.3.6 · FINRA section 3) — A customer dies, and his will names his daughter as executor of his estate. Before following her instructions to sell securities in the estate's account, the firm should obtain:

- A. Her durable power of attorney over the account, signed before the death
- B. Court documents appointing her as his guardian
- C. A death certificate plus the daughter's Social Security number
- D. Letters testamentary and a tax ID number for the estate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** An executor proves her authority with letters testamentary from the court, and the estate account uses the estate's own tax ID.

*Remember:* Estate account: executor needs letters testamentary; account uses the estate's tax ID.

</details>

**Q9064** (§13.6.1 · FINRA section 3) — A broker-dealer fails. A customer has two individual accounts in her own name there: a cash account with $100,000 of securities and $280,000 of cash, and a margin account with a long market value of $200,000 and a debit balance of $80,000. What is her total SIPC coverage?

- A. $350,000
- B. $470,000
- C. $500,000
- D. $550,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Two individual accounts in the same name are combined as one customer. Securities equity is $100,000 + $120,000 = $220,000, and cash is capped at $250,000, so coverage is $470,000; the extra $30,000 of cash is a general creditor claim.

1. Margin equity = $200,000 - $80,000 = $120,000
2. Securities = $100,000 + $120,000 = $220,000; cash capped at $250,000
3. Coverage = $220,000 + $250,000 = $470,000

*Remember:* SIPC: $500,000 per separate customer, cash max $250,000; same-name accounts combine; margin counts at equity.

</details>

**Q9075** (§13.1.3 · FINRA section 4) — Under FINRA Rule 4530, member firms must report statistical summaries of written customer complaints to FINRA:

- A. Monthly
- B. Quarterly
- C. Annually
- D. Only when a complaint leads to arbitration

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Rule 4530 requires quarterly statistical reporting of written customer complaints. Complaints alleging theft, forgery or misappropriation must also be reported promptly.

*Remember:* Rule 4530: complaint statistics filed quarterly; complaint records kept 4 years.

</details>

## Chapter 14: Retirement & Education Plans

**Q133** (§14.4 · FINRA section 3) — A 45-year-old investor withdraws $10,000 from a traditional IRA to buy a boat. The withdrawal is:

- A. Tax-free
- B. Taxed as ordinary income only
- C. Taxed as ordinary income plus a 10% penalty
- D. Taxed as a long-term capital gain

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Distributions before 59½ without an exception are taxed as ordinary income plus a 10% additional tax.

*Remember:* Traditional IRA withdrawals before 59½ without an exception: ordinary income plus 10% penalty.

</details>

**Q134** (§14.4 · FINRA section 3) — Which statement about a Roth IRA is TRUE?

- A. Contributions are tax-deductible
- B. Required minimum distributions begin at age 73
- C. Qualified withdrawals are tax-free after five years and age 59½
- D. Anyone with earned income may contribute regardless of income

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Roth contributions are after-tax, there are no lifetime RMDs, income limits apply, and qualified withdrawals (5-year rule + 59½, death, disability, or first home) are tax-free.

*Remember:* Roth IRA: after-tax contributions, no lifetime RMDs, income limits, qualified withdrawals tax-free (5 years + 59½).

</details>

**Q135** (§14.3 · FINRA section 3) — In a defined benefit pension plan, the investment risk is borne by:

- A. The employee
- B. The employer
- C. The plan trustee
- D. The PBGC

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The employer promises a specific benefit and must fund it regardless of investment results. In a defined contribution plan the employee bears the risk.

*Remember:* Defined benefit: the employer promises the benefit and bears the investment risk.

</details>

**Q136** (§14.2 · FINRA section 3) — A participant in a 401(k) receives a distribution check payable to herself and intends to roll it into an IRA. She must complete the rollover within:

- A. 30 days
- B. 60 days
- C. 90 days
- D. One year

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Indirect rollovers must be completed within 60 days; 20% is withheld from plan distributions, which she must make up to roll over the full amount. Direct transfers avoid these issues.

*Remember:* Indirect (60-day) rollover must be completed within 60 days; plans withhold 20%.

</details>

**Q137** (§14.6 · FINRA section 2) — During the payout phase of a variable annuity, the monthly payment will increase if the separate account's return is:

- A. Positive
- B. Greater than the assumed interest rate
- C. Greater than the prior month's return
- D. Greater than the CPI

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Payments change relative to the AIR: return above the AIR raises the next payment; below lowers it; equal keeps it unchanged.

*Remember:* Annuity payout rises only when separate account return exceeds the AIR.

</details>

**Q138** (§14.6 · FINRA section 2) — Which annuity payout option provides the LARGEST monthly payment?

- A. Life with 10-year period certain
- B. Joint and last survivor
- C. Straight life
- D. Unit refund life

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Straight life pays only for the annuitant's lifetime with no guarantees to beneficiaries, so the insurer can pay the most per month.

*Remember:* Straight life pays the largest monthly amount because nothing is guaranteed after the annuitant dies.

</details>

**Q139** (§14.6 · FINRA section 2) — A non-qualified variable annuity owner, age 50, makes a partial withdrawal. For tax purposes the withdrawal is treated as:

- A. Return of cost basis first, tax-free
- B. Earnings first, taxed as ordinary income plus 10% penalty
- C. Half basis, half earnings
- D. Long-term capital gain

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Annuity withdrawals are LIFO — earnings come out first and are taxed as ordinary income, with a 10% penalty before 59½.

*Remember:* Non-qualified annuity withdrawals are LIFO: earnings out first, taxed as ordinary income, +10% before 59½.

</details>

**Q140** (§14.7 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following is TRUE of a 529 college savings plan?

- A. Contributions are deductible on the federal return
- B. Contributors are subject to income limits
- C. The account owner, not the beneficiary, controls the assets
- D. Withdrawals for any purpose are tax-free after age 18

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** 529 contributions are after-tax federally, have no income limits, the donor/owner keeps control and may change the beneficiary within the family, and non-qualified withdrawals incur tax plus a 10% penalty on earnings.

*Remember:* 529 plan: after-tax federally, no income limits, owner controls assets and can change beneficiary.

</details>

**Q141** (§14.5 · FINRA section 2) — In a variable life insurance policy, which of the following is guaranteed?

- A. The cash value
- B. A minimum death benefit
- C. The rate of return on the separate account
- D. The premium will never be due

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Variable life has a fixed premium and a guaranteed minimum death benefit; the cash value and any additional death benefit vary with separate account performance.

*Remember:* Variable life guarantees a minimum death benefit; cash value and returns fluctuate.

</details>

**Q142** (§14.3 · FINRA section 3) — A 403(b) plan is available to employees of:

- A. Any corporation with fewer than 100 employees
