- B. State and local governments only
- C. Public schools and 501(c)(3) non-profit organizations
- D. Self-employed individuals

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** 403(b) tax-sheltered annuity plans serve public education and non-profit employees; investments are limited to annuities and mutual funds.

*Remember:* 403(b) plans serve public school and 501(c)(3) non-profit employees.

</details>

**Q7046** (§14.1 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following are characteristics of a qualified retirement plan?
I. It must not discriminate in favor of highly paid employees
II. Employer contributions are deductible immediately
III. Contributions are made with after-tax dollars
IV. Withdrawals are fully taxable as ordinary income

- A. I and III only
- B. II and III only
- C. I, II and IV
- D. II, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Qualified plans meet IRS/ERISA rules: non-discriminatory, immediately deductible employer contributions, pre-tax contributions, and fully taxable withdrawals.

*Remember:* Qualified: non-discriminatory, pre-tax, employer deducts now, withdrawals fully taxable.

</details>

**Q7047** (§14.1 · FINRA section 3) — A corporation wants to offer a retirement benefit only to its top five executives. Which type of plan allows this?

- A. A 401(k) plan
- B. A non-qualified deferred compensation plan
- C. A defined benefit pension plan
- D. A profit-sharing plan

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Non-qualified plans are not subject to non-discrimination rules and may favor selected executives. Qualified plans must cover all eligible employees.

*Remember:* Only non-qualified plans may discriminate in favor of selected employees.

</details>

**Q7048** (§14.1 · FINRA section 3) — When does an employer receive a tax deduction for amounts set aside in a non-qualified deferred compensation plan?

- A. When the amounts are credited to the plan
- B. At the end of the employer's fiscal year
- C. Never, since non-qualified amounts aren't deductible
- D. When the employee is paid and taxed on the amounts

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** With non-qualified plans, the employer's deduction is delayed until the employee actually receives (and is taxed on) the compensation.

*Remember:* Non-qualified plan: employer deducts only when the employee is taxed.

</details>

**Q7049** (§14.1 · FINRA section 3) — A customer in the 35% tax bracket wants to add income investments to her traditional IRA. Which would be LEAST suitable?

- A. Investment-grade corporate bonds
- B. Shares of an equity REIT
- C. Treasury STRIPS
- D. A high-quality municipal bond fund

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Municipal interest is already federally tax-free, so holding munis in a tax-deferred account wastes that benefit; withdrawals would be taxed as ordinary income.

*Remember:* Munis don't belong in tax-advantaged accounts; their tax exemption is wasted.

</details>

**Q7050** (§14.2 · FINRA section 3) — ERISA applies to the retirement plans of all of the following EXCEPT:

- A. A manufacturing corporation's 401(k) plan
- B. A state government's employee pension plan
- C. A private hospital's defined benefit plan
- D. A small business's profit-sharing plan

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** ERISA governs private-sector employer plans. Government plans, church plans and IRAs are not covered.

*Remember:* ERISA covers private-sector employer plans; not government plans, church plans or IRAs.

</details>

**Q7051** (§14.2 · FINRA section 3) — An employee leaves her job after two years. Her 401(k) uses a 6-year graded vesting schedule for employer contributions. Which portion of her account is 100% vested?

- A. Her own salary deferrals and their earnings
- B. The employer match, after two years of service
- C. None of it until she reaches six years
- D. Only the profit-sharing contributions

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Employee contributions are always 100% vested immediately. Only employer contributions follow a vesting schedule.

*Remember:* Employee contributions are always 100% vested; employer money vests on a schedule.

</details>

**Q7052** (§14.2 · FINRA section 3) — An investor born in 1955 has a traditional IRA. Required minimum distributions must begin by:

- A. December 31 of the year she turns 70 1/2
- B. April 1 of the year after she turns 72
- C. April 1 of the year after she turns 73
- D. April 1 of the year after she turns 75

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Owners born before 1960 must begin RMDs by April 1 of the year after turning 73; those born in 1960 or later start at 75.

*Remember:* RMDs begin by April 1 after age 73 (75 if born 1960 or later).

</details>

**Q7053** (§14.2 · FINRA section 3) — A retiree was required to withdraw $12,000 from her traditional IRA this year but took nothing and did not correct the shortfall promptly. The penalty is:

- A. $1,200
- B. $3,000
- C. $6,000
- D. $720

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The missed-RMD penalty is 25% of the shortfall: 25% x $12,000 = $3,000 (10% if corrected promptly).

1. Penalty = 25% x shortfall
2. 25% x $12,000 = $3,000

*Remember:* Missed RMD penalty = 25% of shortfall (10% if corrected promptly).

</details>

**Q7054** (§14.2 · FINRA section 3) — A participant leaving her job requests a $100,000 distribution from her 401(k) paid directly to her. She wants to roll over the full $100,000 into an IRA within 60 days. She must:

- A. Deposit the $80,000 check; the withheld $20,000 is rolled automatically
- B. Deposit $80,000 and claim the $20,000 as an extra IRA contribution
- C. Deposit $100,000, using $20,000 from other funds
- D. Deposit $90,000, since only 10% is withheld

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Plan distributions paid to the participant face 20% mandatory withholding, so she receives $80,000. To roll over the full amount she must add $20,000 from other funds; otherwise the shortfall is taxable.

1. Withholding = 20% x $100,000 = $20,000; check = $80,000
2. Full rollover = $80,000 + $20,000 other funds

*Remember:* Plan checks to participant: 20% withheld; replace it from other funds to roll over in full.

</details>

**Q7055** (§14.2 · FINRA section 3) — A 56-year-old leaves his employer and takes a cash distribution from that employer's 401(k) plan. The distribution is:

- A. Taxed as ordinary income plus a 10% penalty
- B. Tax-free because he separated from service
- C. Taxed as long-term capital gain with no penalty
- D. Taxed as ordinary income with no 10% penalty

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Separation from service at age 55 or later is an exception to the 10% penalty for employer plans. Income tax still applies at ordinary rates.

*Remember:* Separation from service at 55+: no 10% penalty from that employer's plan; still ordinary income.

</details>

**Q7056** (§14.2 · FINRA section 3) — A 40-year-old inherits a traditional IRA from her uncle. Under the SECURE Act, she generally must:

- A. Withdraw the entire balance within 10 years
- B. Treat the IRA as her own and delay RMDs
- C. Withdraw everything within 60 days
- D. Take distributions over her life expectancy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Most non-spouse beneficiaries must empty an inherited IRA within 10 years. Only a spouse may treat it as their own.

*Remember:* Inherited IRA: most non-spouse beneficiaries must empty it within 10 years.

</details>

**Q7057** (§14.3 · FINRA section 3) — A defined benefit pension plan tends to favor which employees?

- A. Younger employees with many years until retirement
- B. Part-time employees with variable hours
- C. Older, highly paid employees close to retirement
- D. Employees who want to direct their own investments

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Funding a promised benefit over few remaining years requires large contributions, so DB plans favor older, highly compensated employees.

*Remember:* DB plans favor older, highly paid workers; DC plans favor younger workers.

</details>

**Q7058** (§14.3 · FINRA section 3) — A county employee, age 50, separates from service and withdraws money from her 457 plan. Which is TRUE?

- A. The withdrawal is tax-free since she separated
- B. She owes income tax plus a 10% penalty
- C. She must wait until age 55 to take a withdrawal
- D. She owes income tax but no 10% early withdrawal penalty

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A unique 457 feature: distributions after separation from service are not subject to the 10% early withdrawal penalty, though they are taxed as ordinary income.

*Remember:* 457 plans: no 10% penalty on distributions after separation from service.

</details>

**Q7059** (§14.3 · FINRA section 3) — A small business owner wants a qualified plan but needs flexibility to skip contributions in unprofitable years. Which plan is most appropriate?

- A. Money purchase plan
- B. Defined benefit plan
- C. Profit-sharing plan
- D. SIMPLE IRA

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Profit-sharing plan contributions are discretionary, so the employer can vary or skip them. Money purchase and DB plans require annual funding.

*Remember:* Profit-sharing = discretionary contributions; money purchase = fixed % every year.

</details>

**Q7060** (§14.3 · FINRA section 3) — Which statement about a SEP IRA is TRUE?

- A. Employees may defer salary up to the 401(k) limit
- B. Only the employer contributes, at the same percentage for all eligible employees
- C. Contributions vest on a six-year graded schedule
- D. It may be used only by employers with 100 or fewer employees

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** SEP contributions come only from the employer (up to 25% of compensation), must be the same percentage for all eligible employees, and are immediately vested.

*Remember:* SEP: employer-only contributions, same % for all eligible, immediately vested.

</details>

**Q7061** (§14.3 · FINRA section 3) — A 45-year-old withdraws $10,000 from his SIMPLE IRA 18 months after he first began participating. In addition to income tax, what penalty applies?

- A. $1,000
- B. $2,000
- C. $2,500
- D. None

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Withdrawals from a SIMPLE IRA within the first 2 years of participation carry a 25% penalty instead of 10%: 25% x $10,000 = $2,500.

1. Within 2 years of participation: 25% penalty
2. 25% x $10,000 = $2,500

*Remember:* SIMPLE IRA early withdrawal within first 2 years: 25% penalty instead of 10%.

</details>

**Q7062** (§14.3 · FINRA section 3) — A participant's 401(k) has a vested balance of $70,000 and the plan permits loans. What is the maximum she may borrow?

- A. $35,000
- B. $50,000
- C. $70,000
- D. $10,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** 401(k) loans are limited to the lesser of 50% of the vested balance or $50,000. Half of $70,000 is $35,000.

1. 50% x $70,000 = $35,000
2. Lesser of $35,000 and $50,000 = $35,000

*Remember:* 401(k) loan max = lesser of 50% of vested balance or $50,000.

</details>

**Q7063** (§14.3 · FINRA section 3) — An executive participates in her employer's non-qualified deferred compensation plan. Her main risk is that:

- A. Her deferred amounts are taxed in the year they are earned
- B. She becomes a general creditor if the employer goes bankrupt
- C. The plan must be offered to all employees, diluting her benefit
- D. The PBGC caps the benefit she can receive

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Deferred compensation is an unsecured promise to pay later; if the company fails, the employee is only a general creditor.

*Remember:* Non-qualified deferred comp: unsecured promise; employee is a general creditor in bankruptcy.

</details>

**Q7064** (§14.4 · FINRA section 3) — Which type of income can be used as the basis for an IRA contribution?

- A. Stock dividends
- B. Rental income from an apartment
- C. Social Security benefits
- D. Net self-employment income

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** IRA contributions require earned income such as wages or self-employment income. Investment income, rental income and Social Security don't qualify.

*Remember:* IRA contributions require earned income: wages, salary, self-employment income.

</details>

**Q7065** (§14.4 · FINRA section 3) — A 52-year-old contributes $5,000 to his traditional IRA for 2026. He is eligible for a Roth IRA. What is the most he can contribute to a Roth IRA for 2026?

- A. $2,500
- B. $3,600
- C. $7,500
- D. $8,600

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The 2026 limit is $7,500 plus a $1,100 catch-up at 50+ = $8,600, combined across traditional and Roth IRAs. $8,600 - $5,000 = $3,600.

1. Limit = $7,500 + $1,100 = $8,600
2. $8,600 - $5,000 = $3,600

*Remember:* IRA limit is combined across traditional and Roth: $7,500 + $1,100 catch-up (2026).

</details>

**Q7066** (§14.4 · FINRA section 3) — A 48-year-old converts $50,000 from a traditional IRA (all deductible contributions and earnings) to a Roth IRA. The tax result is:

- A. $50,000 taxed as ordinary income, with no 10% penalty
- B. $50,000 taxed as ordinary income plus a 10% penalty
- C. No tax, because the money stays in an IRA
- D. Only the earnings are taxed, as capital gains

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A Roth conversion is taxable as ordinary income on the pre-tax amount converted, but the 10% early withdrawal penalty does not apply.

*Remember:* Roth conversion: pre-tax amount taxed as ordinary income; no 10% penalty.

</details>

**Q7067** (§14.4 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following may be held in an IRA?
I. A U.S.-minted gold coin
II. A collection of rare stamps
III. Shares of a publicly traded REIT
IV. A whole life insurance policy

- A. I and III
- B. II and IV
- C. I, II and III
- D. III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Collectibles and life insurance are prohibited in IRAs, but U.S. government gold and silver coins and REIT shares are permitted.

*Remember:* IRAs can't hold collectibles or life insurance; U.S. gold/silver coins are an exception.

</details>

**Q7068** (§14.4 · FINRA section 3) — A 40-year-old opened a Roth IRA 3 years ago and has contributed $15,000; the account is now worth $18,000. She withdraws $10,000. The withdrawal is:

- A. Fully taxable plus a 10% penalty
- B. Tax- and penalty-free, as a return of contributions
- C. Taxable on $3,000 of earnings plus a penalty
- D. Penalty-free but taxed as ordinary income

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Roth withdrawals come from contributions first. Contributions were made with after-tax money and can be withdrawn anytime without tax or penalty.

*Remember:* Roth: contributions come out first and are always tax- and penalty-free.

</details>

**Q7069** (§14.4 · FINRA section 3) — A high-income employee is covered by her employer's 401(k) plan and wants to contribute to a traditional IRA. Which is TRUE?

- A. She is barred from contributing to any IRA
- B. She may contribute, and the contribution is fully deductible
- C. She may contribute only to a SEP IRA
- D. She may contribute, but it may be non-deductible

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Being covered by an employer plan with income above the phase-out doesn't prevent a traditional IRA contribution; it only limits deductibility, creating after-tax basis.

*Remember:* Plan coverage + high income limits the traditional IRA deduction, not the right to contribute.

</details>

**Q7070** (§14.5 · FINRA section 2) — A variable life policyholder decides she wants a guaranteed cash value. During the first 24 months after issue, she may:

- A. Exchange it for a fixed whole life policy from the same insurer without evidence of insurability
- B. Cancel and receive a refund of all premiums paid plus interest
- C. Convert it to a variable annuity with a guaranteed rate
- D. Exchange it for term insurance from any insurer without underwriting

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Variable life owners have at least 24 months to exchange into a fixed (whole) life policy from the same insurer without proving insurability.

*Remember:* Variable life: at least 24 months to exchange into fixed life with the same insurer, no evidence of insurability.

</details>

**Q7071** (§14.5 · FINRA section 2) — Over the life of a variable life insurance policy, sales charges are capped at:

- A. 5% of total premiums
- B. 8.5% of total premiums
- C. 9% of total premiums
- D. 20% of first-year premiums

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Sales charges on variable life are limited to 9% of total premiums over the policy's life (measured over a maximum of 20 years).

*Remember:* Variable life sales charges: max 9% of total premiums over the policy life (up to 20 years).

</details>

**Q7072** (§14.5 · FINRA section 2) — All of the following are tax-free Section 1035 exchanges EXCEPT:

- A. A life insurance policy for another life policy
- B. A life insurance policy for an annuity
- C. An annuity for another annuity
- D. An annuity for a life insurance policy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Section 1035 allows life-to-life, life-to-annuity and annuity-to-annuity exchanges. An annuity can't be exchanged tax-free for life insurance.

*Remember:* 1035: life to life, life to annuity, annuity to annuity; never annuity to life.

</details>

**Q7073** (§14.6 · FINRA section 2) — Which risk is borne primarily by the owner of a fixed annuity?

- A. Investment risk on the general account
- B. Purchasing power (inflation) risk
- C. Separate account performance risk
- D. Mortality risk on the insurer's pool

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** In a fixed annuity the insurer guarantees the rate and bears investment risk; the owner's fixed payments can lose value to inflation.

*Remember:* Fixed annuity: insurer bears investment risk; owner bears inflation risk; not a security.

</details>

**Q7074** (§14.6 · FINRA section 2) — When a variable annuity owner annuitizes, the accumulation units are converted into:

- A. A fixed number of annuity units whose value varies
- B. A variable number of annuity units of fixed value
- C. Shares of the insurer's general account
- D. A guaranteed fixed dollar payment

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** At annuitization, accumulation units convert to a fixed number of annuity units; each unit's value, and so each payment, changes with performance relative to the AIR.

*Remember:* Annuitization: fixed number of annuity units; unit value varies with performance vs. AIR.

</details>

**Q7075** (§14.6 · FINRA section 2) — An investor put $100,000 into a variable annuity and made no withdrawals. During the accumulation phase the contract value falls to $82,000, and she dies. Her beneficiary receives at least:

- A. $18,000
- B. $82,000
- C. $91,000
- D. $100,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The guaranteed minimum death benefit is typically the greater of contract value or total premiums paid (less withdrawals), so the beneficiary gets at least $100,000.

*Remember:* VA death benefit during accumulation: greater of contract value or premiums paid, less withdrawals.

</details>

**Q7076** (§14.6 · FINRA section 2) — A retiree annuitizes a non-qualified variable annuity purchased with after-tax money. How is each payment taxed?

- A. Entirely tax-free as a return of premium
- B. Entirely as ordinary income
- C. Partly tax-free return of basis, partly ordinary income
- D. Entirely as long-term capital gain

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** An exclusion ratio splits each annuitized payment into a tax-free return of cost basis and a taxable portion taxed as ordinary income.

*Remember:* Annuitized non-qualified payments: exclusion ratio splits basis (tax-free) and earnings (ordinary income).

</details>

**Q7077** (§14.7 · FINRA section 2) — A grandmother wants to make a large one-time 529 plan contribution for her grandson in 2026 without using any lifetime gift tax exemption. Using the 5-year election, the most she can contribute is:

- A. $19,000
- B. $38,000
- C. $95,000
- D. $190,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The 5-year front-loading election treats one contribution as made over 5 years: 5 x $19,000 = $95,000 per donor ($190,000 for a couple).

1. Annual exclusion = $19,000
2. 5 x $19,000 = $95,000

*Remember:* 529 front-loading = 5 x annual exclusion ($95,000 per donor in 2026).

</details>

**Q7078** (§14.7 · FINRA section 2) — A child's parents and grandparents each want to contribute $2,000 this year to a Coverdell ESA for the same child. In total, how much may be contributed for the child this year?

- A. $2,000
- B. $4,000
- C. $6,000
- D. $8,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The Coverdell limit is $2,000 per beneficiary per year from all contributors combined, not per contributor.

*Remember:* Coverdell ESA: $2,000 per beneficiary per year, all contributors combined.

</details>

**Q7079** (§14.7 · FINRA section 2) — Which of the following are TRUE of a Coverdell Education Savings Account?
I. Contributions are limited to $2,000 per beneficiary per year
II. There are no income limits on contributors
III. Funds generally must be used by the beneficiary's age 30
IV. Qualified K-12 expenses may be paid tax-free

- A. I and II only
- B. II and III only
- C. I, III and IV
- D. I, II, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Coverdells cap contributions at $2,000 per beneficiary, impose income limits on contributors, require use by age 30, and cover qualified K-12 and college expenses.

*Remember:* Coverdell: $2,000/yr, income limits, contribute until 18, use by 30, K-12 allowed.

</details>

**Q7080** (§14.7 · FINRA section 2) — Which account offers deductible contributions, tax-free growth, and tax-free withdrawals for qualified medical expenses?

- A. A Roth IRA
- B. A 529 savings plan
- C. An ABLE account
- D. A health savings account

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** An HSA is the only account with a triple tax advantage: deductible contributions, tax-free growth, and tax-free withdrawals for qualified medical expenses. It requires a high-deductible health plan.

*Remember:* HSA: deductible in, tax-free growth, tax-free out for medical; requires a high-deductible health plan.

</details>

**Q9043** (§14.2 · FINRA section 3) — A 38-year-old first-time homebuyer, who has never used the IRA home-purchase exception, withdraws $16,000 from her traditional IRA (all deductible contributions and earnings) toward a qualified home purchase. The tax consequence is:

- A. $16,000 taxed as ordinary income, with no penalty
- B. $6,000 taxed as ordinary income, plus a $600 penalty
- C. $16,000 taxed as ordinary income, plus a $600 penalty
- D. $16,000 taxed as ordinary income, plus a $1,600 penalty

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The first-home exception waives the 10% penalty on up to $10,000 lifetime, but income tax is still due on the whole withdrawal. The $6,000 excess incurs a 10% penalty: $600.

1. Taxable: all $16,000 (pre-tax money)
2. Penalty-free: $10,000; excess $6,000
3. Penalty = 10% × $6,000 = $600

*Remember:* IRA first-home exception: penalty waived on up to $10,000 lifetime; income tax still due on everything.

</details>

**Q9044** (§14.2 · FINRA section 3) — In February, a customer took a distribution from one IRA and redeposited it into another IRA within 60 days. In June, she wants to move the money in a different IRA to a new custodian. To avoid tax problems, she should:

- A. Use a direct trustee-to-trustee transfer, which has no annual limit
- B. Take a check and redeposit it within 60 days, since a different IRA is involved
- C. Wait until January, when the calendar-year rollover limit resets
- D. Take the distribution, accept 20% withholding, and roll over the rest

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** IRA-to-IRA rollovers are limited to one per 12 months, while trustee-to-trustee transfers are unlimited and untaxed. A transfer moves the money safely.

*Remember:* IRA rollover: 60 days, once per 12 months; direct trustee-to-trustee transfers are unlimited.

</details>

**Q9045** (§14.4 · FINRA section 3) — A 61-year-old opened her first Roth IRA two years ago. The account holds $34,000 of contributions and is worth $40,000. If she withdraws the entire $40,000, the tax result is:

- A. Entirely tax- and penalty-free, because she is over 59½
- B. $6,000 taxed as ordinary income, with no 10% penalty
- C. $6,000 taxed as ordinary income, plus a 10% penalty
- D. $40,000 taxed as ordinary income, with no 10% penalty

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Contributions come out tax-free, but earnings are qualified only after the 5-year holding period is met. She is over 59½, so the $6,000 of earnings is taxable without the 10% penalty.

*Remember:* Roth qualified withdrawal = 5 years since first contribution AND 59½ (or death/disability/first home).

</details>

**Q9046** (§14.3 · FINRA section 3) — A dental practice with 25 employees and no retirement plan is considering a SIMPLE IRA. All of the following are true EXCEPT:

- A. The employer must have 100 or fewer employees
- B. The employer may not maintain another retirement plan
- C. Employer contributions vest on a graded schedule over 6 years
- D. Withdrawals in the first 2 years of participation face a 25% penalty

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** SIMPLE IRA contributions, including the employer's match or 2% contribution, are immediately 100% vested. The other statements are SIMPLE rules.

*Remember:* SIMPLE: ≤100 employees, no other plan, 3% match or 2% contribution, immediately vested, 25% penalty first 2 years.

</details>

**Q9047** (§14.5 · FINRA section 2) — Which life insurance product lets the owner vary premium payments and has a death benefit that can fall below the initial face amount if separate account performance is poor?

- A. Variable life
- B. Universal life
- C. Whole life
- D. Variable universal life

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Variable universal life combines flexible premiums with separate-account investing, and its death benefit may fall below the initial face amount. Scheduled-premium variable life guarantees a minimum death benefit.

*Remember:* VUL = flexible premiums + separate account; death benefit not guaranteed at face.

</details>

**Q9048** (§14.6 · FINRA section 2) — An annuitant receives monthly variable annuity payments based on a 4% AIR. Over the next three months, the separate account earns annualized returns of 6%, 3% and 4%. Compared with each preceding payment, the three payments will:

- A. Rise, fall, and stay the same
- B. Rise, rise, and rise
- C. Rise, stay the same, and fall
- D. Rise, fall, and rise

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Payments rise when the separate account beats the AIR, fall when it trails the AIR, and stay level when it equals the AIR. A positive 3% return still trails the 4% AIR, so that payment falls.

*Remember:* Payout phase: return > AIR → payment rises; < AIR → falls; = AIR → unchanged.

</details>

**Q9049** (§14.6 · FINRA section 2) — The mortality and expense (M&E) risk charge on a variable annuity primarily compensates the insurance company for:

- A. Managing the investments in the sub-accounts
- B. Withdrawals made before the surrender period ends
- C. Optional guaranteed living benefit riders
- D. Guaranteeing the death benefit and the risk that expenses exceed estimates

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The M&E charge pays for the insurer's guarantees, such as the death benefit, and its risk that administrative costs run higher than expected. Sub-account management, surrender charges and riders are billed separately.

*Remember:* M&E charge pays for the insurance guarantees (death benefit) and expense risk.

</details>

**Q9050** (§14.7 · FINRA section 2) — A 529 plan beneficiary receives a $12,000 scholarship, and the account owner withdraws $12,000, which includes $3,000 of earnings, without using it for qualified expenses. The tax result is:

- A. $3,000 taxed as ordinary income, with the 10% penalty waived
- B. The entire withdrawal is tax-free because of the scholarship
- C. $3,000 taxed as ordinary income, plus a 10% penalty
- D. $12,000 taxed as ordinary income, plus a 10% penalty

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Non-qualified 529 withdrawals subject earnings to ordinary income tax. A scholarship waives the 10% penalty up to the scholarship amount, but not the tax on earnings.

*Remember:* 529 non-qualified withdrawal: earnings taxed + 10% penalty; penalty waived for death, disability, scholarship.

</details>

## Chapter 15: Rules & Ethics

**Q143** (§15.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — An attorney working on a merger tells her brother, who buys the target's stock. Which parties have violated insider trading rules?

- A. Only the brother
- B. Only the attorney
- C. Both the attorney (tipper) and the brother (tippee)
- D. Neither, because the attorney did not trade

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Tippers are liable even if they don't trade; tippees who trade on MNPI are liable. Civil penalties can reach three times the profit.

*Remember:* Tippers are liable even if they don't trade; tippees who trade on MNPI are liable too.

</details>

**Q144** (§15.4.6 · FINRA section 3) — A registered representative wants to work weekends as a paid real estate agent. Under FINRA rules she must:

- A. Do nothing; it's unrelated to securities
- B. Give her firm prior written notice
- C. Obtain FINRA's written approval
- D. Register the activity with the state Administrator

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Outside business activities for compensation require prior written notice to the firm (Rule 3270). Private securities transactions require notice and, if compensated, approval (Rule 3280).

*Remember:* Paid outside business activities require prior written notice to the member firm (Rule 3270).

</details>

**Q145** (§15.4.6 · FINRA section 3) — Under FINRA's gifts rule, business gifts to an employee of another member firm may not exceed:

- A. $50 per year
- B. $100 per person per year
- C. $300 per person per year
- D. $500 per year

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The limit is $300 per person per year (FINRA raised it from $100 effective March 30, 2026). Business entertainment where the giver attends is excluded.

*Remember:* FINRA Rule 3220 gift limit: $300 per person per year (raised from $100 in 2026); attended entertainment is excluded.

</details>

**Q146** (§15.4.3 · FINRA section 4) — A form letter sent to 40 retail customers within a week is classified as:

- A. Correspondence, requiring supervisory review
- B. Retail communication, requiring prior principal approval
- C. Institutional communication
- D. A public appearance

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Communications to more than 25 retail investors within 30 days are retail communications and must be approved by a registered principal before use. 25 or fewer = correspondence.

*Remember:* Over 25 retail investors within 30 days = retail communication requiring prior principal approval.

</details>

**Q147** (§15.3.1 · FINRA section 3) — A firm reasonably believes an 80-year-old customer is being financially exploited by a caregiver. The firm may place a temporary hold on disbursements for up to:

- A. 5 business days
- B. 10 business days
- C. 15 business days, extendable by 10 more
- D. 30 calendar days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** FINRA Rule 2165 allows a 15-business-day hold, extendable by 10 business days (and further if a regulator requests), with notification of the trusted contact and an internal review.

*Remember:* Rule 2165 temporary hold: up to 15 business days, extendable 10 more (plus 30 if regulator requests).

</details>

**Q148** (§15.4.5 · FINRA section 4) — A candidate fails the SIE exam for the third time. Before retesting, the candidate must wait:

- A. 30 days
- B. 60 days
- C. 90 days
- D. 180 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Waiting periods are 30 days after the first and second failures and 180 days after the third and each subsequent failure.

*Remember:* Exam retake waits: 30 days after 1st and 2nd failures, 180 days after third and later.

</details>

**Q149** (§15.4.7 · FINRA section 4) — Under SEC and FINRA record-keeping rules, customer complaint records must be retained for:

- A. 3 years
- B. 4 years
- C. 6 years
- D. The life of the firm

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Complaints: 4 years. Blotters, ledgers and account records: 6 years. Order tickets, confirms and communications: 3 years. Organizational documents: permanent.

*Remember:* Customer complaint records are kept 4 years.

</details>

**Q150** (§15.4.2 · FINRA section 3) — Under the Telephone Consumer Protection Act, cold calls to prospective customers may be made only between:

- A. 9 am and 5 pm in the caller's time zone
- B. 8 am and 9 pm in the called party's time zone
- C. 8 am and 8 pm in the caller's time zone
- D. 7 am and 10 pm in the called party's time zone

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Cold calls are limited to 8:00 am–9:00 pm local time of the person called, the caller must identify themselves and the firm, and do-not-call lists must be honored.

*Remember:* TCPA cold calls: 8 am to 9 pm in the called party's local time.

</details>

**Q151** (§15.1 · FINRA section 4) — Which of the following is a self-regulatory organization?

- A. The SEC
- B. FINRA
- C. The Federal Reserve
- D. FinCEN

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** FINRA, the exchanges and the MSRB are SROs operating under SEC oversight. The SEC is a government agency; the Fed and FinCEN are government bodies.

*Remember:* SROs (FINRA, exchanges, MSRB) regulate members under SEC oversight; they aren't government agencies.

</details>

**Q152** (§15.4.5 · FINRA section 4) — Passing the SIE exam allows an individual to:

- A. Open customer accounts at a broker-dealer
- B. Sell mutual funds under supervision
- C. Take a representative-level qualification exam when sponsored by a member firm
- D. Register as an investment adviser representative

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The SIE is a co-requisite; it confers no registration. To do securities business the person must be sponsored by a firm, pass a top-off exam (Series 6, 7, etc.) and be registered on Form U4.

*Remember:* The SIE confers no registration; a firm-sponsored top-off exam and Form U4 are also required.

</details>

**Q153** (§15.4.5 · FINRA section 4) — A registered representative moves to a new address. Form U4 must be amended within:

- A. 10 days
- B. 30 days
- C. 60 days
- D. 90 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Material changes to Form U4 information must be filed within 30 days. Form U5 is also due within 30 days of termination.

*Remember:* Form U4 amendments are due within 30 days of a change; U5 within 30 days of termination.

</details>

**Q154** (§15.4.5 · FINRA section 4) — A registered person who fails to complete the Regulatory Element of continuing education by the deadline:

- A. Is fined $1,000
- B. Has registration become inactive until it is completed
- C. Must retake the SIE
- D. Is suspended for 30 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Registration becomes inactive — no securities business or compensation — until the Regulatory Element is completed; after two years inactive it is terminated.

*Remember:* Missed Regulatory Element CE makes registration inactive until completed; terminated after two years inactive.

</details>

**Q155** (§15.4.3 · FINRA section 4) — A new FINRA member firm must file its retail communications with FINRA:

- A. Within 10 business days after first use
- B. At least 10 business days before first use
- C. Within 30 days after first use
- D. Only if they concern mutual funds

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** For its first year a new member files retail communications 10 business days BEFORE first use. Investment company, DPP and CMO material is filed within 10 business days AFTER first use.

*Remember:* New members file retail communications with FINRA 10 business days BEFORE first use for one year.

</details>

**Q156** (§15.4.7 · FINRA section 4) — Which records must a broker-dealer retain for six years?

- A. Order tickets and trade confirmations
- B. Blotters, general ledger and customer account records
- C. Customer complaints
- D. Articles of incorporation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Six years: blotters, ledgers, stock record, account records. Three years: tickets, confirms, communications. Four years: complaints. Permanent: organizational documents.

*Remember:* Six-year records: blotters, general ledger, stock record and customer account records.

</details>

**Q157** (§15.4.1 · FINRA section 4) — Which law requires corporate bond issues over $50 million to have an indenture and an independent trustee?

- A. Securities Act of 1933
- B. Securities Exchange Act of 1934
- C. Trust Indenture Act of 1939
- D. Investment Company Act of 1940

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The Trust Indenture Act of 1939 applies to public corporate debt (not governments or munis).

*Remember:* Trust Indenture Act of 1939: public corporate debt over $50M needs an indenture and independent trustee.

</details>

**Q158** (§15.4.6 · FINRA section 4) — Regulation Best Interest requires a broker-dealer making a recommendation to a retail customer to satisfy all of the following obligations EXCEPT:

- A. Disclosure
- B. Care
- C. Conflict of interest
- D. Ongoing account monitoring

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Reg BI's four obligations are disclosure, care, conflict of interest and compliance. It does not impose a continuing duty to monitor unless the firm agrees to one.

*Remember:* Reg BI's four obligations: disclosure, care, conflict of interest, compliance.

</details>

**Q159** (§15.4.6 · FINRA section 4) — A registered representative may share in the profits of a customer's account only if:

- A. The customer is an immediate family member
- B. The firm gives prior written approval and the sharing is proportionate to the rep's contribution
- C. The rep guarantees the customer against losses
- D. The account is discretionary

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Sharing requires prior written authorization of the firm (and customer) and must be proportional to the rep's financial contribution — except with immediate family, where proportionality is waived.

*Remember:* Profit sharing requires prior written firm approval and must be proportional, except immediate family accounts.

</details>

**Q160** (§15.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — Two traders agree to buy and sell the same stock back and forth at rising prices to attract other buyers. This is:

- A. Arbitrage
- B. Stabilization
- C. Matched orders / painting the tape
- D. Program trading

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Coordinated trades designed to create a false appearance of activity or price movement are matched orders (painting the tape) — manipulation under the 1934 Act.

*Remember:* Matched orders/painting the tape: coordinated trades creating false activity or price movement are manipulation.

</details>

**Q161** (§15.1 · FINRA section 4) — Registration of agents and securities at the state level is governed by:

- A. The Securities Act of 1933
- B. The Uniform Securities Act (blue-sky laws)
- C. FINRA's Conduct Rules
- D. The Maloney Act

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** State blue-sky laws, based on the Uniform Securities Act and administered by the state Administrator (coordinated via NASAA), govern state-level registration.

*Remember:* State registration of agents and securities is governed by blue-sky laws based on the Uniform Securities Act.

</details>

**Q162** (§15.4.5 · FINRA section 4) — A person convicted of a felony four years ago applies to associate with a member firm. The person is:

- A. Eligible after passing the SIE
- B. Subject to statutory disqualification and needs FINRA approval
- C. Permanently barred
- D. Eligible only as a clerical employee

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Any felony or securities/money-related misdemeanor conviction within 10 years is a statutory disqualification; the firm must seek FINRA approval via an eligibility proceeding.

*Remember:* Any felony within 10 years is a statutory disqualification requiring FINRA approval to associate.

</details>

**Q8001** (§15.1 · FINRA section 4) — How is the Securities and Exchange Commission led?

- A. By five commissioners appointed by the President to staggered five-year terms
- B. By seven governors appointed by the President to fourteen-year terms
- C. By a board elected by the FINRA member firms it supervises
- D. By nine commissioners chosen by Congress with no party limits

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The SEC is headed by five presidentially appointed commissioners serving staggered five-year terms, with no more than three from one political party.

*Remember:* SEC: 5 commissioners, appointed by the President, 5-year staggered terms, max 3 from one party.

</details>

**Q8002** (§15.1 · FINRA section 4) — A bank dealer's municipal securities department violates an MSRB fair-dealing rule. Which body would enforce the rule against the bank dealer?

- A. The MSRB's own enforcement staff
- B. FINRA's Department of Enforcement
- C. The bank dealer's federal banking regulator
- D. The municipal issuer whose bonds were sold

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The MSRB writes rules but cannot enforce them. FINRA and the SEC enforce MSRB rules for securities firms, while bank regulators enforce them for bank dealers.

*Remember:* MSRB writes muni rules but has no enforcement power; bank regulators enforce them for bank dealers.

</details>

**Q8003** (§15.1 · FINRA section 4) — A registered representative loses a FINRA disciplinary hearing and wants to appeal. Under FINRA's Code of Procedure, the first level of appeal is to:

- A. The SEC
- B. The state securities Administrator
- C. A federal court of appeals
- D. The National Adjudicatory Council

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Hearing panel decisions are appealed first to FINRA's National Adjudicatory Council (NAC). After that the SEC can review the case, followed by federal court.

*Remember:* FINRA appeals path: hearing panel, then NAC, then SEC, then federal court.

</details>

**Q8004** (§15.1 · FINRA section 4) — An investment adviser that manages $60 million of client assets and does not qualify for any special exemption generally registers with:

- A. The SEC
- B. FINRA
- C. The state securities Administrator
- D. The MSRB

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Advisers with at least $100 million in assets under management register with the SEC. Smaller advisers generally register with the states.

*Remember:* Investment advisers: $100 million+ AUM register with SEC; below that, with the state.

</details>

**Q8005** (§15.1 · FINRA section 4) — Which statement correctly contrasts broker-dealers and investment advisers?

- A. Advisers owe a fiduciary duty at all times; broker-dealers follow Reg BI for retail recommendations
- B. Broker-dealers are fiduciaries at all times; advisers follow Regulation Best Interest
- C. Both are supervised by FINRA as their self-regulatory organization
- D. Advisers register on Form BD; broker-dealers register on Form ADV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Investment advisers are fiduciaries at all times. Broker-dealers follow Reg BI when recommending to retail customers and suitability for institutional ones.

*Remember:* IA = fiduciary always (Form ADV, no SRO); BD = Reg BI for retail recommendations (Form BD, FINRA).

</details>

**Q8006** (§15.1 · FINRA section 4) — The SEC has all of the following powers EXCEPT:

- A. Approving rule changes proposed by FINRA
- B. Seeking injunctions and disgorgement in civil court
- C. Registering exchanges and transfer agents
- D. Prosecuting criminal securities fraud cases itself

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The SEC brings civil enforcement actions only. It refers criminal cases to the Department of Justice, which prosecutes them.

*Remember:* SEC = civil enforcement; criminal cases are referred to the Department of Justice.

</details>

**Q8007** (§15.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — A trader enters several large buy orders just below the best bid with no intention of letting them execute. Once other buyers push the price up, he sells shares he already owns and cancels the buy orders. This is:

- A. Front-running
- B. Marking the close
- C. Backing away
- D. Spoofing (layering)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Spoofing or layering means entering orders you never intend to execute to move the price, then cancelling them. It is market manipulation.

*Remember:* Spoofing/layering: orders entered without intent to execute, to move the price, then cancelled.

</details>

**Q8008** (§15.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — A rep learns that her firm's analysts will publish a strong buy rating on a stock tomorrow morning. She buys the stock for her own account this afternoon. This is:

- A. Front-running
- B. Interpositioning
- C. Painting the tape
- D. Permitted, because research reports become public

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Trading ahead of a firm's upcoming research report, to profit from the expected price move, is front-running.

*Remember:* Front-running: trading ahead of a large customer order or an upcoming research report.

</details>

**Q8009** (§15.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — A stock's IPO is priced at $20.00 per share. Which bid by the managing underwriter is permitted?

- A. A bid at $20.25 to show strong demand
- B. A disclosed stabilizing bid at $19.90
- C. An undisclosed bid at $19.90
- D. A bid at $21.00 to push trading above the offering price

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Stabilization is the only permitted price-supporting activity. The bid must be disclosed and placed at or below the public offering price.

*Remember:* Stabilizing bid: allowed only if disclosed and at or below the public offering price.

</details>

**Q8010** (§15.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — A customer buys $8,000 of stock in a cash account and sells it two days later without ever paying, planning to cover the purchase with the sale proceeds. What happens to the account?

- A. It is converted to a margin account
- B. It is frozen for 30 days
- C. It is frozen for 90 days
- D. It is closed and reported to FINRA

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Buying and selling without paying is freeriding. The account is frozen for 90 days, and during that time the customer must pay in full before any purchase.

*Remember:* Freeriding in a cash account results in a 90-day freeze.

</details>

**Q8011** (§15.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — A market maker quotes a stock at 25.10-25.20 for 500 shares. Another dealer tries to sell 500 shares at 25.10, and the market maker refuses the trade. This is:

- A. Backing away
- B. Capping
- C. Interpositioning
- D. Trading ahead

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A market maker must honor its firm quotes. Refusing to trade at a firm quote for the size shown is backing away.

*Remember:* Backing away: a market maker refusing to honor its own firm quote.

</details>

**Q8012** (§15.2.1 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following are forms of market manipulation?
I. Marking the close
II. Interpositioning
III. A disclosed stabilizing bid at the offering price
IV. Pegging

- A. I and III only
- B. II and IV only
- C. I, II and IV
- D. I, II, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Marking the close, interpositioning and pegging are all prohibited. A disclosed stabilizing bid at or below the offering price is the only permitted price support.

*Remember:* Only a disclosed stabilizing bid at or below the POP is permitted; the rest is manipulation.

</details>

**Q8013** (§15.2.2 · FINRA section 3) — When a regulator decides whether a customer's account was churned, the central question is whether:

- A. The customer lost money on most of the trades
- B. The account had written discretionary authority
- C. Each trade, viewed alone, was suitable
- D. Trading was excessive for the customer's objectives and resources

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Churning is trading that is excessive given the customer's objectives and resources, done to generate commissions. A loss in the account is not required.

*Remember:* Churning = excessive trading relative to the customer's profile; the rep's commissions matter, not the customer's losses.

</details>

**Q8014** (§15.2.2 · FINRA section 3) — A rep puts 10% of the money into a joint account with her husband, who is her customer. With the firm's and husband's prior written approval, she may share in the account's profits:

- A. Only up to 10%, matching her contribution
- B. In any proportion, because her husband is immediate family
- C. Not at all, since reps may never share in customer accounts
- D. Only after FINRA approves the arrangement

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Sharing requires prior written approval from the firm and the customer. Proportional sharing is not required when the customer is an immediate family member, such as a spouse.

*Remember:* Account sharing: written approval needed; proportional to contribution unless the customer is immediate family.

</details>

**Q8015** (§15.2.2 · FINRA section 3) — A nervous customer will buy a recommended stock only if the rep promises to cover any loss. The rep agrees to repay losses from his own funds. This arrangement is:

- A. Permitted if the customer signs a written agreement
- B. Permitted if a principal approves it in advance
- C. Prohibited in all cases
- D. Permitted only for immediate family accounts

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Reps and firms may never guarantee a customer against loss. No written agreement, approval or family relationship changes this.

*Remember:* Guaranteeing a customer against loss is always prohibited.

</details>

**Q8016** (§15.2.2 · FINRA section 3) — A rep recommends that a customer sell one fund family's Class A shares and buy another family's Class A fund with the same objective. The only result is a new front-end sales charge. This practice is:

- A. Switching
- B. Breakpoint selling
- C. Commingling
- D. Blanket recommending

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Moving a customer between fund families or variable annuities to create new sales charges, without a legitimate reason, is switching.

*Remember:* Switching: moving a customer between funds or VAs to generate new sales charges without reason.

</details>

**Q8017** (§15.2.2 · FINRA section 3) — Without asking, a rep buys 200 shares of a stock for a customer whose account is non-discretionary, expecting the customer to approve. The stock rises 15% and the customer is pleased. Which statement is true?

- A. There is no violation because the customer approved afterward
- B. There is a violation only if the customer files a complaint
- C. The trade is acceptable if it is confirmed within 24 hours
- D. The trade was unauthorized and a violation despite the profit

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A trade entered without the customer's permission or written discretionary authority is unauthorized. That is true even when the trade makes money.

*Remember:* Unauthorized trading is a violation even if it's profitable or later accepted.

</details>

**Q8018** (§15.2.2 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following is NOT a prohibited practice?

- A. Keeping customer checks in the rep's personal bank account until month-end
- B. Accurately telling a customer a bond pays its stated 5% coupon
- C. Holding a customer's position in a relative's account to avoid limits
- D. Telling a customer the SEC has approved a fund's prospectus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Accurately describing an issuer's own obligation, such as a bond's stated coupon, is not a guarantee. Commingling, parking and claiming SEC approval are all violations.

*Remember:* Describing an issuer's own obligation is fine; reps may not personally guarantee returns.

</details>

**Q8019** (§15.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — Before a failed drug trial is announced, a company executive sells 10,000 shares at $40. After the announcement the stock trades at $28. What is the maximum civil penalty under ITSFEA?

- A. $120,000
- B. $240,000
- C. $360,000
- D. $400,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The executive avoided a $120,000 loss. ITSFEA allows a civil penalty of up to three times the profit gained or loss avoided, which is $360,000.

1. Loss avoided = ($40 - $28) x 10,000 = $120,000
2. Penalty = 3 x $120,000 = $360,000

*Remember:* ITSFEA civil penalty = up to 3 x (profit gained or loss avoided).

</details>

**Q8020** (§15.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following would NOT be insider trading?
I. Buying a stock after overhearing an unconfirmed rumor at a restaurant
II. A lawyer buying shares of a target her client plans to acquire
III. An officer selling under a 10b5-1 plan set up a year earlier
IV. An officer telling a friend about a pending merger, without trading himself

- A. I and III
- B. II and IV
- C. I and IV
- D. II and III

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Trading on rumors and trading under a pre-arranged 10b5-1 plan are not insider trading. The lawyer is liable under misappropriation, and the officer is liable as a tipper even without trading.

*Remember:* Rumors, public info, mosaic research and 10b5-1 plans aren't insider trading; tipping and misappropriation are.

</details>

**Q8021** (§15.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — An employee of a financial printer typesets a confidential tender offer document and buys the target company's stock. He has no relationship with the target. He is liable under:

- A. Regulation FD
- B. The Section 16 short-swing rule
- C. No theory, since he is not a corporate insider
- D. The misappropriation theory

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Under the misappropriation theory, anyone who trades on confidential information taken from a source that trusted them is liable, even with no duty to the issuer.

*Remember:* Misappropriation theory: trading on confidential information taken from any source is insider trading.

</details>

**Q8022** (§15.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — A company director buys her company's stock in March and sells it at a profit in July. She had no material non-public information. Which statement is true?

- A. No rule applies because she had no inside information
- B. She must return the profit to the company under the short-swing rule
- C. She owes treble damages under ITSFEA
- D. Her broker-dealer must file a SAR on the trade

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Section 16 requires insiders to give back profits from purchases and sales within six months, whether or not they had MNPI.

*Remember:* Section 16 short-swing rule: insiders return profits on buys and sells within 6 months.

</details>

**Q8023** (§15.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — During a private call, a CFO accidentally reveals next quarter's sales outlook to a few institutional analysts. Under Regulation FD, the company must:

- A. Disclose the information to the public promptly
- B. Do nothing, because the disclosure was unintentional
- C. Notify FINRA within 30 days
- D. Bar the analysts from trading the stock for 6 months

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Reg FD requires simultaneous public disclosure for intentional selective disclosures and prompt public disclosure for accidental ones.

*Remember:* Reg FD: intentional selective disclosure means simultaneous public release; accidental means prompt public release.

</details>

**Q8024** (§15.2.3 · FINRA section 3) — While its investment bankers advise on a pending acquisition, a broker-dealer bars employees from trading or soliciting the target's stock and announces the ban internally. The company has been placed on the firm's:

- A. Watch list
- B. Restricted list
- C. Do-not-call list
- D. OFAC sanctions list

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** A restricted list limits trading, solicitation and research in an issuer while the firm has MNPI. A watch list is kept confidential and used only for monitoring.

*Remember:* Restricted list limits employee trading/solicitation; watch list is confidential monitoring by compliance.

</details>

**Q8025** (§15.3.1 · FINRA section 3) — A 79-year-old customer names her nephew as her trusted contact person. Which action may the firm take with the nephew?

- A. Accept trade instructions from him
- B. Let him request withdrawals from the account
- C. Ask him about the customer's health status or possible exploitation
- D. Send him copies of all account statements automatically

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** A trusted contact may be contacted about possible exploitation, the customer's contact information or health, or a guardian or POA. The trusted contact has no authority over the account.

*Remember:* Trusted contact (18+): firm may consult them, but they have no authority over the account.

</details>

**Q8026** (§15.3.1 · FINRA section 3) — Which customer is a specified adult under FINRA Rule 2165?

- A. A 45-year-old whose cognitive impairment keeps him from protecting his own interests
- B. A healthy 64-year-old retiree
- C. A 16-year-old beneficiary of an UTMA account
- D. A healthy 58-year-old widow who just inherited money

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** A specified adult is anyone age 65 or older, or anyone 18 or older with a mental or physical impairment that keeps them from protecting their own interests.

*Remember:* Specified adult: age 65+, or 18+ with an impairment preventing self-protection.

</details>

**Q8027** (§15.3.1 · FINRA section 3) — A firm places a temporary hold on a specified adult's disbursement and extends it once based on its own internal review. No regulator or agency has asked for more time. What is the maximum total length of the hold?

- A. 10 business days
- B. 15 business days
- C. 25 business days
- D. 55 business days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The initial hold is up to 15 business days, and the firm may extend it by 10 more. A further 30 days is available only if a regulator or agency requests it.

1. Initial hold: 15 business days
2. Firm extension: +10 business days
3. Total = 15 + 10 = 25 business days

*Remember:* Temporary hold: 15 business days + 10-day firm extension (+30 more only on regulator/agency request).

</details>

**Q8028** (§15.3.1 · FINRA section 3) — After placing a temporary hold because it suspects financial exploitation of a specified adult, the firm must notify the trusted contact and all authorized parties (other than suspected exploiters) within:

- A. 2 business days
- B. 5 business days
- C. 10 business days
- D. 30 calendar days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Notice to the trusted contact and all authorized parties must go out within 2 business days of the hold. Anyone suspected of the exploitation is not notified.

*Remember:* Temporary hold notice to trusted contact and authorized parties: within 2 business days.

</details>

**Q8029** (§15.3.1 · FINRA section 3) — Under Regulation S-P, a broker-dealer's customers have the right to opt out of having their nonpublic personal information shared with:

- A. Regulators examining the firm
- B. Nonaffiliated third parties
- C. The firm's own registered representatives
- D. Law enforcement acting under a subpoena

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Reg S-P gives customers the right to opt out of information sharing with nonaffiliated third parties. Customers also get a privacy notice at account opening and every year after.

*Remember:* Reg S-P: privacy notice at opening and annually; customers may opt out of sharing with nonaffiliated third parties.

</details>

**Q8030** (§15.3.2 · FINRA section 3) — A rep's firm files a Suspicious Activity Report on a customer's repeated cash deposits just under $10,000. The customer later asks the rep why compliance has been asking questions. The rep should:

- A. Explain that a SAR was filed so the customer can respond
- B. Tell the customer that a CTR was filed instead
- C. Suggest combining future deposits into one deposit over $10,000
- D. Not reveal that a SAR was filed

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** A customer may never be told that a SAR was filed. Deposits kept just under $10,000 to avoid a CTR are structuring, which is itself a crime.

*Remember:* Never tip off a customer that a SAR was filed; structuring to avoid CTRs is illegal.

</details>

**Q8031** (§15.3.2 · FINRA section 3) — A prospect describes a private fund that has paid a steady 2% every month for six years, even during market crashes. The manager won't provide audited statements, and withdrawals are often delayed. These facts most strongly suggest:

- A. A Ponzi scheme
- B. A pump-and-dump
- C. An advance-fee scheme
- D. A legitimate hedge fund strategy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Consistent high returns regardless of the market, no audited statements and trouble getting paid out are classic Ponzi red flags. Early investors are usually paid with new investors' money.

*Remember:* Ponzi red flags: steady high returns in any market, secrecy, unaudited statements, payout delays.

</details>

**Q8032** (§15.3.2 · FINRA section 3) — A whistleblower gives the SEC original information that leads to $4 million in sanctions. Under the Dodd-Frank program, the whistleblower's award would range from:

- A. $40,000 to $400,000
- B. $400,000 to $1,200,000
- C. $400,000 to $1,000,000
- D. $1,000,000 to $1,200,000

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Awards are 10% to 30% of sanctions when sanctions exceed $1 million. Of $4 million, that is $400,000 to $1,200,000.

1. Low end: 10% x $4,000,000 = $400,000
2. High end: 30% x $4,000,000 = $1,200,000

*Remember:* Dodd-Frank whistleblower award = 10%-30% of sanctions over $1 million.

</details>

**Q8033** (§15.3.2 · FINRA section 3) — Which of the following are anti-money laundering red flags?
I. A new customer is reluctant to provide identification
II. Funds are wired in from a high-risk country and withdrawn days later without being invested
III. A customer invests the same amount each month through payroll deduction
IV. Deposits arrive from unrelated third parties with no explanation

- A. I and II only
- B. III and IV only
- C. I, II and IV
- D. I, II, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Reluctance to provide ID, quick in-and-out wires with high-risk countries and unexplained third-party payments are all red flags. Regular payroll investing is normal behavior.

*Remember:* AML red flags: secrecy, reluctance to provide ID, high-risk wires, in-and-out funds, unexplained third-party payments.

</details>

**Q8034** (§15.4.1 · FINRA section 4) — Which law amended the Securities Exchange Act of 1934 to allow a national securities association to regulate the over-the-counter market?

- A. The Trust Indenture Act of 1939
- B. The Securities Acts Amendments of 1975
- C. The Maloney Act of 1938
- D. The Investment Advisers Act of 1940

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** The Maloney Act of 1938 allowed a national securities association (the NASD, now FINRA) to regulate the OTC market.

*Remember:* Maloney Act (1938): created the framework for NASD/FINRA to self-regulate the OTC market.

</details>

**Q8035** (§15.4.1 · FINRA section 4) — Regulation S-P, which requires broker-dealers to protect customers' financial privacy, was adopted under which law?

- A. The Sarbanes-Oxley Act
- B. The USA PATRIOT Act
- C. The Dodd-Frank Act
- D. The Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** The Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act of 1999 addressed the privacy of consumer financial information. The SEC carried this out through Regulation S-P.

*Remember:* Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act (1999) leads to Regulation S-P (privacy).

</details>

**Q8036** (§15.4.1 · FINRA section 4) — Which SEC regulation governs short sales, including the locate requirement before a short sale?

- A. Regulation M
- B. Regulation SHO
- C. Regulation NMS
- D. Regulation T

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Regulation SHO governs short selling, including locating shares before the sale. Naked shorting without a locate is a short-sale abuse.

*Remember:* Reg SHO = short sales; Reg M = distributions; Reg NMS = national market system; Reg T = margin.

</details>

**Q8037** (§15.4.1 · FINRA section 4) — Which law eased capital raising by creating equity crowdfunding and permitting general solicitation in Rule 506(c) offerings?

- A. The JOBS Act of 2012
- B. The Dodd-Frank Act of 2010
- C. The Sarbanes-Oxley Act of 2002
- D. The Securities Act of 1933

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The JOBS Act of 2012 created emerging growth company status, crowdfunding and Reg A+, and allowed general solicitation in 506(c) offerings.

*Remember:* JOBS Act (2012): EGCs, crowdfunding, Reg A+, general solicitation under 506(c).

</details>

**Q8038** (§15.4.2 · FINRA section 3) — A rep in New York (Eastern time) wants to cold-call a prospect in California (Pacific time, 3 hours behind). What is the earliest Eastern time the rep may place the call?

- A. 8:00 am
- B. 9:00 am
- C. 5:00 am
- D. 11:00 am

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Cold calls are allowed only from 8 am to 9 pm in the called party's time zone. 8 am Pacific is 11 am Eastern.

1. Earliest allowed: 8:00 am Pacific
2. Eastern = Pacific + 3 hours
3. 8:00 am + 3 = 11:00 am Eastern

*Remember:* TCPA calling window: 8 am-9 pm in the called party's local time.

</details>

**Q8039** (§15.4.2 · FINRA section 3) — Which call is exempt from the TCPA cold-calling restrictions, even if the number is on the National Do-Not-Call Registry?

- A. A call to a customer who last traded with the firm 12 months ago
- B. A call to a former customer whose last trade was 3 years ago
- C. A call to a number taken from a purchased list of local doctors
- D. A prerecorded sales message sent to a prospect's cell phone

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** An established business relationship within the past 18 months exempts the call. Older relationships, purchased lists and robocalls to cell phones without consent are not exempt.

*Remember:* TCPA established-relationship exemption: transaction or account within the past 18 months.

</details>

**Q8040** (§15.4.2 · FINRA section 3) — A cold caller must do all of the following EXCEPT:

- A. Give the caller's name and the firm's name
- B. Provide the firm's telephone number or address
- C. State that the call's purpose is to solicit securities business
- D. Disclose the rep's CRD number and exam history

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Callers must identify themselves, their firm, and the firm's phone number or address, and state that the call is to sell securities. CRD details are not required.

*Remember:* Cold-call disclosures: caller name, firm name, firm phone or address, and the securities sales purpose.

</details>

**Q8041** (§15.4.3 · FINRA section 4) — Within a 30-day period, a rep emails the same market commentary to 22 retail investors and 10 institutional investors. Under FINRA Rule 2210 this is:

- A. A retail communication, because it reached 32 people
- B. Correspondence
- C. An institutional communication
- D. A public appearance

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Only retail investors count toward the more-than-25 threshold for retail communications. Sending to 22 retail investors within 30 days is correspondence.

*Remember:* Correspondence: 25 or fewer retail investors in 30 days; institutional recipients don't count toward the threshold.

</details>

**Q8042** (§15.4.3 · FINRA section 4) — A broker-dealer that has been a FINRA member for five years creates a new mutual fund sales brochure. When must it be filed with FINRA?

- A. At least 10 business days before first use
- B. Within 30 days of first use
- C. Within 10 business days of first use
- D. It never needs to be filed

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Retail communications about investment companies, such as mutual funds and variable annuities, are filed within 10 business days of first use. Filing 10 days before first use applies to a new firm's first year.

*Remember:* Investment company retail communications: file within 10 business days of first use (new firms: 10 days before).

</details>

**Q8043** (§15.4.3 · FINRA section 4) — Which social media content requires prior approval by a registered principal?

- A. A reply posted in a real-time chat
- B. A direct message to one existing customer
- C. An interactive comment on a firm blog post
- D. A static profile page describing the rep's services

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Static content, such as a profile or background page, is a retail communication and needs principal pre-approval. Interactive posts are supervised like correspondence.

*Remember:* Social media: static content = retail communication (pre-approval); interactive content = supervised like correspondence.

</details>

**Q8044** (§15.4.3 · FINRA section 4) — A hypothetical variable annuity illustration used with retail investors may show a 0% gross rate of return and a maximum gross rate of:

- A. 6%
- B. 8%
- C. 10%
- D. 12%

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** VA hypothetical illustrations may show a 0% rate and other rates up to 12% gross. Otherwise, performance predictions and projections are prohibited.

*Remember:* VA illustrations: may show 0% and rates up to a maximum 12% gross.

</details>

**Q8045** (§15.4.3 · FINRA section 4) — A retail communication shows a mutual fund's performance. It must include all of the following EXCEPT:

- A. Standardized 1-, 5- and 10-year average annual total returns
- B. Returns that reflect the maximum sales charge
- C. A statement that past performance doesn't guarantee future results
- D. A projection of next year's expected return

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Fund performance must show standardized 1-, 5- and 10-year returns reflecting the maximum sales charge, with a past-performance disclaimer. Projections of future performance are prohibited.

*Remember:* Fund performance: 1/5/10-year standardized returns at maximum load plus a past-performance disclaimer; no projections.

</details>

**Q8046** (§15.4.3 · FINRA section 4) — Which of the following are institutional investors for purposes of FINRA's communications rules?
I. An insurance company
II. An individual with a $5 million portfolio
III. An employee benefit plan with 150 participants
IV. A registered investment adviser

- A. I and II only
- B. II and III only
- C. I, III and IV
- D. I, II, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Insurers, investment advisers and benefit plans with at least 100 participants are institutional investors. An individual with $5 million falls well short of the $50 million total-assets test.

*Remember:* Institutional: banks, insurers, investment companies, RIAs, $50M+ entities, plans with 100+ participants, FINRA members.

</details>

**Q8047** (§15.4.4 · FINRA section 4) — A customer whose shares are held in street name does not return voting instructions by the deadline. On which matter may the broker-dealer vote the shares?

- A. An uncontested election of directors
- B. Ratification of the company's independent auditor
- C. A proposed merger
- D. An advisory say-on-pay vote on executive compensation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Without instructions, a broker-dealer may vote street-name shares only on routine matters such as auditor ratification. Director elections, mergers and compensation are non-routine.

*Remember:* Uninstructed street-name shares: broker may vote only routine items, such as auditor ratification.

</details>

**Q8048** (§15.4.4 · FINRA section 4) — A broker-dealer holds shares in street name for its customers. Who pays the cost of forwarding proxy materials and annual reports to the beneficial owners?

- A. The issuer
- B. The broker-dealer
- C. The beneficial owners
- D. The transfer agent

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The broker-dealer must forward proxy materials and annual reports to beneficial owners, and the issuer pays for it.

*Remember:* Broker-dealer forwards proxy materials to street-name owners; the issuer pays the costs.

</details>

**Q8049** (§15.4.5 · FINRA section 4) — Ana passed the SIE in March 2025 but has never been associated with a member firm. To use her SIE result, she must pass a representative exam and become registered no later than:

- A. March 2026
- B. March 2027
- C. March 2029
- D. March 2030

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** SIE results stay valid for 4 years. Ana must pass a top-off exam and register by March 2029 or retake the SIE.

1. SIE validity = 4 years
2. March 2025 + 4 years = March 2029

*Remember:* SIE result valid 4 years from the pass date.

</details>

**Q8050** (§15.4.5 · FINRA section 4) — A candidate fails the Series 7 for the second time on June 1. Under FINRA's waiting-period rules, the earliest date she may retest is about:

- A. June 2
- B. July 1
- C. September 1
- D. November 28

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** After a first or second failure the wait is 30 days. The 180-day wait applies only after a third or later failure.

1. Second failure: 30-day wait
2. June 1 + 30 days = July 1

*Remember:* Exam retest waits: 30 days after 1st and 2nd failures; 180 days after 3rd and later.

</details>

**Q8051** (§15.4.5 · FINRA section 4) — A broker-dealer's Firm Element continuing education program must be based on:

- A. The Regulatory Element content FINRA assigns
- B. An annual needs analysis and a written training plan
- C. Each rep's score on the SIE exam
- D. A three-year cycle tied to each rep's registration anniversary

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The Firm Element is the firm's own annual training. It is built from an annual needs analysis and a written training plan, and covers products, sales practices, regulation and ethics.

*Remember:* Firm Element CE: annual, firm-run, based on a needs analysis and a written training plan.

</details>

**Q8052** (§15.4.5 · FINRA section 4) — A registered rep leaves the industry and does not enroll in the Maintaining Qualifications Program. Her qualification exams will lapse if she does not re-register within:

- A. 1 year
- B. 2 years
- C. 4 years
- D. 5 years

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Without MQP, exam qualifications lapse after 2 years out of the industry. With MQP and annual CE, they can be kept for up to 5 years.

*Remember:* Out of industry: qualifications lapse after 2 years; MQP extends this to up to 5 years with annual CE.

</details>

**Q8053** (§15.4.6 · FINRA section 3) — Under Regulation Best Interest, a broker-dealer must deliver Form CRS to a new retail customer:

- A. Within 30 days after the account is opened
- B. Only if the customer asks for it
- C. At or before the earliest of a recommendation, an order or account opening
- D. With the first quarterly account statement

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Form CRS, the customer relationship summary, must be delivered at or before the earliest of a recommendation, an order or the opening of an account.

*Remember:* Form CRS: delivered at or before the earliest of a recommendation, order or account opening.

</details>

**Q8054** (§15.4.6 · FINRA section 3) — Under Reg BI's conflict-of-interest obligation, which of the following must a broker-dealer eliminate?

- A. Commission-based compensation for retail accounts
- B. A firm limited to selling proprietary funds
- C. A sales contest rewarding reps for selling a specific fund within one quarter
- D. Differences in fees between share classes

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Reg BI requires firms to eliminate sales contests, quotas, bonuses and non-cash compensation tied to specific securities within a limited time. Other conflicts may be disclosed and mitigated.

*Remember:* Reg BI: eliminate time-limited sales contests and quotas tied to specific securities; disclose and mitigate other conflicts.

</details>

**Q8055** (§15.4.6 · FINRA section 4) — A registered rep's spouse wants to open an account at a different broker-dealer to trade individual stocks. Under FINRA Rule 3210, which is required?

- A. Nothing, because the account is in the spouse's name
- B. Prior written consent from the executing firm's CEO
- C. Approval from FINRA's Department of Enforcement
- D. Prior written consent from the rep's employing firm

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Rule 3210 covers accounts in which the rep has a beneficial interest, including a spouse's account. The employer must give prior written consent and may ask for duplicate confirmations and statements.

*Remember:* Outside accounts (Rule 3210): employer's prior written consent; covers spouse and dependent accounts; funds-only accounts exempt.

</details>

**Q8056** (§15.4.6 · FINRA section 4) — In February, a rep gives a $180 gift to a customer's purchasing manager. In June, the rep takes the manager to a $400 dinner and attends. Under FINRA Rule 3220, what is the most the rep can spend on another business gift to that person this year?

- A. $0
- B. $120
- C. $200
- D. $300

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** The limit is $300 per person per year. Business entertainment the giver attends doesn't count, so only the $180 gift counts against the limit, leaving $120.

1. Annual limit: $300 per person
2. Dinner attended by rep = entertainment, excluded
3. $300 - $180 = $120 remaining

*Remember:* Gift limit = $300 per person per year; entertainment the giver attends doesn't count.

</details>

**Q8057** (§15.4.6 · FINRA section 4) — If the firm's written procedures permit it, a registered rep may borrow money from which of these customers?
I. Her mother
II. A bank in the business of lending
III. A customer she knows only through his brokerage account
IV. A registered person at her own firm

- A. I and II only
- B. I, II and IV
- C. III and IV only
- D. I, II, III and IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Allowed lenders include immediate family, lending institutions, registered persons at the same firm, and people with a personal or business relationship outside the brokerage account. A purely brokerage relationship is not enough.

*Remember:* Rule 3240 lenders: immediate family, lending institutions, same-firm registered persons, outside personal/business relationships.

</details>

**Q8058** (§15.4.7 · FINRA section 4) — Which record must a broker-dealer keep for the life of the firm?

- A. Minute books
- B. Blotters
- C. Order tickets
- D. Customer complaint files

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Organizational documents such as articles of incorporation, minute books and stock certificate books are kept for the life of the firm.

*Remember:* Permanent records: articles of incorporation/partnership, minute books, stock certificate books.

</details>

**Q8059** (§15.4.7 · FINRA section 4) — A customer closes her account in June 2026. Under the record retention rules, the firm must keep her new account form until at least:

- A. June 2029
- B. June 2030
- C. June 2031
- D. June 2032

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Customer account records must be kept for 6 years after the account is closed. June 2026 plus 6 years is June 2032.

1. Retention = 6 years after the account closes
